# Grammar Lab: The Complete Course

Every lesson of the Grammar Lab course, written out in full, in Oomba's own words. Assembled from the five band lesson files, then reviewed and corrected (see the review notes): k-2-lessons.md, grades-3-5-lessons.md, grades-6-8-part-1-lessons.md, grades-6-8-part-2-lessons.md, grades-9-12-lessons.md. Bands run in order: K-2, 3-5, 6-8, 9-12. Topics shared across pathways are written once and cross-referenced from the other pathways. US spelling throughout. No em dashes anywhere.

# Grammar Lab, K-2 Lessons

Band: K-2. Pathways: 6. Topics: 12. All lesson text is original, written fresh for Oomba. Sentences are short and concrete, about animals, toys, food, and school. US spelling throughout. No em dashes anywhere.

Topic count check: 10 topics are written in full below, and 2 topics are shared with another pathway and are cross-referenced. 10 plus 2 is 12, which matches the map. Quiz items: 10 full topics times 12 items is 120 items.

## Pathway 1: Adjectives and Adverbs (2 topics)

### Topic: Identifying Adjectives

**Teaching Card**
- Definition: An adjective is a word that tells about a noun. It tells what kind, how many, or which one.
- Worked example: "The fluffy kitten slept in the basket." Fluffy is the adjective. Kitten is the noun it tells about.
- How it works: Fluffy tells what kind of kitten it is.
- Tip box: "The yellow bus." Yellow tells about the bus. "Five birds." Five tells how many birds.

**Quiz**
1. "The round ball rolled down the hill." Answer: round. Hint: Look for the word that tells what shape the ball is.
2. "Ana wore a purple hat." Answer: purple. Hint: Look for the word that tells what color the hat is.
3. "The sleepy bear yawned in the cave." Answer: sleepy. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the bear feels.
4. "Sam ate a sweet apple." Answer: sweet. Hint: Look for the word that tells what the apple tastes like.
5. "The long snake slid through the grass." Answer: long. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the snake looks.
6. "A tiny ant carried a crumb." Answer: tiny. Hint: Look for the word that tells the size of the ant.
7. "The brave knight held up his sword." Answer: brave. Hint: Look for the word that tells what kind of knight he is.
8. "Leo kicked the muddy ball." Answer: muddy. Hint: Look for the word that tells what the ball looks like.
9. "Three frogs sat on the log." Answer: Three. Hint: Look for the word that tells how many frogs there are.
10. "The hot soup warmed my hands." Answer: hot. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the soup feels.
11. "A gentle horse walked down the path." Answer: gentle. Hint: Look for the word that tells what kind of horse it is.
12. "The spotted pup chased the ball." Answer: spotted. Hint: Look for the word that tells what the pup looks like.

### Topic: Identifying Adverbs

**Teaching Card**
- Definition: An adverb is a word that tells about a verb. Some adverbs tell how something is done. Many of these adverbs end in -ly.
- Worked example: "The puppy ate quickly." Quickly is the adverb. Ate is the verb it tells about.
- How it works: Quickly tells how the puppy ate.
- Tip box: "The bee buzzed loudly." Loudly tells how the bee buzzed. "Nia hums softly." Softly tells how Nia hums.

**Quiz**
1. "The cat slowly licked its paw." Answer: slowly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the cat licked its paw.
2. "Priya sang loudly at school." Answer: loudly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Priya sang.
3. "Softly, the kitten purred." Answer: Softly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the kitten purred.
4. "The dog wagged its tail happily." Answer: happily. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the dog wagged its tail.
5. "Mateo carefully carried the eggs." Answer: carefully. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Mateo carried the eggs.
6. "Amara runs fast in the race." Answer: fast. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Amara runs.
7. "The baby clapped her hands gently." Answer: gently. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the baby clapped her hands.
8. "Kenji draws well." Answer: well. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Kenji draws.
9. "Zoe neatly folded her shirt." Answer: neatly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Zoe folded her shirt.
10. "Quickly, the bear grabbed the fish." Answer: Quickly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the bear grabbed the fish.
11. "The class waited quietly for lunch." Answer: quietly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the class waited.
12. "Lily kindly shared her snack with Omar." Answer: kindly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Lily shared her snack.

## Pathway 2: Capitalization (2 topics)

### Topic: Capitalizing People's Names and Dates

**Teaching Card**
- Definition: Names of people and names of days and months start with a big letter. A big letter is called a capital letter.
- Worked example: "My friend sam was born on july 3." The words that need big letters are Sam and July. The correct sentence is "My friend Sam was born on July 3."
- How it works: Sam is a person's name, and July is a month, so both start with a big letter.
- Tip box: "Today is monday." Monday is a day, so it starts with a big letter. The correct sentence is "Today is Monday." "I play with leo." Leo is a name, so it starts with a big letter. The correct sentence is "I play with Leo."

**Quiz**
1. "My friend ana likes to draw." Answer: Ana. Hint: A person's name starts with a big letter.
2. "We go to school on monday." Answer: Monday. Hint: Days of the week start with a big letter.
3. "My birthday is in june." Answer: June. Hint: Months start with a big letter.
4. "Sam and leo play soccer." Answer: Leo. Hint: A person's name starts with a big letter.
5. "The party is on friday." Answer: Friday. Hint: Days of the week start with a big letter.
6. "My dog max ran to the park." Answer: Max. Hint: A pet's name is a name, so it starts with a big letter.
7. "School starts in september." Answer: September. Hint: Months start with a big letter.
8. "Ana met her friend sam at lunch." Answer: Sam. Hint: A person's name starts with a big letter.
9. "We swim on tuesday." Answer: Tuesday. Hint: Days of the week start with a big letter.
10. "The fair is in october." Answer: October. Hint: Months start with a big letter.
11. "Leo gave a book to ana." Answer: Ana. Hint: A person's name starts with a big letter.
12. "My mom was born in may." Answer: May. Hint: Months start with a big letter.

### Topic: Rules of Capitalizing Titles

**Teaching Card**
- Definition: The title of a book, a song, or a movie is its name. The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
- Worked example: We read the book "the brave little fox." The title is "The Brave Little Fox." The first word and the big words, Brave, Little, and Fox, start with big letters.
- How it works: A title is a name, so its first word and its big words start with big letters.
- Tip box: The song is called "jump, little frog." The title is "Jump, Little Frog." We watched "the bear and the bees." The title is "The Bear and the Bees."

**Quiz**
1. "My favorite book is 'the brave little fox.'" Answer: The Brave Little Fox. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
2. "We sang 'my yellow kite.'" Answer: My Yellow Kite. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
3. "Leo read 'a day at the farm.'" Answer: A Day at the Farm. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
4. "The movie is called 'the lost puppy.'" Answer: The Lost Puppy. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
5. "Ana likes the song 'rain on my roof.'" Answer: Rain on My Roof. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
6. "Our class read 'the big green frog.'" Answer: The Big Green Frog. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
7. "Sam wrote a story called 'my trip to the zoo.'" Answer: My Trip to the Zoo. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
8. "The book 'bears in the snow' is funny." Answer: Bears in the Snow. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
9. "We watched 'the magic pond.'" Answer: The Magic Pond. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
10. "Leo sang 'the happy farmer.'" Answer: The Happy Farmer. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
11. "My favorite story is 'the sleepy owl.'" Answer: The Sleepy Owl. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.
12. "Ana read 'dogs and ducks at play.'" Answer: Dogs and Ducks at Play. Hint: The first word and the big words in a title start with big letters.

## Pathway 3: Commas (2 topics)

### Topic: Commas with Single Words in a Series

**Teaching Card**
- Definition: When you list three or more things, put a comma between them. A comma is a small mark that tells the reader to pause.
- Worked example: "I packed a sandwich an apple and a cookie." With commas: "I packed a sandwich, an apple, and a cookie." The commas split the list into parts.
- How it works: The commas go between the things in the list, so each thing stands on its own.
- Tip box: "We saw a lion a tiger and a bear." With commas: "We saw a lion, a tiger, and a bear." "I like red blue and green." With commas: "I like red, blue, and green."

**Quiz**
1. "My bag has a book a pencil and a snack." Answer: My bag has a book, a pencil, and a snack. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.
2. "We need milk eggs and bread." Answer: We need milk, eggs, and bread. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.
3. "Sam likes cats dogs and birds." Answer: Sam likes cats, dogs, and birds. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.
4. "The box holds crayons markers and paper." Answer: The box holds crayons, markers, and paper. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.
5. "Ana wore a hat gloves and boots." Answer: Ana wore a hat, gloves, and boots. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.
6. "We saw monkeys zebras and seals at the zoo." Answer: We saw monkeys, zebras, and seals at the zoo. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.
7. "Leo ate rice beans and corn." Answer: Leo ate rice, beans, and corn. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.
8. "The garden grows peas carrots and beans." Answer: The garden grows peas, carrots, and beans. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.
9. "My lunch has a sandwich an orange and juice." Answer: My lunch has a sandwich, an orange, and juice. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.
10. "We painted the wall red yellow and blue." Answer: We painted the wall red, yellow, and blue. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.
11. "The farm has cows pigs and hens." Answer: The farm has cows, pigs, and hens. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.
12. "Ana packed socks shoes and a jacket." Answer: Ana packed socks, shoes, and a jacket. Hint: Put a comma between each thing in a list.

### Topic: Commas with Prepositional Phrases

**Teaching Card**
- Definition: Sometimes a sentence starts with a short phrase that tells when or where. Put a comma after that phrase.
- Worked example: "In the morning we eat breakfast." With a comma: "In the morning, we eat breakfast." The comma comes after the starting phrase.
- How it works: The comma gives a small pause after the phrase that tells when.
- Tip box: "After lunch we read books." With a comma: "After lunch, we read books." "On Monday we go to the park." With a comma: "On Monday, we go to the park."

**Quiz**
1. "In the garden the bees buzz all day." Answer: In the garden, the bees buzz all day. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.
2. "After school Sam plays soccer." Answer: After school, Sam plays soccer. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.
3. "On Tuesday we visit the library." Answer: On Tuesday, we visit the library. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.
4. "In the winter the pond turns to ice." Answer: In the winter, the pond turns to ice. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.
5. "Before lunch Ana washes her hands." Answer: Before lunch, Ana washes her hands. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.
6. "At night the stars come out." Answer: At night, the stars come out. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.
7. "In the spring the flowers bloom." Answer: In the spring, the flowers bloom. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.
8. "After dinner Leo reads a book." Answer: After dinner, Leo reads a book. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.
9. "On the farm the hens lay eggs." Answer: On the farm, the hens lay eggs. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.
10. "In the rain the frogs sing." Answer: In the rain, the frogs sing. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.
11. "Before bed Sam brushes his teeth." Answer: Before bed, Sam brushes his teeth. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.
12. "At the beach we build sand castles." Answer: At the beach, we build sand castles. Hint: Put a comma after the short phrase at the start.

## Pathway 4: Commonly Confused Words (1 topic)

### Topic: "Your" vs. "You're"

**Teaching Card**
- Definition: "Your" means it belongs to you. "You're" is a short way to say "you are." The mark in "you're" takes the place of the letter a.
- Worked example: "Is this your lunch box?" Your tells us the lunch box belongs to you. "You're a fast runner." You're means you are.
- How it works: If you can swap in "you are," use "you're." If it shows belonging, use "your."
- Tip box: "Your dog is cute." The dog belongs to you. "You're my best friend." You're means you are.

**Quiz**
1. "Is this ___ red bike?" Answer: your. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.
2. "___ a great helper today." Answer: You're. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.
3. "___ going to love this story." Answer: You're. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.
4. "Please put ___ toys in the box." Answer: your. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.
5. "___ lunch is on the table." Answer: Your. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.
6. "___ the fastest runner in our class." Answer: You're. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.
7. "Wash ___ hands before lunch." Answer: your. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.
8. "___ my favorite singer." Answer: You're. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.
9. "___ doing a super job." Answer: You're. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.
10. "Is this ___ new coat?" Answer: your. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.
11. "Take ___ umbrella. It is raining." Answer: your. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.
12. "___ invited to the party." Answer: You're. Hint: Try saying "you are" in the blank. If it makes sense, use you're. If it shows something belongs to you, use your.

## Pathway 5: Identifying Parts of Speech I (3 topics)

### Topic: Identifying Common Nouns

**Teaching Card**
- Definition: A noun is a word that names a person, a place, or a thing. A common noun names any person, place, or thing, not one special one.
- Worked example: "The dog ran to the park." Dog is a noun. It names a thing. Park is a noun. It names a place.
- How it works: Ask, "Does this word name a person, a place, or a thing?" If yes, it is a noun.
- Tip box: "The teacher read a book." Teacher and book are nouns. "We ate lunch in the cafeteria." Lunch and cafeteria are nouns.

**Quiz**
1. "The dog barked loudly." Answer: dog. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a thing.
2. "The baby cried." Answer: baby. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a person.
3. "The sun shone brightly." Answer: sun. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a thing.
4. "My teacher smiled." Answer: teacher. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a person.
5. "The frog jumped high." Answer: frog. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a thing.
6. "The library opened early." Answer: library. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a place.
7. "A bird sang sweetly." Answer: bird. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a thing.
8. "The cook hummed happily." Answer: cook. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a person.
9. "The rain fell softly." Answer: rain. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a thing.
10. "Our school is big." Answer: school. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a place.
11. "The horse galloped fast." Answer: horse. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a thing.
12. "The singer bowed." Answer: singer. Hint: A noun names a person, a place, or a thing. Look for the word that names a person.

### Topic: Identifying Action Verbs

**Teaching Card**
- Definition: A verb is a word that tells what someone or something does. An action verb shows an action you can do.
- Worked example: "The cat jumps over the fence." Jumps is the verb. It tells what the cat does.
- How it works: Ask, "What does it do?" The word that answers is the action verb.
- Tip box: "Sam runs to school." Runs is the verb. "The bird sings a song." Sings is the verb.

**Quiz**
1. "The dog runs to the park." Answer: runs. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what the dog does.
2. "Ana kicks the ball." Answer: kicks. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what Ana does.
3. "The fish swims in the pond." Answer: swims. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what the fish does.
4. "Leo climbs the big tree." Answer: climbs. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what Leo does.
5. "The bee buzzes around the flowers." Answer: buzzes. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what the bee does.
6. "Sam reads a funny book." Answer: reads. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what Sam does.
7. "The horse gallops across the field." Answer: gallops. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what the horse does.
8. "Ana draws a picture of a cat." Answer: draws. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what Ana does.
9. "The frog hops onto a rock." Answer: hops. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what the frog does.
10. "Leo builds a tall tower." Answer: builds. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what Leo does.
11. "The bird sings in the morning." Answer: sings. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what the bird does.
12. "Sam eats a red apple." Answer: eats. Hint: An action verb tells what someone does. Look for what Sam does.

### Topic: Identifying Adjectives

Shared with the Identifying Adjectives topic in Pathway 1: Adjectives and Adverbs. See Identifying Adjectives above.

## Pathway 6: Verbs I: Simple Tenses (2 topics)

### Topic: Identifying Action Verbs

Shared with the Identifying Action Verbs topic in Pathway 5: Identifying Parts of Speech I. See Identifying Action Verbs above.

### Topic: Using Action Verbs in Simple Tenses

**Teaching Card**
- Definition: Verbs change to show when an action happens. "Today" means it happens now. "Yesterday" means it already happened. "Tomorrow" means it will happen later.
- Worked example: "Today Sam walks to school. Yesterday Sam walked to school. Tomorrow Sam will walk to school." Walks is now. Walked is past. Will walk is later.
- How it works: The word that tells when, like today or yesterday, helps you pick the right verb form.
- Tip box: "Today Ana sings. Yesterday Ana sang." "Today Leo plays. Tomorrow Leo will play."

**Quiz**
1. "Today, the dog ___ (play) in the yard." Answer: plays. Hint: Today means now, so use the present form.
2. "Yesterday, Ana ___ (draw) a picture." Answer: drew. Hint: Yesterday means it already happened, so use the past form.
3. "Tomorrow, Leo will ___ (fly) a kite." Answer: fly. Hint: Tomorrow means it will happen later, so use will plus the verb.
4. "Today, Sam ___ (eat) his lunch." Answer: eats. Hint: Today means now, so use the present form.
5. "Yesterday, the cat ___ (climb) up the tree." Answer: climbed. Hint: Yesterday means it already happened, so use the past form.
6. "Tomorrow, we will ___ (play) soccer." Answer: play. Hint: Tomorrow means it will happen later, so use will plus the verb.
7. "Today, the bird ___ (sing) a sweet song." Answer: sings. Hint: Today means now, so use the present form.
8. "Yesterday, Sam ___ (walk) to school." Answer: walked. Hint: Yesterday means it already happened, so use the past form.
9. "Tomorrow, Ana will ___ (bake) a cake." Answer: bake. Hint: Tomorrow means it will happen later, so use will plus the verb.
10. "Today, Leo ___ (ride) his bike." Answer: rides. Hint: Today means now, so use the present form.
11. "Yesterday, the frog ___ (jump) into the pond." Answer: jumped. Hint: Yesterday means it already happened, so use the past form.
12. "Tomorrow, Sam will ___ (visit) his grandma." Answer: visit. Hint: Tomorrow means it will happen later, so use will plus the verb.
# Grammar Lab: Grades 3-5 Lessons

Band: Grades 3-5. Pathways: 17. Topic slots: 28. Distinct lessons written in full: 23 (5 slots are shared across pathways and are cross-referenced, not repeated). Quiz items: 276 (12 per lesson).

All text is original, written fresh for Oomba. US spelling throughout. No em dashes anywhere.

Mastery rules for every quiz: two correct answers in a row complete a level; a wrong answer restarts the current level's progress and shows the hint; a standard lesson has 3 levels and ends in mastery after 12 answered questions.

## Pathway 1: Adjectives and Adverbs

### Topic: Identifying Adjectives

Teaching card:
- Definition: An adjective is a word that describes a noun. It tells what kind, how many, or which one.
- Worked example: "The fluffy kitten slept in the sun." Fluffy is the adjective. Kitten is the noun it describes.
- Explanation: Fluffy tells what kind of kitten it is.
- Tip box: "The green frog." Green describes the frog. "The five balloons." Five tells how many balloons.

Quiz (tap the adjective):
1. "The brave firefighter climbed the ladder." Answer: brave. Hint: Look for the word that tells what kind of firefighter.
2. "Mia baked a sweet cake." Answer: sweet. Hint: Look for the word that tells what the cake tastes like.
3. "The noisy parrot talked all morning." Answer: noisy. Hint: Look for the word that tells what kind of parrot.
4. "Leo found a smooth stone by the river." Answer: smooth. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the stone feels.
5. "Seven ducks waddled across the yard." Answer: Seven. Hint: Look for the word that tells how many ducks.
6. "The cold rain fell on the roof." Answer: cold. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the rain feels.
7. "Ana wore a striped shirt to school." Answer: striped. Hint: Look for the word that tells what the shirt looks like.
8. "The giant pumpkin sat on the porch." Answer: giant. Hint: Look for the word that tells what size the pumpkin is.
9. "A gentle breeze moved the curtains." Answer: gentle. Hint: Look for the word that tells what kind of breeze.
10. "Sam built a wooden bridge from blocks." Answer: wooden. Hint: Look for the word that tells what the bridge is made of.
11. "The round moon rose over the hills." Answer: round. Hint: Look for the word that tells what shape the moon is.
12. "The sleepy kittens curled up in the basket." Answer: sleepy. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the kittens feel.

### Topic: Adverbs That Tell How, When, and Where

Teaching card:
- Definition: An adverb is a word that tells more about a verb. Many adverbs tell how, when, or where an action happens.
- Worked example: "Nadia whispered softly to her baby brother." Softly is the adverb. It tells how Nadia whispered.
- Explanation: Ask how, when, or where about the verb. The word that answers is the adverb.
- Tip box: "We will swim tomorrow." Tomorrow tells when. "Dad waited downstairs." Downstairs tells where.

Quiz (tap the adverb):
1. "Tomas quietly closed the lid of the piano." Answer: quietly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Tomas closed the lid.
2. "Yesterday our class planted sunflower seeds in the garden." Answer: Yesterday. Hint: Look for the word that tells when the class planted the seeds.
3. "The children played outside during recess." Answer: outside. Hint: Look for the word that tells where the children played.
4. "Grandma searched everywhere for her reading glasses." Answer: everywhere. Hint: Look for the word that tells where Grandma searched.
5. "Kwame carefully poured the lemonade into each cup." Answer: carefully. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Kwame poured the lemonade.
6. "The new library books will arrive soon." Answer: soon. Hint: Look for the word that tells when the books will arrive.
7. "We visited the aquarium today with our cousins." Answer: today. Hint: Look for the word that tells when we visited the aquarium.
8. "Mr. Haddad asked us to stack the chairs here." Answer: here. Hint: Look for the word that tells where we should stack the chairs.
9. "The puppy proudly carried the stick across the yard." Answer: proudly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how the puppy carried the stick.
10. "Jin finished her science project early." Answer: early. Hint: Look for the word that tells when Jin finished her project.
11. "Cheerfully, Rafael greeted every guest at the party." Answer: Cheerfully. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Rafael greeted the guests.
12. "The kitten followed Ayesha upstairs after dinner." Answer: upstairs. Hint: Look for the word that tells where the kitten followed Ayesha.

## Pathway 2: Building Compound and Complex Sentences

### Topic: Combining Ideas to Make Compound Sentences

Teaching card:
- Definition: A compound sentence joins two complete thoughts with a word like and, but, or so. Each part could stand alone as a sentence.
- Worked example: "The rain stopped, and the sun came out." "The rain stopped" is one complete thought. "The sun came out" is another. The word and joins them.
- Explanation: The word and connects two complete thoughts into one longer sentence.
- Tip box: "Sam likes soccer, but Ana likes tennis." But joins two thoughts. "We can walk, or we can ride our bikes." Or joins two thoughts.

Quiz (join the two thoughts into one compound sentence):
1. "The dog barked. The cat hid." Answer: The dog barked, and the cat hid. Hint: Both things happened. Pick the joining word that adds one thought to the other.
2. "It was cold. We wore our coats." Answer: It was cold, so we wore our coats. Hint: Wearing coats was the result of the cold. Pick the joining word that shows a result.
3. "Leo wanted to play outside. It started to rain." Answer: Leo wanted to play outside, but it started to rain. Hint: The rain was not what Leo wanted. Pick the joining word that shows a difference.
4. "We can bake cookies. We can make popcorn." Answer: We can bake cookies, or we can make popcorn. Hint: These are two choices. Pick the joining word that shows a choice.
5. "The class read a book. The class acted out the story." Answer: The class read a book, and the class acted out the story. Hint: Both things happened in the same class. Pick the joining word that adds one thought to the other.
6. "Ana practiced the piano. Her brother played the drums." Answer: Ana practiced the piano, and her brother played the drums. Hint: Two people did two things. Pick the joining word that adds one thought to the other.
7. "The store was closed. We went home." Answer: The store was closed, so we went home. Hint: Going home happened because the store was closed. Pick the joining word that shows a result.
8. "Sam is fast. Leo is faster." Answer: Sam is fast, but Leo is faster. Hint: Sam and Leo are not the same. Pick the joining word that shows a difference.
9. "We can plant carrots. We can plant beans." Answer: We can plant carrots, or we can plant beans. Hint: These are two choices. Pick the joining word that gives a choice between them.
10. "The baby was tired. She fell asleep." Answer: The baby was tired, so she fell asleep. Hint: Falling asleep happened because the baby was tired. Pick the joining word that shows a result.
11. "Mia likes apples. She does not like pears." Answer: Mia likes apples, but she does not like pears. Hint: One thought is a like and one is a dislike. Pick the joining word that shows a difference.
12. "The sun set. The stars came out." Answer: The sun set, and the stars came out. Hint: One thing happened after the other. Pick the joining word that adds one thought to the other.

### Topic: Adding Ideas with a Middle Connecting Word

Teaching card:
- Definition: A complex sentence adds an extra idea to a main thought with a connecting word in the middle, like because, when, or after. The extra idea cannot stand alone.
- Worked example: "We stayed inside because it was raining." "We stayed inside" is the main thought. "Because it was raining" adds the reason.
- Explanation: Because connects the reason to the main thought.
- Tip box: "Sam smiled when he saw his friend." When adds the time. "We packed our bags after lunch." After adds the time.

Quiz (combine the two thoughts with a connecting word in the middle):
1. "The garden grew. It rained all week." Answer: The garden grew because it rained all week. Hint: Rain all week is the reason the garden grew. Pick the connecting word that gives a reason.
2. "Leo ran to the door. He heard the bell." Answer: Leo ran to the door when he heard the bell. Hint: Hearing the bell tells the moment Leo ran. Pick the connecting word that tells the time.
3. "We washed the dishes. We ate dinner." Answer: We washed the dishes after we ate dinner. Hint: Dinner came first, then the dishes. Pick the connecting word that tells the time.
4. "Ana wore her boots. The mud was deep." Answer: Ana wore her boots because the mud was deep. Hint: The deep mud is the reason for the boots. Pick the connecting word that gives a reason.
5. "The cat hid. The dog barked." Answer: The cat hid when the dog barked. Hint: The cat hid as soon as the dog barked. Pick the connecting word that tells the time.
6. "Sam practiced every day. He got better." Answer: Sam got better because he practiced every day. Hint: Practicing is the reason Sam got better. Start with the main thought, then pick the connecting word that gives a reason.
7. "We will go swimming. The pool opens." Answer: We will go swimming when the pool opens. Hint: The pool opening tells the time we will swim. Pick the connecting word that tells the time.
8. "Mia read her book. The lights went out." Answer: Mia read her book until the lights went out. Hint: Mia kept reading up to the moment the lights went out. Pick the connecting word that tells how long it lasted.
9. "The team cheered. They won the game." Answer: The team cheered because they won the game. Hint: Winning is the reason the team cheered. Pick the connecting word that gives a reason.
10. "Leo brushed his teeth. He went to bed." Answer: Leo brushed his teeth before he went to bed. Hint: Brushing came first, then bed. Pick the connecting word that tells the time.
11. "We stayed on the path. We did not want to get lost." Answer: We stayed on the path because we did not want to get lost. Hint: Not wanting to get lost is the reason. Pick the connecting word that gives a reason.
12. "Ana watered the plants. The sun came up." Answer: Ana watered the plants after the sun came up. Hint: The sun came up first, then Ana watered the plants. Pick the connecting word that tells the time.

---

## Pathway 3: Capitalization

### Topic: Capitalizing People's Names and Dates

Teaching card:
- Definition: Capitalize the first letter of a person's name and the names of days, months, and holidays. A capital letter shows that the word names one special person or time.
- Worked example: "Sam and Ana will visit on Tuesday in March." Sam, Ana, Tuesday, and March all start with capital letters.
- Explanation: Names of people, days, and months always begin with a capital letter.
- Tip box: "Leo was born on Friday, June 5." Friday and June are capitalized. "We met Grandma Rose at the park." Grandma Rose is a name, so it is capitalized.

Quiz (fix the capitalization):
1. "my friend sam likes to read." Answer: My friend Sam likes to read. Hint: Capitalize the first word of a sentence and the name of a person.
2. "we will go to the fair on saturday." Answer: We will go to the fair on Saturday. Hint: Capitalize the name of a day.
3. "ana and leo planted seeds in april." Answer: Ana and Leo planted seeds in April. Hint: Capitalize names of people and months.
4. "the class party is on friday, december 12." Answer: The class party is on Friday, December 12. Hint: Capitalize the day and the month.
5. "did ben see the parade on july 4?" Answer: Did Ben see the parade on July 4? Hint: Capitalize names of people and months.
6. "mrs. lee read a story on monday." Answer: Mrs. Lee read a story on Monday. Hint: Capitalize a person's title and name, and the day.
7. "we celebrate thanksgiving in november." Answer: We celebrate Thanksgiving in November. Hint: Capitalize the name of a holiday and the month.
8. "tom and june walked to school on wednesday." Answer: Tom and June walked to school on Wednesday. Hint: Capitalize names of people and the day.
9. "my birthday is in may." Answer: My birthday is in May. Hint: Capitalize the name of a month.
10. "sara met dr. patel on tuesday." Answer: Sara met Dr. Patel on Tuesday. Hint: Capitalize names of people and the day.
11. "the picnic is on sunday, august 9." Answer: The picnic is on Sunday, August 9. Hint: Capitalize the day and the month.
12. "liam played soccer with max in october." Answer: Liam played soccer with Max in October. Hint: Capitalize names of people and the month.

### Topic: Rules of Capitalizing Titles

Teaching card:
- Definition: Capitalize the important words in the title of a book, movie, song, or story. Small words like a, an, and, and the stay lowercase unless they are the first word.
- Worked example: "We read the book The Brave Little Fox." Brave, Little, and Fox are capitalized. The word The is capitalized because it is the first word.
- Explanation: Important words in a title begin with capital letters.
- Tip box: "Ana sang the song 'The Humming Kettle.'" Humming and Kettle are capitalized. "Leo watched the movie The Lost Balloon." Lost and Balloon are capitalized.

Quiz (fix the title capitalization):
1. "We read the story 'the brave little fox.'" Answer: We read the story "The Brave Little Fox." Hint: Capitalize the important words in a title.
2. "Ana's favorite book is 'a day at the lake.'" Answer: Ana's favorite book is "A Day at the Lake." Hint: Capitalize the first word of a title and the important words after it.
3. "The class sang 'the humming kettle.'" Answer: The class sang "The Humming Kettle." Hint: Capitalize the important words in a song title.
4. "Leo watched 'the lost balloon' on Friday." Answer: Leo watched "The Lost Balloon" on Friday. Hint: Capitalize the important words in a movie title.
5. "Our poem is called 'the busy garden.'" Answer: Our poem is called "The Busy Garden." Hint: Capitalize the important words in a title.
6. "Sam read 'the cat and the moon.'" Answer: Sam read "The Cat and the Moon." Hint: Capitalize the important words. The word and stays lowercase.
7. "The story 'a walk in the meadow' is funny." Answer: The story "A Walk in the Meadow" is funny. Hint: Capitalize the first word and the important words. The word in stays lowercase.
8. "Mia wrote a story called 'the red kite.'" Answer: Mia wrote a story called "The Red Kite." Hint: Capitalize the important words in a title.
9. "We will read 'the deep snow' today." Answer: We will read "The Deep Snow" today. Hint: Capitalize the important words in a title.
10. "The song 'little star, shining star' is new." Answer: The song "Little Star, Shining Star" is new. Hint: Capitalize the first word and the important words.
11. "Ana's book is 'the secret garden gate.'" Answer: Ana's book is "The Secret Garden Gate." Hint: Capitalize the important words in a title.
12. "Our class play is 'the dragon of the hill.'" Answer: Our class play is "The Dragon of the Hill." Hint: Capitalize the important words. The word of stays lowercase.

---

## Pathway 4: Capitalizing and Formatting Titles

### Topic: Rules of Capitalizing Titles

See Rules of Capitalizing Titles under Pathway 3: Capitalization above. Same lesson, shared across both pathways.

---

## Pathway 5: Commas

### Topic: Commas with Single Words in a Series

Teaching card:
- Definition: Use commas to separate three or more single words in a list. The comma tells the reader where one word ends and the next begins.
- Worked example: "We packed shirts, pants, socks, and hats." Commas separate the words in the list.
- Explanation: Each comma separates one item in the list from the next.
- Tip box: "The farm has cows, pigs, and chickens." Commas separate the animals. "Sam likes red, blue, and green." Commas separate the colors.

Quiz (add commas to the list):
1. "The box holds crayons markers and pencils." Answer: The box holds crayons, markers, and pencils. Hint: Use commas to separate words in a list.
2. "We saw lions tigers and bears at the zoo." Answer: We saw lions, tigers, and bears at the zoo. Hint: Use commas between the animals in the list.
3. "Ana bought milk bread and eggs." Answer: Ana bought milk, bread, and eggs. Hint: Use commas to separate the foods in the list.
4. "The garden grows peas beans and corn." Answer: The garden grows peas, beans, and corn. Hint: Use commas between the plants in the list.
5. "Leo packed a sandwich an apple and a cookie." Answer: Leo packed a sandwich, an apple, and a cookie. Hint: Use commas to separate the foods in the list.
6. "My favorite colors are purple orange and yellow." Answer: My favorite colors are purple, orange, and yellow. Hint: Use commas between the colors in the list.
7. "The band has drums guitars and trumpets." Answer: The band has drums, guitars, and trumpets. Hint: Use commas to separate the instruments in the list.
8. "We need paper glue and scissors." Answer: We need paper, glue, and scissors. Hint: Use commas between the supplies in the list.
9. "The pond is home to frogs fish and ducks." Answer: The pond is home to frogs, fish, and ducks. Hint: Use commas to separate the animals in the list.
10. "Sam wore a hat gloves and a scarf." Answer: Sam wore a hat, gloves, and a scarf. Hint: Use commas between the clothes in the list.
11. "The tree has apples pears and plums." Answer: The tree has apples, pears, and plums. Hint: Use commas to separate the fruits in the list.
12. "Mia collects shells rocks and feathers." Answer: Mia collects shells, rocks, and feathers. Hint: Use commas between the things in the list.

### Topic: Commas with Prepositional Phrases

Teaching card:
- Definition: Use a comma after a prepositional phrase that starts a sentence. The comma gives the reader a small pause before the main part of the sentence.
- Worked example: "After lunch, we went to the library." The phrase "After lunch" starts the sentence, so a comma follows it.
- Explanation: A comma after the starting phrase separates it from the rest of the sentence.
- Tip box: "In the morning, the birds sang." A comma follows "In the morning." "Under the bed, the cat slept." A comma follows "Under the bed."

Quiz (add the comma after the starting phrase):
1. "After school we played tag." Answer: After school, we played tag. Hint: Put a comma after the phrase that starts the sentence.
2. "In the garden the bees buzzed." Answer: In the garden, the bees buzzed. Hint: Put a comma after the starting phrase.
3. "Before dinner Sam washed his hands." Answer: Before dinner, Sam washed his hands. Hint: Find the phrase that tells when, and put a comma after it.
4. "On Monday we will visit the farm." Answer: On Monday, we will visit the farm. Hint: Put a comma after the starting phrase.
5. "Under the table the puppy hid." Answer: Under the table, the puppy hid. Hint: Put a comma after the starting phrase.
6. "During the storm the lights went out." Answer: During the storm, the lights went out. Hint: Put a comma after the starting phrase.
7. "After the game the team ate pizza." Answer: After the game, the team ate pizza. Hint: Find the phrase that tells when, and put a comma after it.
8. "In the winter the lake freezes." Answer: In the winter, the lake freezes. Hint: Put a comma after the starting phrase.
9. "Beside the river we built a fort." Answer: Beside the river, we built a fort. Hint: Put a comma after the starting phrase.
10. "Before the show Ana practiced her lines." Answer: Before the show, Ana practiced her lines. Hint: Find the phrase that tells when, and put a comma after it.
11. "On the shelf the books stood in a row." Answer: On the shelf, the books stood in a row. Hint: Put a comma after the starting phrase.
12. "After breakfast Leo fed the dog." Answer: After breakfast, Leo fed the dog. Hint: Find the phrase that tells when, and put a comma after it.

---

## Pathway 6: Commonly Confused Words

### Topic: "Your" vs. "You're"

Teaching card:
- Definition: Your shows that something belongs to you. You're is a short way to say you are.
- Worked example: "Is this your coat? You're going to need it today." Your shows the coat belongs to you. You're means you are.
- Explanation: If you can swap in you are, use you're. If not, use your.
- Tip box: "Your lunch is on the table." The lunch belongs to you. "You're a fast runner." You're means you are.

Quiz (choose your or you're):
1. "Is this (your/you're) bike?" Answer: your. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.
2. "(Your/You're) going to love this book." Answer: You're. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.
3. "(Your/You're) the fastest runner in our class." Answer: You're. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.
4. "Please put (your/you're) shoes by the door." Answer: your. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.
5. "I like (your/you're) drawing of the dragon." Answer: your. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.
6. "(Your/You're) doing a great job on the puzzle." Answer: You're. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.
7. "Don't forget (your/you're) lunchbox." Answer: your. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.
8. "(Your/You're) invited to my birthday party." Answer: You're. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.
9. "(Your/You're) going to be late if you don't hurry." Answer: You're. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.
10. "(Your/You're) dog is very friendly." Answer: Your. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.
11. "Is that (your/you're) pencil on the floor?" Answer: your. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.
12. "(Your/You're) my best friend." Answer: You're. Hint: Try reading it with "you are." If that makes sense, use you're. If something belongs to you, use your.

---

## Pathway 7: Conjunctions

### Topic: Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions

Teaching card:
- Definition: A coordinating conjunction is a small word that joins two words, phrases, or thoughts. The main ones are and, but, or, so, for, nor, and yet.
- Worked example: "We wanted to play outside, but it was raining." But joins the two thoughts.
- Explanation: But shows that the second thought is different from the first.
- Tip box: "Sam likes apples and oranges." And joins two things. "We can walk or ride." Or joins a choice.

Quiz (tap the coordinating conjunction):
1. "The cat slept, and the dog played." Answer: and. Hint: Look for the small word that joins what the cat did with what the dog did.
2. "We can swim, or we can hike." Answer: or. Hint: Look for the small word that gives a choice.
3. "It was late, so we went home." Answer: so. Hint: Look for the small word that joins a cause with what happened next.
4. "Leo likes soccer, but he does not like tennis." Answer: but. Hint: Look for the small word that joins two different thoughts.
5. "The soup was hot, yet Sam ate it all." Answer: yet. Hint: Look for the small word that joins two thoughts that seem to disagree.
6. "We stayed inside, for it was snowing." Answer: for. Hint: Look for the small word that joins a reason to a thought.
7. "Mia did not want cake, nor did she want pie." Answer: nor. Hint: Look for the small word that joins two things that are both not wanted.
8. "Ana read a book and wrote a story." Answer: and. Hint: Look for the small word that joins two things Ana did.
9. "You can have juice or milk." Answer: or. Hint: Look for the small word that gives a choice between two drinks.
10. "The baby was tired, so she slept." Answer: so. Hint: Look for the small word that shows what happened because the baby was tired.
11. "Sam is small, but he is strong." Answer: but. Hint: Look for the small word that joins two different ideas.
12. "We packed sandwiches, and we packed fruit." Answer: and. Hint: Look for the small word that joins the two things we packed.

---

## Pathway 8: Evidence for Informational Writing

### Topic: Selecting Evidence for Informational Writing

Teaching card, three sections:
- Section 1, Facts. Guiding question: What is a fact? Worked passage: "Bees help flowers grow by moving pollen from one flower to another. A single hive can hold 50,000 bees." Both sentences are facts, because both can be checked. The second one also gives a number.
- Section 2, Matching evidence. Guiding question: Does the fact fit the point? Worked passage: "Bees are important to farms. A single hive can hold 50,000 bees. Bees help flowers grow by moving pollen." The sentence about pollen fits the point about farms. It explains why bees matter to farmers.
- Section 3, Strong vs. weak. Guiding question: Which fact is stronger? Worked passage: "Bees are important to farms. Bees are nice. A farm with bees grows more food." The sentence about growing more food is stronger evidence. It connects bees to what farms do.

Quiz (which sentence is the evidence?):
1. Passage: "The moon changes shape in the sky. The moon takes about a month to go around Earth. The moon looks bright at night." Which sentence is evidence about how long the moon takes to circle Earth? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact that fits the point.
2. Passage: "Volcanoes can erupt with hot lava. One volcano in Hawaii erupted for more than 30 years. Lava is very hot." Which sentence gives a fact about how long an eruption lasted? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A fact with a number is strong evidence.
3. Passage: "A goldfish can live for 20 years. Fish breathe with gills. Fish swim in water." Which sentence is evidence about how long a goldfish can live? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact that can be checked.
4. Passage: "The desert is dry. Some deserts get less than one inch of rain a year. Deserts are hot." Which sentence gives a fact about rain in the desert? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A number makes the evidence stronger.
5. Passage: "Trees clean the air. One big tree can make enough oxygen for four people each day. Trees have leaves." Which sentence is evidence about oxygen? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact that fits the point about clean air.
6. Passage: "Penguins cannot fly. Penguins swim well. A penguin can swim 15 miles in a day." Which sentence gives a fact about how far a penguin can swim? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A fact with a number is strong evidence.
7. Passage: "The heart pumps blood. The heart is in the chest. The heart beats about 100,000 times a day." Which sentence is evidence about how often the heart beats? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact that can be checked.
8. Passage: "Spiders have eight legs. A spider can spin a web in one hour. Spiders are small." Which sentence gives a fact about how fast a spider spins a web? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A fact with a time or number is strong evidence.
9. Passage: "Rain comes from clouds. A cloud can hold millions of drops of water. Clouds float in the sky." Which sentence is evidence about what a cloud holds? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact that fits the point.
10. Passage: "One ant can lift 50 times its own weight. Ants work together. Ants live in colonies." Which sentence gives a fact about how much an ant can lift? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A number makes the evidence stronger.
11. Passage: "The sun is a star. The sun is about 93 million miles from Earth. The sun gives us light." Which sentence is evidence about how far away the sun is? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact that can be checked.
12. Passage: "Bears sleep in winter. Bears eat a lot before winter. A bear can sleep for up to seven months." Which sentence gives a fact about how long a bear sleeps? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A fact with a number is strong evidence.

---

## Pathway 9: Evidence for Opinion Writing

### Topic: Selecting Evidence for Opinion Writing

Teaching card, three sections:
- Section 1, Opinions need facts. Guiding question: Why does an opinion need evidence? Worked passage: "Our school should plant a garden. Gardens give students fresh food to eat. I like gardens." The sentence about fresh food supports the opinion. The sentence about liking gardens does not.
- Section 2, Fitting evidence. Guiding question: Does the fact fit the opinion? Worked passage: "Recess should be longer. Kids who play outside learn better. The playground has a slide." The sentence about learning fits the opinion. The sentence about the slide does not.
- Section 3, Strong vs. weak. Guiding question: Which fact is stronger? Worked passage: "The library should stay open later. Many students need a quiet place to study. The library has blue chairs." The sentence about a quiet place to study is stronger. It gives a reason the library matters.

Quiz (which sentence best supports the opinion?):
1. Passage: "School lunch should include more fruit. Fruit gives students energy for class. The lunchroom has long tables." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Good evidence gives a reason that fits the opinion.
2. Passage: "Every class should have a pet. Caring for a pet teaches students to be responsible. Pets are cute." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence should give a reason, not just a feeling.
3. Passage: "The town should build a new park. The park would have green grass. Parks give families a place to play together." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Good evidence explains why the park matters.
4. Passage: "Kids should read every day. Reading every day helps students learn new words. Books have colorful covers." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence should connect to the opinion with a reason.
5. Passage: "Our class should take more field trips. Field trips let students see what they are learning in real life. The bus is yellow." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Good evidence gives a reason that fits the opinion.
6. Passage: "The school day should start later. The school has a big clock. Students who sleep more do better on tests." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Evidence should give a fact that backs up the opinion.
7. Passage: "Everyone should learn to swim. Swimming is a skill that can keep you safe in water. The pool is cold." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Good evidence explains why the skill matters.
8. Passage: "The library should buy more books about animals. Students read more when the books match what they love. The library has tall shelves." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence should give a reason that fits the opinion.
9. Passage: "Homework should be shorter. Homework is written on paper. Students need time to play and rest after school." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Good evidence gives a reason, not just a fact about homework.
10. Passage: "Our town should plant more trees. Trees clean the air and give shade on hot days. Trees have bark." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence should explain why trees matter to the town.
11. Passage: "Art class should meet twice a week. Making art helps students think in new ways. Art supplies are colorful." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Good evidence gives a reason that fits the opinion.
12. Passage: "The playground needs a new swing set. The playground is near the school. The old swings are broken and unsafe." Which sentence best supports the opinion? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Evidence should give a fact that backs up the opinion.

---

## Pathway 10: Formatting Dialogue and Quotations

### Topic: Using Quotation Marks with Dialogue

Teaching card:
- Definition: Quotation marks go around the exact words a person says. A comma usually comes before the opening marks, and the end mark goes inside the closing marks.
- Worked example: "Sam said, 'I found a turtle.'" The words Sam spoke are inside quotation marks.
- Explanation: The marks show where Sam's own words begin and end.
- Tip box: "'Can we go to the park?' asked Ana." The question is inside the marks. "'I love this book,' said Leo." The comma sits inside the closing marks.

Quiz (fix the dialogue punctuation):
1. "Sam said I found a turtle." Answer: Sam said, "I found a turtle." Hint: Put quotation marks around the exact words Sam said, and a comma before the opening marks.
2. "Ana asked can we go to the park" Answer: Ana asked, "Can we go to the park?" Hint: Think about what kind of sentence Ana says. The end mark goes inside the closing quotation marks.
3. "Leo said I love this book" Answer: Leo said, "I love this book." Hint: Put quotation marks around the words Leo said.
4. "Mia shouted watch out" Answer: Mia shouted, "Watch out!" Hint: Think about how Mia says the words. The end mark goes inside the closing quotation marks.
5. "The teacher said please sit down" Answer: The teacher said, "Please sit down." Hint: Put a comma before the opening marks and the end mark inside the closing marks.
6. "Ben asked where is my backpack" Answer: Ben asked, "Where is my backpack?" Hint: Think about what kind of sentence Ben says. The end mark goes inside the closing marks.
7. "Sara said the show starts at noon" Answer: Sara said, "The show starts at noon." Hint: Put quotation marks around the exact words Sara said.
8. "Tom whispered I hid the note" Answer: Tom whispered, "I hid the note." Hint: Put a comma before the opening marks.
9. "Mom said dinner is ready" Answer: Mom said, "Dinner is ready." Hint: Put quotation marks around the words Mom said, and the end mark inside the closing marks.
10. "Liam asked can I have a cookie" Answer: Liam asked, "Can I have a cookie?" Hint: Think about what kind of sentence Liam says. The end mark goes inside the closing marks.
11. "The coach yelled run faster" Answer: The coach yelled, "Run faster!" Hint: Think about how the coach says the words. The end mark goes inside the closing marks.
12. "Ana said I will help you" Answer: Ana said, "I will help you." Hint: Put a comma before the opening marks and the end mark inside the closing marks.

### Topic: Using Quotation Marks around Quotes

Teaching card:
- Definition: Quotation marks go around words you copy from a book or from what someone else wrote. The marks show that the words are not your own.
- Worked example: "In her book, Ana wrote, 'The river sang all night.'" The words from the book are inside quotation marks.
- Explanation: The marks show the words came from the book, not from you.
- Tip box: "The sign says, 'No swimming today.'" The words on the sign are inside marks. "Leo copied this line: 'The moon rose over the hill.'" The copied line is inside marks.

Quiz (fix the quotation punctuation):
1. "The book says the river sang all night." Answer: The book says, "The river sang all night." Hint: Put quotation marks around words copied from a book.
2. "The sign says no swimming today." Answer: The sign says, "No swimming today." Hint: Put the words on the sign inside quotation marks.
3. "Leo copied the line the moon rose over the hill." Answer: Leo copied the line "The moon rose over the hill." Hint: Put quotation marks around the copied words.
4. "The note says please come to the office." Answer: The note says, "Please come to the office." Hint: Put the words from the note inside quotation marks.
5. "In the poem, the writer says the snow fell soft and white." Answer: In the poem, the writer says, "The snow fell soft and white." Hint: Put quotation marks around the words from the poem.
6. "The poster reads art show on Friday." Answer: The poster reads, "Art show on Friday." Hint: Put the words on the poster inside quotation marks.
7. "Sam read the line the brave knight rode off." Answer: Sam read the line "The brave knight rode off." Hint: Put the copied line inside quotation marks.
8. "The recipe says stir the soup slowly." Answer: The recipe says, "Stir the soup slowly." Hint: Put the words from the recipe inside quotation marks.
9. "The letter says thank you for the gift." Answer: The letter says, "Thank you for the gift." Hint: Put the words from the letter inside quotation marks.
10. "Ana copied this sentence the garden smelled of rain." Answer: Ana copied this sentence: "The garden smelled of rain." Hint: Put the copied sentence inside quotation marks.
11. "The map says turn left at the bridge." Answer: The map says, "Turn left at the bridge." Hint: Put the words on the map inside quotation marks.
12. "The song says row your boat to the shore." Answer: The song says, "Row your boat to the shore." Hint: Put the words from the song inside quotation marks.

---

## Pathway 11: Fragments and Run-Ons

### Topic: Identifying Action Verbs

Teaching card:
- Definition: An action verb is a word that shows what someone or something does. It tells about an action like run, jump, read, or build.
- Worked example: "The dog chased the ball." Chased is the action verb. It shows what the dog did.
- Explanation: Chased tells the action in the sentence.
- Tip box: "Ana reads every night." Reads is the action verb. "The birds sing at dawn." Sing is the action verb.

Quiz (tap the action verb):
1. "The cat climbed the tree." Answer: climbed. Hint: Look for the word that shows what the cat did.
2. "Sam kicked the ball across the field." Answer: kicked. Hint: Look for the word that shows what Sam did.
3. "The class sang a song." Answer: sang. Hint: Look for the word that shows what the class did.
4. "Ana drew a picture of a castle." Answer: drew. Hint: Look for the word that shows what Ana did.
5. "The rain fell all night." Answer: fell. Hint: Look for the word that shows what the rain did.
6. "Leo built a tower from blocks." Answer: built. Hint: Look for the word that shows what Leo did.
7. "The birds flew south for winter." Answer: flew. Hint: Look for the word that shows what the birds did.
8. "Mia baked cookies for her friends." Answer: baked. Hint: Look for the word that shows what Mia did.
9. "The dog barked at the mail truck." Answer: barked. Hint: Look for the word that shows what the dog did.
10. "We planted seeds in the garden." Answer: planted. Hint: Look for the word that shows what we did.
11. "The baby laughed at the funny face." Answer: laughed. Hint: Look for the word that shows what the baby did.
12. "Tom swam across the pool." Answer: swam. Hint: Look for the word that shows what Tom did.

### Topic: Recognizing Fragments

Teaching card:
- Definition: A fragment is a group of words that is not a complete sentence. It is missing a subject, a verb, or a complete thought.
- Worked example: "Ran to the store." This is a fragment. It has a verb but no subject, so we do not know who ran. Fix it: "Sam ran to the store."
- Explanation: A sentence needs a subject and a verb and a complete thought.
- Tip box: "The big red ball." This is a fragment. It has no verb. Fix it: "The big red ball bounced." "After the rain stopped." This is a fragment. It does not finish the thought. Fix it: "After the rain stopped, we played outside."

Quiz (sentence or fragment?):
1. "Ran to the store." Answer: Fragment. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?
2. "The dog barked at the mail truck." Answer: Sentence. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?
3. "The big red ball." Answer: Fragment. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?
4. "After the rain stopped." Answer: Fragment. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?
5. "Sam and Ana played tag." Answer: Sentence. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?
6. "Under the table." Answer: Fragment. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?
7. "The baby slept in her crib." Answer: Sentence. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?
8. "Because it was cold." Answer: Fragment. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?
9. "Leo read three books." Answer: Sentence. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?
10. "Jumped over the fence." Answer: Fragment. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?
11. "The class will visit the farm on Friday." Answer: Sentence. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?
12. "When the bell rang." Answer: Fragment. Hint: Ask: Does it tell who or what? Does it tell what they do? Does it finish the thought?

### Topic: Avoiding Run-Ons

Teaching card:
- Definition: A run-on is two complete sentences joined with no punctuation or joining word. Fix it by splitting it into two sentences or by joining it with a comma and a word like and, but, or so.
- Worked example: "The sun came out we went outside." This is a run-on. Fix it like this: "The sun came out, and we went outside."
- Explanation: The comma and the word and join the two complete thoughts the right way.
- Tip box: "Sam was hungry he ate a sandwich." Fix it: "Sam was hungry, so he ate a sandwich." "It rained all day the game was canceled." Fix it: "It rained all day. The game was canceled."

Quiz (fix the run-on):
1. "The dog barked the cat ran away." Answer: The dog barked, and the cat ran away. Hint: Find where the first thought ends. Join the two thoughts with a comma and a word that adds one thought to the other.
2. "It was late we went to bed." Answer: It was late, so we went to bed. Hint: Going to bed was the result of the late hour. Join the thoughts with a comma and a word that shows a result.
3. "Ana likes apples she does not like pears." Answer: Ana likes apples, but she does not like pears. Hint: Liking apples and not liking pears are different. Join the thoughts with a comma and a word that shows a difference.
4. "The store was closed we went home." Answer: The store was closed. We went home. Hint: Find where the first complete thought ends. Each part can stand alone as its own sentence.
5. "Leo ran fast he won the race." Answer: Leo ran fast, and he won the race. Hint: Find where the first thought ends. Join the two thoughts with a comma and a word that adds one thought to the other.
6. "The baby cried her mom picked her up." Answer: The baby cried, so her mom picked her up. Hint: The mom picked up the baby because the baby cried. Join the thoughts with a comma and a word that shows a result.
7. "We can walk we can ride our bikes." Answer: We can walk, or we can ride our bikes. Hint: Walking and riding are two choices. Join the thoughts with a comma and a word that gives a choice.
8. "The rain stopped the sun came out." Answer: The rain stopped. The sun came out. Hint: Find where the first complete thought ends. Each part can stand alone as its own sentence.
9. "Mia practiced every day she got better." Answer: Mia practiced every day, and she got better. Hint: Find where the first thought ends. Join the two thoughts with a comma and a word that adds one thought to the other.
10. "The cake burned we made a new one." Answer: The cake burned, so we made a new one. Hint: Making a new cake was the result of the burned one. Join the thoughts with a comma and a word that shows a result.
11. "Sam wanted to play it started to rain." Answer: Sam wanted to play, but it started to rain. Hint: The rain was not what Sam wanted. Join the thoughts with a comma and a word that shows a difference.
12. "The class read a book they acted out the story." Answer: The class read a book. They acted out the story. Hint: Find where the first complete thought ends. Each part can stand alone as its own sentence.

---

## Pathway 12: Identifying Parts of Speech I

### Topic: Identifying Common Nouns

Teaching card:
- Definition: A common noun names a person, place, thing, or idea. It does not name one special person or place, so it starts with a lowercase letter.
- Worked example: "The teacher read a book in the library." Teacher, book, and library are common nouns.
- Explanation: Each one names a general person, thing, or place, not one special one.
- Tip box: "The dog chased the ball." Dog and ball are common nouns. "Ana felt joy at the fair." Joy is a common noun that names an idea.

Quiz (tap the common noun):
1. "Sam fed the puppy." Answer: puppy. Hint: Sam names one special person. Look for the word that names a thing in general.
2. "The teacher smiled." Answer: teacher. Hint: Look for the word that names a person in general.
3. "Ana kicked the ball." Answer: ball. Hint: Ana names one special person. Look for the word that names a thing.
4. "We visited a museum." Answer: museum. Hint: Look for the word that names a place in general.
5. "Leo read a book." Answer: book. Hint: Leo names one special person. Look for the word that names a thing.
6. "She laughed with joy." Answer: joy. Hint: Look for the word that names an idea, something you feel but cannot touch.
7. "Mia planted a flower." Answer: flower. Hint: Mia names one special person. Look for the word that names a thing.
8. "The class sang loudly." Answer: class. Hint: Look for the word that names a group of people in general.
9. "Tom swam in the river." Answer: river. Hint: Tom names one special person. Look for the word that names a place in general.
10. "The bird sang sweetly." Answer: bird. Hint: Look for the word that names a thing.
11. "Ana felt happiness." Answer: happiness. Hint: Ana names one special person. Look for the word that names an idea.
12. "We watched the sunset." Answer: sunset. Hint: Look for the word that names a thing.

See Identifying Action Verbs under Pathway 11: Fragments and Run-Ons above. Same lesson, shared across both pathways.

### Topic: Identifying Adjectives

See Identifying Adjectives under Pathway 1: Adjectives and Adverbs above. Same lesson, shared across both pathways.

## Pathway 13: Identifying Parts of Speech II

### Topic: Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions

See Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions under Pathway 7: Conjunctions above. Same lesson, shared across both pathways.
