## Pathway 14: Introduction to Evidence

### Topic: Which Pieces of Evidence Support the Opinion?

Teaching card, three sections:
- Section 1, Opinions and facts. Guiding question: What makes a fact good evidence? Worked passage: "Dogs make great pets. Dogs are loyal and love to play. I like dogs." The sentence about loyalty supports the opinion. The sentence about liking dogs does not.
- Section 2, Checking the fit. Guiding question: Does the fact match the opinion? Worked passage: "Schools should have gardens. Gardens teach students where food comes from. The garden has a fence." The sentence about where food comes from matches the opinion. The sentence about the fence does not.
- Section 3, Picking the best one. Guiding question: Which fact is the strongest? Worked passage: "Bikes are the best way to get to school. Riding a bike keeps you healthy and costs nothing. Bikes have wheels." The sentence about health and cost is the strongest. It gives two reasons in one fact.

Quiz (which sentence is the evidence that supports the opinion?):
1. Passage: "Cats make good pets. Cats are quiet and clean. Cats have whiskers." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Good evidence gives a reason that fits the opinion.
2. Passage: "Everyone should eat breakfast. Breakfast is served in the morning. Breakfast gives you energy for the day." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Evidence should give a reason, not just a fact about breakfast.
3. Passage: "The park needs more benches. People need a place to sit and rest. The park has trees." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Good evidence explains why benches matter.
4. Passage: "Reading before bed is a good habit. Reading helps you fall asleep and learn new words. Books sit on a shelf." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence should give reasons that fit the opinion.
5. Passage: "Our class should recycle. The bin is blue. Recycling keeps trash out of landfills." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Good evidence gives a reason that fits the opinion.
6. Passage: "Swimming is the best sport. Swimming uses your whole body and is easy on your joints. Pools have water." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence should give reasons, not just facts about pools.
7. Passage: "The school should have a garden. A garden gives students fresh vegetables. The garden is behind the school." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Good evidence explains why the garden matters.
8. Passage: "Kids should wear helmets when they ride bikes. Helmets come in many colors. Helmets protect your head if you fall." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Evidence should give a safety reason that fits the opinion.
9. Passage: "Music class is important. Music helps students remember and focus. The music room has a piano." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Good evidence gives reasons that fit the opinion.
10. Passage: "The library should open on Saturdays. Many families can only visit on the weekend. The library has red chairs." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence should explain why Saturday hours matter.
11. Passage: "Drinking water is healthy. Water is clear. Water keeps your body working well." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Good evidence gives a reason that fits the opinion.
12. Passage: "Every student should learn first aid. First aid lets you help someone who is hurt. The kit has bandages." Which sentence supports the opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence should give a reason that fits the opinion.

---

## Pathway 15: Showing vs. Telling

### Topic: Using Actions to Show Character Responses

Teaching card, three sections:
- Section 1, Telling vs. showing. Guiding question: What is the difference? Worked passage: Telling: "Ana was nervous." Showing: "Ana twisted her hair and bit her lip." The showing sentence uses actions. The reader can see the nerves.
- Section 2, Showing feelings. Guiding question: How do actions show feelings? Worked passage: "Leo was excited. Leo jumped up and cheered when his team scored." The second sentence shows excitement with actions. The reader feels it.
- Section 3, Showing character. Guiding question: How do actions show what a person is like? Worked passage: "Sam is kind. Sam shared his lunch with the new student." The second sentence shows kindness with an action. The reader learns about Sam from what he does.

Quiz (which sentence shows instead of telling?):
1. "A) Ana was sad. B) Ana wiped her eyes and sniffled." Which sentence shows how Ana feels? Answer: B. Hint: Showing uses actions the reader can see.
2. "A) Leo stomped his foot and crossed his arms. B) Leo was angry." Which sentence shows how Leo feels? Answer: A. Hint: Look for the sentence that lets you see what Leo does, instead of naming the feeling.
3. "A) Mia was brave. B) Mia stepped forward and faced the big dog." Which sentence shows that Mia is brave? Answer: B. Hint: Look for the sentence that shows bravery through what Mia does.
4. "A) Sam was tired. B) Sam yawned and rubbed his eyes." Which sentence shows that Sam is tired? Answer: B. Hint: Look for the sentence where you can see what Sam's body does.
5. "A) The girl hid behind her mother and held her hand tight. B) The girl was scared." Which sentence shows that the girl is scared? Answer: A. Hint: Look for the sentence that shows fear through actions, not a feeling word.
6. "A) Ben was proud. B) Ben held up his trophy and grinned." Which sentence shows that Ben is proud? Answer: B. Hint: Look for the sentence that shows pride through what Ben does.
7. "A) Sara helped her friend carry the heavy box. B) Sara was kind." Which sentence shows that Sara is kind? Answer: A. Hint: Look for the sentence that shows kindness through an action.
8. "A) Tom was excited. B) Tom bounced on his toes and clapped." Which sentence shows that Tom is excited? Answer: B. Hint: Look for the sentence with actions that show excitement.
9. "A) Kai was nervous. B) Kai tapped his pencil and kept looking at the clock." Which sentence shows that Kai is nervous? Answer: B. Hint: Look for the sentence with actions that show nerves.
10. "A) Leo's stomach growled as he stared at the cookies. B) Leo was hungry." Which sentence shows that Leo is hungry? Answer: A. Hint: Look for the sentence that shows hunger with things you can see and hear.
11. "A) Mia's eyes went wide, and her mouth fell open. B) Mia was surprised." Which sentence shows that Mia is surprised? Answer: A. Hint: Look for the sentence that shows surprise on Mia's face.
12. "A) Sam was happy. B) Sam laughed and danced around the room." Which sentence shows that Sam is happy? Answer: B. Hint: Look for the sentence with actions that show happiness.

---

## Pathway 16: Verbs I: Simple Tenses

### Topic: Identifying Action Verbs

See Identifying Action Verbs under Pathway 11: Fragments and Run-Ons above. Same lesson, shared across both pathways.

### Topic: Using Action Verbs in Simple Tenses

Teaching card:
- Definition: A verb in the simple present tells what happens now or often. A verb in the simple past tells what already happened. A verb in the simple future tells what will happen later.
- Worked example: "Today, Ana reads. Yesterday, Ana read. Tomorrow, Ana will read." Reads is present. Read is past. Will read is future.
- Explanation: The verb changes to show when the action happens.
- Tip box: "Sam plays soccer now. Sam played soccer yesterday. Sam will play soccer tomorrow." Plays, played, and will play show present, past, and future.

Quiz (choose the correct verb form for the time):
1. "Yesterday, Sam (walk/walked) to school." Answer: walked. Hint: Look at the word that tells when: Yesterday. Pick the verb that matches that time.
2. "Every day, Ana (read/reads) a book before she goes to bed." Answer: reads. Hint: Look at the words that tell when: Every day. Also look at the other verb, goes. Pick the verb that matches that time.
3. "Tomorrow, Leo (will swim/swam) in the race." Answer: will swim. Hint: Look at the word that tells when: Tomorrow. Pick the verb that matches that time.
4. "Last week, we (planted/plant) seeds." Answer: planted. Hint: Look at the words that tell when: Last week. Pick the verb that matches that time.
5. "Mia (bake/bakes) cookies on Saturdays." Answer: bakes. Hint: Look at the words that tell when: on Saturdays. Pick the verb that matches that time.
6. "Next month, the class (visit/will visit) the farm." Answer: will visit. Hint: Look at the words that tell when: Next month. Pick the verb that matches that time.
7. "This morning, Tom (ate/eats) toast." Answer: ate. Hint: Look at the words that tell when: This morning. Pick the verb that matches that time.
8. "The baby (cried/cries) when she is hungry." Answer: cries. Hint: Look at the other verb in the sentence, is. Pick the verb that matches its time.
9. "Yesterday, the dog (chased/chase) the ball." Answer: chased. Hint: Look at the word that tells when: Yesterday. Pick the verb that matches that time.
10. "Tomorrow, we (will play/played) tag at recess." Answer: will play. Hint: Look at the word that tells when: Tomorrow. Pick the verb that matches that time.
11. "Every summer, Sam (swims/swim) in the lake." Answer: swims. Hint: Look at the words that tell when: Every summer. Pick the verb that matches that time.
12. "Last night, Ana (finish/finished) her book." Answer: finished. Hint: Look at the words that tell when: Last night. Pick the verb that matches that time.

---

## Pathway 17: Verbs II: Progressive, Perfect, and Shifts in Tense

### Topic: Using Progressive Verbs

Teaching card:
- Definition: A progressive verb shows an action that is happening right now or was happening over time. It uses a form of be plus a verb that ends in ing.
- Worked example: "Sam is reading a book." Is reading shows the action is happening now.
- Explanation: Is plus reading makes the progressive form.
- Tip box: "Ana was singing when the phone rang." Was singing shows the action was happening over time. "The dogs are barking at the mail truck." Are barking shows the action is happening now.

Quiz (choose the progressive form):
1. "Sam (is reading/reads) a book right now." Answer: is reading. Hint: Right now tells you the action is still going on. Pick the verb that shows it in progress.
2. "Ana (sings/was singing) when the phone rang." Answer: was singing. Hint: The singing was going on when the phone rang. Pick the verb that shows it was in progress.
3. "The dogs (are barking/bark) at the mail truck." Answer: are barking. Hint: The barking is going on as you read. Pick the verb that shows it in progress.
4. "Leo (is building/builds) a tower at this moment." Answer: is building. Hint: At this moment tells you the action is still going on. Pick the verb that shows it in progress.
5. "We (ate/were eating) dinner when the lights went out." Answer: were eating. Hint: Dinner was going on when the lights went out. Pick the verb that shows it was in progress.
6. "Mia (is drawing/draws) a picture right now." Answer: is drawing. Hint: Right now tells you the action is still going on. Pick the verb that shows it in progress.
7. "The baby (cries/was crying) all morning." Answer: was crying. Hint: All morning tells you the action went on over time. Pick the verb that shows it was in progress.
8. "Tom (is swimming/swims) in the pool at this moment." Answer: is swimming. Hint: At this moment tells you the action is still going on. Pick the verb that shows it in progress.
9. "The birds (sang/were singing) when the sun came up." Answer: were singing. Hint: The singing was going on when the sun came up. Pick the verb that shows it was in progress.
10. "Ana (is practicing/practices) the piano right now." Answer: is practicing. Hint: Right now tells you the action is still going on. Pick the verb that shows it in progress.
11. "We (were walking/walked) home when it started to rain." Answer: were walking. Hint: The walk was going on when the rain started. Pick the verb that shows it was in progress.
12. "Sam (cooks/is cooking) dinner at this moment." Answer: is cooking. Hint: At this moment tells you the action is still going on. Pick the verb that shows it in progress.

### Topic: Review: Correcting Shifts in Verb Tense

Teaching card:
- Definition: A sentence should keep the same verb tense unless the time really changes. A tense shift is when the verbs jump from one time to another for no reason.
- Worked example: "Sam walked to school and buys lunch." Walked is past, but buys is present. Fix it: "Sam walked to school and bought lunch."
- Explanation: Both verbs should be past, because both actions happened in the past.
- Tip box: "Ana read her book and writes a note." Fix it: "Ana read her book and wrote a note." "Leo will swim and played tag." Fix it: "Leo will swim and play tag."

Quiz (fix the tense shift):
1. "Sam walked to school and buys lunch." Answer: Sam walked to school and bought lunch. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.
2. "Ana read her book and writes a note." Answer: Ana read her book and wrote a note. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.
3. "Leo will swim and played tag." Answer: Leo will swim and play tag. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.
4. "Mia baked cookies and eats one." Answer: Mia baked cookies and ate one. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.
5. "Tom plays soccer and scored a goal." Answer: Tom plays soccer and scores a goal. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.
6. "We planted seeds and cover them with soil." Answer: We planted seeds and covered them with soil. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.
7. "The dog barked and chases the cat." Answer: The dog barked and chased the cat. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.
8. "Ana will read her book and wrote a note." Answer: Ana will read her book and write a note. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.
9. "Sam eats lunch and drank milk." Answer: Sam eats lunch and drinks milk. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.
10. "Leo built a tower and knocks it down." Answer: Leo built a tower and knocked it down. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.
11. "We will visit the farm and fed the goats." Answer: We will visit the farm and feed the goats. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.
12. "Mia sings a song and danced on stage." Answer: Mia sings a song and dances on stage. Hint: The first verb sets the time. Change the other verb to match it.

# Grammar Lab, Grades 6-8, Part 1

Band: Grades 6-8. Pathways 1 through 27, in order, from Active and Passive Voice through Identifying Parts of Speech I. 27 pathways, 113 topics. 86 topics are written in full below. 27 topics are shared with an earlier pathway in this file and are cross-referenced to their first appearance instead of repeated. Each topic has a teaching card and a quiz of exactly 12 original items. All text is original, written fresh for Oomba, from the topic names only. US spelling. No em dashes.

## Pathway 1: Active and Passive Voice (4 topics)

### 1.1 Identifying Active Voice Verbs

**Teaching card**
- Definition: In an active voice sentence, the subject does the action. The verb tells what the subject does.
- Example: "Maya kicked the ball." Maya is the subject. Kicked is the active voice verb. Maya does the kicking.
- How it works: Ask who does the action. If the subject does it, the verb is active.
- Tip box: "The dog chased the squirrel." Chased is active because the dog does the chasing. "Leo built a tower from wooden blocks." Built is active because Leo does the building.

**Quiz**
1. "Maya kicked the ball across the field." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: kicked. Hint: Find the word that tells what Maya does.
2. "The gardener watered the tomato plants." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: watered. Hint: Find the word that tells what the gardener does.
3. "Ana read three chapters before bed." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: read. Hint: Find the word that tells what Ana does.
4. "The wind pushed the sailboat across the lake." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: pushed. Hint: Find the word that tells what the wind does.
5. "Sam solved the puzzle in ten minutes." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: solved. Hint: Find the word that tells what Sam does.
6. "The choir sang a song about the ocean." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: sang. Hint: Find the word that tells what the choir does.
7. "Zoe painted a mural on the gym wall." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: painted. Hint: Find the word that tells what Zoe does.
8. "The river carried the leaves downstream." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: carried. Hint: Find the word that tells what the river does.
9. "Ben fixed the flat tire on his bike." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: fixed. Hint: Find the word that tells what Ben does.
10. "The teacher explained the new math game." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: explained. Hint: Find the word that tells what the teacher does.
11. "A hawk circled above the meadow." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: circled. Hint: Find the word that tells what the hawk does.
12. "Lily wrote a poem about the first snow." Which word is the active voice verb? Answer: wrote. Hint: Find the word that tells what Lily does.

### 1.2 Is the Sentence Active or Passive?

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A sentence is active when the subject does the action. A sentence is passive when the subject receives the action, and the verb usually follows a form of be plus a past participle.
- Example: Active: "Ana painted the fence." Passive: "The fence was painted by Ana." In the passive sentence, the fence receives the action.
- How it works: Find the subject. If the subject does the action, the sentence is active. If the action happens to the subject, it is passive.
- Tip box: "The cook tasted the soup." Active, the cook does the tasting. "The soup was tasted by the cook." Passive, the soup receives the action.

**Quiz**
1. "The gardener watered the plants." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: active. Hint: Ask whether the gardener does the watering or receives it.
2. "The plants were watered by the gardener." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: passive. Hint: Ask whether the plants do the watering or receive it.
3. "The band played a new song." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: active. Hint: Ask whether the band does the playing or receives it.
4. "The homework was finished by Leo before dinner." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: passive. Hint: Ask whether the homework does the finishing or receives it, and look at the verb.
5. "Maya caught the ball in her glove." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: active. Hint: Ask whether Maya does the catching or receives it.
6. "The bridge was built in 1950." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: passive. Hint: Ask whether the bridge does the building or receives it.
7. "The cookies were eaten by the whole class." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: passive. Hint: Ask whether the cookies do the eating or receive it.
8. "The storm knocked down the old oak tree." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: active. Hint: Ask whether the storm does the knocking or receives it.
9. "Zoe solved the hardest problem on the test." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: active. Hint: Ask whether Zoe does the solving or receives it.
10. "The mural was painted by the art club." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: passive. Hint: Ask whether the mural does the painting or receives it, and look at the verb.
11. "The invitations were sent on Friday." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: passive. Hint: Ask whether the invitations do the sending or receive it.
12. "The cat chased the butterfly across the yard." Is this sentence active or passive? Answer: active. Hint: Ask whether the cat does the chasing or receives it.

### 1.3 Creating Active Voice Sentences 1

**Teaching card**
- Definition: To write in active voice, put the doer first, then the verb, then the receiver of the action.
- Example: Passive: "The cake was baked by Sam." Active: "Sam baked the cake." Sam, the doer, comes first.
- How it works: Find who does the action, move that person or thing to the front, and follow with the verb.
- Tip box: "The letter was written by Zoe." becomes "Zoe wrote the letter." "The goal was scored by Max." becomes "Max scored the goal."

**Quiz**
1. Rewrite in active voice: "The window was washed by Ana." Answer: "Ana washed the window." Hint: Put Ana, the doer, first, then the verb.
2. Rewrite in active voice: "The book was returned by Leo." Answer: "Leo returned the book." Hint: Put Leo, the doer, first, then the verb.
3. Rewrite in active voice: "The song was sung by the choir." Answer: "The choir sang the song." Hint: Put the choir, the doer, first, then the verb.
4. Rewrite in active voice: "The fence was painted by the neighbors." Answer: "The neighbors painted the fence." Hint: Put the neighbors, the doers, first, then the verb.
5. Rewrite in active voice: "The pizza was delivered by the driver." Answer: "The driver delivered the pizza." Hint: Put the driver, the doer, first, then the verb.
6. Rewrite in active voice: "The poster was designed by Maya." Answer: "Maya designed the poster." Hint: Put Maya, the doer, first, then the verb.
7. Rewrite in active voice: "The ball was caught by the goalie." Answer: "The goalie caught the ball." Hint: Put the goalie, the doer, first, then the verb.
8. Rewrite in active voice: "The seeds were planted by the science class." Answer: "The science class planted the seeds." Hint: Put the science class, the doer, first, then the verb.
9. Rewrite in active voice: "The award was won by the robotics team." Answer: "The robotics team won the award." Hint: Put the robotics team, the doer, first, then the verb.
10. Rewrite in active voice: "The story was told by the librarian." Answer: "The librarian told the story." Hint: Put the librarian, the doer, first, then the verb.
11. Rewrite in active voice: "The map was drawn by Ben." Answer: "Ben drew the map." Hint: Put Ben, the doer, first, then the verb.
12. Rewrite in active voice: "The cake was shared by the whole class." Answer: "The whole class shared the cake." Hint: Put the whole class, the doer, first, then the verb.

### 1.4 Creating Emphasis with Voice

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Writers choose active voice for clear, direct sentences, and passive voice when the receiver of the action matters more than the doer, or when the doer is unknown.
- Example: "The new library opens on Monday." Active voice keeps the news direct. "The trophy was won by our school." Passive voice puts the trophy first because the trophy is the news.
- How it works: Ask what deserves the spotlight. Put that part in the subject spot.
- Tip box: Active for action: "The team won the game." Passive for focus on the receiver: "The record was broken on Saturday."

**Quiz**
1. "The fire alarm rang during lunch." Which voice is used, and what does it emphasize? Answer: active voice, it emphasizes the alarm ringing. Hint: Ask whether the alarm does the action or receives it.
2. "The winner was announced at the assembly." Which voice is used, and what does it emphasize? Answer: passive voice, it emphasizes the winner. Hint: Ask whether the winner does the action or receives it.
3. "The principal opened the new playground." Which voice is used? Answer: active voice. Hint: Ask whether the principal does the opening or receives it.
4. "The new playground was opened on Friday." Which voice is used? Answer: passive voice. Hint: Ask whether the playground does the opening or receives it.
5. Which sentence puts the emphasis on the painting: "Ana painted a mural." or "A mural was painted by Ana."? Answer: "A mural was painted by Ana." Hint: Ask which sentence puts the receiver of the action in the subject spot.
6. Which sentence is more direct about who did it: "The experiment was done by the class." or "The class did the experiment."? Answer: "The class did the experiment." Hint: Ask which sentence names the doer in the subject spot.
7. "The books were returned to the library." Why might a writer choose passive voice here? Answer: The doer is unknown or unimportant, and the books matter most. Hint: Ask whether the reader needs to know who returned the books.
8. "The dog buried the bone under the tree." Which voice is used, and what does it emphasize? Answer: active voice, it emphasizes the dog doing the burying. Hint: Ask whether the dog does the action or receives it.
9. "The science fair was held in the gym." Which voice is used? Answer: passive voice. Hint: Ask whether the science fair does the action or receives it.
10. Which sentence puts the emphasis on the storm damage: "The storm damaged the roof." or "The roof was damaged by the storm."? Answer: "The roof was damaged by the storm." Hint: Think about which voice puts the receiver of the action in the spotlight.
11. "The coach praised the whole team after the game." Which voice is used? Answer: active voice. Hint: Ask whether the coach does the praising or receives it.
12. A writer wants the reader to focus on the lost homework, not on who lost it. Which is the better sentence: "Sam lost the homework." or "The homework was lost."? Answer: "The homework was lost." Hint: Ask which voice lets a writer leave out the doer.

## Pathway 2: Adjectives (5 topics)

### 2.1 What Does the Adjective Describe?

**Teaching card**
- Definition: An adjective is a word that describes a noun. It tells what kind, how many, or which one. To find the adjective, ask what it describes.
- Example: "The brave firefighter climbed the ladder." Brave is the adjective. It describes firefighter.
- How it works: Find a describing word, then ask which noun it tells about. The adjective and its noun work as a pair.
- Tip box: "The round clock hung on the wall." Round describes clock. "Three birds sat on the fence." Three describes birds.

**Quiz**
1. "The brave firefighter climbed the ladder." What does brave describe? Answer: firefighter. Hint: Ask which noun the describing word tells about.
2. "A tiny mouse hid under the porch." What does tiny describe? Answer: mouse. Hint: Ask which noun tiny tells about.
3. "The old library smells like paper and rain." What does old describe? Answer: library. Hint: Find the noun that old tells about.
4. "Seven runners finished the race." What does seven describe? Answer: runners. Hint: Ask which noun seven tells about.
5. "The silver key opened the garden gate." What does silver describe? Answer: key. Hint: Find the noun that silver tells about.
6. "A quiet hallway led to the music room." What does quiet describe? Answer: hallway. Hint: Ask which noun quiet tells about.
7. "The thick book sat on the desk all week." What does thick describe? Answer: book. Hint: Find the noun that thick tells about.
8. "Two hungry cats waited by the door." What does hungry describe? Answer: cats. Hint: Ask which noun hungry tells about.
9. "The warm bread came out of the oven." What does warm describe? Answer: bread. Hint: Find the noun that warm tells about.
10. "A steep hill rose behind the school." What does steep describe? Answer: hill. Hint: Ask which noun steep tells about.
11. "The red bicycle leaned against the fence." What does red describe? Answer: bicycle. Hint: Find the noun that red tells about.
12. "Five friends built a fort in the woods." What does five describe? Answer: friends. Hint: Ask which noun five tells about.

### 2.2 Identifying Adjectives Before and After Nouns

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Most adjectives come before the noun they describe, but some come after a linking verb such as is, was, seems, or feels.
- Example: "The cold wind blew across the field." Cold comes before wind. "The wind was cold." Cold comes after was, and it still describes wind.
- How it works: An adjective can sit right before its noun, or it can follow a linking verb and describe the subject.
- Tip box: "A soft blanket covered the couch." Soft comes before blanket. "The blanket was soft." Soft comes after was and describes blanket.

**Quiz**
1. "The cold wind blew across the field." Where is the adjective, and what does it describe? Answer: cold, before the noun wind. Hint: Find the describing word, then check whether it sits before its noun or after a linking verb.
2. "The wind was cold all morning." Where is the adjective, and what does it describe? Answer: cold, after the linking verb was, describing wind. Hint: Find the describing word, then check whether it sits before its noun or after a linking verb.
3. "A bright lamp lit the corner of the room." Where is the adjective? Answer: bright, before lamp. Hint: Find the describing word, then check whether it sits before its noun or after a linking verb.
4. "The soup tasted salty." Where is the adjective, and what does it describe? Answer: salty, after the linking verb tasted, describing soup. Hint: Find the describing word, then check whether it sits before its noun or after a linking verb.
5. "The tall giraffe reached the highest leaves." Where is the adjective? Answer: tall, before giraffe. Hint: Find the describing word, then check whether it sits before its noun or after a linking verb.
6. "The giraffe seemed calm at the zoo." Where is the adjective, and what does it describe? Answer: calm, after the linking verb seemed, describing giraffe. Hint: Find the describing word, then check whether it sits before its noun or after a linking verb.
7. "The noisy parrots squawked in the cage." Where is the adjective? Answer: noisy, before parrots. Hint: Find the describing word, then check whether it sits before its noun or after a linking verb.
8. "The cake smelled sweet when it came out of the oven." Where is the adjective, and what does it describe? Answer: sweet, after the linking verb smelled, describing cake. Hint: Find the describing word, then check whether it sits before its noun or after a linking verb.
9. "A muddy puppy ran through the garden." Where is the adjective? Answer: muddy, before puppy. Hint: Find the describing word, then check whether it sits before its noun or after a linking verb.
10. "The puppy felt tired after the long walk." Where is the adjective, and what does it describe? Answer: tired, after the linking verb felt, describing puppy. Hint: Find the describing word, then check whether it sits before its noun or after a linking verb.
11. "The deep lake was still and clear." Where are the adjectives, and what do they describe? Answer: deep before lake, and still and clear after was, all describing lake. Hint: Look for every describing word, then ask where each one sits.
12. "An icy path crossed the frozen pond." Where are the adjectives? Answer: icy before path, and frozen before pond. Hint: Look at each noun and ask which word describes it.

### 2.3 Ordering Adjectives

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When several adjectives describe one noun, they usually follow an order: opinion, size, age, shape, color, origin, material, purpose.
- Example: "A lovely big old round red wooden toy chest." Lovely is opinion, big is size, old is age, round is shape, red is color, wooden is material, and toy is purpose.
- How it works: Put opinion first, then size, then age, then shape, then color, then origin, then material, then purpose, right before the noun.
- Tip box: "A tiny old stone bridge." Tiny is size, old is age, stone is material. "A beautiful blue silk scarf." Beautiful is opinion, blue is color, silk is material.

**Quiz**
1. Put the adjectives in order: "red / big / ball" Answer: big red ball. Hint: Decide which word tells size and which tells color, then use the adjective order.
2. Put the adjectives in order: "old / lovely / house" Answer: lovely old house. Hint: Decide which word tells opinion and which tells age, then use the adjective order.
3. Put the adjectives in order: "wooden / round / table" Answer: round wooden table. Hint: Decide which word tells shape and which tells material, then use the adjective order.
4. Put the adjectives in order: "French / delicious / bread" Answer: delicious French bread. Hint: Decide which word tells opinion and which tells origin, then use the adjective order.
5. Which order sounds right: "a small blue car" or "a blue small car"? Answer: "a small blue car." Hint: Decide which word tells size and which tells color, then use the adjective order.
6. Which order sounds right: "a stone ancient wall" or "an ancient stone wall"? Answer: "an ancient stone wall." Hint: Decide which word tells age and which tells material, then use the adjective order.
7. Put the adjectives in order: "silk / red / beautiful / dress" Answer: beautiful red silk dress. Hint: Name the type of each adjective, then use the adjective order.
8. Which order sounds right: "a square wooden box" or "a wooden square box"? Answer: "a square wooden box." Hint: Decide which word tells shape and which tells material, then use the adjective order.
9. Put the adjectives in order: "metal / huge / gray / bridge" Answer: huge gray metal bridge. Hint: Name the type of each adjective, then use the adjective order.
10. Which order sounds right: "an old kind teacher" or "a kind old teacher"? Answer: "a kind old teacher." Hint: Decide which word tells opinion and which tells age, then use the adjective order.
11. Put the adjectives in order: "cotton / white / soft / shirt" Answer: soft white cotton shirt. Hint: Name the type of each adjective, then use the adjective order.
12. Which order sounds right: "a round red balloon" or "a red round balloon"? Answer: "a round red balloon." Hint: Decide which word tells shape and which tells color, then use the adjective order.

### 2.4 Forming Comparative Adjectives

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A comparative adjective compares two things. For most short adjectives, add er. For most longer adjectives, use more before the adjective.
- Example: "The river is wider than the stream." Wider compares river and stream. "This puzzle is more difficult than the last one." More difficult compares two puzzles.
- How it works: Compare two things. Add er to a short adjective, or put more in front of a longer one.
- Tip box: "The hill is taller than the fence." Taller compares two things. "The book is more interesting than the movie." More interesting compares two things.

**Quiz**
1. Form the comparative of tall: "The oak is ___ than the maple." Answer: taller. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take er, and longer ones take more.
2. Form the comparative of fast: "The cheetah is ___ than the lion." Answer: faster. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take er, and longer ones take more.
3. Form the comparative of careful: "Ana is ___ than Ben at packing." Answer: more careful. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take er, and longer ones take more.
4. Form the comparative of big: "The gym is ___ than the library." Answer: bigger. Hint: Look at the last three letters of big, then recall the spelling rule for adding an ending.
5. Form the comparative of happy: "The puppy is ___ than the kitten." Answer: happier. Hint: Look at the last letter of happy, then recall the spelling rule for adding an ending.
6. Form the comparative of interesting: "The museum is ___ than the mall." Answer: more interesting. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take er, and longer ones take more.
7. Form the comparative of cold: "Today is ___ than yesterday." Answer: colder. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take er, and longer ones take more.
8. Form the comparative of beautiful: "The garden is ___ than the yard." Answer: more beautiful. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take er, and longer ones take more.
9. Form the comparative of hot: "The soup is ___ than the salad." Answer: hotter. Hint: Look at the last three letters of hot, then recall the spelling rule for adding an ending.
10. Form the comparative of easy: "This game is ___ than the last one." Answer: easier. Hint: Look at the last letter of easy, then recall the spelling rule for adding an ending.
11. Form the comparative of brave: "The firefighter is ___ than the crowd." Answer: braver. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take er, and longer ones take more.
12. Form the comparative of expensive: "The bike is ___ than the scooter." Answer: more expensive. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take er, and longer ones take more.

### 2.5 Forming Superlative Adjectives

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A superlative adjective compares three or more things and picks out the most. For most short adjectives, add est. For most longer adjectives, use most before the adjective.
- Example: "The mountain is the tallest in the range." Tallest picks out one mountain from three or more. "This is the most exciting trip of the year." Most exciting picks out one trip from many.
- How it works: Compare three or more things. Add est to a short adjective, or put most in front of a longer one. Use the before a superlative.
- Tip box: "The whale is the largest animal in the sea." Largest compares three or more animals. "She is the most helpful student in class." Most helpful compares three or more students.

**Quiz**
1. Form the superlative of tall: "The oak is the ___ tree in the park." Answer: tallest. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take est, and longer ones take most.
2. Form the superlative of fast: "The cheetah is the ___ animal on land." Answer: fastest. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take est, and longer ones take most.
3. Form the superlative of careful: "Ana is the ___ packer in the group." Answer: most careful. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take est, and longer ones take most.
4. Form the superlative of big: "The gym is the ___ room in the school." Answer: biggest. Hint: Look at the last three letters of big, then recall the spelling rule for adding an ending.
5. Form the superlative of happy: "The puppy is the ___ pet in the shelter." Answer: happiest. Hint: Look at the last letter of happy, then recall the spelling rule for adding an ending.
6. Form the superlative of interesting: "The museum is the ___ place in town." Answer: most interesting. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take est, and longer ones take most.
7. Form the superlative of cold: "January is the ___ month of the year." Answer: coldest. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take est, and longer ones take most.
8. Form the superlative of beautiful: "The garden is the ___ spot in the neighborhood." Answer: most beautiful. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take est, and longer ones take most.
9. Form the superlative of hot: "The desert is the ___ place on the trip." Answer: hottest. Hint: Look at the last three letters of hot, then recall the spelling rule for adding an ending.
10. Form the superlative of easy: "This level is the ___ one in the game." Answer: easiest. Hint: Look at the last letter of easy, then recall the spelling rule for adding an ending.
11. Form the superlative of brave: "The lifeguard is the ___ swimmer on the team." Answer: bravest. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take est, and longer ones take most.
12. Form the superlative of expensive: "The bike is the ___ gift in the shop." Answer: most expensive. Hint: Count the syllables. Short adjectives take est, and longer ones take most.

## Pathway 3: Adjectives vs. Adverbs (9 topics)

### 3.1 What Does the Adjective Describe?

See 2.1 What Does the Adjective Describe? above.

### 3.2 Identifying Adjectives Before and After Nouns

See 2.2 Identifying Adjectives Before and After Nouns above.

### 3.3 What Does the Adverb Describe?

**Teaching card**
- Definition: An adverb is a word that describes a verb, an adjective, or another adverb. It often tells how, when, where, or how much.
- Example: "The turtle walked slowly across the yard." Slowly is the adverb. It describes walked, and it tells how the turtle walked.
- How it works: Find a word that tells how, when, where, or how much, then ask which verb, adjective, or adverb it describes.
- Tip box: "She sang loudly." Loudly describes sang. "He arrived early." Early describes arrived.

**Quiz**
1. "The turtle walked slowly across the yard." What does slowly describe? Answer: walked. Hint: Ask which word slowly tells more about.
2. "She sang loudly in the shower." What does loudly describe? Answer: sang. Hint: Ask which word loudly tells more about.
3. "He arrived early for the game." What does early describe? Answer: arrived. Hint: Ask which word early tells more about.
4. "The baby slept soundly all night." What does soundly describe? Answer: slept. Hint: Ask which word soundly tells more about.
5. "They looked everywhere for the lost key." What does everywhere describe? Answer: looked. Hint: Ask which word everywhere tells more about.
6. "The wind blew fiercely during the storm." What does fiercely describe? Answer: blew. Hint: Ask which word fiercely tells more about.
7. "Maya spoke softly to the rabbit." What does softly describe? Answer: spoke. Hint: Ask which word softly tells more about.
8. "The class worked quietly on the posters." What does quietly describe? Answer: worked. Hint: Ask which word quietly tells more about.
9. "Leo ran quickly to catch the bus." What does quickly describe? Answer: ran. Hint: Ask which word quickly tells more about.
10. "The snow fell gently on the rooftops." What does gently describe? Answer: fell. Hint: Ask which word gently tells more about.
11. "She almost finished the whole puzzle." What does almost describe? Answer: finished. Hint: Ask which word almost tells more about.
12. "The birds sang sweetly at dawn." What does sweetly describe? Answer: sang. Hint: Ask which word sweetly tells more about.

### 3.4 Identifying Adverbs of Time, Place, and Manner

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Adverbs of time tell when. Adverbs of place tell where. Adverbs of manner tell how.
- Example: "We will leave tomorrow." Tomorrow is an adverb of time. "The cat hid underneath." Underneath is an adverb of place. "She danced gracefully." Gracefully is an adverb of manner.
- How it works: Ask when, where, or how about the verb. The answer is the adverb, and the question tells its kind.
- Tip box: "He practiced yesterday." Yesterday tells when. "The ball rolled away." Away tells where. "They laughed happily." Happily tells how.

**Quiz**
1. "We will leave tomorrow." What kind of adverb is tomorrow? Answer: adverb of time. Hint: Ask whether tomorrow tells when, where, or how.
2. "The cat crawled underneath and hid." What kind of adverb is underneath? Answer: adverb of place. Hint: Ask whether underneath tells when, where, or how.
3. "She danced gracefully across the stage." What kind of adverb is gracefully? Answer: adverb of manner. Hint: Ask whether gracefully tells when, where, or how.
4. "He practiced yesterday after school." What kind of adverb is yesterday? Answer: adverb of time. Hint: Ask whether yesterday tells when, where, or how.
5. "The ball rolled away down the hill." What kind of adverb is away? Answer: adverb of place. Hint: Ask whether away tells when, where, or how.
6. "They laughed happily at the joke." What kind of adverb is happily? Answer: adverb of manner. Hint: Ask whether happily tells when, where, or how.
7. "The baby cried loudly all night." What kind of adverb is loudly? Answer: adverb of manner. Hint: Ask whether loudly tells when, where, or how.
8. "Please sit here for the story." What kind of adverb is here? Answer: adverb of place. Hint: Ask whether here tells when, where, or how.
9. "The train arrives soon." What kind of adverb is soon? Answer: adverb of time. Hint: Ask whether soon tells when, where, or how.
10. "We visited the fair recently." What kind of adverb is recently? Answer: adverb of time. Hint: Ask whether recently tells when, where, or how.
11. "The kite flew high above the field." What kind of adverb is high? Answer: adverb of place. Hint: Ask whether high tells when, where, or how.
12. "Ana read the poem softly to the class." What kind of adverb is softly? Answer: adverb of manner. Hint: Ask whether softly tells when, where, or how.

### 3.5 Is the Word an Adverb or Adjective?

**Teaching card**
- Definition: An adjective describes a noun. An adverb describes a verb, an adjective, or another adverb. To tell them apart, find what the word describes.
- Example: "The quick rabbit hopped away." Quick is an adjective because it describes rabbit, a noun. "The rabbit hopped quickly away." Quickly is an adverb because it describes hopped, a verb.
- How it works: Ask what the word tells about. If it tells about a noun, it is an adjective. If it tells about a verb, an adjective, or another adverb, it is an adverb.
- Tip box: "A soft voice." Soft describes voice, a noun, so it is an adjective. "She spoke softly." Softly describes spoke, a verb, so it is an adverb.

**Quiz**
1. "The quick rabbit hopped away." Is quick an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adjective. Hint: Ask what quick describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.
2. "The rabbit hopped quickly away." Is quickly an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adverb. Hint: Ask what quickly describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.
3. "A soft voice called from the hall." Is soft an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adjective. Hint: Ask what soft describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.
4. "She spoke softly to the baby." Is softly an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adverb. Hint: Ask what softly describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.
5. "The slow turtle crossed the road." Is slow an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adjective. Hint: Ask what slow describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.
6. "The turtle crossed the road slowly." Is slowly an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adverb. Hint: Ask what slowly describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.
7. "The sun shone brightly all day." Is brightly an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adverb. Hint: Ask what brightly describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.
8. "It was a bright morning." Is bright an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adjective. Hint: Ask what bright describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.
9. "A careful driver checked the mirror." Is careful an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adjective. Hint: Ask what careful describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.
10. "The driver checked the mirror carefully." Is carefully an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adverb. Hint: Ask what carefully describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.
11. "The thunder shook the windows loudly." Is loudly an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adverb. Hint: Ask what loudly describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.
12. "The loud thunder shook the windows." Is loud an adjective or an adverb? Answer: adjective. Hint: Ask what loud describes, then check whether that word is a noun or a verb.

### 3.6 Using Adverbs and Adjectives

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Good writers pick adjectives to describe nouns and adverbs to describe verbs. Using the right one makes a sentence clear and correct.
- Example: "The careful painter mixed the colors." Careful is an adjective describing painter. "The painter mixed the colors carefully." Carefully is an adverb describing mixed.
- How it works: Decide what you want to describe. A noun takes an adjective. A verb takes an adverb.
- Tip box: "A good singer" uses good, an adjective, for the noun singer. "She sings well" uses well, an adverb, for the verb sings.

**Quiz**
1. Fill in the blank: "The ___ painter mixed the colors." Choose careful or carefully. Answer: careful. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.
2. Fill in the blank: "The painter mixed the colors ___." Choose careful or carefully. Answer: carefully. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.
3. Fill in the blank: "She is a ___ singer." Choose good or well. Answer: good. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.
4. Fill in the blank: "She sings ___." Choose good or well. Answer: well. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.
5. Fill in the blank: "The ___ runner finished first." Choose quick or quickly. Answer: quick. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.
6. Fill in the blank: "The runner finished ___." Choose quick or quickly. Answer: quickly. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.
7. Fill in the blank: "The sun shone ___ all afternoon." Choose beautiful or beautifully. Answer: beautifully. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.
8. Fill in the blank: "It was a ___ day for a picnic." Choose beautiful or beautifully. Answer: beautiful. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.
9. Fill in the blank: "Leo gave a ___ answer." Choose clear or clearly. Answer: clear. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.
10. Fill in the blank: "Leo explained the rules ___." Choose clear or clearly. Answer: clearly. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.
11. Fill in the blank: "The dog waited ___ by the door." Choose patient or patiently. Answer: patiently. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.
12. Fill in the blank: "The ___ dog waited by the door." Choose patient or patiently. Answer: patient. Hint: Find the word the blank describes, then ask whether it is a noun or a verb.

### 3.7 Forming Comparative Adjectives

See 2.4 Forming Comparative Adjectives above.

### 3.8 Forming Superlative Adjectives

See 2.5 Forming Superlative Adjectives above.

### 3.9 Forming Comparative and Superlative Adverbs

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Adverbs also have comparative and superlative forms. For most short adverbs, add er for two things and est for three or more. For most longer adverbs, use more and most.
- Example: "Ana runs faster than Ben." Faster compares two runners. "Ana runs the fastest on the team." Fastest compares three or more runners. "She practiced more carefully than last week." More carefully compares two efforts.
- How it works: Compare two actions with er or more. Compare three or more actions with est or most.
- Tip box: "He spoke louder than the bell." Louder compares two sounds. "She finished the most quickly of all." Most quickly compares three or more finishers.

**Quiz**
1. Form the comparative adverb of fast: "Ana runs ___ than Ben." Answer: faster. Hint: Short adverbs take er or est, and most adverbs ending in ly take more or most.
2. Form the superlative adverb of fast: "Ana runs the ___ on the team." Answer: fastest. Hint: Short adverbs take er or est, and most adverbs ending in ly take more or most.
3. Form the comparative adverb of carefully: "She practiced ___ than last week." Answer: more carefully. Hint: Short adverbs take er or est, and most adverbs ending in ly take more or most.
4. Form the superlative adverb of carefully: "She practiced the ___ of all." Answer: most carefully. Hint: Short adverbs take er or est, and most adverbs ending in ly take more or most.
5. Form the comparative adverb of loud: "He spoke ___ than the bell." Answer: louder. Hint: Short adverbs take er or est, and most adverbs ending in ly take more or most.
6. Form the superlative adverb of loud: "He spoke the ___ in the room." Answer: loudest. Hint: Short adverbs take er or est, and most adverbs ending in ly take more or most.
7. Form the comparative adverb of early: "The bus arrived ___ than usual." Answer: earlier. Hint: Early is a short adverb. Look at its last letter, then recall the spelling rule for adding an ending.
8. Form the superlative adverb of early: "The bus arrived the ___ of all." Answer: earliest. Hint: Early is a short adverb. Look at its last letter, then recall the spelling rule for adding an ending.
9. Form the comparative adverb of hard: "Maya worked ___ than yesterday." Answer: harder. Hint: Short adverbs take er or est, and most adverbs ending in ly take more or most.
10. Form the superlative adverb of hard: "Maya worked the ___ in the class." Answer: hardest. Hint: Short adverbs take er or est, and most adverbs ending in ly take more or most.
11. Form the comparative adverb of slowly: "The turtle moved ___ than the snail." Answer: more slowly. Hint: Short adverbs take er or est, and most adverbs ending in ly take more or most.
12. Form the superlative adverb of slowly: "The turtle moved the ___ of all the animals." Answer: most slowly. Hint: Short adverbs take er or est, and most adverbs ending in ly take more or most.

## Pathway 4: Articles (2 topics)

### 4.1 Identifying Articles

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Articles are the words a, an, and the. They come before a noun and tell whether the noun is general or specific.
- Example: "The cat sat on a mat." The points to one specific cat. A points to any mat, not a particular one.
- How it works: A and an introduce a general noun. The introduces a specific noun that the reader already knows about.
- Tip box: "I saw a bird." Any bird, not a certain one. "I saw the bird in our yard." One certain bird.

**Quiz**
1. "The cat sat on a mat." Which word is the article that points to a specific cat? Answer: The. Hint: Of the two articles, ask which one points to something specific.
2. "I saw a bird in our park." Which word is the article? Answer: a. Hint: Look for a, an, or the in front of a noun.
3. "An owl hooted all night." Which word is the article? Answer: An. Hint: Look for a, an, or the in front of a noun.
4. "The library opens at nine." Which word is the article? Answer: The. Hint: Look for a, an, or the in front of a noun.
5. "Sam found a coin on his path." Which word is the article? Answer: a. Hint: Look for a, an, or the in front of a noun.
6. "Please pass the salt." Which word is the article? Answer: the. Hint: Look for a, an, or the in front of a noun.
7. "An apple fell from our tree." Which word is the article? Answer: An. Hint: Look for a, an, or the in front of a noun.
8. "The moon rose over those hills." Which word is the article? Answer: The. Hint: Look for a, an, or the in front of a noun.
9. "Leo built a fort in his backyard." Which word is the article? Answer: a. Hint: Look for a, an, or the in front of a noun.
10. "The teacher read a story to the class." Which words are the articles? Answer: The, a, and the. Hint: Look for a, an, or the in front of each noun.
11. "An ant carried a crumb across the floor." Which words are the articles? Answer: An and a. Hint: Look for a, an, or the in front of each noun.
12. "The river flows through the valley." Which words are the articles? Answer: The and the. Hint: Look for a, an, or the in front of each noun.

### 4.2 "A" vs. "An"

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Use a before a word that starts with a consonant sound. Use an before a word that starts with a vowel sound.
- Example: "A dog barked at a cat." Dog and cat start with consonant sounds. "An owl sat on an oak branch." Owl and oak start with vowel sounds.
- How it works: Say the next word out loud. If it starts with a vowel sound, use an. If it starts with a consonant sound, use a.
- Tip box: "A hat" and "an hour." Hour starts with a vowel sound even though it starts with the letter h. "A university" and "an umbrella." University starts with a consonant sound even though it starts with a vowel letter.

**Quiz**
1. "Sam saw ___ eagle." Choose a or an. Answer: an. Hint: Say eagle out loud and listen to its first sound.
2. "Sam saw ___ dog." Choose a or an. Answer: a. Hint: Say dog out loud and listen to its first sound.
3. "It was ___ honor to meet the mayor." Choose a or an. Answer: an. Hint: Say honor out loud and listen to its first sound.
4. "It was ___ nice day for a picnic." Choose a or an. Answer: a. Hint: Say nice out loud and listen to its first sound.
5. "Ana is ___ honest student." Choose a or an. Answer: an. Hint: Say honest out loud and listen to its first sound.
6. "Leo ate ___ orange for lunch." Choose a or an. Answer: an. Hint: Say orange out loud and listen to its first sound.
7. "Leo rode ___ bike to school." Choose a or an. Answer: a. Hint: Say bike out loud and listen to its first sound.
8. "She wrote ___ essay about the ocean." Choose a or an. Answer: an. Hint: Say essay out loud and listen to its first sound.
9. "He joined ___ club for young coders." Choose a or an. Answer: a. Hint: Say club out loud and listen to its first sound.
10. "It took ___ hour to finish the puzzle." Choose a or an. Answer: an. Hint: Say hour out loud and listen to its first sound.
11. "Maya saw ___ unicorn in her picture book." Choose a or an. Answer: a. Hint: Say unicorn out loud and listen to its first sound.
12. "Ben found ___ old map in the attic." Choose a or an. Answer: an. Hint: Say old out loud and listen to its first sound.

## Pathway 5: Body Paragraphs: Claims, Evidence, and Reasoning (2 topics)

### 5.1 Recognizing Strong Topic Sentences 1: Claims

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, What a claim is. Guiding question: What is the writer trying to prove? A claim is the main opinion in a paragraph. It is a statement the writer must support with facts and reasons. Worked passage: "Schools should keep art classes. Art helps students think in new ways, and it gives every student a chance to shine." The claim is the first sentence. It states the opinion the paragraph will prove.
- Section 2, What makes a claim strong. Guiding question: Is the claim clear and debatable? A strong claim is clear, specific, and something a reasonable person could disagree with. Worked passage: "Recess should last 30 minutes." This claim is clear and specific, and someone could argue for a different length. A weak claim would be "Recess is nice," which is vague and hard to argue about.
- Section 3, Claims in a paragraph. Guiding question: Where does the claim belong? The claim usually comes first, as the topic sentence, and the rest of the paragraph supports it. Worked passage: "Every student should learn to swim. Swimming is a life skill, pools are open all year, and lessons are safe with a trained teacher." The claim opens the paragraph, and the sentences after it give reasons.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "Schools should keep art classes. Art helps students think in new ways, and it gives every student a chance to shine." Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim is the opinion the paragraph will prove.
2. Passage: "The town needs a new library. The old one is too small for the crowds on weekends. A bigger library would hold more books and more computers." Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim states what the writer thinks should happen.
3. Which is the stronger claim: "Dogs are good." or "Every family with a yard should consider adopting a dog from the shelter."? Answer: "Every family with a yard should consider adopting a dog from the shelter." Hint: A strong claim is clear, specific, and debatable.
4. Which is the stronger claim: "The school garden is the best garden." or "The school garden should grow food for the cafeteria."? Answer: "The school garden should grow food for the cafeteria." Hint: A strong claim is specific and can be supported with reasons.
5. Passage: "Homework should be shorter on Fridays. Students are tired by the end of the week. Shorter homework would still cover the key ideas." Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Find the sentence that the other sentences support.
6. Passage: "Evening games end in the dark. Players cannot see the ball after sunset. The soccer field needs new lights." Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: The claim is the opinion the writer wants to prove.
7. Which is the stronger claim: "Every classroom should have a shelf of books students can borrow." or "Books are nice."? Answer: "Every classroom should have a shelf of books students can borrow." Hint: A strong claim is clear and specific, not vague.
8. Passage: "The park should add a skate ramp. Many students ride skateboards after school. A ramp would give them a safe place to practice." Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Ask which sentence gives an opinion and which sentences give reasons.
9. Which is the stronger claim: "Music is fun." or "Music class should meet twice a week so students can practice their instruments."? Answer: "Music class should meet twice a week so students can practice their instruments." Hint: A strong claim names something specific that can be argued about.
10. Passage: "Fresh fruit is healthy and easy to eat. The cafeteria should serve fresh fruit every day. Students would choose it over chips." Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The claim states the opinion the paragraph will prove.
11. Which is the weaker claim: "The library should stay open later." or "Libraries are great."? Answer: "Libraries are great." Hint: A weak claim is vague and hard to support with reasons.
12. Passage: "Bikes should be allowed on the path by the river. The path is wide enough for walkers and riders. Signs would keep everyone safe." Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Find the sentence that the rest of the paragraph supports.

### 5.2 Identifying Claims, Evidence, and Reasoning

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, Claims. Guiding question: What is the writer trying to prove? A claim is the opinion the writer wants to prove. Worked passage: "Our school should start classes later in the morning. Students learn better when they are rested, and a later start would help everyone focus." The claim is the first sentence. It states the opinion the paragraph will prove.
- Section 2, Evidence. Guiding question: What fact backs up the claim? Evidence is a fact, an example, or a quote that supports the claim. Worked passage: "A study of 200 schools found that students scored higher on morning tests after start times moved later." The evidence is the sentence with the study result. It gives a fact that supports the claim.
- Section 3, Reasoning. Guiding question: How does the evidence connect to the claim? Reasoning explains why the evidence matters and how it proves the claim. Worked passage: "When students sleep longer, they pay attention in class, so later start times lead to better learning." The reasoning is the sentence that links the fact to the claim. It explains why the evidence matters.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "The town library should stay open on Sundays. A survey found that many families can visit the library only on weekends. Opening on Sundays would let those families use the library when they are free." Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim is the opinion the writer wants to prove.
2. Same passage. Which sentence is evidence for the claim? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact that backs up the claim.
3. Passage: "Recess should be longer. Children who play outside every day concentrate better in class. A longer break gives them time to run, play, and return ready to learn." Which sentence is the reasoning? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Reasoning explains how the evidence connects to the claim.
4. Passage: "Every student should learn to cook a simple meal. Cooking teaches planning and measuring. A school cooking club met once a week, and members cooked lunch for their class." Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim states what the writer thinks should happen.
5. Same passage. Which sentence is evidence? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Evidence is something that actually happened, a fact that supports the claim.
6. Passage: "The class trip to the river taught us more than any textbook. We measured the water, counted the fish, and wrote down what we saw. Doing the work ourselves made the science stick." Which sentence is the reasoning? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Reasoning says why the evidence matters for the claim.
7. Passage: "The school should plant a vegetable garden. At one school, students who worked in a garden ate twice as many vegetables at lunch. When students grow food, they care more about what they eat, so a garden builds healthy habits." Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim is the opinion the paragraph will prove.
8. Same passage. Which sentence is evidence? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence gives facts that support the claim.
9. Same passage. Which sentence is reasoning? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Reasoning links the evidence to the claim.
10. Passage: "Walking to school is good for students. A survey found that students who walk arrive more awake and ready to learn. Being awake in the morning helps them focus in their first classes." Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim is the opinion the writer wants to prove.
11. Same passage. Which sentence is evidence? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact, an example, or a result that supports the claim.
12. Same passage. Which sentence is reasoning? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Reasoning explains how the evidence proves the claim.

## Pathway 6: Body Paragraphs: Embedding Evidence (5 topics)

### 6.1 Context for Literary Evidence 1: Identifying Speaker, Listener, and Plot Clue

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, What context is. Guiding question: What does the reader need to know before the quote? Context is the background a reader needs to understand a piece of evidence from a story. It names who is speaking, who is listening, and what is happening in the plot. Worked passage: "In the chapter where the young sailor first meets the captain, the sailor says, 'I have never seen a storm like this.'" The context tells us the speaker is the sailor, the listener is the captain, and the plot moment is their first meeting.
- Section 2, Why the speaker and listener matter. Guiding question: Who says it, and to whom? Knowing the speaker and listener helps the reader judge what the words mean. Worked passage: "When the teacher asks the class why the bridge fell, one student answers, 'The wood was rotten.'" The speaker is a student, the listener is the teacher, and that tells us the words are an answer to a question.
- Section 3, Why the plot clue matters. Guiding question: What is happening in the story at this moment? A plot clue places the quote in the story so the reader can follow it. Worked passage: "Just after the storm destroys the barn, the farmer looks at the field and says, 'We will rebuild.'" The plot clue is the storm destroying the barn, and it explains why the farmer speaks of rebuilding.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "In the chapter where the young sailor first meets the captain, the sailor says, 'I have never seen a storm like this.'" Who is the speaker? Answer: the young sailor. Hint: The speaker is the person whose words are quoted.
2. Same passage. Who is the listener? Answer: the captain. Hint: The listener is the person the speaker is talking to.
3. Same passage. What is the plot clue? Answer: the sailor is meeting the captain for the first time. Hint: A plot clue tells what is happening in the story at this moment.
4. Passage: "When the teacher asks the class why the bridge fell, one student answers, 'The wood was rotten.'" Who is the speaker? Answer: one student. Hint: The speaker is the person whose words are quoted.
5. Same passage. Who is the listener? Answer: the teacher. Hint: The listener is the person the speaker is talking to.
6. Same passage. What is the plot clue? Answer: the class is talking about why the bridge fell. Hint: A plot clue tells what is happening in the story at this moment.
7. Passage: "Just after the storm destroys the barn, the farmer looks at the field and says, 'We will rebuild.'" Who is the speaker? Answer: the farmer. Hint: The speaker is the person whose words are quoted.
8. Same passage. What is the plot clue? Answer: the storm just destroyed the barn. Hint: A plot clue tells what is happening in the story at this moment.
9. Passage: "At the end of the race, the coach tells the runner, 'You ran your best race today.'" Who is the speaker? Answer: the coach. Hint: The speaker is the person whose words are quoted.
10. Same passage. Who is the listener? Answer: the runner. Hint: The listener is the person the speaker is talking to.
11. Same passage. What is the plot clue? Answer: the race has just ended. Hint: A plot clue tells what is happening in the story at this moment.
12. Which context is missing: "Maya said, 'I will never forget this day.'" with no other information? Answer: the listener and the plot clue are missing. Hint: Good context names the speaker, the listener, and what is happening in the plot.

### 6.2 Context for Nonfiction Evidence 1: Evaluating Source Information and Reporting Verbs

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, What source information is. Guiding question: Where did this fact come from? Source information tells the reader who wrote or said the fact and where it was published, so the reader can judge whether to trust it. Worked passage: "Dr. Patel, a scientist who studies oceans, writes in her 2024 book that sea levels are rising." The source information is Dr. Patel, a scientist who studies oceans, and her 2024 book.
- Section 2, Reporting verbs. Guiding question: Which verb introduces the fact? A reporting verb such as states, explains, notes, or warns introduces a fact from a source. The verb you choose shapes how the reader hears the fact. Worked passage: "The report warns that the river is polluted." Warns tells the reader the fact is serious. "The report notes that the river is polluted." Notes tells the reader the fact is simply stated.
- Section 3, Putting them together. Guiding question: How do source information and a reporting verb work together? Together they let the reader know who said it and how strongly it was said. Worked passage: "In her guide to city parks, ranger Ana Lopez explains that the new trail is safe for families." The source is ranger Ana Lopez and her guide, and explains introduces the fact in a calm, clear way.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "Dr. Patel, a scientist who studies oceans, writes in her 2024 book that sea levels are rising." What is the source information? Answer: Dr. Patel, a scientist who studies oceans, and her 2024 book. Hint: Source information tells who wrote the fact and where it was published.
2. Same passage. Which word is the reporting verb? Answer: writes. Hint: A reporting verb introduces a fact from a source.
3. Passage: "The report warns that the river is polluted." What does warns tell the reader? Answer: The fact is serious. Hint: The reporting verb you choose shapes how the reader hears the fact.
4. Passage: "The report notes that the river is polluted." What does notes tell the reader? Answer: The fact is simply stated. Hint: The reporting verb you choose shapes how the reader hears the fact.
5. Passage: "In her guide to city parks, ranger Ana Lopez explains that the new trail is safe for families." What is the source information? Answer: ranger Ana Lopez and her guide to city parks. Hint: Source information tells who wrote the fact and where it was published.
6. Same passage. Which word is the reporting verb? Answer: explains. Hint: A reporting verb introduces a fact from a source.
7. Which reporting verb fits a serious warning: "The study ___ that the ice is melting fast." Choose states or warns. Answer: warns. Hint: Think about which verb sounds urgent and which sounds neutral.
8. Which reporting verb fits a simple fact: "The chart ___ that rain fell on ten days last month." Choose warns or shows. Answer: shows. Hint: Think about which verb sounds calm and which sounds urgent.
9. Passage: "Coach Rivera, who has trained runners for 20 years, says that rest days prevent injuries." What is the source information? Answer: Coach Rivera, who has trained runners for 20 years. Hint: Source information tells who said the fact and why that person knows about it.
10. Same passage. Which word is the reporting verb? Answer: says. Hint: A reporting verb introduces a fact from a source.
11. Why does source information matter? Answer: It helps the reader judge whether to trust the fact. Hint: Knowing who said it and where it was published lets the reader check it.
12. Passage: "A librarian who studies local history reports that the first school opened in 1910." Which word is the reporting verb? Answer: reports. Hint: A reporting verb introduces a fact from a source.

### 6.3 Evaluating Paraphrases for Plagiarism

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, What a paraphrase is. Guiding question: What does it mean to put an idea in your own words? A paraphrase restates someone else's idea using your own words and your own sentence structure. It is not a copy with a few words swapped. Worked passage: Original: "Bees carry pollen from flower to flower, which helps plants make seeds." Good paraphrase: "When bees move pollen between flowers, plants can produce seeds." The idea is the same, but the words and the sentence shape are new.
- Section 2, What plagiarism looks like. Guiding question: When is a restatement too close to the original? Copying a sentence and changing one or two words is still plagiarism. Worked passage: Original: "Bees carry pollen from flower to flower, which helps plants make seeds." Too close: "Bees carry pollen from bloom to bloom, which helps plants make seeds." Only one word changed, so it is still a copy.
- Section 3, How to paraphrase safely. Guiding question: How do I make it truly my own? Read the idea, look away, write it in your own words, and name the source. Worked passage: Original: "The moon has no light of its own. It shines because it reflects light from the sun." Safe paraphrase: "Moonlight is really sunlight bouncing off the moon, since the moon does not glow by itself." The words and the shape are new. When you use it in your writing, name the source too.

**Quiz**
1. Original: "Bees carry pollen from flower to flower, which helps plants make seeds." Which is a good paraphrase? Answer: "When bees move pollen between flowers, plants can produce seeds." Hint: A good paraphrase uses new words and a new sentence shape.
2. Same original. Which is too close to the original? Answer: "Bees carry pollen from bloom to bloom, which helps plants make seeds." Hint: Changing one word is still a copy.
3. Original: "The moon has no light of its own. It shines because it reflects light from the sun." Which is a good paraphrase? Answer: "Moonlight is really sunlight bouncing off the moon, since the moon does not glow by itself." Hint: A good paraphrase restates the idea with new words and a new shape.
4. Same original. Which is too close to the original? Answer: "The moon has no light of its own. It glows because it reflects light from the sun." Hint: Changing one word is still a copy.
5. Original: "Wolves hunt in packs to catch large animals." Which is a good paraphrase? Answer: "By hunting together, wolves can bring down animals that are bigger than one wolf alone." Hint: A good paraphrase uses new words and a new sentence shape.
6. Same original. Which is too close to the original? Answer: "Wolves hunt in groups to catch large animals." Hint: Changing one word is still a copy.
7. What is the first step to paraphrase safely? Answer: Read the idea, look away, and write it in your own words. Hint: Looking away keeps you from copying the original wording.
8. Original: "Rain fills the rivers that farmers use to water their crops." Which is a good paraphrase? Answer: "Farmers depend on river water from rainfall to grow their crops." Hint: A good paraphrase restates the idea with new words and a new shape.
9. Same original. Which is too close to the original? Answer: "Rain fills the streams that farmers use to water their crops." Hint: Changing one word is still a copy.
10. Why must you name the source even when you paraphrase? Answer: The idea still belongs to someone else, so the reader needs to know where it came from. Hint: A paraphrase is not your own idea, so credit the source.
11. Original: "The library stays open late during exams." Which is a good paraphrase? Answer: "During exam week, students can use the library into the evening." Hint: A good paraphrase uses new words and a new sentence shape.
12. Same original. Which is too close to the original? Answer: "The library remains open late during exams." Hint: Changing one word is still a copy.

### 6.4 Matching Pronouns and Verbs between Context and Quotations

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, What matching means. Guiding question: Why must the words around a quote agree with the quote? When you introduce a quote, the pronouns and verbs in your own sentence must match the pronouns and verbs inside the quote, or the sentence will not make sense. Worked passage: Quote: "I will finish the poster." Your sentence: "Maya said that she would finish the poster." She matches I, since both point to Maya, and would finish matches the plan Maya stated. A mismatch would be "Maya said that he would finish the poster," which changes who does the work.
- Section 2, Pronouns. Guiding question: Whose words are these? The pronoun in your lead-in must point to the same person as the pronoun in the quote. Worked passage: Quote: "You played well." Your sentence: "The coach told the team that they had played well." You in the quote points to the team, so your sentence uses they, not a pronoun for one player.
- Section 3, Verbs. Guiding question: When did it happen? The verb tense in your lead-in must match the time in the quote. Worked passage: "Ana explained that she had read the book." Had read is past, and explained is past, so the times agree. A mismatch would be "Ana explains that she had read the book," which mixes present and past.

**Quiz**
1. Maya's words: "I will finish the poster." Which sentence matches: "Maya said that she would finish the poster." or "Maya said that he would finish the poster."? Answer: "Maya said that she would finish the poster." Hint: The pronoun in your sentence must point to the same person as in the quote.
2. The coach's words to the team: "You played well." Which sentence matches: "The coach told the team that he had played well." or "The coach told the team that they had played well."? Answer: "The coach told the team that they had played well." Hint: Ask who you points to in the quote, then pick the pronoun for that group.
