3. Ana's words: "I read the book." Which sentence matches: "Ana explained that she had read the book." or "Ana explains that she had read the book."? Answer: "Ana explained that she had read the book." Hint: The verb tense in your sentence must match the time in the quote.
4. Ben's words: "My bike is broken." Which sentence matches: "Ben said that his bike was broken." or "Ben said that her bike was broken."? Answer: "Ben said that his bike was broken." Hint: Ask who my points to in the quote.
5. The twins' words: "We are ready." Which sentence matches: "The twins said that she was ready." or "The twins said that they were ready."? Answer: "The twins said that they were ready." Hint: Ask who we points to in the quote.
6. Leo's words to us: "I am coming." Which sentence matches: "Leo told us that he was coming." or "Leo told us that we were coming."? Answer: "Leo told us that he was coming." Hint: Ask who I points to in the quote.
7. The teacher's words: "The test is tomorrow." Which sentence matches: "The teacher says that the test was tomorrow." or "The teacher said that the test was tomorrow."? Answer: "The teacher said that the test was tomorrow." Hint: The verb tense in your sentence must match the time in the quote.
8. Zoe's words in her journal: "I loved the trip." Which sentence matches: "Zoe wrote that she loved the trip." or "Zoe wrote that they loved the trip."? Answer: "Zoe wrote that she loved the trip." Hint: Ask who wrote the words and who I points to.
9. The players' words: "Our coach is proud." Which sentence matches: "The players said that their coach was proud." or "The players said that his coach was proud."? Answer: "The players said that their coach was proud." Hint: Ask who our points to in the quote.
10. Sam's words to our group: "Are you ready?" Which sentence matches: "Sam asked if they were ready." or "Sam asked if we were ready."? Answer: "Sam asked if we were ready." Hint: Ask who you points to when Sam speaks to our group.
11. Maya's words: "I will help." Which sentence matches: "Maya promised that she will help." or "Maya promised that she would help."? Answer: "Maya promised that she would help." Hint: Look at the tense of promised, then match the verb that follows it.
12. The guide's words: "The trail is safe." Which sentence matches: "The guide explained that the trail was safe." or "The guide explains that the trail was safe."? Answer: "The guide explained that the trail was safe." Hint: The verb tense in your sentence must match the time in the quote.

### 6.5 Formatting In-Text Citations

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, What an in-text citation is. Guiding question: How do I show where a fact came from inside my writing? An in-text citation is a short note in parentheses after a fact or quote that names the source. Worked passage: "Wolves hunt in packs (Rivera 24)." The note in parentheses tells the reader the fact came from Rivera, page 24.
- Section 2, Where it goes. Guiding question: Where does the citation sit? The citation comes right after the fact or quote, before the period at the end of the sentence. Worked passage: "The river is the longest in the state (Lopez 41)." The citation sits before the period, not after it.
- Section 3, What it contains. Guiding question: What goes inside the parentheses? For a book, put the author's last name and the page number, with no comma between them. Worked passage: "Bees visit hundreds of flowers in one day (Patel 12)." Patel is the author, and 12 is the page.

**Quiz**
1. Which sentence formats the citation correctly: "Wolves hunt in packs (Rivera 24)." or "Wolves hunt in packs. (Rivera 24)"? Answer: "Wolves hunt in packs (Rivera 24)." Hint: Check where the period sits compared with the parentheses.
2. Which sentence formats the citation correctly: "The river is the longest in the state (Lopez, 41)." or "The river is the longest in the state (Lopez 41)."? Answer: "The river is the longest in the state (Lopez 41)." Hint: Check what sits between the name and the page number.
3. Which sentence formats the citation correctly: "Bees visit hundreds of flowers in one day (Patel 12)." or "Bees visit hundreds of flowers in one day (Patel p. 12)."? Answer: "Bees visit hundreds of flowers in one day (Patel 12)." Hint: Look closely at what comes before the page number.
4. Which sentence formats the citation correctly: "The moon reflects sunlight (Kim 8)." or "The moon reflects sunlight (Kim, page 8)."? Answer: "The moon reflects sunlight (Kim 8)." Hint: Look closely at what goes inside the parentheses.
5. Add the citation to this sentence: "The first school opened in 1910." The source is Rivera, page 33. Answer: "The first school opened in 1910 (Rivera 33)." Hint: Find the end of the fact, then check where the period belongs.
6. What goes inside the parentheses for a book source? Answer: The author's last name and the page number. Hint: Look back at the worked examples and see what each set of parentheses holds.
7. Which sentence formats the citation correctly: "The trail is safe for families (Lopez 15)." or "The trail is safe for families (Lopez 15.)"? Answer: "The trail is safe for families (Lopez 15)." Hint: Look at which side of the closing parenthesis the period is on.
8. Which sentence formats the citation correctly: "Rain fills the rivers (Patel 19) ." or "Rain fills the rivers (Patel 19)."? Answer: "Rain fills the rivers (Patel 19)." Hint: Look closely at the space before the period.
9. Add the citation to this sentence: "The library holds 20,000 books." The source is Ana Lopez, page 7. Answer: "The library holds 20,000 books (Lopez 7)." Hint: Put the author's last name and the page number in parentheses before the period.
10. Add the citation to this sentence: "Wolves can run for miles without stopping." The source is Sam Rivera, page 52. Answer: "Wolves can run for miles without stopping (Rivera 52)." Hint: Put the author's last name and the page number in parentheses before the period.
11. Which sentence formats the citation correctly: "The bridge was built in 1950 (Kim, 21)." or "The bridge was built in 1950 (Kim 21)."? Answer: "The bridge was built in 1950 (Kim 21)." Hint: Check what sits between the name and the page number.
12. What is the purpose of an in-text citation? Answer: It shows the reader where a fact or quote came from. Hint: A citation names the source so the reader can check it.

## Pathway 7: Body Paragraphs: Transition Words and Phrases (1 topic)

### 7.1 Using the Appropriate Transition Words and Phrases

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, What transitions do. Guiding question: How do I connect one idea to the next? Transition words and phrases link sentences and show how ideas relate, such as adding, contrasting, or showing cause and effect. Worked passage: "The team practiced every day. As a result, they won the final game." As a result shows that the win followed from the practice.
- Section 2, Choosing the right transition. Guiding question: Which transition fits the link? For adding an idea, use also, in addition, or furthermore. For contrast, use however, on the other hand, or nevertheless. For cause and effect, use therefore, as a result, or consequently. Worked passage: "The rain was heavy. However, the game went on." However shows a contrast between the rain and the game going on.
- Section 3, Where transitions belong. Guiding question: Where does the transition sit? A transition usually starts the sentence it connects, followed by a comma. Worked passage: "First, the class planned the garden. Next, they dug the beds. Finally, they planted the seeds." First, next, and finally show the order of steps.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "The team practiced every day. As a result, they won the final game." What does as a result show? Answer: cause and effect. Hint: Ask how winning the final game connects to practicing every day.
2. Which transition adds an idea: "The garden grew tomatoes. ___, it grew peppers." Choose however or in addition. Answer: in addition. Hint: Ask whether the peppers add to the tomatoes or contrast with them.
3. Which transition shows contrast: "The rain was heavy. ___, the game went on." Choose however or therefore. Answer: however. Hint: Ask whether the game going on fits with the heavy rain or goes against it.
4. Which transition shows cause and effect: "The bus was late. ___, we missed the first class." Choose on the other hand or as a result. Answer: as a result. Hint: Ask whether missing class happened because of the late bus or in contrast to it.
5. Passage: "First, the class planned the garden. Next, they dug the beds. Finally, they planted the seeds." What do first, next, and finally show? Answer: the order of steps. Hint: Ask what first, next, and finally tell you about the steps.
6. Which transition fits: "The movie was long. ___, it was exciting." Choose however or furthermore. Answer: however. Hint: Ask whether exciting goes along with long or goes against it.
7. Which transition fits: "The library is quiet. ___, it has many books." Choose furthermore or although. Answer: furthermore. Hint: Ask whether having many books adds to being quiet or contrasts with it.
8. Which transition fits: "She studied hard. ___, she passed the test." Choose on the other hand or therefore. Answer: therefore. Hint: Ask whether passing happened because of studying or in contrast to it.
9. Where does a transition usually sit? Answer: At the start of the sentence it connects, followed by a comma. Hint: Look back at the worked passages and notice where each transition sits and what punctuation follows it.
10. Which transition shows time order: "We packed our bags. ___, we left for camp." Choose then or however. Answer: then. Hint: Ask whether leaving came after packing or went against it.
11. Which transition fits: "The soup was hot. ___, it was delicious." Choose although or in addition. Answer: in addition. Hint: Ask whether being delicious adds to being hot or goes against it.
12. Passage: "The road was closed. Therefore, we took a different route." What does therefore show? Answer: cause and effect. Hint: Ask how taking a different route connects to the closed road.

## Pathway 8: Building Compound and Complex Sentences (10 topics)

### 8.1 Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions (FANBOYS)

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Coordinating conjunctions join two equal parts of a sentence, such as two words, two phrases, or two complete sentences. The seven are for, and, nor, but, or, yet, and so. The first letters spell FANBOYS.
- Example: "Sam wanted to play soccer, but it was raining." But joins two complete sentences.
- How it works: Look for for, and, nor, but, or, yet, or so between two equal parts. Each one shows a different link, such as adding, contrasting, or choosing.
- Tip box: "Maya likes apples and oranges." And joins two words. "We can walk or ride our bikes." Or joins two choices.

**Quiz**
1. "Sam wanted to play soccer, but it was raining." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: but. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two complete sentences.
2. "Maya likes apples and oranges." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: and. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two things Maya likes.
3. "We can walk or ride our bikes." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: or. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two ways to travel.
4. "The team practiced hard, so they won the game." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: so. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two complete sentences.
5. "Leo did not want soup, nor did he want salad." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: nor. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two complete sentences.
6. "She was tired, yet she finished the race." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: yet. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two complete sentences.
7. "He studied all week, for the test was hard." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: for. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two complete sentences.
8. "Ana brought a book and a snack." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: and. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two things Ana brought.
9. "You can sit here or over there." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: or. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two places to sit.
10. "The rain fell, but the picnic went on." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: but. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two complete sentences.
11. "The dog barked, yet no one woke up." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: yet. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two complete sentences.
12. "We packed lunch, so we ate at the park." Which word is the coordinating conjunction? Answer: so. Hint: Look for one of the FANBOYS words between the two complete sentences.

### 8.2 Identifying the Function of FANBOYS

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Each FANBOYS conjunction has a job. For gives a reason. And adds. Nor gives a negative choice. But shows a contrast. Or gives a choice. Yet shows a surprising contrast. So shows a result.
- Example: "She was hungry, so she ate lunch." So shows a result. "She was hungry, but she waited." But shows a contrast.
- How it works: Read the two parts the conjunction joins, then ask what link it makes. The link tells you the function.
- Tip box: "He ran fast, for he was late." For gives a reason. "You can swim or sunbathe." Or gives a choice.

**Quiz**
1. "She was hungry, so she ate lunch." What is the function of so? Answer: It shows a result. Hint: Ask how eating lunch connects to being hungry.
2. "She was hungry, but she waited." What is the function of but? Answer: It shows a contrast. Hint: Ask how waiting connects to being hungry.
3. "He ran fast, for he was late." What is the function of for? Answer: It gives a reason. Hint: Ask how being late connects to running fast.
4. "You can swim or sunbathe." What is the function of or? Answer: It gives a choice. Hint: Ask how swimming and sunbathing are linked.
5. "Maya likes apples and oranges." What is the function of and? Answer: It adds. Hint: Ask how oranges and apples are linked.
6. "Leo did not want soup, nor did he want salad." What is the function of nor? Answer: It gives a negative choice. Hint: Ask how the soup and the salad are linked.
7. "She was tired, yet she finished the race." What is the function of yet? Answer: It shows a surprising contrast. Hint: Ask whether finishing is what you would expect from someone who was tired.
8. "We packed lunch, so we ate at the park." What is the function of so? Answer: It shows a result. Hint: Ask how eating at the park connects to packing lunch.
9. "He likes to read, for books open new worlds." What is the function of for? Answer: It gives a reason. Hint: Ask how books opening new worlds connects to liking to read.
10. "You can draw or paint." What is the function of or? Answer: It gives a choice. Hint: Ask how drawing and painting are linked.
11. "The room was dark, but the lamp was on." What is the function of but? Answer: It shows a contrast. Hint: Ask how the lamp being on connects to the dark room.
12. "Ana brought a map and a compass." What is the function of and? Answer: It adds. Hint: Ask how the map and the compass are linked.

### 8.3 Punctuating FANBOYS (checkpoint)

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When a FANBOYS conjunction joins two complete sentences, put a comma before the conjunction. When it joins two words or phrases, do not use a comma.
- Example: "Sam wanted to play, but it rained." A comma comes before but because two complete sentences are joined. "Sam wanted to play soccer and basketball." No comma, because and joins two words.
- How it works: Check both sides of the conjunction. If each side could stand alone as a sentence, add a comma before the conjunction. If not, leave the comma out.
- Tip box: "Maya read a book, and Leo read a magazine." Comma before and, two complete sentences. "Maya read a book and a magazine." No comma, and joins two words.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "Sam wanted to play but it rained." Answer: "Sam wanted to play, but it rained." Hint: Check whether each side of but could stand alone as a sentence.
2. Fix the punctuation: "Maya read a book and Leo read a magazine." Answer: "Maya read a book, and Leo read a magazine." Hint: Check whether each side of and could stand alone as a sentence.
3. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "Maya read a book and a magazine." or "Maya read a book, and a magazine."? Answer: "Maya read a book and a magazine." Hint: Check whether each side of and could stand alone as a sentence.
4. Fix the punctuation: "The team practiced hard so they won." Answer: "The team practiced hard, so they won." Hint: Check whether each side of so could stand alone as a sentence.
5. Fix the punctuation: "We can walk or ride our bikes." Is a comma needed? Answer: No comma is needed. Hint: Check whether each side of or could stand alone as a sentence.
6. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "She was tired yet she finished the race." or "She was tired, yet she finished the race."? Answer: "She was tired, yet she finished the race." Hint: Check whether each side of yet could stand alone as a sentence.
7. Fix the punctuation: "He studied all week for the test was hard." Answer: "He studied all week, for the test was hard." Hint: Check whether each side of for could stand alone as a sentence.
8. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "Ana brought a book, and a snack." or "Ana brought a book and a snack."? Answer: "Ana brought a book and a snack." Hint: Check whether each side of and could stand alone as a sentence.
9. Fix the punctuation: "The rain fell but the picnic went on." Answer: "The rain fell, but the picnic went on." Hint: Check whether each side of but could stand alone as a sentence.
10. Fix the punctuation: "You can sit here or over there." Is a comma needed? Answer: No comma is needed. Hint: Check whether each side of or could stand alone as a sentence.
11. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "Leo did not want soup nor did he want salad." or "Leo did not want soup, nor did he want salad."? Answer: "Leo did not want soup, nor did he want salad." Hint: Check whether each side of nor could stand alone as a sentence.
12. Fix the punctuation: "The dog barked and the cat ran away." Answer: "The dog barked, and the cat ran away." Hint: Check whether each side of and could stand alone as a sentence.

### 8.4 Correcting Comma Splices and Fused Sentences

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A comma splice joins two complete sentences with only a comma. A fused sentence joins two complete sentences with nothing at all. Both are errors. Fix them with a period, a semicolon, or a comma plus a FANBOYS conjunction.
- Example: Comma splice: "The bell rang, the students left." Fixed: "The bell rang, so the students left." Fused: "The bell rang the students left." Fixed: "The bell rang. The students left."
- How it works: Find the two complete sentences. Then separate them with a period, join them with a semicolon, or join them with a comma and a FANBOYS word.
- Tip box: "It rained all day, we stayed inside." Fix: "It rained all day, so we stayed inside." "The dog barked the cat ran." Fix: "The dog barked. The cat ran."

**Quiz**
1. Fix this comma splice with a comma and a FANBOYS word: "The bell rang, the students left." Answer: "The bell rang, so the students left." Hint: Find where the first complete sentence ends, then choose the FANBOYS word that shows how the ideas connect.
2. Fix this fused sentence with a period: "The dog barked the cat ran." Answer: "The dog barked. The cat ran." Hint: Find where the first complete sentence ends.
3. Fix this comma splice with a comma and a FANBOYS word: "It rained all day, we stayed inside." Answer: "It rained all day, so we stayed inside." Hint: Find where the first complete sentence ends, then choose the FANBOYS word that shows how the ideas connect.
4. Fix this fused sentence with a period: "Maya finished her book she started a new one." Answer: "Maya finished her book. She started a new one." Hint: Find where the first complete sentence ends.
5. Is this a comma splice, a fused sentence, or correct: "The sun set, the sky turned orange."? Answer: comma splice. Hint: Look at what sits between the two complete sentences.
6. Is this a comma splice, a fused sentence, or correct: "The sun set the sky turned orange."? Answer: fused sentence. Hint: Find where the first complete sentence ends and see what is there.
7. Fix this comma splice with a comma and a FANBOYS word: "Leo won the race, he smiled at the crowd." Answer: "Leo won the race, and he smiled at the crowd." Hint: Find where the first complete sentence ends, then choose the FANBOYS word that shows how the ideas connect.
8. Fix this fused sentence with a period: "The library was quiet everyone was reading." Answer: "The library was quiet. Everyone was reading." Hint: Find where the first complete sentence ends.
9. Fix this comma splice with a semicolon: "The cake was ready, we ate it at once." Answer: "The cake was ready; we ate it at once." Hint: A semicolon can join two complete sentences.
10. Fix this fused sentence with a comma and a FANBOYS word: "Ana packed her bag she left for camp." Answer: "Ana packed her bag, and she left for camp." Hint: Find where the first complete sentence ends, then choose the FANBOYS word that shows how the ideas connect.
11. Is this sentence correct: "The bus arrived on time, so we left early."? Answer: Yes, it is correct. Hint: Check what joins the two complete sentences.
12. Fix this comma splice with a comma and a FANBOYS word: "The garden grew fast, the tomatoes ripened in July." Answer: "The garden grew fast, and the tomatoes ripened in July." Hint: Find where the first complete sentence ends, then choose the FANBOYS word that shows how the ideas connect.

### 8.5 Identifying Conjunctive Adverbs

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Conjunctive adverbs connect ideas between sentences or clauses. Common ones are therefore, however, also, moreover, furthermore, meanwhile, otherwise, instead, and nevertheless.
- Example: "The bus was late. Therefore, we missed the first class." Therefore connects the two sentences and shows a result.
- How it works: Look for a word that links one sentence to the one before it and shows how the ideas relate, such as a result, a contrast, or an addition.
- Tip box: "It rained. However, the game went on." However shows a contrast. "She practiced. Meanwhile, her brother read." Meanwhile shows two things happening at once.

**Quiz**
1. "The bus was late. Therefore, we missed the first class." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: Therefore. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.
2. "It rained. However, the game went on." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: However. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.
3. "She practiced. Meanwhile, her brother read." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: Meanwhile. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.
4. "He forgot his umbrella. Otherwise, he would have stayed dry." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: Otherwise. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.
5. "The class was quiet. Furthermore, everyone finished early." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: Furthermore. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.
6. "The path was closed. Instead, we took the long way." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: Instead. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.
7. "The team was tired. Nevertheless, they kept playing." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: Nevertheless. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.
8. "Ana loves to read. Also, she loves to write." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: Also. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.
9. "The store was closed. Consequently, we went home." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: Consequently. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.
10. "The test was hard. Still, Leo passed." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: Still. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.
11. "It snowed all night. Finally, the plows cleared the road." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: Finally. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.
12. "The garden grew well. Indeed, it grew better than ever." Which word is the conjunctive adverb? Answer: Indeed. Hint: Look for the word at the start of the second sentence that links it to the first.

### 8.6 Punctuating Conjunctive Adverbs at the Beginning of a Sentence

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When a conjunctive adverb starts a sentence, put a comma after it. The conjunctive adverb links the new sentence to the one before it.
- Example: "The bus was late. Therefore, we missed the first class." A comma follows therefore.
- How it works: Find the conjunctive adverb at the start of the sentence, then add a comma right after it before the rest of the sentence.
- Tip box: "It rained. However, the game went on." A comma follows however. "She practiced. Meanwhile, her brother read." A comma follows meanwhile.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "The bus was late. Therefore we missed the first class." Answer: "The bus was late. Therefore, we missed the first class." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.
2. Fix the punctuation: "It rained. However the game went on." Answer: "It rained. However, the game went on." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.
3. Fix the punctuation: "She practiced. Meanwhile her brother read." Answer: "She practiced. Meanwhile, her brother read." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.
4. Fix the punctuation: "He forgot his umbrella. Otherwise he would have stayed dry." Answer: "He forgot his umbrella. Otherwise, he would have stayed dry." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.
5. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "The class was quiet. Furthermore everyone finished early." or "The class was quiet. Furthermore, everyone finished early."? Answer: "The class was quiet. Furthermore, everyone finished early." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.
6. Fix the punctuation: "The path was closed. Instead we took the long way." Answer: "The path was closed. Instead, we took the long way." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.
7. Fix the punctuation: "The team was tired. Nevertheless they kept playing." Answer: "The team was tired. Nevertheless, they kept playing." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.
8. Fix the punctuation: "Ana loves to read. Also she loves to write." Answer: "Ana loves to read. Also, she loves to write." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.
9. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "The store was closed. Consequently we went home." or "The store was closed. Consequently, we went home."? Answer: "The store was closed. Consequently, we went home." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.
10. Fix the punctuation: "The test was hard. Still Leo passed." Answer: "The test was hard. Still, Leo passed." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.
11. Fix the punctuation: "It snowed all night. Finally the plows cleared the road." Answer: "It snowed all night. Finally, the plows cleared the road." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.
12. Fix the punctuation: "The garden grew well. Indeed it grew better than ever." Answer: "The garden grew well. Indeed, it grew better than ever." Hint: Find the word that links the second sentence to the first, and check what should follow it.

### 8.7 Punctuating Conjunctive Adverbs in the Middle of a Sentence

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When a conjunctive adverb sits in the middle of a sentence, put a comma before it and a comma after it. The conjunctive adverb breaks the sentence into two parts and links the ideas.
- Example: "The bus was late; therefore, we missed the first class." The conjunctive adverb therefore is set off by a semicolon before it and a comma after it. "We missed the first class, therefore, because the bus was late." Commas on both sides set the conjunctive adverb apart.
- How it works: A conjunctive adverb in the middle of a sentence needs a comma before it and a comma after it. When it joins two complete sentences, a semicolon comes before it instead of a comma.
- Tip box: "It rained; however, the game went on." A semicolon comes before however, and a comma comes after it. "The game, however, went on." Commas sit on both sides of however.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "It rained however the game went on." Answer: "It rained; however, the game went on." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.
2. Fix the punctuation: "The game however went on." Answer: "The game, however, went on." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.
3. Fix the punctuation: "The bus was late therefore we missed the first class." Answer: "The bus was late; therefore, we missed the first class." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.
4. Fix the punctuation: "She practiced meanwhile her brother read." Answer: "She practiced; meanwhile, her brother read." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.
5. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "The class was quiet furthermore everyone finished early." or "The class was quiet; furthermore, everyone finished early."? Answer: "The class was quiet; furthermore, everyone finished early." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.
6. Fix the punctuation: "The team was tired nevertheless they kept playing." Answer: "The team was tired; nevertheless, they kept playing." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.
7. Fix the punctuation: "Ana loves to read also she loves to write." Answer: "Ana loves to read; also, she loves to write." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.
8. Fix the punctuation: "The store was closed consequently we went home." Answer: "The store was closed; consequently, we went home." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.
9. Fix the punctuation: "The test was hard still Leo passed." Answer: "The test was hard; still, Leo passed." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.
10. Fix the punctuation: "It snowed all night finally the plows cleared the road." Answer: "It snowed all night; finally, the plows cleared the road." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.
11. Fix the punctuation: "The garden grew well indeed it grew better than ever." Answer: "The garden grew well; indeed, it grew better than ever." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.
12. Fix the punctuation: "He forgot his umbrella otherwise he would have stayed dry." Answer: "He forgot his umbrella; otherwise, he would have stayed dry." Hint: Check whether the linking word joins two complete sentences or interrupts just one.

### 8.8 Identifying Subordinating Conjunctions

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Subordinating conjunctions join a dependent clause to an independent clause. Common ones are since, when, although, because, if, and while. The group is wider, including after, before, unless, until, and though.
- Example: "We stayed inside because it rained." Because starts the dependent clause because it rained and joins it to the main clause we stayed inside.
- How it works: Look for a word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone, and joins it to a clause that can.
- Tip box: "Although it was late, Ana finished her homework." Although starts a dependent clause. "We will leave when the bus arrives." When starts a dependent clause.

**Quiz**
1. "We stayed inside because it rained." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: because. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.
2. "Although it was late, Ana finished her homework." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: Although. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.
3. "We will leave when the bus arrives." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: when. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.
4. "Since it was raining, we stayed inside." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: Since. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.
5. "If you finish early, you can read." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: If. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.
6. "While the baby slept, Maya read a book." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: While. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.
7. "We went home after the game ended." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: after. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.
8. "You cannot play unless you finish your chores." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: unless. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.
9. "The dog waited until Leo came home." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: until. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.
10. "Though it was cold, they played outside." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: Though. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.
11. "We left before the rain started." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: before. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.
12. "Ana smiled because she won the prize." Which word is the subordinating conjunction? Answer: because. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone.

### 8.9 Punctuating Subordinating Conjunctions at the Beginning of a Sentence

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When a dependent clause with a subordinating conjunction comes first, put a comma after the whole dependent clause, before the main clause starts.
- Example: "Although it was late, Ana finished her homework." A comma follows the dependent clause although it was late.
- How it works: Find the dependent clause at the start, then add a comma where it ends and the main clause begins.
- Tip box: "When the bus arrives, we will leave." A comma follows when the bus arrives. "Since it was raining, we stayed inside." A comma follows since it was raining.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "Although it was late Ana finished her homework." Answer: "Although it was late, Ana finished her homework." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
2. Fix the punctuation: "When the bus arrives we will leave." Answer: "When the bus arrives, we will leave." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
3. Fix the punctuation: "Since it was raining we stayed inside." Answer: "Since it was raining, we stayed inside." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
4. Fix the punctuation: "If you finish early you can read." Answer: "If you finish early, you can read." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
5. Fix the punctuation: "While the baby slept Maya read a book." Answer: "While the baby slept, Maya read a book." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
6. Fix the punctuation: "After the game ended we went home." Answer: "After the game ended, we went home." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
7. Fix the punctuation: "Unless you finish your chores you cannot play." Answer: "Unless you finish your chores, you cannot play." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
8. Fix the punctuation: "Until Leo came home the dog waited." Answer: "Until Leo came home, the dog waited." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
9. Fix the punctuation: "Though it was cold they played outside." Answer: "Though it was cold, they played outside." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
10. Fix the punctuation: "Before the rain started we left." Answer: "Before the rain started, we left." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
11. Fix the punctuation: "Because it rained we stayed inside." Answer: "Because it rained, we stayed inside." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
12. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "When the bell rang the students left." or "When the bell rang, the students left."? Answer: "When the bell rang, the students left." Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.

### 8.10 Punctuating Subordinating Conjunctions in the Middle of a Sentence

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When a dependent clause with a subordinating conjunction comes after the main clause, no comma is needed. The sentence reads smoothly without one.
- Example: "We stayed inside because it rained." No comma comes before because. "Ana worked on her homework until it was done." No comma comes before until.
- How it works: Read the sentence. If the main clause comes first and the dependent clause follows, leave the comma out.
- Tip box: "We will leave when the bus arrives." No comma. "You cannot play unless you finish your chores." No comma.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "We stayed inside, because it rained." Answer: "We stayed inside because it rained." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.
2. Fix the punctuation: "Ana started her homework, as soon as she got home." Answer: "Ana started her homework as soon as she got home." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.
3. Fix the punctuation: "We will leave, when the bus arrives." Answer: "We will leave when the bus arrives." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.
4. Fix the punctuation: "You cannot play, unless you finish your chores." Answer: "You cannot play unless you finish your chores." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.
5. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "The dog waited, until Leo came home." or "The dog waited until Leo came home."? Answer: "The dog waited until Leo came home." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.
6. Fix the punctuation: "We went home, after the game ended." Answer: "We went home after the game ended." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.
7. Fix the punctuation: "They played outside, whenever it was sunny." Answer: "They played outside whenever it was sunny." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.
8. Fix the punctuation: "We left, before the rain started." Answer: "We left before the rain started." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.
9. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "Ana smiled, because she won the prize." or "Ana smiled because she won the prize."? Answer: "Ana smiled because she won the prize." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.
10. Fix the punctuation: "The baby slept, while Maya read a book." Answer: "The baby slept while Maya read a book." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.
11. Fix the punctuation: "We stayed home, since it was raining." Answer: "We stayed home since it was raining." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.
12. Fix the punctuation: "You can read, if you finish early." Answer: "You can read if you finish early." Hint: Check whether the main clause or the dependent clause comes first.

## Pathway 9: Capitalizing and Formatting Titles (2 topics)

### 9.1 Recognizing Titles of Long-Form and Short-Form Works

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Long-form works are books, movies, plays, albums, magazines, and newspapers. Short-form works are chapters, poems, songs, articles, and short stories. The kind of work tells you how to format its title.
- Example: "The Prince and the Pauper" is a book, a long-form work. "The Road Not Taken" is a poem, a short-form work.
- How it works: Ask how long the work is and how it is published. A work that stands alone is long-form. A work that appears inside a bigger work is short-form.
- Tip box: A movie is long-form. A song on an album is short-form. A newspaper is long-form. An article in a newspaper is short-form.

**Quiz**
1. "The Prince and the Pauper" is a book. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: long-form. Hint: Ask whether a book stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.
2. "The Road Not Taken" is a poem in a book of poems. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: short-form. Hint: Ask whether a poem stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.
3. "The Hillcrest Herald" is a newspaper. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: long-form. Hint: Ask whether a newspaper stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.
4. "How to Plant a Garden" is an article in a magazine. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: short-form. Hint: Ask whether an article stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.
5. "The Secret of Pine Lake" is a movie. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: long-form. Hint: Ask whether a movie stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.
6. "Twinkle, Twinkle, Little Star" is a song on an album. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: short-form. Hint: Ask whether a song stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.
7. "The First Day of School" is a chapter in a book. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: short-form. Hint: Ask whether a chapter stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.
8. "Romeo and Juliet" is a play. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: long-form. Hint: Ask whether a play stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.
9. "Songs from the Harbor" is an album. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: long-form. Hint: Ask whether an album stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.
10. "The Gift of the Magi" is a short story in a collection. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: short-form. Hint: Ask whether a short story stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.
11. "Backyard Science Monthly" is a magazine. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: long-form. Hint: Ask whether a magazine stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.
12. "Why the Sky Is Blue" is a chapter in a science book. Is it long-form or short-form? Answer: short-form. Hint: Ask whether a chapter stands alone or appears inside a bigger work.

### 9.2 Capitalizing and Formatting Titles 1

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Capitalize the first word and all important words in a title. Do not capitalize small words such as a, an, the, and, or, of, and to, unless they are the first word. In print, long-form titles are italicized and short-form titles go in quotation marks.
- Example: "The Prince and the Pauper" capitalizes The, Prince, and Pauper, and leaves the small words and and the lower case. In print it would be italicized because it is a book. "The Road Not Taken" is in quotation marks because it is a poem.
- How it works: Capitalize the first word and every important word. Leave small words lower case. Italicize long-form titles and put short-form titles in quotation marks.
- Tip box: "Black Beauty" is a book, so it is italicized in print. "The Itsy Bitsy Spider" is a song, so it goes in quotation marks.

**Quiz**
1. Capitalize this title: "the prince and the pauper" Answer: "The Prince and the Pauper" Hint: Capitalize the first word and all important words, and check which small words stay lower case.
2. Capitalize this title: "the road not taken" Answer: "The Road Not Taken" Hint: Capitalize the first word and all important words.
3. Capitalize this title: "how to plant a garden" Answer: "How to Plant a Garden" Hint: Capitalize the first word and all important words, and check which small words stay lower case.
4. Capitalize this title: "the gift of the magi" Answer: "The Gift of the Magi" Hint: Capitalize the first word and all important words, and check which small words stay lower case.
5. Capitalize this title: "twinkle, twinkle, little star" Answer: "Twinkle, Twinkle, Little Star" Hint: Capitalize the first word and all important words.
6. "Black Beauty" is a book. In print, should it be italicized or in quotation marks? Answer: italicized. Hint: Decide whether a book is long-form or short-form, then use the matching format.
7. "The Itsy Bitsy Spider" is a song. In print, should it be italicized or in quotation marks? Answer: in quotation marks. Hint: Decide whether a song is long-form or short-form, then use the matching format.
8. Capitalize this title: "a tale of two cities" Answer: "A Tale of Two Cities" Hint: Capitalize the first word and all important words, and check which small words stay lower case.
9. Capitalize this title: "the wonderful wizard of oz" Answer: "The Wonderful Wizard of Oz" Hint: Capitalize the first word and all important words, and check which small words stay lower case.
10. "The Hillcrest Herald" is a newspaper. In print, should it be italicized or in quotation marks? Answer: italicized. Hint: Decide whether a newspaper is long-form or short-form, then use the matching format.
11. "Why the Sky Is Blue" is a chapter. In print, should it be italicized or in quotation marks? Answer: in quotation marks. Hint: Decide whether a chapter is long-form or short-form, then use the matching format.
12. Capitalize this title: "romeo and juliet" Answer: "Romeo and Juliet" Hint: Capitalize the first word and all important words, and check which small words stay lower case.

## Pathway 10: Choosing the Appropriate Conjunctions (6 topics)

### 10.1 Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions (FANBOYS)

See 8.1 Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions (FANBOYS) above.

### 10.2 Identifying Subordinating Conjunctions

See 8.8 Identifying Subordinating Conjunctions above.

### 10.3 Identifying Conjunctive Adverbs

See 8.5 Identifying Conjunctive Adverbs above.

### 10.4 Using the Appropriate FANBOYS

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Pick the FANBOYS word that fits the link between the two parts. And adds, but contrasts, or chooses, so shows a result, for gives a reason, nor joins negative choices, and yet shows a surprising contrast.
- Example: "She studied hard, so she passed." So fits because passing is the result of studying. "She studied hard, but she was nervous." But fits because being nervous contrasts with studying hard.
- How it works: Read both parts, ask what link they need, then pick the FANBOYS word that makes that link.
- Tip box: "It rained, but we played." But fits a contrast. "It rained, so we stayed home." So fits a result.

**Quiz**
1. Fill in the blank: "She studied hard, ___ she passed." Choose so or but. Answer: so. Hint: Ask whether passing is a result of studying or a contrast with it.
2. Fill in the blank: "She studied hard, ___ she was nervous." Choose so or but. Answer: but. Hint: Ask whether being nervous is a result of studying hard or a contrast with it.
3. Fill in the blank: "It rained, ___ we played." Choose but or so. Answer: but. Hint: Ask whether playing is a result of the rain or a contrast with it.
4. Fill in the blank: "It rained, ___ we stayed home." Choose but or so. Answer: so. Hint: Ask whether staying home is a result of the rain or a contrast with it.
5. Fill in the blank: "You can walk ___ ride your bike." Choose and or or. Answer: or. Hint: Ask whether walking and riding are added together or offered as a choice.
6. Fill in the blank: "Ana brought a map ___ a compass." Choose and or but. Answer: and. Hint: Ask whether the compass adds to the map or contrasts with it.
7. Fill in the blank: "He ran fast, ___ he was late." Choose so or for. Answer: for. Hint: Ask whether being late is a reason for running fast or a result of it.
8. Fill in the blank: "Leo did not want soup, ___ did he want salad." Choose nor or and. Answer: nor. Hint: Notice the not in the first part, and ask which conjunction carries a negative idea forward.
9. Fill in the blank: "She was tired, ___ she finished the race." Choose so or yet. Answer: yet. Hint: Ask whether finishing is what you would expect from someone who was tired.
10. Fill in the blank: "We packed lunch, ___ we ate at the park." Choose so or but. Answer: so. Hint: Ask whether eating at the park is a result of packing lunch or a contrast with it.
11. Fill in the blank: "Maya likes apples ___ oranges." Choose and or or. Answer: and. Hint: Ask whether Maya likes both fruits or must choose one.
12. Fill in the blank: "The lamp was on, ___ the room was still dark." Choose so or but. Answer: but. Hint: Ask whether the room staying dark is a result of the lamp being on or a contrast with it.

### 10.5 Matching Correlative Conjunctions

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Correlative conjunctions work in pairs. The pairs are both and, either or, neither nor, not only but also, and whether or.
- Example: "Both Ana and Leo finished the race." Both pairs with and. "She not only won the race but also set a record." Not only pairs with but also.
- How it works: When you see the first half of a pair, the second half must follow. Both needs and. Either needs or. Neither needs nor. Not only needs but also. Whether needs or.
- Tip box: "Either you come now or you stay home." Either pairs with or. "Neither the rain nor the wind stopped them." Neither pairs with nor.

**Quiz**
1. Match the pair: "Both Ana ___ Leo finished the race." Answer: and. Hint: Look at the first word of the pair, then recall its partner.
2. Match the pair: "Either you come now ___ you stay home." Answer: or. Hint: Look at the first word of the pair, then recall its partner.
3. Match the pair: "Neither the rain ___ the wind stopped them." Answer: nor. Hint: Look at the first word of the pair, then recall its partner.
4. Match the pair: "She not only won the race ___ set a record." Answer: but also. Hint: Look at the first words of the pair, then recall their partner.
5. Match the pair: "I do not know whether we will go ___ stay." Answer: or. Hint: Look at the first word of the pair, then recall its partner.
6. Match the pair: "Both the dog ___ the cat slept by the fire." Answer: and. Hint: Look at the first word of the pair, then recall its partner.
7. Match the pair: "Either the bus ___ the train will get you there." Answer: or. Hint: Look at the first word of the pair, then recall its partner.
8. Match the pair: "Neither the soup ___ the salad was hot." Answer: nor. Hint: Look at the first word of the pair, then recall its partner.
9. Match the pair: "The cake was not only delicious ___ beautiful." Answer: but also. Hint: Look at the first words of the pair, then recall their partner.
10. Match the pair: "I cannot tell whether it will rain ___ snow." Answer: or. Hint: Look at the first word of the pair, then recall its partner.
11. Match the pair: "Both the library ___ the museum are open today." Answer: and. Hint: Look at the first word of the pair, then recall its partner.
12. Match the pair: "Neither the key ___ the map was in the bag." Answer: nor. Hint: Look at the first word of the pair, then recall its partner.

### 10.6 Correcting FANBOYS Errors

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A FANBOYS error happens when the wrong conjunction is used, or when two complete sentences are joined with a FANBOYS word but no comma. Fix it by picking the right word and adding the comma.
- Example: "She was hungry but she waited." The word but is right, but the comma is missing. Fixed: "She was hungry, but she waited." "It rained, so we played outside." The word so is wrong, because playing outside is not a result of rain. Fixed: "It rained, but we played outside."
- How it works: First check the link. Does the conjunction match the link? Then check the punctuation. Two complete sentences need a comma before the conjunction.
- Tip box: "We were tired, but we kept playing." But fits the contrast, and the comma is there. "We were hungry, but we ate lunch." But does not fit, because eating lunch does not contrast with being hungry. Fixed: "We were hungry, so we ate lunch."

**Quiz**
1. Fix the error: "She was hungry but she waited." Answer: "She was hungry, but she waited." Hint: Check whether each side of but could stand alone, then check the punctuation.
2. Fix the error: "It rained, so we played outside." Answer: "It rained, but we played outside." Hint: Ask whether playing outside is a result of the rain or a contrast with it.
3. Fix the error: "Maya read a book and Leo read a magazine." Answer: "Maya read a book, and Leo read a magazine." Hint: Check whether each side of and could stand alone, then check the punctuation.
4. Fix the error: "We were hungry, but we ate lunch." Is this correct? Answer: No. The correct sentence is "We were hungry, so we ate lunch." Hint: Ask whether eating lunch contrasts with being hungry or follows from it.
5. Fix the error: "The team practiced hard so they won." Answer: "The team practiced hard, so they won." Hint: Check whether each side of so could stand alone, then check the punctuation.
6. Fix the error: "Is the answer true and false?" Answer: "Is the answer true or false?" Hint: Ask whether true and false can both be the answer or are two choices.
7. Fix the error: "Ana brought a map, or a compass." Answer: "Ana brought a map and a compass." Hint: Ask whether the map and the compass were both brought or offered as a choice, then check the comma.
8. Fix the error: "The rain fell but the picnic went on." Answer: "The rain fell, but the picnic went on." Hint: Check whether each side of but could stand alone, then check the punctuation.
9. Fix the error: "Leo did not want soup, or did he want salad." Answer: "Leo did not want soup, nor did he want salad." Hint: Notice the not in the first part, and ask which FANBOYS word carries a negative idea forward.
10. Fix the error: "She was tired, so she finished the race." Answer: "She was tired, yet she finished the race." Hint: Ask whether finishing is what you would expect from someone who was tired.
11. Fix the error: "We packed lunch so we ate at the park." Answer: "We packed lunch, so we ate at the park." Hint: Check whether each side of so could stand alone, then check the punctuation.
12. Fix the error: "The dog barked, and the cat ran away." Is this correct? Answer: Yes, it is correct. Hint: Check the link between the two parts, then check the punctuation.

## Pathway 11: Commas for Clarity (2 topics)

### 11.1 Commas with Introductory Phrases and Clauses

**Teaching card**
- Definition: An introductory phrase or clause comes at the start of a sentence and sets the scene. Put a comma after it, before the main part of the sentence starts.
- Example: "After the rain stopped, we went outside." After the rain stopped is an introductory clause, and a comma follows it. "In the morning, the birds sang." In the morning is an introductory phrase, and a comma follows it.
- How it works: Find the opening phrase or clause, then add a comma where it ends and the main clause begins.
- Tip box: "Before dinner, Ana set the table." A comma follows before dinner. "Under the bridge, a fox made its home." A comma follows under the bridge.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "After the rain stopped we went outside." Answer: "After the rain stopped, we went outside." Hint: Find where the opening clause ends and the main clause begins.
2. Fix the punctuation: "In the morning the birds sang." Answer: "In the morning, the birds sang." Hint: Find where the opening phrase ends and the main clause begins.
3. Fix the punctuation: "Before dinner Ana set the table." Answer: "Before dinner, Ana set the table." Hint: Find where the opening phrase ends and the main clause begins.
4. Fix the punctuation: "Under the bridge a fox made its home." Answer: "Under the bridge, a fox made its home." Hint: Find where the opening phrase ends and the main clause begins.
5. Fix the punctuation: "When the bell rang the students left." Answer: "When the bell rang, the students left." Hint: Find where the opening clause ends and the main clause begins.
6. Fix the punctuation: "On Saturday we visited the museum." Answer: "On Saturday, we visited the museum." Hint: Find where the opening phrase ends and the main clause begins.
7. Fix the punctuation: "After lunch the class went to the library." Answer: "After lunch, the class went to the library." Hint: Find where the opening phrase ends and the main clause begins.
8. Fix the punctuation: "In the garden the tomatoes grew tall." Answer: "In the garden, the tomatoes grew tall." Hint: Find where the opening phrase ends and the main clause begins.
9. Fix the punctuation: "Before the game the coach gave a speech." Answer: "Before the game, the coach gave a speech." Hint: Find where the opening phrase ends and the main clause begins.
10. Fix the punctuation: "During the storm the lights went out." Answer: "During the storm, the lights went out." Hint: Find where the opening phrase ends and the main clause begins.
11. Fix the punctuation: "After the movie we walked home." Answer: "After the movie, we walked home." Hint: Find where the opening phrase ends and the main clause begins.
12. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "In the evening the stars came out." or "In the evening, the stars came out."? Answer: "In the evening, the stars came out." Hint: Find where the opening phrase ends and the main clause begins.

### 11.2 Commas Separating Adjectives

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When two or more adjectives describe the same noun and each one works on its own, put a comma between them. Adjectives like these are called coordinate adjectives.
- Example: "The tall, leafy tree shaded the yard." Tall and leafy each describe the tree on their own, so a comma goes between them. "The bright red balloon floated away." Bright and red do not each work alone, so no comma goes between them.
- How it works: Try swapping the adjectives, and try putting and between them. If both tests work, the adjectives are coordinate and need a comma. If they fail, no comma is needed.
- Tip box: "A cold, rainy day." Cold and rainy can swap, and you can say cold and rainy, so use a comma. "A little black dog." Little and black cannot swap easily, so no comma.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "The tall leafy tree shaded the yard." Answer: "The tall, leafy tree shaded the yard." Hint: Try swapping tall and leafy, and try putting and between them.
2. Fix the punctuation: "The bright red balloon floated away." Is a comma needed? Answer: No comma is needed. Hint: Try swapping bright and red, and try putting and between them.
3. Fix the punctuation: "A cold rainy day kept us inside." Answer: "A cold, rainy day kept us inside." Hint: Try swapping cold and rainy, and try putting and between them.
4. Fix the punctuation: "A little black dog ran across the field." Is a comma needed? Answer: No comma is needed. Hint: Try swapping little and black, and try putting and between them.
5. Fix the punctuation: "The old dusty book sat on the shelf." Answer: "The old, dusty book sat on the shelf." Hint: Try swapping old and dusty, and try putting and between them.
6. Fix the punctuation: "A warm sunny morning greeted us." Answer: "A warm, sunny morning greeted us." Hint: Try swapping warm and sunny, and try putting and between them.
7. Fix the punctuation: "The deep blue lake sparkled in the sun." Is a comma needed? Answer: No comma is needed. Hint: Try swapping deep and blue, and try putting and between them.
8. Fix the punctuation: "A soft fluffy kitten purred in her lap." Answer: "A soft, fluffy kitten purred in her lap." Hint: Try swapping soft and fluffy, and try putting and between them.
9. Fix the punctuation: "The big round table filled the room." Is a comma needed? Answer: No comma is needed. Hint: Try swapping big and round, and try putting and between them.
10. Fix the punctuation: "A loud noisy crowd cheered at the game." Answer: "A loud, noisy crowd cheered at the game." Hint: Try swapping loud and noisy, and try putting and between them.
11. Fix the punctuation: "The fresh green grass covered the hill." Is a comma needed? Answer: No comma is needed. Hint: Try swapping fresh and green, and try putting and between them.
12. Fix the punctuation: "A brave strong firefighter climbed the ladder." Answer: "A brave, strong firefighter climbed the ladder." Hint: Try swapping brave and strong, and try putting and between them.

## Pathway 12: Commas for Formatting (2 topics)

### 12.1 Commas with Dates

**Teaching card**
- Definition: In a date, put a comma between the day and the year, and a comma after the year when the sentence goes on. When only the month and year appear, no comma is needed.
- Example: "School starts on September 2, 2026." A comma comes between 2 and 2026. "On September 2, 2026, we will meet at the park." A comma also comes after 2026.
- How it works: Write the month, then the day, then a comma, then the year. If the date sits in the middle of a sentence, add a comma after the year too.
- Tip box: "July 4, 1776, was a famous day." Commas sit on both sides of 1776. "July 1776 was a famous month." No commas, because only the month and year appear.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "School starts on September 2 2026." Answer: "School starts on September 2, 2026." Hint: Check the spot between the day and the year, and check whether the sentence keeps going after the year.
2. Fix the punctuation: "On September 2 2026 we will meet at the park." Answer: "On September 2, 2026, we will meet at the park." Hint: Check the spot between the day and the year, and check whether the sentence keeps going after the year.
3. Fix the punctuation: "July 4 1776 was a famous day." Answer: "July 4, 1776, was a famous day." Hint: Check the spot between the day and the year, and check whether the sentence keeps going after the year.
4. Fix the punctuation: "July 1776 was a famous month." Is a comma needed? Answer: No comma is needed. Hint: Check whether the date includes a day number.
5. Fix the punctuation: "The trip is on May 10 2027." Answer: "The trip is on May 10, 2027." Hint: Check the spot between the day and the year, and check whether the sentence keeps going after the year.
6. Fix the punctuation: "On May 10 2027 the class will visit the museum." Answer: "On May 10, 2027, the class will visit the museum." Hint: Check the spot between the day and the year, and check whether the sentence keeps going after the year.
7. Fix the punctuation: "Her birthday is March 3 2015." Answer: "Her birthday is March 3, 2015." Hint: Check the spot between the day and the year, and check whether the sentence keeps going after the year.
8. Fix the punctuation: "The library opened in June 2020." Is a comma needed? Answer: No comma is needed. Hint: Check whether the date includes a day number.
9. Fix the punctuation: "The fair runs from August 1 2026 to August 5 2026." Answer: "The fair runs from August 1, 2026, to August 5, 2026." Hint: Check the spot between the day and the year, and check whether the sentence keeps going after the year.
10. Fix the punctuation: "December 25 2026 falls on a Friday." Answer: "December 25, 2026, falls on a Friday." Hint: Check the spot between the day and the year, and check whether the sentence keeps going after the year.
11. Fix the punctuation: "We moved here in April 2019." Is a comma needed? Answer: No comma is needed. Hint: Check whether the date includes a day number.
12. Fix the punctuation: "On November 11 2018 the town held a parade." Answer: "On November 11, 2018, the town held a parade." Hint: Check the spot between the day and the year, and check whether the sentence keeps going after the year.

### 12.2 Commas with Items in a Series

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When three or more items appear in a list, put a comma after each item except the last one. The comma before and is called the Oxford comma, and it keeps the list clear.
- Example: "We packed apples, oranges, and bananas." Commas sit after apples and oranges. "Sam likes to swim, run, and bike." Commas sit after swim and run.
- How it works: List the items, put a comma after each one except the last, and put and before the last item.
- Tip box: "The flag is red, white, and blue." Commas sit after red and white. "We had soup, salad, and bread for lunch." Commas sit after soup and salad.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "We packed apples oranges and bananas." Answer: "We packed apples, oranges, and bananas." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.
2. Fix the punctuation: "Sam likes to swim run and bike." Answer: "Sam likes to swim, run, and bike." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.
3. Fix the punctuation: "The flag is red white and blue." Answer: "The flag is red, white, and blue." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.
4. Fix the punctuation: "We had soup salad and bread for lunch." Answer: "We had soup, salad, and bread for lunch." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.
5. Fix the punctuation: "Ana bought pencils pens and paper." Answer: "Ana bought pencils, pens, and paper." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.
6. Fix the punctuation: "The zoo has lions tigers and bears." Answer: "The zoo has lions, tigers, and bears." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.
7. Fix the punctuation: "We visited Paris London and Rome." Answer: "We visited Paris, London, and Rome." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.
8. Fix the punctuation: "The band played drums guitar and piano." Answer: "The band played drums, guitar, and piano." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.
9. Fix the punctuation: "Leo collects rocks shells and coins." Answer: "Leo collects rocks, shells, and coins." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.
10. Fix the punctuation: "We need seeds soil and water for the garden." Answer: "We need seeds, soil, and water for the garden." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.
11. Fix the punctuation: "The recipe calls for flour sugar and butter." Answer: "The recipe calls for flour, sugar, and butter." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.
12. Which sentence is punctuated correctly: "Maya likes cats dogs and birds." or "Maya likes cats, dogs, and birds."? Answer: "Maya likes cats, dogs, and birds." Hint: Count the items in the list, then check what should separate them.

## Pathway 13: Commonly Confused Words I (6 topics)

### 13.1 "It's" vs. "Its"

**Teaching card**
- Definition: It's is short for it is or it has. Its shows that something belongs to it.
- Example: "It's raining outside." It's means it is. "The dog wagged its tail." Its shows the tail belongs to the dog.
- How it works: Try replacing the word with it is. If the sentence still makes sense, use it's with an apostrophe. If not, use its with no apostrophe.
- Tip box: "It's time for lunch." It is time, so use it's. "The tree lost its leaves." The leaves belong to the tree, so use its.

**Quiz**
1. Fill in the blank: "___ raining outside." Choose it's or its. Answer: It's. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.
2. Fill in the blank: "The dog wagged ___ tail." Choose it's or its. Answer: its. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.
3. Fill in the blank: "___ time for lunch." Choose it's or its. Answer: It's. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.
4. Fill in the blank: "The tree lost ___ leaves." Choose it's or its. Answer: its. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.
5. Fill in the blank: "___ been a long day." Choose it's or its. Answer: It's. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.
6. Fill in the blank: "The cat licked ___ paws." Choose it's or its. Answer: its. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.
7. Fill in the blank: "___ a beautiful morning." Choose it's or its. Answer: It's. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.
8. Fill in the blank: "The bike lost ___ chain." Choose it's or its. Answer: its. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.
9. Fill in the blank: "___ going to snow tonight." Choose it's or its. Answer: It's. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.
10. Fill in the blank: "The school raised ___ flag." Choose it's or its. Answer: its. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.
11. Fill in the blank: "___ hard to learn a new language." Choose it's or its. Answer: It's. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.
12. Fill in the blank: "The robot moved ___ arm." Choose it's or its. Answer: its. Hint: Try reading the sentence with it is or it has in the blank. If that makes sense, you need the apostrophe. If the word shows belonging, you do not.

### 13.2 "A" vs. "An"

See 4.2 "A" vs. "An" above.

### 13.3 "To" vs. "Too" vs. "Two"

**Teaching card**
- Definition: To shows direction or purpose. Too means also, or more than enough. Two is the number 2.
- Example: "We went to the park." To shows direction. "I want to come too." Too means also. "She ate two apples." Two is the number.
- How it works: Ask what the word does. Direction or purpose means to. Also or too much means too. A number means two.
- Tip box: "Give the book to Ana." To shows direction. "The soup is too hot." Too means more than enough. "Two dogs ran past." Two is the number.

**Quiz**
1. Fill in the blank: "We are going ___ the library after lunch." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: to. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.
2. Fill in the blank: "I want to come ___." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: too. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.
3. Fill in the blank: "She ate ___ apples for lunch." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: two. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.
4. Fill in the blank: "Give the book ___ Ana." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: to. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.
5. Fill in the blank: "The soup is ___ hot to eat." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: too. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.
6. Fill in the blank: "___ dogs ran past the fence." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: Two. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.
7. Fill in the blank: "Leo went ___ the store for milk." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: to. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.
8. Fill in the blank: "The bag is ___ heavy to carry." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: too. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.
9. Fill in the blank: "It takes ___ hours to drive there." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: two. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.
10. Fill in the blank: "Maya wants to play, ___." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: too. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.
11. Fill in the blank: "Please listen ___ the teacher." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: to. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.
12. Fill in the blank: "The twins are ___ years old." Choose to, too, or two. Answer: two. Hint: Ask what the word does here: show direction or purpose, mean also or more than enough, or name the number 2.

### 13.4 "Know" vs. "No"

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Know means to have information in your mind. No means not any, or the opposite of yes.
- Example: "I know the answer." Know means I have the information. "There is no milk left." No means not any.
- How it works: Ask what the word does. Having information in your mind means know. Saying not any or refusing means no.
- Tip box: "Do you know the way home?" Know asks about information. "No, I do not." No is the opposite of yes.

**Quiz**
1. Fill in the blank: "I ___ the answer to that question." Choose know or no. Answer: know. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.
2. Fill in the blank: "There is ___ milk left in the fridge." Choose know or no. Answer: no. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.
3. Fill in the blank: "Do you ___ the way home?" Choose know or no. Answer: know. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.
4. Fill in the blank: "___, I do not want dessert." Choose Know or No. Answer: No. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.
5. Fill in the blank: "She does not ___ his name." Choose know or no. Answer: know. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.
6. Fill in the blank: "There are ___ clouds in the sky." Choose know or no. Answer: no. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.
7. Fill in the blank: "I ___ how to ride a bike." Choose know or no. Answer: know. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.
8. Fill in the blank: "___ one knew the secret." Choose Know or No. Answer: No. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.
9. Fill in the blank: "We ___ the words to the song." Choose know or no. Answer: know. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.
10. Fill in the blank: "There is ___ time to waste." Choose know or no. Answer: no. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.
11. Fill in the blank: "Does he ___ the rules of the game?" Choose know or no. Answer: know. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.
12. Fill in the blank: "___, the store is not open yet." Choose Know or No. Answer: No. Hint: Ask whether the word means having information in your mind, or means not any or the opposite of yes.

### 13.5 "A lot" vs. "Alot"

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A lot is two words and means a large amount. Alot is not a word at all.
- Example: "We had a lot of fun at the fair." A lot means a large amount of fun. "We had alot of fun." Alot is spelled wrong. Fixed: "We had a lot of fun."
- How it works: Always write a lot as two words. If you are tempted to write alot, split it into a lot.
- Tip box: "She has a lot of books." Two words. "There was a lot of snow." Two words.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the spelling: "We had alot of fun at the fair." Answer: "We had a lot of fun at the fair." Hint: Find the phrase that means a large amount, and check whether it should be written as one word or two.
2. Fix the spelling: "She has alot of books." Answer: "She has a lot of books." Hint: Find the phrase that means a large amount, and check whether it should be written as one word or two.
3. Fix the spelling: "There was alot of snow." Answer: "There was a lot of snow." Hint: Find the phrase that means a large amount, and check whether it should be written as one word or two.
4. Which is correct: "a lot of rain" or "alot of rain"? Answer: "a lot of rain." Hint: Find the phrase that means a large amount, and check whether it should be written as one word or two.
5. Fix the spelling: "Leo ate alot of pizza." Answer: "Leo ate a lot of pizza." Hint: Find the phrase that means a large amount, and check whether it should be written as one word or two.
6. Fix the spelling: "We learned alot today." Answer: "We learned a lot today." Hint: Find the phrase that means a large amount, and check whether it should be written as one word or two.
7. Which is correct: "alot of time" or "a lot of time"? Answer: "a lot of time." Hint: Find the phrase that means a large amount, and check whether it should be written as one word or two.
8. Fix the spelling: "The garden grew alot of tomatoes." Answer: "The garden grew a lot of tomatoes." Hint: Find the phrase that means a large amount, and check whether it should be written as one word or two.
9. Fix the spelling: "Ana has alot of friends." Answer: "Ana has a lot of friends." Hint: Find the phrase that means a large amount, and check whether it should be written as one word or two.
