### 18.9 Is the Thought Complete or Incomplete?

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A complete thought has a subject and a verb and makes sense on its own. An incomplete thought is missing a subject, a verb, or a full idea, so it cannot stand alone.
- Example: "The dog barked." Complete. It has a subject and a verb and makes sense. "When the dog barked." Incomplete. It leaves you waiting for more. Fix it: "When the dog barked, the cat ran."
- How it works: Check for a subject and a verb, then ask if the idea is finished. If you are left waiting for more, the thought is incomplete.
- Tip box: "Maya finished her homework." Complete. "After Maya finished her homework." Incomplete. Fix it: "After Maya finished her homework, she read a book." "Because it rained." Incomplete. Fix it: "Because it rained, we stayed inside."

**Quiz**
1. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "The dog barked."? Answer: complete. Hint: Check for a subject and a verb, then ask if the idea is finished.
2. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "When the dog barked."? Answer: incomplete. Hint: Ask if the words make sense on their own or leave you waiting for more.
3. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "Maya finished her homework."? Answer: complete. Hint: Check for a subject and a verb, then ask if the idea is finished.
4. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "After Maya finished her homework."? Answer: incomplete. Hint: Ask if the words make sense on their own or leave you waiting for more.
5. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "Because it rained."? Answer: incomplete. Hint: Check for a subject and a verb, then ask if the idea is finished.
6. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "The sun rose over the hills."? Answer: complete. Hint: Ask if the words make sense on their own or leave you waiting for more.
7. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "While the baby slept."? Answer: incomplete. Hint: Check for a subject and a verb, then ask if the idea is finished.
8. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "Leo scored the winning goal."? Answer: complete. Hint: Ask if the words make sense on their own or leave you waiting for more.
9. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "If you finish early."? Answer: incomplete. Hint: Check for a subject and a verb, then ask if the idea is finished.
10. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "The library opens at nine."? Answer: complete. Hint: Ask if the words make sense on their own or leave you waiting for more.
11. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "Before the game started."? Answer: incomplete. Hint: Check for a subject and a verb, then ask if the idea is finished.
12. Is this thought complete or incomplete: "Ana planted a garden."? Answer: complete. Hint: Ask if the words make sense on their own or leave you waiting for more.

### 18.10 Identifying Dependent Clauses

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A dependent clause has a subject and a verb, but it cannot stand alone. It starts with a subordinating word such as because, when, although, if, or while, and it leaves you waiting for more.
- Example: "Because it rained, we stayed home." Because it rained is the dependent clause. It has a subject and a verb, but it cannot stand alone.
- How it works: Find the clause, then ask if it makes sense on its own. If it starts with a subordinating word and leaves you waiting, it is dependent.
- Tip box: "When the bell rings." Dependent. It needs a main clause. Fix it: "When the bell rings, we go to lunch." "The bell rings." Independent. It stands alone.

**Quiz**
1. "Because it rained, we stayed home." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: Because it rained. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.
2. "When the bell rings, the students leave." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: When the bell rings. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.
3. "Although it was late, Ana finished her work." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: Although it was late. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.
4. "If you finish early, you can read." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: If you finish early. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.
5. "While the baby slept, Maya read." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: While the baby slept. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.
6. "We will leave when the bus arrives." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: when the bus arrives. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.
7. "She smiled because she won." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: because she won. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.
8. "The dog waited until Leo came home." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: until Leo came home. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.
9. "After the game ended, we went home." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: After the game ended. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.
10. "You cannot play unless you finish your chores." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: unless you finish your chores. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.
11. "Though it was cold, they played outside." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: Though it was cold. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.
12. "We left before the rain started." Which is the dependent clause? Answer: before the rain started. Hint: Look for the clause that begins with a subordinating word and cannot stand alone.

## Pathway 19: Conclusion Paragraphs (1 topic)

### 19.1 The Two Jobs of a Conclusion

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, Job one: restate the claim. Guiding question: How do I remind the reader what I argued? A conclusion restates the claim in fresh words, so the reader remembers the main point. Worked passage: Claim: "Schools should keep art classes." Conclusion restatement: "Art classes belong in every school." The idea is the same, but the words are new.
- Section 2, Job two: leave a final thought. Guiding question: What should the reader take away? A conclusion ends with a final thought that gives the reader something to remember, such as a call to action or a look ahead. Worked passage: "If we protect art classes now, the next generation of artists, builders, and thinkers will thank us." The final thought looks ahead and gives the reader a reason to care.
- Section 3, What a conclusion does not do. Guiding question: What should I avoid? A conclusion should not bring in new evidence, start a new argument, or repeat the essay word for word. Worked passage: Weak conclusion: "Art classes are good. Art classes are good. Art classes are good." This repeats the same words and adds nothing new, so it fails both jobs.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: Claim: "Schools should keep art classes." Which restates the claim in fresh words? Answer: "Art classes belong in every school." Hint: A conclusion restates the claim in fresh words.
2. Which is a good final thought for an essay about art classes? Answer: "If we protect art classes now, the next generation of artists, builders, and thinkers will thank us." Hint: A final thought looks ahead and gives the reader a reason to care.
3. Which is NOT a job of a conclusion: restating the claim, leaving a final thought, or bringing in new evidence? Answer: bringing in new evidence. Hint: Think about the two jobs of a conclusion. Which choice is not one of them?
4. Passage: Claim: "The town needs a new library." Which restates the claim in fresh words? Answer: "A new library would serve the whole town." Hint: A conclusion restates the claim in fresh words.
5. Which is a good final thought for an essay about a new library? Answer: "A bigger library would open its doors to every reader in town for years to come." Hint: A final thought looks ahead and gives the reader a reason to care.
6. Which is a weak conclusion: "Art classes are good. Art classes are good. Art classes are good." or "Art classes belong in every school, and protecting them now will help the next generation."? Answer: "Art classes are good. Art classes are good. Art classes are good." Hint: Check each choice against the two jobs: restating the claim in fresh words and leaving a final thought.
7. Passage: Claim: "Recess should last 30 minutes." Which restates the claim in fresh words? Answer: "Thirty minutes of recess gives every student time to play and recharge." Hint: A conclusion restates the claim in fresh words.
8. Which is a good final thought for an essay about recess? Answer: "Longer recess would send students back to class ready to learn." Hint: A final thought gives the reader something to remember.
9. Which should a conclusion NOT do: repeat the essay word for word, or restate the claim in fresh words? Answer: repeat the essay word for word. Hint: Check each choice against the two jobs of a conclusion.
10. Passage: Claim: "Every student should learn to cook." Which restates the claim in fresh words? Answer: "Cooking is a skill every student should carry into adult life." Hint: A conclusion restates the claim in fresh words.
11. Which is a good final thought for an essay about cooking? Answer: "A student who can cook a simple meal will never go hungry." Hint: A final thought gives the reader something to remember.
12. What are the two jobs of a conclusion? Answer: Restate the claim in fresh words, and leave a final thought. Hint: A conclusion reminds the reader of the main point and ends with something to remember.

## Pathway 20: Contractions (4 topics)

### 20.1 Distinguishing Contractions and Possessives

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A contraction joins two words with an apostrophe and leaves out letters. A possessive shows ownership and usually uses an apostrophe too, but it does not join two words.
- Example: "Don't" is a contraction of do not. "Ana's book" is a possessive. The book belongs to Ana.
- How it works: Ask what the apostrophe does. If it joins two words and letters are missing, it is a contraction. If it shows ownership, it is a possessive.
- Tip box: "It's" is a contraction of it is. "Its" is a possessive with no apostrophe. "The dog's bone" is a possessive. The bone belongs to the dog.

**Quiz**
1. Is "don't" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: contraction. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.
2. Is "Ana's book" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: possessive. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.
3. Is "it's" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: contraction. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.
4. Is "its" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: possessive. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.
5. Is "the dog's bone" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: possessive. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.
6. Is "can't" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: contraction. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.
7. Is "Leo's bike" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: possessive. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.
8. Is "we're" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: contraction. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.
9. Is "the cat's toy" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: possessive. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.
10. Is "didn't" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: contraction. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.
11. Is "the school's flag" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: possessive. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.
12. Is "she'll" a contraction or a possessive? Answer: contraction. Hint: Ask whether the word joins two words with letters left out, or shows that something belongs to someone.

### 20.2 "Who's" vs. "Whose"

See 14.2 "Who's" vs. "Whose" above.

### 20.3 "It's" vs. "Its"

See 13.1 "It's" vs. "Its" above.

### 20.4 Correcting Errors with "Yours," "Hers," "Theirs," and "Ours"

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Yours, hers, theirs, and ours are possessive pronouns. They show ownership, and they never take an apostrophe.
- Example: "The red bike is yours." Yours shows the bike belongs to you, with no apostrophe. "The book is hers." Hers shows the book belongs to her, with no apostrophe.
- How it works: These words already show ownership on their own. Adding an apostrophe is always wrong.
- Tip box: "Is this yours?" No apostrophe. "The house is ours." No apostrophe. "The win was theirs." No apostrophe.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the error: "The red bike is your's." Answer: "The red bike is yours." Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.
2. Fix the error: "The book is her's." Answer: "The book is hers." Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.
3. Fix the error: "The house is our's." Answer: "The house is ours." Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.
4. Fix the error: "The win was their's." Answer: "The win was theirs." Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.
5. Is this correct: "Is this yours?"? Answer: Yes, it is correct. Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.
6. Fix the error: "That jacket is her's." Answer: "That jacket is hers." Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.
7. Fix the error: "The garden is our's to care for." Answer: "The garden is ours to care for." Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.
8. Fix the error: "The trophy is their's." Answer: "The trophy is theirs." Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.
9. Is this correct: "The choice is yours."? Answer: Yes, it is correct. Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.
10. Fix the error: "The dog is her's." Answer: "The dog is hers." Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.
11. Fix the error: "The classroom is our's." Answer: "The classroom is ours." Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.
12. Fix the error: "The idea was their's." Answer: "The idea was theirs." Hint: Possessive pronouns already show ownership on their own. Check whether this one needs an apostrophe.

## Pathway 21: Counterargument Paragraphs (1 topic)

### 21.1 Planning a Counterargument Paragraph

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, What a counterargument is. Guiding question: What would someone who disagrees say? A counterargument is the strongest point on the other side. Naming it shows the writer has thought about both sides. Worked passage: Claim: "School should start later." Counterargument: "Some people say a later start would push sports and jobs into the evening." This names the other side's best point.
- Section 2, How to answer it. Guiding question: How do I respond without dismissing the other side? A good response takes the counterargument seriously and answers it with a fact or a reason. Worked passage: "Sports can end a little later, and studies show that rested students learn more, so the trade is worth it." The response respects the concern and answers it with a fact.
- Section 3, Where it belongs. Guiding question: Where does the counterargument paragraph go? It usually comes after the main reasons and before the conclusion, so the writer answers the other side and then ends strong. Worked passage: "Some people worry about evening sports. Still, the gain in learning is greater than the change in schedule, so a later start is the better choice." The paragraph answers the concern and leads into the conclusion.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: Claim: "School should start later." Which is the counterargument? Answer: "Some people say a later start would push sports and jobs into the evening." Hint: A counterargument names the strongest point on the other side.
2. Same passage. Which is a good response? Answer: "Sports can end a little later, and studies show that rested students learn more, so the trade is worth it." Hint: A good response takes the concern seriously and answers it with a fact.
3. Passage: Claim: "The town needs a new library." Which is the counterargument? Answer: "Some people say a new library would cost too much money." Hint: A counterargument names the strongest point on the other side.
4. Same passage. Which is a good response? Answer: "The cost would be shared over many years, and a bigger library would serve the whole town." Hint: A good response takes the concern seriously and answers it with a reason.
5. Passage: Claim: "Recess should last 30 minutes." Which is the counterargument? Answer: "Some people say longer recess would take time from lessons." Hint: A counterargument names the strongest point on the other side.
6. Same passage. Which is a good response? Answer: "Rested students learn faster, so the time is not lost." Hint: A good response takes the concern seriously and answers it with a reason.
7. Where does a counterargument paragraph usually go? Answer: After the main reasons and before the conclusion. Hint: The writer answers the other side and then ends strong.
8. Passage: Claim: "Every student should learn to cook." Which is the counterargument? Answer: "Some people say cooking lessons would take time from other classes." Hint: A counterargument names the strongest point on the other side.
9. Same passage. Which is a good response? Answer: "Cooking teaches math and science too, so the lessons do double duty." Hint: A good response takes the concern seriously and answers it with a reason.
10. Which response is weak: "That worry is silly." or "The worry is real, and here is how to handle it."? Answer: "That worry is silly." Hint: Ask which response takes the other side seriously and which one brushes it off.
11. Passage: Claim: "Bikes should be allowed on the river path." Which is the counterargument? Answer: "Some people say bikes would make the path unsafe for walkers." Hint: A counterargument names the strongest point on the other side.
12. Same passage. Which is a good response? Answer: "Marked lanes and signs would keep walkers and riders safe." Hint: A good response takes the concern seriously and answers it with a reason.

## Pathway 25: Embedding Quotations (4 topics)

### 25.1 Capitalizing Quotes

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When a quote starts a sentence, capitalize its first word. When a quote continues a sentence after words such as that, do not capitalize it unless it is a proper noun.
- Example: "Ana said, 'The game starts at noon.'" The game is capitalized because the quote starts the sentence. "Ana said that the game starts at noon." The game is not capitalized because that continues the sentence.
- How it works: Ask where the quote sits. A quote that starts a sentence gets a capital first word. A quote that follows that stays lower case.
- Tip box: "Leo asked, 'Can we go now?'" Can is capitalized. "Leo asked if we can go now." Can is not capitalized.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "Ana said, 'the game starts at noon.'" Answer: "Ana said, 'The game starts at noon.'" Hint: A quote that starts a sentence gets a capital first word.
2. Fix the capitalization: "Ana said that The game starts at noon." Answer: "Ana said that the game starts at noon." Hint: A quote that follows that stays lower case.
3. Fix the capitalization: "Leo asked, 'can we go now?'" Answer: "Leo asked, 'Can we go now?'" Hint: A quote that starts a sentence gets a capital first word.
4. Fix the capitalization: "Leo asked if We can go now." Answer: "Leo asked if we can go now." Hint: A quote that follows if stays lower case.
5. Fix the capitalization: "Maya whispered, 'the baby is asleep.'" Answer: "Maya whispered, 'The baby is asleep.'" Hint: A quote that starts a sentence gets a capital first word.
6. Fix the capitalization: "Maya whispered that The baby is asleep." Answer: "Maya whispered that the baby is asleep." Hint: A quote that follows that stays lower case.
7. Which is correct: "Ben said, 'I will help.'" or "Ben said, 'i will help.'"? Answer: "Ben said, 'I will help.'" Hint: Compare the first word inside the quotation marks in each choice.
8. Which is correct: "Ben said that I will help." or "Ben said that i will help."? Answer: "Ben said that I will help." Hint: Some words are capitalized no matter where they appear in a sentence. Check the pronoun.
9. Fix the capitalization: "The teacher said, 'open your books.'" Answer: "The teacher said, 'Open your books.'" Hint: A quote that starts a sentence gets a capital first word.
10. Fix the capitalization: "The teacher said that We should open our books." Answer: "The teacher said that we should open our books." Hint: A quote that follows that stays lower case.
11. Fix the capitalization: "Zoe shouted, 'we won!'" Answer: "Zoe shouted, 'We won!'" Hint: A quote that starts a sentence gets a capital first word.
12. Fix the capitalization: "Zoe shouted that We won." Answer: "Zoe shouted that we won." Hint: A quote that follows that stays lower case.

### 25.2 Is There a Pause Before the Quote?

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When you introduce a quote with a verb such as said, asked, or shouted, put a comma before the quote. When you introduce it with that, do not use a comma.
- Example: "Ana said, 'The game starts at noon.'" A comma comes before the quote. "Ana said that the game starts at noon." No comma comes before that.
- How it works: Ask how the quote is introduced. A speaking verb takes a comma before the quote. That takes no comma.
- Tip box: "Leo asked, 'Can we go?'" A comma comes before the quote. "Leo asked if we can go." No comma, because if introduces the question.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "Ana said 'The game starts at noon.'" Answer: "Ana said, 'The game starts at noon.'" Hint: A speaking verb takes a comma before the quote.
2. Fix the punctuation: "Ana said, that the game starts at noon." Answer: "Ana said that the game starts at noon." Hint: That takes no comma before it.
3. Fix the punctuation: "Leo asked 'Can we go?'" Answer: "Leo asked, 'Can we go?'" Hint: A speaking verb takes a comma before the quote.
4. Fix the punctuation: "Leo asked, if we can go." Answer: "Leo asked if we can go." Hint: If takes no comma before it.
5. Fix the punctuation: "Maya whispered 'The baby is asleep.'" Answer: "Maya whispered, 'The baby is asleep.'" Hint: A speaking verb takes a comma before the quote.
6. Fix the punctuation: "Maya whispered, that the baby is asleep." Answer: "Maya whispered that the baby is asleep." Hint: That takes no comma before it.
7. Fix the punctuation: "Ben shouted 'We won!'" Answer: "Ben shouted, 'We won!'" Hint: A speaking verb takes a comma before the quote.
8. Fix the punctuation: "The teacher said, to open your books." Answer: "The teacher said to open your books." Hint: To takes no comma before it.
9. Which is correct: "Zoe said 'I will help.'" or "Zoe said, 'I will help.'"? Answer: "Zoe said, 'I will help.'" Hint: Compare what comes right after said in each choice.
10. Which is correct: "Zoe said, that she will help." or "Zoe said that she will help."? Answer: "Zoe said that she will help." Hint: Compare what comes right after said in each choice.
11. Fix the punctuation: "Sam asked 'Where is the library?'" Answer: "Sam asked, 'Where is the library?'" Hint: A speaking verb takes a comma before the quote.
12. Fix the punctuation: "Sam asked, whether the library is open." Answer: "Sam asked whether the library is open." Hint: Whether takes no comma before it.

### 25.3 Formatting Dialogue and Flow Quotes 1

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A dialogue quote is a character's exact words in a story. A flow quote is a short quote woven into your own sentence so the sentence reads smoothly. Both need quotation marks and the right punctuation.
- Example: Dialogue: "'Where are you going?' asked Ana." The quote comes first, then the speaker. Flow: "Ana said the fair was 'the best part of summer.'" The quote fits inside the writer's sentence.
- How it works: For dialogue, put the quote in quotation marks, add a comma or question mark inside the closing mark, and name the speaker. For a flow quote, weave the quoted words into your sentence so it reads as one smooth sentence.
- Tip box: "'I am ready,' said Leo." Dialogue with a comma inside the quote. "Leo said he was 'ready to go.'" Flow quote woven into the sentence.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "'Where are you going' asked Ana." Answer: "'Where are you going?' asked Ana." Hint: The quote is a question. Check what mark it needs and where that mark goes.
2. Fix the punctuation: "'I am ready' said Leo." Answer: "'I am ready,' said Leo." Hint: A comma goes inside the closing quotation mark when the speaker follows.
3. Which is correct: "'We won!' shouted Zoe." or "'We won'! shouted Zoe."? Answer: "'We won!' shouted Zoe." Hint: Compare where the exclamation mark sits in each choice.
4. Fix the punctuation: "Leo said he was 'ready to go'." Answer: "Leo said he was 'ready to go.'" Hint: In US style, a period goes inside the closing quotation mark, even after a flow quote.
5. Fix the punctuation: "'The game starts soon' said Ana." Answer: "'The game starts soon,' said Ana." Hint: A comma goes inside the closing quotation mark when the speaker follows.
6. Fix the punctuation: "'Can we go now' asked Leo." Answer: "'Can we go now?' asked Leo." Hint: The quote is a question. Check what mark it needs and where that mark goes.
7. Which is correct: "'Stop!' yelled the coach." or "'Stop'! yelled the coach."? Answer: "'Stop!' yelled the coach." Hint: Compare where the exclamation mark sits in each choice.
8. Fix the punctuation: "'I love this book' said Maya." Answer: "'I love this book,' said Maya." Hint: A comma goes inside the closing quotation mark when the speaker follows.
9. Fix the punctuation: "Ana called the trip 'the best day ever'." Answer: "Ana called the trip 'the best day ever.'" Hint: In US style, a period goes inside the closing quotation mark, even after a flow quote.
10. Fix the punctuation: "'Where is my bag' asked Ben." Answer: "'Where is my bag?' asked Ben." Hint: The quote is a question. Check what mark it needs and where that mark goes.
11. Which is correct: "'Time to go,' said the teacher." or "'Time to go' said the teacher."? Answer: "'Time to go,' said the teacher." Hint: Compare the punctuation just before the closing quotation mark in each choice.
12. Is this correct: "The class called the garden 'our green classroom.'"? Answer: Yes, it is correct. Hint: Check where the period sits next to the closing quotation mark.

### 25.4 Formatting Dialogue and Colon Quotes

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A colon quote is a quote introduced with a colon after a complete sentence. The colon sets up the quote like a drumroll. A dialogue quote uses a comma or question mark instead.
- Example: Colon: "The sign gave one warning: 'Stay on the path.'" The colon follows a complete sentence. Dialogue: "'Stay on the path,' said the guide." A comma follows the quote.
- How it works: If the lead-in is a complete sentence, use a colon before the quote. If the lead-in is a speaking verb, use a comma.
- Tip box: "The coach had one rule: 'Never give up.'" Colon after a complete sentence. "The coach said, 'Never give up.'" Comma after a speaking verb.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the punctuation: "The sign gave one warning 'Stay on the path.'" Answer: "The sign gave one warning: 'Stay on the path.'" Hint: A complete sentence before a quote takes a colon.
2. Fix the punctuation: "The guide said 'Stay on the path.'" Answer: "The guide said, 'Stay on the path.'" Hint: A speaking verb takes a comma before the quote.
3. Fix the punctuation: "The coach had one rule 'Never give up.'" Answer: "The coach had one rule: 'Never give up.'" Hint: A complete sentence before a quote takes a colon.
4. Fix the punctuation: "The coach said 'Never give up.'" Answer: "The coach said, 'Never give up.'" Hint: A speaking verb takes a comma before the quote.
5. Which is correct: "The note had one message, 'Meet at noon.'" or "The note had one message: 'Meet at noon.'"? Answer: "The note had one message: 'Meet at noon.'" Hint: Ask whether the words before the quote form a complete sentence or end with a speaking verb.
6. Fix the punctuation: "Ana asked 'Can we go?'" Answer: "Ana asked, 'Can we go?'" Hint: A speaking verb takes a comma before the quote.
7. Fix the punctuation: "The rule was simple 'Be kind.'" Answer: "The rule was simple: 'Be kind.'" Hint: A complete sentence before a quote takes a colon.
8. Fix the punctuation: "Maya whispered 'The baby is asleep.'" Answer: "Maya whispered, 'The baby is asleep.'" Hint: A speaking verb takes a comma before the quote.
9. Which is correct: "The poster gave one order: 'Wash your hands.'" or "The poster gave one order, 'Wash your hands.'"? Answer: "The poster gave one order: 'Wash your hands.'" Hint: Ask whether the words before the quote form a complete sentence or end with a speaking verb.
10. Fix the punctuation: "Leo shouted 'We won!'" Answer: "Leo shouted, 'We won!'" Hint: A speaking verb takes a comma before the quote.
11. Fix the punctuation: "The motto was clear 'Try your best.'" Answer: "The motto was clear: 'Try your best.'" Hint: A complete sentence before a quote takes a colon.
12. Which is correct: "The teacher said, 'Open your books.'" or "The teacher said: 'Open your books.'"? Answer: "The teacher said, 'Open your books.'" Hint: Ask whether the words before the quote form a complete sentence or end with a speaking verb.

## Pathway 26: Formal and Informal Language (1 topic)

### 26.1 Recognizing Language That Is Too Formal or Too Informal

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Formal language is polite and careful, and it fits school essays, letters to adults, and reports. Informal language is casual and fits friends, texts, and notes. Language is too formal or too informal when it does not fit the setting.
- Example: "Hey, what's up?" is too informal for a letter to the principal. "I humbly request your esteemed presence at the assembly." is too formal for a note to a friend.
- How it works: Ask who will read it and where it will appear. Match the language to the setting.
- Tip box: To a friend: "Want to play after school?" To the principal: "May I please speak with you after school?" Both are right for their setting.

**Quiz**
1. Which is too informal for a letter to the principal: "Hey, what's up?" or "May I please speak with you?"? Answer: "Hey, what's up?" Hint: Informal language does not fit a letter to the principal.
2. Which is too formal for a note to a friend: "Want to play after school?" or "I humbly request your esteemed presence at the park."? Answer: "I humbly request your esteemed presence at the park." Hint: Formal language does not fit a note to a friend.
3. Which fits a school essay: "The results show that..." or "This stuff is super cool..."? Answer: "The results show that..." Hint: Formal language fits a school essay.
4. Which fits a text to a friend: "Yo, game at 4?" or "I cordially invite you to attend the game at 4 o'clock."? Answer: "Yo, game at 4?" Hint: Informal language fits a text to a friend.
5. Which is too informal for a book report: "The book was awesome!" or "The book tells a moving story about friendship."? Answer: "The book was awesome!" Hint: A book report needs more formal language.
6. Which is too formal for a thank-you note to a classmate: "Thanks for sharing your lunch!" or "I extend my deepest gratitude for your generous sharing of sustenance."? Answer: "I extend my deepest gratitude for your generous sharing of sustenance." Hint: A note to a classmate should be friendly and simple.
7. Which fits an email to a teacher: "Hi Ms. Lee, I have a question about homework." or "Yo Ms. Lee, what's the homework?"? Answer: "Hi Ms. Lee, I have a question about homework." Hint: Polite and careful language fits an email to a teacher.
8. Which is too formal for a birthday card: "Happy birthday! Hope your day is great!" or "I wish you the most splendid anniversary of your birth."? Answer: "I wish you the most splendid anniversary of your birth." Hint: A birthday card should be warm and simple.
9. Which fits a science report: "The plants went crazy in the sun!" or "The plants grew taller in sunlight."? Answer: "The plants grew taller in sunlight." Hint: Formal language fits a science report.
10. Which is too informal for a speech to the school: "Hey everyone, listen up!" or "Good morning, everyone. Thank you for coming."? Answer: "Hey everyone, listen up!" Hint: A speech to the school needs more formal language.
11. Which fits a note to a friend: "I anticipate our meeting at the park with great pleasure." or "See you at the park!"? Answer: "See you at the park!" Hint: Informal language fits a note to a friend.
12. Which is too formal for a caption under a class photo: "Our class at the fair!" or "The assembled students of grade six at the annual fair."? Answer: "The assembled students of grade six at the annual fair." Hint: A photo caption should be simple and friendly.

## Pathway 27: Identifying Parts of Speech I (5 topics)

### 27.1 Identifying Concrete Nouns

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A concrete noun names something you can see, touch, hear, smell, or taste. It names a real thing in the world.
- Example: "The dog barked at the moon." Dog and moon are concrete nouns. You can see them both.
- How it works: Ask if you can sense it with one of your five senses. If yes, it is a concrete noun.
- Tip box: "The cake smelled sweet." Cake is a concrete noun. You can smell it. "The music filled the hall." Music is a concrete noun. You can hear it.

**Quiz**
1. "The dog barked at the moon." Which words are concrete nouns? Answer: dog and moon. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.
2. "The cake smelled sweet." Which word is a concrete noun? Answer: cake. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.
3. "The music filled the hall." Which word is a concrete noun? Answer: music. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.
4. "Ana picked a flower." Which word is a concrete noun? Answer: flower. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.
5. "The soup tasted salty." Which word is a concrete noun? Answer: soup. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.
6. "Leo kicked the ball." Which words are concrete nouns? Answer: Leo and ball. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.
7. "The soft blanket kept her warm." Which word is a concrete noun? Answer: blanket. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.
8. "A bell rang in the tower." Which words are concrete nouns? Answer: bell and tower. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.
9. "The rain fell on the roof." Which words are concrete nouns? Answer: rain and roof. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.
10. "Maya read a book." Which word is a concrete noun? Answer: book. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.
11. "The wind pushed the boat." Which words are concrete nouns? Answer: wind and boat. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.
12. "The fire crackled in the fireplace." Which words are concrete nouns? Answer: fire and fireplace. Hint: Look for nouns that name things you can see, hear, touch, smell, or taste.

### 27.2 Identifying Common Nouns

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A common noun names a general person, place, thing, or idea. It does not name one specific thing, and it is not capitalized unless it starts a sentence.
- Example: "The girl rode her bike to the park." Girl, bike, and park are common nouns. They name general things.
- How it works: Ask if the word names a general thing or one specific thing. General means a common noun. One specific thing means a proper noun.
- Tip box: "The teacher read a book." Teacher and book are common nouns. "Ms. Lee read a book." Ms. Lee is a proper noun because it names one specific teacher.

**Quiz**
1. "The girl rode her bike to the park." Which words are common nouns? Answer: girl, bike, and park. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.
2. "Ms. Lee read a book." Which word is a common noun? Answer: book. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.
3. "The dog chased a squirrel." Which words are common nouns? Answer: dog and squirrel. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.
4. "We visited the city on Monday." Which word is a common noun? Answer: city. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.
5. "The cat slept on the rug." Which words are common nouns? Answer: cat and rug. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.
6. "Ana went to the store." Which word is a common noun? Answer: store. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.
7. "The river flows through the valley." Which words are common nouns? Answer: river and valley. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.
8. "He bought a sandwich." Which word is a common noun? Answer: sandwich. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.
9. "The class planted a garden." Which words are common nouns? Answer: class and garden. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.
10. "She played soccer on Saturday." Which word is a common noun? Answer: soccer. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.
11. "The bird sang in the tree." Which words are common nouns? Answer: bird and tree. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.
12. "Leo found a coin." Which word is a common noun? Answer: coin. Hint: Look for nouns that name a general person, place, thing, or idea, not one specific one.

### 27.3 Distinguishing Common and Proper Nouns (checkpoint)

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A common noun names a general person, place, thing, or idea. A proper noun names one specific person, place, thing, or idea, and it is always capitalized.
- Example: "The girl visited the city." Girl and city are common nouns. "Ana visited Paris." Ana and Paris are proper nouns because they name one specific girl and one specific city.
- How it works: Ask if the word names a general thing or one specific thing. General means common. One specific thing means proper, and it is capitalized.
- Tip box: "The dog is friendly." Dog is common. "Rex is friendly." Rex is proper. "We read a book." Book is common. "We read Black Beauty." Black Beauty is proper.

**Quiz**
1. "The girl visited the city." Which words are common nouns? Answer: girl and city. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.
2. "Ana visited Paris." Which words are proper nouns? Answer: Ana and Paris. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.
3. "The dog is friendly." Is dog a common or proper noun? Answer: common. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.
4. "Rex is friendly." Is Rex a common or proper noun? Answer: proper. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.
5. "We read a book." Is book a common or proper noun? Answer: common. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.
6. "We read Black Beauty." Is Black Beauty a common or proper noun? Answer: proper. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.
7. "The teacher gave us homework." Is teacher a common or proper noun? Answer: common. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.
8. "Ms. Lee gave us homework." Is Ms. Lee a common or proper noun? Answer: proper. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.
9. "They swam in the river." Is river a common or proper noun? Answer: common. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.
10. "They swam in the Nile." Is Nile a common or proper noun? Answer: proper. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.
11. "The team won the game." Is team a common or proper noun? Answer: common. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.
12. "The Tigers won the game." Is Tigers a common or proper noun? Answer: proper. Hint: Ask whether the word names any one of a general kind, or one specific, named person, place, or thing.

### 27.4 Identifying the Verb in a Sentence

See 17.3 Identifying the Verb in a Sentence above.

### 27.5 Identifying Verbs in Simple, Progressive, and Perfect Tenses

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Verbs change form to show time. Simple tenses show one time: past, present, or future. Progressive tenses show an action in progress, using a form of be plus ing. Perfect tenses show an action finished before another time, using a form of have plus a past participle.
- Example: "She walked." Simple past. "She is walking." Present progressive, an action in progress. "She has walked." Present perfect, an action finished before now.
- How it works: Look at the verb form. The main verb alone, or will plus the verb, means simple. A form of be plus ing means progressive. A form of have plus a past participle means perfect.
- Tip box: "They played." Simple past. "They are playing." Present progressive. "They have played." Present perfect.

**Quiz**
1. "She walked to school." Which tense is walked? Answer: simple past. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
2. "She is walking to school." Which tense is is walking? Answer: present progressive. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
3. "She has walked to school." Which tense is has walked? Answer: present perfect. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
4. "They played soccer." Which tense is played? Answer: simple past. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
5. "They are playing soccer." Which tense is are playing? Answer: present progressive. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
6. "They have played soccer." Which tense is have played? Answer: present perfect. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
7. "Ana will sing tomorrow." Which tense is will sing? Answer: simple future. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
8. "Ana is singing now." Which tense is is singing? Answer: present progressive. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
9. "Ana has sung many songs." Which tense is has sung? Answer: present perfect. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
10. "Leo had finished his homework before dinner." Which tense is had finished? Answer: past perfect. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
11. "Leo was reading when the bell rang." Which tense is was reading? Answer: past progressive. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
12. "Leo reads every night." Which tense is reads? Answer: simple present. Hint: Look at the verb's form. Is it the main verb alone or will plus the verb, a form of be plus ing, or a form of have plus a past participle? Then check the time.
# Grammar Lab: Grades 6-8, Part 2 Lessons

Band: 6-8. Pathways in this file: 26. Topic slots covered: 114. Topics written fresh: 100. Topics cross-referenced (shared with another pathway): 14. Quiz items: 1200 (12 per written topic).

All text is original, written fresh for Oomba. US spelling. No em dashes.

## Pathway: Identifying Parts of Speech II (9 topics)

### Topic: Identifying Concrete Nouns

Teaching card:
- Definition: A concrete noun names something you can see, touch, hear, smell, or taste. It names a real thing in the world, not an idea or a feeling.
- Worked example: "The desk held a lamp and a stack of books." Concrete nouns: desk, lamp, stack, books. Each one is a thing you can touch.
- How it works: Ask whether you could point to it or pick it up. If you can, it is a concrete noun.
- Tip box: "The rain fell on the roof." Rain and roof are concrete. "The dog barked at the mail carrier." Dog and mail carrier are concrete.

Quiz:
1. "Her courage grew as she climbed the ladder." Answer: ladder. Hint: Look for the word that names something you could touch.
2. "The rain brought a feeling of peace." Answer: rain. Hint: Look for the word that names something you can see and feel on your skin.
3. "She felt pride when she held the trophy." Answer: trophy. Hint: Look for the word that names a real object you could hold.
4. "The dog filled our days with joy." Answer: dog. Hint: Look for the word that names a living thing you could see and hear.
5. "He kicked the ball with great skill." Answer: ball. Hint: Look for the word that names something you could touch and kick.
6. "Honesty was the theme of the poster." Answer: poster. Hint: Look for the word that names something you could hang on a wall.
7. "He poured the milk with great care." Answer: milk. Hint: Look for the word that names something you could taste and pour.
8. "The trumpet gave him confidence." Answer: trumpet. Hint: Look for the word that names an object you could hold and hear.
9. "The river was a source of wonder." Answer: river. Hint: Look for the word that names a part of nature you could see and hear.
10. "She wore the coat with pride." Answer: coat. Hint: Look for the word that names clothing you could touch and wear.
11. "The bread was baked with love." Answer: bread. Hint: Look for the word that names a food you could smell and taste.
12. "A butterfly brought a moment of delight." Answer: butterfly. Hint: Look for the word that names a living thing you could see.

### Topic: Identifying Common Nouns

Teaching card:
- Definition: A common noun names a general person, place, thing, or idea. It does not name one specific person, place, or thing, so it is not capitalized.
- Worked example: "The girl walked to the library with her brother." Common nouns: girl, library, brother. They name general people and a general place.
- How it works: A common noun names a kind of thing. A proper noun names one exact thing and starts with a capital letter.
- Tip box: "The city has a large park." City and park are common nouns. "The student read a book." Student and book are common nouns.

Quiz:
1. "The girl walked to Cedar Library with Rosa." Answer: girl. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of person, not one named person.
2. "Our city sits on the Blue River." Answer: city. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of place, not one named place.
3. "The student wrote to Dr. Okafor on Monday." Answer: student. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of person, not one named person.
4. "Our team played at Lakeview School on Friday." Answer: team. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of group, not one named place or day.
5. "The baker opened Rosa's Bakery on Main Street." Answer: baker. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of worker, not one named place.
6. "A bird landed near Lake Harmon in May." Answer: bird. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of animal, not one named place or month.
7. "The museum in Riverton opens on Sundays." Answer: museum. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of place, not one named town or day.
8. "The child waved to Omar and Priya." Answer: child. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of person, not one named person.
9. "The river runs past Millbrook." Answer: river. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of thing in nature, not one named town.
10. "My cousin visits Denver every July." Answer: cousin. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of family member, not one named city or month.
11. "The farmer drove to Greenfield on Tuesday." Answer: farmer. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of worker, not one named town or day.
12. "A firefighter visited Oak Hill School in October." Answer: firefighter. Hint: Look for the word that names a kind of worker, not one named school or month.

### Topic: Checkpoint: Distinguishing Common and Proper Nouns

Teaching card:
- Definition: A common noun names a general person, place, or thing and is not capitalized. A proper noun names one specific person, place, or thing and always starts with a capital letter.
- Worked example: "The girl visited the museum on Tuesday." Common nouns: girl and museum. They name a general person and a general place. Proper noun: Tuesday. It names one specific day.
- How it works: Ask whether the word names one exact thing. If it does, capitalize it. If it names a kind of thing, leave it lowercase.
- Tip box: "The dog ran through the park." Dog and park are common. "The river flows past the town." River and town are common.

Quiz:
1. "The girl visited the museum on Tuesday." Answer: Tuesday. Hint: Look for the noun that names one exact person, place, or time. It starts with a capital letter.
2. "The dog ran through Cedar Park." Answer: dog. Hint: Look for the noun that names a general kind of person, place, or thing, not one exact one.
3. "Our class visited Pine Lake in May." Answer: class. Hint: Look for the noun that names a general kind of person, place, or thing, not one exact one.
4. "Sam finished his homework before dinner." Answer: Sam. Hint: Look for the noun that names one exact person, place, or time. It starts with a capital letter.
5. "The library on Elm Street opens on Monday." Answer: library. Hint: Look for the noun that names a general kind of person, place, or thing, not one exact one.
6. "Ana baked cookies for the school fair." Answer: Ana. Hint: Look for the noun that names one exact person, place, or time. It starts with a capital letter.
7. "The river flows past Fairview and Oakdale." Answer: river. Hint: Look for the noun that names a general kind of person, place, or thing, not one exact one.
8. "The band practiced the drums on Saturday." Answer: Saturday. Hint: Look for the noun that names one exact person, place, or time. It starts with a capital letter.
9. "The teacher met Omar in Chicago." Answer: teacher. Hint: Look for the noun that names a general kind of person, place, or thing, not one exact one.
10. "The gardener planted seeds in April." Answer: April. Hint: Look for the noun that names one exact person, place, or time. It starts with a capital letter.
11. "A squirrel lives in Maple Park." Answer: squirrel. Hint: Look for the noun that names a general kind of person, place, or thing, not one exact one.
12. "The chorus sang in Springfield on Friday." Answer: chorus. Hint: Look for the noun that names a general kind of person, place, or thing, not one exact one.

### Topic: Identifying the Verb in a Sentence

Teaching card:
- Definition: A verb is a word that shows action or a state of being. Every sentence needs a verb.
- Worked example: "The students built a tower from blocks." Verb: built. It shows the action the students did.
- How it works: Find the word that tells what the subject does or is. That word is the verb.
- Tip box: "The rain fell all night." Fell is the verb. "The soup smells delicious." Smells is the verb.

Quiz:
1. "The students built a tower from blocks." Answer: built. Hint: Ask what the students did.
2. "The rain fell all night long." Answer: fell. Hint: Ask what the rain did.
3. "Ana read three chapters before bed." Answer: read. Hint: Ask what Ana did.
4. "The soup smells delicious." Answer: smells. Hint: Look for the word that links the soup to a word that describes it.
5. "Leo kicked the ball into the goal." Answer: kicked. Hint: Ask what Leo did.
6. "The baby slept through the noise." Answer: slept. Hint: Ask what the baby did.
7. "Our teacher collects old coins." Answer: collects. Hint: Ask what the teacher does.
8. "The river runs fast after the rain." Answer: runs. Hint: Ask what the river does.
9. "Mia is the captain of the team." Answer: is. Hint: Look for the word that links Mia to what she is.
10. "The birds sang at sunrise." Answer: sang. Hint: Ask what the birds did.
11. "Sam drew a map of the island." Answer: drew. Hint: Ask what Sam did.
12. "The bread tastes fresh from the oven." Answer: tastes. Hint: Look for the word that links the bread to a word that describes it.

### Topic: Identifying Verbs in Simple, Progressive, and Perfect Tenses

Teaching card:
- Definition: A simple tense verb shows an action that happens, happened, or will happen. A progressive verb shows an action in progress, using a form of be plus -ing. A perfect verb shows a completed action, using a form of have plus a past participle.
- Worked example: "She walks, she is walking, she has walked." Walks is simple present. Is walking is present progressive. Has walked is present perfect.
- How it works: Look at the helping verb. A form of be plus -ing means progressive. A form of have plus a past participle means perfect. A single verb with no helper is simple.
- Tip box: "They played." Played is simple past. "They were playing." Were playing is past progressive. "They have played." Have played is present perfect.

Quiz:
1. "She walks to school every day." Answer: walks. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.
2. "They were playing soccer at noon." Answer: were playing. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.
3. "We have finished the puzzle." Answer: have finished. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.
4. "He will read the book tonight." Answer: will read. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.
5. "Ana is drawing a poster for the fair." Answer: is drawing. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.
6. "The class has visited the museum twice." Answer: has visited. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.
7. "Leo baked cookies yesterday." Answer: baked. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.
8. "Sam had cleaned his room before lunch." Answer: had cleaned. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.
9. "The birds are singing in the trees." Answer: are singing. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.
10. "Mia will join the choir next week." Answer: will join. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.
11. "They had left before sunrise." Answer: had left. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.
12. "The rain falls on the roof." Answer: falls. Hint: Find the main verb, and include any helping verb that goes with it.

### Topic: What Does the Adjective Describe?

Teaching card:
- Definition: An adjective describes a noun or a pronoun. To find what it describes, ask which noun or pronoun the adjective tells about.
- Worked example: "The brave firefighter climbed the tall ladder." Brave describes firefighter. Tall describes ladder.
- How it works: Find the adjective, then look for the noun or pronoun right nearby that it tells about. The adjective answers what kind, how many, or which one.
- Tip box: "Three hungry cats waited by the door." Three and hungry describe cats. "The old book smelled of dust." Old describes book.

Quiz:
1. "The brave firefighter climbed the ladder." Answer: firefighter. Hint: Find the adjective, then ask which person it tells about.
2. "Three hungry cats waited by the door." Answer: cats. Hint: Find the describing words, then ask which animals they tell about.
3. "The old book smelled of dust." Answer: book. Hint: Find the adjective, then ask which thing it tells about.
4. "Ana wore a bright yellow jacket." Answer: jacket. Hint: Find the color word, then ask which piece of clothing it tells about.
5. "The wind blew across the empty field." Answer: field. Hint: Find the adjective, then ask which place it tells about.
6. "Leo found a shiny coin under the bench." Answer: coin. Hint: Find the adjective, then ask which object it tells about.
7. "The quiet library closed at nine." Answer: library. Hint: Find the adjective, then ask which place it tells about.
8. "Sam drank a glass of cold water." Answer: water. Hint: Find the adjective, then ask which drink it tells about.
9. "The market sold fresh bread." Answer: bread. Hint: Find the adjective, then ask which food it tells about.
10. "Mia planted seven small seeds in the garden." Answer: seeds. Hint: Find the describing words, then ask which things they tell about.
11. "The steep hill slowed the hikers." Answer: hill. Hint: Find the adjective, then ask which place it tells about.
12. "A gentle rain fell on the roof." Answer: rain. Hint: Find the adjective, then ask which kind of weather it tells about.

### Topic: Identifying Adjectives Before and After Nouns

Teaching card:
- Definition: An adjective usually comes right before the noun it describes, but it can also come after a linking verb and describe the subject. Both positions do the same job.
- Worked example: "The tall tower stood on the hill. The tower is tall." Tall comes before tower in the first sentence and after is in the second, and it describes tower in both.
- How it works: Find the adjective, then find the noun or pronoun it tells about. The adjective may sit before the noun or after a linking verb like is, was, seems, or feels.
- Tip box: "The soup was hot." Hot comes after was and describes soup. "The hot soup steamed." Hot comes before soup.

Quiz:
1. "The tall tower stood on the hill." Answer: tall. Hint: Look for the word that tells what the tower is like.
2. "The tower on the hill is tall." Answer: tall. Hint: Look after the linking verb for the word that tells what the tower is like.
3. "Ana felt happy after the race." Answer: happy. Hint: Look after the linking verb for the word that tells how Ana felt.
4. "The happy runner waved to the crowd." Answer: happy. Hint: Look before the noun for the word that tells what the runner was like.
5. "The cake smells sweet." Answer: sweet. Hint: Look after the linking verb for the word that tells how the cake smells.
6. "Leo baked a sweet cake for the fair." Answer: sweet. Hint: Look before the noun for the word that tells what kind of cake Leo baked.
7. "The water was cold in the morning." Answer: cold. Hint: Look after the linking verb for the word that tells what the water was like.
8. "Sam jumped into the cold water." Answer: cold. Hint: Look before the noun for the word that tells what the water was like.
9. "The music sounds loud in the hall." Answer: loud. Hint: Look after the linking verb for the word that tells how the music sounds.
10. "Mia played a loud song on the piano." Answer: loud. Hint: Look before the noun for the word that tells what kind of song Mia played.
11. "The bread tastes fresh from the oven." Answer: fresh. Hint: Look after the linking verb for the word that tells how the bread tastes.
12. "The fresh bread sat on the table." Answer: fresh. Hint: Look before the noun for the word that tells what the bread was like.

### Topic: What Does the Adverb Describe?

Teaching card:
- Definition: An adverb describes a verb, an adjective, or another adverb. It often tells how, when, where, or to what degree.
- Worked example: "The dog ran quickly to the gate." Quickly describes ran. It tells how the dog ran.
- How it works: Find the adverb, then find the word it tells about. If it tells how an action happens, it describes a verb. Many adverbs end in -ly.
- Tip box: "She sang very softly." Softly describes sang. Very describes softly. "They arrived early." Early describes arrived.

Quiz:
1. "The dog ran quickly to the gate." Answer: ran. Hint: Find the adverb quickly, then ask which action it tells about.
2. "She sang very softly on the stage." Answer: softly. Hint: Find the adverb very, then ask which word it makes stronger.
3. "They arrived early for the game." Answer: arrived. Hint: Find the adverb early, then ask which action it tells about.
4. "Ana spoke clearly to the class." Answer: spoke. Hint: Find the adverb clearly, then ask which action it tells about.
5. "The rain fell softly on the roof." Answer: fell. Hint: Find the adverb softly, then ask which action it tells about.
6. "Leo worked carefully on the model." Answer: worked. Hint: Find the adverb carefully, then ask which action it tells about.
7. "The train moved slowly through the tunnel." Answer: moved. Hint: Find the adverb slowly, then ask which action it tells about.
8. "The pond was very cold." Answer: cold. Hint: Find the adverb very, then ask which describing word it makes stronger.
9. "Mia practiced daily at the piano." Answer: practiced. Hint: Find the adverb daily, then ask which action it tells about.
10. "The wind blew hard across the field." Answer: blew. Hint: Find the adverb hard, then ask which action it tells about.
11. "The cat crept quietly toward the door." Answer: crept. Hint: Find the adverb quietly, then ask which action it tells about.
12. "He waited patiently for his turn." Answer: waited. Hint: Find the adverb patiently, then ask which action it tells about.

### Topic: Identifying Adverbs of Time, Place, and Manner

Teaching card:
- Definition: An adverb of time tells when an action happens. An adverb of place tells where it happens. An adverb of manner tells how it happens.
- Worked example: "Yesterday the birds sang outside, and they sang sweetly." Yesterday is time. Outside is place. Sweetly is manner.
- How it works: Ask when, where, or how about the verb. When gives time, where gives place, how gives manner.
- Tip box: "They will meet tomorrow." Tomorrow is time. "Put the box here." Here is place. "She read quietly." Quietly is manner.

Quiz:
1. "Yesterday the birds sang in the oak tree." Answer: Yesterday. Hint: Ask when the birds sang.
2. "The children played outside after lunch." Answer: outside. Hint: Ask where the children played.
3. "She read the poem sweetly." Answer: sweetly. Hint: Ask how she read the poem.
4. "We will visit the farm tomorrow." Answer: tomorrow. Hint: Ask when we will visit the farm.
5. "Put the box here by the door." Answer: here. Hint: Ask where to put the box.
6. "Leo answered the question boldly." Answer: boldly. Hint: Ask how Leo answered the question.
7. "Ana finished her homework early." Answer: early. Hint: Ask when Ana finished her homework.
8. "The dog waited there by the gate." Answer: there. Hint: Ask where the dog waited.
9. "Sam spoke softly to the baby." Answer: softly. Hint: Ask how Sam spoke to the baby.
10. "They leave for camp next week." Answer: next week. Hint: Ask when they leave for camp.
11. "Mia looked everywhere for her keys." Answer: everywhere. Hint: Ask where Mia looked for her keys.
12. "The choir sang loudly at the concert." Answer: loudly. Hint: Ask how the choir sang.

---

## Pathway: Identifying Parts of Speech III (9 topics)

Part 1, Identifying Adjectives, is shared with Identifying Parts of Speech II above. See "What Does the Adjective Describe?" and "Identifying Adjectives Before and After Nouns" above.

Part 2, Identifying Adverbs, is shared with Identifying Parts of Speech II above. See "What Does the Adverb Describe?" and "Identifying Adverbs of Time, Place, and Manner" above.

### Topic: Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions (FANBOYS)

Teaching card:
- Definition: A coordinating conjunction joins two words, phrases, or clauses of equal rank. The seven are for, and, nor, but, or, yet, and so. Remember them with the word FANBOYS.
- Worked example: "Ana wanted the red kite, but Leo chose the blue one." But joins the two clauses. It shows a contrast between them.
- How it works: Find the word that joins two equal parts. If it is one of the FANBOYS words, it is a coordinating conjunction.
- Tip box: "Sam likes apples and pears." And joins two words. "We can walk or ride." Or joins two verbs.

Quiz:
1. "Ana wanted the red kite, but Leo chose the blue one." Answer: but. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two clauses.
2. "Sam likes apples and pears." Answer: and. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two fruits.
3. "We can walk or ride to the park." Answer: or. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two actions.
4. "The rain was cold, yet the team kept playing." Answer: yet. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two clauses.
5. "He studied hard, so he passed the test." Answer: so. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two clauses.
6. "Leo did not want soup, nor did he want salad." Answer: nor. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two clauses.
7. "The cat hid under the bed, for it was scared." Answer: for. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two clauses.
8. "Mia brought a pen and a notebook." Answer: and. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two things Mia brought.
9. "You can stay home, or you can come with us." Answer: or. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two clauses.
10. "The hike was long, but the view was worth it." Answer: but. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two clauses.
11. "Sam read the map, and Ana carried the bag." Answer: and. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two clauses.
12. "The soup was hot, so we waited to eat." Answer: so. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins the two clauses.

### Topic: Identifying Subordinating Conjunctions

Teaching card:
- Definition: A subordinating conjunction starts a dependent clause and joins it to the main clause. Common ones are since, when, although, because, if, and as.
- Worked example: "Because it rained, the game moved inside." Because starts the dependent clause and joins it to the main clause.
- How it works: Find the word that starts a clause that cannot stand alone. If it is a word like because, when, or if, it is a subordinating conjunction.
- Tip box: "We stayed inside when the storm came." When starts the dependent clause. "Ana read while Leo cooked." While starts the dependent clause.

Quiz:
1. "Because it rained, the game moved inside." Answer: Because. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.
2. "We stayed inside when the storm came." Answer: when. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.
3. "Ana read while Leo cooked dinner." Answer: while. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.
4. "Since you asked, I will tell you the story." Answer: Since. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.
5. "Although it was late, the crowd stayed." Answer: Although. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.
6. "If you finish early, we can play a game." Answer: If. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.
7. "The seeds grew as the rain fell." Answer: as. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.
8. "Sam hid the gift until the party started." Answer: until. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.
9. "Mia smiled because her team won." Answer: because. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.
10. "After the bell rang, the students lined up." Answer: After. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.
11. "Leo waited where the path met the road." Answer: where. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.
12. "The dog barked whenever a car passed." Answer: whenever. Hint: Find the word that starts the part of the sentence that cannot stand alone.

### Topic: Identifying Conjunctive Adverbs

Teaching card:
- Definition: A conjunctive adverb joins two independent clauses and shows how they relate, such as by showing a result, a contrast, an addition, or what happens if not. Common ones are therefore, however, moreover, otherwise, consequently, and furthermore.
- Worked example: "The rain stopped. However, the field was still wet." However joins the two sentences and shows a contrast.
- How it works: Find the word that links two full thoughts and tells how they connect. If it shows a result, a contrast, or an addition, it may be a conjunctive adverb.
- Tip box: "Sam studied hard. Therefore, he felt ready." Therefore shows a result. "The bus was late. Otherwise, we would have left on time." Otherwise shows what would have happened.

Quiz:
1. "The rain stopped. However, the field was still wet." Answer: However. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.
2. "Sam studied hard. Therefore, he felt ready." Answer: Therefore. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.
3. "The class read the book. Moreover, they wrote about it." Answer: Moreover. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.
4. "Bring a coat. Otherwise, you will be cold." Answer: Otherwise. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.
5. "The team lost the game. Consequently, they practiced harder." Answer: Consequently. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.
6. "Ana loves to paint. Furthermore, she teaches others." Answer: Furthermore. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.
7. "The store was closed. Instead, we went to the market." Answer: Instead. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.
8. "Leo set the table. Meanwhile, his sister cooked the rice." Answer: Meanwhile. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.
9. "The hike was steep. Nevertheless, the group reached the top." Answer: Nevertheless. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.
10. "Mia saved her money. As a result, she bought the book." Answer: As a result. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.
11. "The river was high. Still, the bridge stayed open." Answer: Still. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.
12. "We planted seeds in spring. Finally, the flowers bloomed in June." Answer: Finally. Hint: Look at the start of the second sentence for the word or phrase that links it to the first.

### Topic: Identifying Pronouns

Teaching card:
- Definition: A pronoun takes the place of a noun. It lets you talk about a person, place, or thing without repeating its name.
- Worked example: "Ana found the map. She showed it to Leo." She takes the place of Ana. It takes the place of the map.
- How it works: Find the word that stands in for a noun. Common pronouns are he, she, it, they, we, you, I, him, her, us, and them.
- Tip box: "The dogs barked. They wanted dinner." They takes the place of the dogs. "Sam lost his pen. He looked for it." He takes the place of Sam.

Quiz:
1. "Ana found the map. She was thrilled." Answer: She. Hint: Find the word that stands in for Ana.
2. "The dogs barked. They wanted dinner." Answer: They. Hint: Find the word that stands in for the dogs.
3. "Sam lost a pen. He looked under the desk." Answer: He. Hint: Find the word that stands in for Sam.
4. "Mia and Leo built a fort. They played there all day." Answer: They. Hint: Find the word that stands in for Mia and Leo.
5. "The book fell off the shelf. It landed on the rug." Answer: It. Hint: Find the word that stands in for the book.
6. "We walked to the park and ate there." Answer: We. Hint: Find the word that stands in for the speaker and the people with the speaker.
7. "The teacher asked us to open the books." Answer: us. Hint: Find the word that stands in for the students.
8. "Ana gave the ball to him." Answer: him. Hint: Find the word that stands in for the person who got the ball.
9. "The flowers need water. Give them a drink." Answer: them. Hint: Find the word that stands in for the flowers.
10. "You should bring a coat tomorrow." Answer: You. Hint: Find the word that stands in for the person being spoken to.
11. "Leo and I finished the puzzle together." Answer: I. Hint: Find the word that stands in for the speaker.
12. "Ana's bike has a flat tire. She will fix the tube tonight." Answer: She. Hint: Find the word that stands in for Ana.

### Topic: Identifying Prepositions

Teaching card:
- Definition: A preposition shows how a noun or pronoun relates to another word in the sentence. It often tells where, when, or how. Common prepositions are in, on, under, over, by, with, after, and before.
- Worked example: "The cat slept under the table." Under is the preposition. It shows where the cat slept in relation to the table.
- How it works: Find the word that comes before a noun or pronoun and shows a relationship like place or time. That word is the preposition.
- Tip box: "The book is on the shelf." On shows where the book is. "We met after lunch." After shows when we met.

Quiz:
1. "The cat slept under the table." Answer: under. Hint: Find the word that shows where the cat slept in relation to the table.
2. "The book is on the shelf." Answer: on. Hint: Find the word that shows where the book is in relation to the shelf.
3. "We met after lunch." Answer: after. Hint: Find the word that shows when we met in relation to lunch.
4. "Ana walked with her friend." Answer: with. Hint: Find the word that links Ana to her friend.
5. "The ball rolled behind the couch." Answer: behind. Hint: Find the word that shows where the ball rolled in relation to the couch.
6. "Leo jumped over the puddle." Answer: over. Hint: Find the word that shows where Leo jumped in relation to the puddle.
7. "The keys are in the drawer." Answer: in. Hint: Find the word that shows where the keys are in relation to the drawer.
8. "Sam stood before the class and spoke." Answer: before. Hint: Find the word that shows where Sam stood in relation to the class.
9. "Mia read the note from her teacher." Answer: from. Hint: Find the word that links the note to the person who wrote it.
10. "The birds flew across the field." Answer: across. Hint: Find the word that shows where the birds flew in relation to the field.
11. "We will meet at noon." Answer: at. Hint: Find the word that shows when we will meet in relation to noon.
12. "The plant grew beside the window." Answer: beside. Hint: Find the word that shows where the plant grew in relation to the window.

---

## Pathway: Identifying Sentences and Fragments (5 topics)

### Topic: Is the Thought Complete or Incomplete?

Teaching card:
- Definition: A complete thought can stand alone as a sentence. It has a subject and a verb and says something finished. An incomplete thought is missing a piece, so it cannot stand alone.
- Worked example: "The students finished the project." This is complete. "When the students finished." This is incomplete, because it leaves the reader waiting for more.
- How it works: Read the group of words and ask whether it says something finished. If it leaves a question hanging, it is incomplete.
- Tip box: "The dog barked." Complete. "Because the dog barked." Incomplete. "She is happy." Complete.

Quiz:
1. "The students finished the project." Answer: complete. Hint: Look for a subject and a verb, then ask whether the thought feels finished.
2. "When the students finished." Answer: incomplete. Hint: Look at the first word, then ask whether the thought feels finished or leaves you waiting.
3. "The dog barked at the mail carrier." Answer: complete. Hint: Look for a subject and a verb, then ask whether the thought feels finished.
4. "Because the dog barked." Answer: incomplete. Hint: Look at the first word, then ask whether the thought feels finished or leaves you waiting.
5. "She is happy about the news." Answer: complete. Hint: Look for a subject and a verb, then ask whether the thought feels finished.
6. "After the rain stopped." Answer: incomplete. Hint: Look at the first word, then ask whether the thought feels finished or leaves you waiting.
7. "The train arrived on time." Answer: complete. Hint: Look for a subject and a verb, then ask whether the thought feels finished.
8. "If you come early." Answer: incomplete. Hint: Look at the first word, then ask whether the thought feels finished or leaves you waiting.
