9. "Leo built a tall tower." Answer: complete. Hint: Look for a subject and a verb, then ask whether the thought feels finished.
10. "While Ana read her book." Answer: incomplete. Hint: Look at the first word, then ask whether the thought feels finished or leaves you waiting.
11. "The river flows under the bridge." Answer: complete. Hint: Look for a subject and a verb, then ask whether the thought feels finished.
12. "Before the bell rang." Answer: incomplete. Hint: Look at the first word, then ask whether the thought feels finished or leaves you waiting.

### Topic: Identifying Dependent Clauses

Teaching card:
- Definition: A dependent clause has a subject and a verb, but it cannot stand alone as a sentence. It depends on the main clause to finish the thought. It usually starts with a word like because, when, if, although, or while.
- Worked example: "When the bell rang, the students lined up." When the bell rang is the dependent clause. It has a subject and a verb, but it needs the main clause to finish the thought.
- How it works: Find the clause that starts with a subordinating word. Ask whether it could stand alone. If it cannot, it is a dependent clause.
- Tip box: "Because it snowed, school closed." Because it snowed is dependent. "If you hurry, you will catch the bus." If you hurry is dependent.

Quiz:
1. "When the bell rang, the students lined up." Answer: When the bell rang. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.
2. "Because it snowed, school closed early." Answer: Because it snowed. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.
3. "If you hurry, you will catch the bus." Answer: If you hurry. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.
4. "Ana stayed home while Leo went to practice." Answer: while Leo went to practice. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.
5. "The game ended after the rain started." Answer: after the rain started. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.
6. "Although she was tired, Mia kept reading." Answer: Although she was tired. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.
7. "We will eat when the soup is ready." Answer: when the soup is ready. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.
8. "Sam hid the gift until the party began." Answer: until the party began. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.
9. "The dog barked as the car pulled in." Answer: as the car pulled in. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.
10. "Since you asked, I will tell you." Answer: Since you asked. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.
11. "Leo waited where the path met the road." Answer: where the path met the road. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.
12. "The seeds grew because the sun shone." Answer: because the sun shone. Hint: Find the part that starts with a word like because, when, or if and cannot stand alone.

### Topic: Recognizing Fragments in Dialogue

Teaching card:
- Definition: A fragment is a group of words that looks like a sentence but is missing a subject, a verb, or a complete thought. In dialogue, short replies like "Yes" or "Not yet" are fine in real speech, but a fragment is still not a sentence.
- Worked example: "'Where did you go?' 'To the store.'" To the store is a fragment. It has no subject and no verb.
- How it works: Check every line of dialogue for a subject and a verb. If one is missing, the line is a fragment.
- Tip box: "'Are you coming?' 'In a minute.'" In a minute is a fragment. "'Did you finish?' 'Almost.'" Almost is a fragment.

Quiz:
1. "'Where did you go?' 'To the store.'" Answer: To the store. Hint: This line has no subject and no verb, so it is a fragment.
2. "'Are you coming?' 'In a minute.'" Answer: In a minute. Hint: This line has no subject and no verb, so it is a fragment.
3. "'Did you finish?' 'Almost.'" Answer: Almost. Hint: Check the reply for a subject and a verb.
4. "'What time is it?' 'Half past three.'" Answer: Half past three. Hint: This line has no subject and no verb, so it is a fragment.
5. "'Who won the game?' 'Our team.'" Answer: Our team. Hint: This line has no subject and no verb, so it is a fragment.
6. "'Do you like the soup?' 'Very much.'" Answer: Very much. Hint: This line has no subject and no verb, so it is a fragment.
7. "'Where is my coat?' 'On the hook.'" Answer: On the hook. Hint: This line has no subject and no verb, so it is a fragment.
8. "'Did you see the comet?' 'Last night.'" Answer: Last night. Hint: This line has no subject and no verb, so it is a fragment.
9. "'Can I help?' 'With the boxes.'" Answer: With the boxes. Hint: This line has no subject and no verb, so it is a fragment.
10. "'What did you buy?' 'A new kite.'" Answer: A new kite. Hint: This line has no subject and no verb, so it is a fragment.
11. "'Are we there yet?' 'Not quite.'" Answer: Not quite. Hint: This line has no subject and no verb, so it is a fragment.
12. "'Who called?' 'The teacher.'" Answer: The teacher. Hint: This line has no subject and no verb, so it is a fragment.

### Topic: Recognizing Sentences with Pronoun Subjects

Teaching card:
- Definition: A sentence can have a pronoun as its subject. The pronoun takes the place of a noun, but the sentence is still complete as long as it has a verb and a finished thought.
- Worked example: "They built a fort in the yard." They is a pronoun, and it is the subject. Built is the verb. The sentence is complete.
- How it works: Find the verb first, then ask who or what did it. If the answer is a pronoun like they, she, it, or we, the sentence has a pronoun subject and is complete.
- Tip box: "She read the book." She is the subject. "It rained all day." It is the subject.

Quiz:
1. "They built a fort in the yard." Answer: They. Hint: Ask who built the fort.
2. "She read the book in one evening." Answer: She. Hint: Ask who read the book.
3. "It rained all day long." Answer: It. Hint: Find the verb, then look for the pronoun that comes before the verb.
4. "We walked to the market together." Answer: We. Hint: Ask who walked to the market.
5. "He painted the fence blue." Answer: He. Hint: Ask who painted the fence.
6. "They sang songs around the fire." Answer: They. Hint: Ask who sang songs.
7. "It smells like rain outside." Answer: It. Hint: Find the verb, then look for the pronoun that comes before the verb.
8. "She won the race by a step." Answer: She. Hint: Ask who won the race.
9. "We planted seeds in the spring." Answer: We. Hint: Ask who planted seeds.
10. "He fixed the broken chair." Answer: He. Hint: Ask who fixed the chair.
11. "They left before the storm arrived." Answer: They. Hint: Ask who left before the storm.
12. "It was cold on the mountain." Answer: It. Hint: Find the verb, then look for the pronoun that comes before the verb.

### Topic: Sentences and Fragments with "That," "Which," and "Especially"

Teaching card:
- Definition: Words like that, which, and especially often start a group of words that adds detail, and that group alone is a fragment. A clause starting with that or which needs a main clause to finish the thought.
- Worked example: "The book that I borrowed was about space." That I borrowed is a clause that adds detail, and the whole sentence is complete. "That I borrowed." Alone, it is a fragment. Fix it: "The book that I borrowed is due on Monday."
- How it works: Find the clause that starts with that or which. Ask whether it can stand alone. If it cannot, it is a fragment unless it is joined to a main clause.
- Tip box: "The kite, which was red, flew high." The whole sentence is complete. "Which was red." Alone, it is a fragment. Fix it: "The ball, which was red, rolled away."

Quiz:
1. "The book that I borrowed was about space." Answer: sentence. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.
2. "That I borrowed." Answer: fragment. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.
3. "The kite, which was red, flew high." Answer: sentence. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.
4. "Which was red." Answer: fragment. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.
5. "The soup, especially the tomato kind, was delicious." Answer: sentence. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.
6. "Especially the tomato kind." Answer: fragment. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.
7. "The dog that barked all night slept all day." Answer: sentence. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.
8. "That barked all night." Answer: fragment. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.
9. "The town, which sits by the river, is quiet." Answer: sentence. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.
10. "Which sits by the river." Answer: fragment. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.
11. "Leo loves board games, especially the ones with trains." Answer: sentence. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.
12. "Especially the ones with trains." Answer: fragment. Hint: Look for a main clause with its own subject and verb, then ask whether the words can stand alone.

---

## Pathway: Identifying Subjects and Verbs (3 topics)

### Topic: Identifying the Subject of a Sentence

Teaching card:
- Definition: The subject is who or what the sentence is about. It is the noun, pronoun, or group of words that does the action or is described.
- Worked example: "The tall oak tree shaded the yard." The subject is the tall oak tree. It is what the sentence is about.
- How it works: Find the verb first, then ask who or what does it. The answer is the subject.
- Tip box: "Ana and Leo painted the fence." The subject is Ana and Leo. "The rain fell hard." The subject is the rain.

Quiz:
1. "The tall oak tree shaded the yard." Answer: The tall oak tree. Hint: Ask who or what shaded the yard. The answer is the subject.
2. "Ana and Leo painted the fence." Answer: Ana and Leo. Hint: Ask who painted the fence. The answer is the subject.
3. "The rain fell hard all night." Answer: The rain. Hint: Ask what fell. The answer is the subject.
4. "My little brother hid behind the couch." Answer: My little brother. Hint: Ask who hid. The answer is the subject.
5. "The old bridge crossed the river." Answer: The old bridge. Hint: Ask what crossed the river. The answer is the subject.
6. "Three hungry cats waited by the door." Answer: Three hungry cats. Hint: Ask who waited. The answer is the subject.
7. "The school choir sang at the fair." Answer: The school choir. Hint: Ask who sang. The answer is the subject.
8. "A strong wind blew the leaves away." Answer: A strong wind. Hint: Ask what blew the leaves away. The answer is the subject.
9. "The baker opened the shop early." Answer: The baker. Hint: Ask who opened the shop. The answer is the subject.
10. "Two red bicycles leaned against the wall." Answer: Two red bicycles. Hint: Ask what leaned against the wall. The answer is the subject.
11. "Our teacher read a funny story." Answer: Our teacher. Hint: Ask who read the story. The answer is the subject.
12. "The first snow covered the roofs." Answer: The first snow. Hint: Ask what covered the roofs. The answer is the subject.

### Topic: Identifying Pronouns as Subjects

Teaching card:
- Definition: A pronoun can be the subject of a sentence. It takes the place of a noun and does the action of the verb.
- Worked example: "They planted a garden behind the school." They is a pronoun, and it is the subject. It does the action of planting.
- How it works: Find the verb first, then ask who or what does it. If the answer is a pronoun like they, she, he, we, or it, the pronoun is the subject.
- Tip box: "She won the spelling contest." She is the subject. "It rained all morning." It is the subject.

Quiz:
1. "They planted a garden behind the school." Answer: They. Hint: Ask who planted a garden.
2. "She won the spelling contest." Answer: She. Hint: Ask who won the contest.
3. "It rained all morning." Answer: It. Hint: Find the verb, then look for the pronoun that comes before the verb.
4. "We built a bridge from sticks." Answer: We. Hint: Ask who built a bridge.
5. "He carried the heavy box upstairs." Answer: He. Hint: Ask who carried the box.
6. "They cleaned the classroom after lunch." Answer: They. Hint: Ask who cleaned the classroom.
7. "She practiced the violin every day." Answer: She. Hint: Ask who practiced the violin.
8. "It snowed on the first day of winter." Answer: It. Hint: Find the verb, then look for the pronoun that comes before the verb.
9. "We visited the farm in the fall." Answer: We. Hint: Ask who visited the farm.
10. "He fixed the flat tire himself." Answer: He. Hint: Ask who fixed the tire.
11. "They cheered when the team scored." Answer: They. Hint: Ask who cheered.
12. "She baked bread for the whole class." Answer: She. Hint: Ask who baked bread.

Part 2, Identifying Verbs, is shared with Identifying Parts of Speech II above. See "Identifying the Verb in a Sentence" above.

---

## Pathway: Introductory Paragraphs (3 topics)

### Topic: Is the Thesis a Fact or Opinion?

Teaching card (three sections):
- Section 1, What is a fact? Guiding question: Can it be proven true or false? Worked passage: "Water boils at 100 degrees Celsius at sea level." This is a fact. It can be tested and proven.
- Section 2, What is an opinion? Guiding question: Is it what someone thinks or feels? Worked passage: "Swimming is the best summer sport." This is an opinion. It states what someone thinks, and others may disagree.
- Section 3, What makes a good thesis? Guiding question: Is it an opinion that can be argued? Worked passage: "Our school should add a garden club." This is a good thesis. It is an opinion that a writer can argue with reasons and evidence. "Our school has a garden." This is a fact, so it cannot be a thesis.

Quiz:
1. Passage: "Water boils at 100 degrees Celsius at sea level. Watching a pot boil is the most boring part of cooking." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fact can be tested and proven true.
2. Passage: "Swimming is the best summer sport. The town pool opens in June." Ask: Which sentence is an opinion? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: An opinion states what someone thinks, and others may disagree.
3. Passage: "Our school should add a garden club. Students would learn to grow food and care for plants." Ask: Which sentence could be a thesis? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A good thesis is an opinion a writer can argue with reasons.
4. Passage: "Libraries are the coziest places in town. The library has 2,000 books." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A fact can be counted or checked.
5. Passage: "Our library lends books for three weeks at a time. Reading before bed is the best way to relax." Ask: Which sentence is an opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: An opinion states what someone thinks, and others may disagree.
6. Passage: "The nearest skate park is 20 miles away. The town should build a skate park." Ask: Which sentence could be a thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A good thesis is an opinion a writer can argue with reasons.
7. Passage: "The moon orbits Earth about once a month. A full moon is the prettiest sight in the night sky." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fact can be measured and proven.
8. Passage: "Chocolate cake is better than vanilla cake. Both cakes use flour, eggs, and sugar." Ask: Which sentence is an opinion? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: An opinion states what someone thinks, and others may disagree.
9. Passage: "Every student should learn to cook. Cooking teaches planning, measuring, and patience." Ask: Which sentence could be a thesis? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A good thesis is an opinion a writer can argue with reasons.
10. Passage: "Our school has the friendliest teachers in the city. The school has 400 students." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A fact can be counted or checked.
11. Passage: "Our gym class plays soccer on Fridays. Soccer is more fun than baseball." Ask: Which sentence is an opinion? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: An opinion states what someone thinks, and others may disagree.
12. Passage: "Recess at our school lasts 15 minutes. Recess should be longer." Ask: Which sentence could be a thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A good thesis is an opinion a writer can argue with reasons.

### Topic: Selecting a Strong Hook 1: Agreeing

Teaching card (three sections):
- Section 1, What is a hook? Guiding question: Does the first sentence grab the reader? Worked passage: "Most students agree that homework takes too long." This hooks the reader by starting with something many people already believe.
- Section 2, Why start with agreement? Guiding question: Does it make the reader nod before you argue? Worked passage: "Everyone knows that practice makes a player better. But practice only helps when the drills match the game." The first sentence gets the reader to agree, so the second sentence lands harder.
- Section 3, What makes a hook weak? Guiding question: Does it just restate the topic? Worked passage: Weak hook: "This essay is about homework." This is a weak hook. It tells the reader the topic but gives no reason to keep reading.

Quiz:
1. Passage: "Most students agree that homework takes too long. They finish one subject and start another, and the evening disappears." Ask: Which sentence is the hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for the sentence that grabs the reader and makes them want to read on.
2. Passage: "Everyone knows that practice makes a player better. But practice only helps when the drills match the game." Ask: Which sentence starts with agreement? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Starting with something many people believe gets the reader to nod.
3. Passage: "This essay is about homework. Homework is given every night." Ask: Which sentence is a weak hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A weak hook only restates the topic and gives no reason to keep reading.
4. Passage: "Most families agree that dinner together matters. The table is where stories get told." Ask: Which sentence is the hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for the sentence that grabs the reader and makes them want to read on.
5. Passage: "Everybody likes a good story. A good story pulls you in from the first line." Ask: Which sentence starts with agreement? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Starting with something many people believe gets the reader to nod.
6. Passage: "This paper is about the library. The library has many books." Ask: Which sentence is a weak hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A weak hook only restates the topic and gives no reason to keep reading.
7. Passage: "Most kids agree that recess is the best part of the day. The playground is where friendships grow." Ask: Which sentence is the hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for the sentence that grabs the reader and makes them want to read on.
8. Passage: "Everyone agrees that clean water matters. But many towns still struggle to keep their rivers clean." Ask: Which sentence starts with agreement? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Starting with something many people believe gets the reader to nod.
9. Passage: "This essay will discuss gardens. Gardens are places where plants grow." Ask: Which sentence is a weak hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A weak hook only restates the topic and gives no reason to keep reading.
10. Passage: "Most runners agree that warming up prevents injuries. A slow jog wakes the muscles before the race." Ask: Which sentence is the hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for the sentence that grabs the reader and makes them want to read on.
11. Passage: "Everybody knows that reading opens new worlds. A single book can carry you across an ocean." Ask: Which sentence starts with agreement? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Starting with something many people believe gets the reader to nod.
12. Passage: "This report is about trains. Trains carry people and goods." Ask: Which sentence is a weak hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A weak hook only restates the topic and gives no reason to keep reading.

### Topic: Recognizing Redundant, Unnecessary, and Wordy Phrases

Teaching card (three sections):
- Section 1, What is a redundant phrase? Guiding question: Does it say the same thing twice? Worked passage: "The two twins looked exactly alike." Two and twins say the same thing. The cleaner version is "The twins looked exactly alike."
- Section 2, What is an unnecessary phrase? Guiding question: Does the sentence lose nothing without it? Worked passage: "In my opinion, I think recess should be longer." In my opinion and I think say the same thing. The cleaner version is "I think recess should be longer."
- Section 3, What is a wordy phrase? Guiding question: Can fewer words say the same thing? Worked passage: "Due to the fact that it rained, we stayed inside." The cleaner version is "Because it rained, we stayed inside."

Quiz:
1. Passage: "The two twins looked exactly alike. They wore matching shirts to the fair." Ask: Which sentence has a redundant phrase? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for two words that say the same thing.
2. Passage: "Recess is a favorite part of the day. In my opinion, I think recess should be longer." Ask: Which sentence has an unnecessary phrase? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for two phrases that say the same thing, where one could be dropped.
3. Passage: "Due to the fact that it rained, we stayed inside. The storm lasted all afternoon." Ask: Which sentence is wordy? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for a long phrase that one or two short words could replace.
4. Passage: "The students looked confused. She repeated the answer again slowly." Ask: Which sentence has a redundant phrase? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for two words that say the same thing.
5. Passage: "The crowd is on its feet. At this point in time, the team is winning." Ask: Which sentence is wordy? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for a long phrase that one or two short words could replace.
6. Passage: "Both teams scored twice. The final outcome of the game was a tie." Ask: Which sentence has a redundant phrase? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for two words that say the same thing.
7. Passage: "The coach set up extra drills. In order to win, the team practiced daily." Ask: Which sentence is wordy? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for a long phrase that one or two short words could replace.
8. Passage: "He returned back to the shop for his bag. The owner had kept it safe." Ask: Which sentence has a redundant phrase? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for two words that say the same thing.
9. Passage: "The reason why she left early was the storm. Her mother drove her home." Ask: Which sentence has an unnecessary phrase? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for a word that adds nothing because another word already says it.
10. Passage: "The new club met on Monday. A large number of students joined the club." Ask: Which sentence is wordy? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for a long phrase that one or two short words could replace.
11. Passage: "They met at 12 noon in the park. The sun was high and bright." Ask: Which sentence has a redundant phrase? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for two words that say the same thing.
12. Passage: "The wind blew hard across the field. Despite the fact that it was cold, they played on." Ask: Which sentence is wordy? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for a long phrase that one or two short words could replace.

## Pathway: MLA Citation I (2 topics)

### Topic: Formatting In-Text Citations

Teaching card:
- Definition: An in-text citation shows where borrowed words or ideas came from. In MLA style, it gives the author's last name and the page number in parentheses, right after the quote and before the period.
- Worked example: "One writer calls the river 'the town's slow heartbeat' (Marsh 42)." Marsh is the author's last name. 42 is the page number. The citation comes before the period.
- How it works: Put the author's last name and page number in parentheses at the end of the sentence that uses the source. No comma between the name and the number.
- Tip box: "The study found that bees visit 5,000 flowers a day (Ortiz 18)." The citation follows the borrowed fact. "Dogs can learn over 100 words (Chen 9)." The citation follows the borrowed fact.

Quiz:
1. "One writer calls the river 'the town's slow heartbeat' (Marsh 42)." Answer: (Marsh 42). Hint: An MLA in-text citation gives the author's last name and the page number in parentheses.
2. "The study found that bees visit 5,000 flowers a day (Ortiz 18)." Answer: (Ortiz 18). Hint: The citation comes after the borrowed fact and before the period.
3. "Dogs can learn over 100 words (Chen 9)." Answer: (Chen 9). Hint: The author's last name and the page number go in parentheses.
4. "The bridge was built in 1920 (Lopez 77)." Answer: (Lopez 77). Hint: An MLA in-text citation gives the author's last name and the page number.
5. "Ancient farmers stored grain in clay jars (Patel 31)." Answer: (Patel 31). Hint: The citation comes after the borrowed fact and before the period.
6. "The volcano erupted three times in one century (Nguyen 104)." Answer: (Nguyen 104). Hint: The author's last name and the page number go in parentheses.
7. "Wolves hunt in packs of six to ten (Garcia 56)." Answer: (Garcia 56). Hint: An MLA in-text citation gives the author's last name and the page number.
8. "The first maps were drawn on clay tablets (Kim 23)." Answer: (Kim 23). Hint: The citation comes after the borrowed fact and before the period.
9. "Sharks have been on Earth for 400 million years (Diaz 88)." Answer: (Diaz 88). Hint: The author's last name and the page number go in parentheses.
10. "The lighthouse keeper climbed 97 steps a day (Ward 12)." Answer: (Ward 12). Hint: An MLA in-text citation gives the author's last name and the page number.
11. "Bees dance to tell others where to find food (Frost 47)." Answer: (Frost 47). Hint: The citation comes after the borrowed fact and before the period.
12. "The town library holds 20,000 books (Reed 63)." Answer: (Reed 63). Hint: The author's last name and the page number go in parentheses.

### Topic: Question Marks and Exclamation Points with Citations

Teaching card:
- Definition: When a quoted sentence ends with a question mark or an exclamation point, that mark stays inside the closing quotation mark. The citation still goes after the quote, and the sentence ends with a period after the citation.
- Worked example: "Did the explorers really cross the desert?" asks one writer (Marsh 42). The question mark stays inside the quotes. The period comes after the citation.
- How it works: Keep the question mark or exclamation point with the quoted words. Put the citation in parentheses after the closing quote. End the sentence with a period after the citation.
- Tip box: "'What a storm!' cried the sailor (Lopez 15)." The exclamation point stays inside the quotes. "'Can we leave now?' asked Ana (Chen 8)." The question mark stays inside the quotes.

Quiz:
1. "'Did the explorers really cross the desert?' asks one writer (Marsh 42)." Answer: the question mark stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the question mark is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.
2. "'What a storm!' cried the sailor (Lopez 15)." Answer: the exclamation point stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the exclamation point is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.
3. "'Can we leave now?' asked Ana (Chen 8)." Answer: the question mark stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the question mark is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.
4. "'How tall is the mountain?' the hiker wondered (Kim 21)." Answer: the question mark stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the question mark is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.
5. "'Watch out!' shouted the coach (Diaz 33)." Answer: the exclamation point stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the exclamation point is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.
6. "'Did the bell ring yet?' asked Leo (Ward 7)." Answer: the question mark stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the question mark is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.
7. "'What a catch!' cheered the crowd (Frost 19)." Answer: the exclamation point stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the exclamation point is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.
8. "'Where is the map?' Mia asked (Reed 44)." Answer: the question mark stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the question mark is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.
9. "'Stop!' called the guard (Ortiz 26)." Answer: the exclamation point stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the exclamation point is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.
10. "'Is the bridge safe?' the driver asked (Nguyen 58)." Answer: the question mark stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the question mark is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.
11. "'How wonderful!' said the teacher (Patel 11)." Answer: the exclamation point stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the exclamation point is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.
12. "'Will it rain today?' Sam wondered (Garcia 29)." Answer: the question mark stays inside the quotes. Hint: Ask whether the question mark is part of the quoted words or part of your own sentence.

## Pathway: Misplaced and Dangling Modifiers (5 topics)

### Topic: Identifying Modifiers

Teaching card:
- Definition: A modifier is a word or phrase that describes another word in the sentence. It usually tells what kind, how, when, or where.
- Worked example: "The dog with the red collar barked." With the red collar is the modifier. It describes the dog.
- How it works: Find the describing words, then find the word they tell about. The describing words are the modifier.
- Tip box: "Running fast, Leo reached the finish line." Running fast modifies Leo. "The cake, warm from the oven, smelled sweet." Warm from the oven modifies the cake.

Quiz:
1. "The dog with the red collar barked." Answer: with the red collar. Hint: This phrase describes the dog, so it is a modifier.
2. "Running fast, Leo reached the finish line." Answer: Running fast. Hint: This phrase describes Leo, so it is a modifier.
3. "The cake, warm from the oven, smelled sweet." Answer: warm from the oven. Hint: This phrase describes the cake, so it is a modifier.
4. "Ana, tired from the hike, yawned." Answer: tired from the hike. Hint: This phrase describes Ana, so it is a modifier.
5. "The house on the hill belongs to Rosa." Answer: on the hill. Hint: This phrase describes the house, so it is a modifier.
6. "Smiling broadly, Mia accepted the prize." Answer: Smiling broadly. Hint: This phrase describes Mia, so it is a modifier.
7. "The river, wide and slow, carried the boats." Answer: wide and slow. Hint: This phrase describes the river, so it is a modifier.
8. "Sam found the keys under the couch." Answer: under the couch. Hint: This phrase describes where the keys were, so it is a modifier.
9. "The students, excited about the trip, packed their bags." Answer: excited about the trip. Hint: This phrase describes the students, so it is a modifier.
10. "Wearing a blue coat, the teacher waved." Answer: Wearing a blue coat. Hint: This phrase describes the teacher, so it is a modifier.
11. "The book with the torn cover belongs to Leo." Answer: with the torn cover. Hint: This phrase describes the book, so it is a modifier.
12. "Barking loudly, the puppy chased its tail." Answer: Barking loudly. Hint: This phrase describes the puppy, so it is a modifier.

### Topic: Interpreting Misplaced Modifiers

Teaching card:
- Definition: A misplaced modifier is a describing phrase that sits too far from the word it should describe, so the sentence seems to say something silly. To read it right, you have to spot which word the modifier really belongs to.
- Worked example: "Running down the hall, the bell surprised the students." The sentence seems to say the bell was running. The modifier running down the hall really belongs to the students, not the bell. Fixed: "Running down the hall, the students heard the bell."
- How it works: Find the modifier, then find the word right next to it. Ask whether that word makes sense with the modifier. If it does not, the modifier is misplaced.
- Tip box: "Covered in mud, Sam washed the dog." Covered in mud should describe the dog, not Sam. Fixed: "Sam washed the dog, which was covered in mud." "The teacher gave a book to the student with a torn cover." With a torn cover should describe the book, not the student. Fixed: "The teacher gave the student a book with a torn cover."

Quiz:
1. "Running down the hall, the bell surprised the students." Answer: The sentence seems to say the bell was running. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits next to the wrong word and changes the meaning.
2. "Covered in mud, Sam washed the dog." Answer: Covered in mud describes the dog, not Sam. Hint: The modifier belongs to the word it really describes, not the nearest one.
3. "The teacher gave a book to the student with a torn cover." Answer: With a torn cover describes the book, not the student. Hint: The modifier belongs to the word it really describes, not the nearest one.
4. "Flying over the field, Leo watched the kite." Answer: The sentence seems to say Leo was flying. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits next to the wrong word and changes the meaning.
5. "The baker handed a loaf to the boy with burnt crust." Answer: With burnt crust describes the loaf, not the boy. Hint: The modifier belongs to the word it really describes, not the nearest one.
6. "Hanging on the wall, Ana admired the painting." Answer: The sentence seems to say Ana was hanging on the wall. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits next to the wrong word and changes the meaning.
7. "Sam saw a deer driving home last night." Answer: The sentence seems to say the deer was driving. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits next to the wrong word and changes the meaning.
8. "Wearing a red scarf, the snow fell on Mia." Answer: The sentence seems to say the snow wore a red scarf. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits next to the wrong word and changes the meaning.
9. "The chef served soup to the guests in a silver pot." Answer: In a silver pot describes the soup, not the guests. Hint: The modifier belongs to the word it really describes, not the nearest one.
10. "Climbing the tree, the bird watched Leo." Answer: The sentence seems to say the bird was climbing the tree. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits next to the wrong word and changes the meaning.
11. "The librarian shelved the books with a smile." Answer: With a smile describes the librarian, not the books. Hint: The modifier belongs to the word it really describes, not the nearest one.
12. "Riding her bike, the rain soaked Ana." Answer: The sentence seems to say the rain was riding a bike. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits next to the wrong word and changes the meaning.

### Topic: Interpreting Dangling Modifiers

Teaching card:
- Definition: A dangling modifier is a describing phrase with nothing to describe. The word it should modify is missing from the sentence, so the phrase hangs loose and the sentence seems to say something odd.
- Worked example: "After reading the book, the movie was disappointing." After reading the book has no reader in the sentence. The sentence seems to say the movie read the book. Fixed: "After we read the book, the movie was disappointing."
- How it works: Find the opening phrase, then look for the word it should describe. If that word is missing, the modifier is dangling.
- Tip box: "While walking to school, the rain began." While walking to school has no walker. Fixed: "While Leo was walking to school, the rain began." "To win the prize, hard work is needed." To win the prize has no winner. Fixed: "To win the prize, you need hard work."

Quiz:
1. "After reading the book, the movie was disappointing." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence read the book. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.
2. "While walking to school, the rain began." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence is walking. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.
3. "To win the prize, hard work is needed." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence is trying to win. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.
4. "Before leaving the house, the keys were lost." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence is leaving. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.
5. "Having finished dinner, the dishes were washed." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence finished dinner. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.
6. "Excited about the trip, the bags were packed." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence is excited. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.
7. "After studying all night, the test was easy." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence studied. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.
8. "To bake the cake, the oven must be hot." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence is baking. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.
9. "Running to catch the bus, the rain soaked his coat." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence is running. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.
10. "While cooking dinner, the phone rang." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence is cooking. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.
11. "Hoping for snow, the window was watched all day." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence is hoping. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.
12. "After winning the game, the trophy was lifted high." Answer: The modifier dangles because no one in the sentence won the game. Hint: A dangling modifier has no word in the sentence to describe.

### Topic: Correcting Misplaced Modifier Errors 1

Teaching card:
- Definition: To fix a misplaced modifier, move the describing phrase next to the word it really describes. The fix keeps the same words but changes the order.
- Worked example: "Running down the hall, the bell surprised the students." Fixed: "The bell surprised the students running down the hall." Now the modifier sits next to the students, the ones who were running.
- How it works: Find the modifier, find the word it should describe, then move the modifier next to that word or rewrite the sentence so the two are together.
- Tip box: "The teacher gave a book to the student with a torn cover." Fixed: "The teacher gave the student a book with a torn cover." "Sam saw a deer driving home." Fixed: "Driving home, Sam saw a deer."

Quiz:
1. "Running down the hall, the bell surprised the students." Answer: "The bell surprised the students running down the hall." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.
2. "Covered in mud, Sam washed the dog." Answer: "Sam washed the dog covered in mud." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.
3. "The teacher gave a book to the student with a torn cover." Answer: "The teacher gave the student a book with a torn cover." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.
4. "Sam saw a deer driving home last night." Answer: "Driving home last night, Sam saw a deer." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.
5. "Flying over the field, Leo watched the kite." Answer: "Leo watched the kite flying over the field." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.
6. "The baker handed a loaf to the boy with burnt crust." Answer: "The baker handed the boy a loaf with burnt crust." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.
7. "Hanging on the wall, Ana admired the painting." Answer: "Ana admired the painting hanging on the wall." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.
8. "Wearing a red scarf, the snow fell on Mia." Answer: "The snow fell on Mia wearing a red scarf." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.
9. "The chef served soup to the guests in a silver pot." Answer: "The chef served soup in a silver pot to the guests." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.
10. "Riding her bike, the rain soaked Ana." Answer: "The rain soaked Ana riding her bike." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.
11. "The librarian shelved the books with a smile." Answer: "With a smile, the librarian shelved the books." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.
12. "Climbing the tree, the bird watched Leo." Answer: "The bird watched Leo climbing the tree." Hint: Move the modifier next to the word it really describes.

### Topic: Correcting Dangling Modifier Errors

Teaching card:
- Definition: To fix a dangling modifier, add the missing word it should describe, or rewrite the sentence so the modifier has something to hold on to.
- Worked example: "After reading the book, the movie was disappointing." Fixed: "After reading the book, Ana found the movie disappointing." Now Ana is the reader, so the modifier has something to describe.
- How it works: Find the opening phrase, ask who or what should do the action in it, then add that word as the subject of the main clause.
- Tip box: "While walking to school, the rain began." Fixed: "While walking to school, Leo felt the rain begin." "To win the prize, hard work is needed." Fixed: "To win the prize, the team must work hard."

Quiz:
1. "After reading the book, the movie was disappointing." Answer: "After reading the book, Ana found the movie disappointing." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.
2. "While walking to school, the rain began." Answer: "While walking to school, Leo felt the rain begin." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.
3. "To win the prize, hard work is needed." Answer: "To win the prize, the team must work hard." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.
4. "Before leaving the house, the keys were lost." Answer: "Before leaving the house, Sam lost the keys." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.
5. "Having finished dinner, the dishes were washed." Answer: "Having finished dinner, Mia washed the dishes." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.
6. "Excited about the trip, the bags were packed." Answer: "Excited about the trip, the students packed their bags." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.
7. "After studying all night, the test was easy." Answer: "After studying all night, Leo found the test easy." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.
8. "To bake the cake, the oven must be hot." Answer: "To bake the cake, Ana heated the oven." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.
9. "Running to catch the bus, the rain soaked his coat." Answer: "Running to catch the bus, Sam felt the rain soak his coat." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.
10. "While cooking dinner, the phone rang." Answer: "While cooking dinner, Mia heard the phone ring." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.
11. "Hoping for snow, the window was watched all day." Answer: "Hoping for snow, the children watched the window all day." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.
12. "After winning the game, the trophy was lifted high." Answer: "After winning the game, the team lifted the trophy high." Hint: Add the missing word the modifier should describe.

## Pathway: Phrases and Dependent Clauses (6 topics)

### Topic: What Does the Appositive Refer To?

Teaching card:
- Definition: An appositive is a noun or noun phrase that renames another noun right beside it. It tells you more about that noun.
- Worked example: "Ana, the team captain, spoke first." The team captain is the appositive. It renames Ana.
- How it works: Find the noun phrase set off by commas, then look at the noun right before it. The appositive renames that noun.
- Tip box: "The Nile, a long river in Africa, flows north." A long river in Africa renames the Nile. "My dog, a small brown terrier, loves to run." A small brown terrier renames my dog.

Quiz:
1. "Ana, the team captain, spoke first." Answer: Ana. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.
2. "The Nile, a long river in Africa, flows north." Answer: the Nile. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.
3. "My dog, a small brown terrier, loves to run." Answer: my dog. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.
4. "Leo, the fastest runner in class, won the race." Answer: Leo. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.
5. "The library, a quiet brick building, opens at nine." Answer: The library. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.
6. "Sam, my best friend, moved to a new town." Answer: Sam. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.
7. "The violin, a gift from her grandmother, gleamed in the light." Answer: The violin. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.
8. "Mia, the youngest singer in the choir, sang the solo." Answer: Mia. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.
9. "The oak, a tree over a hundred years old, shaded the yard." Answer: The oak. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.
10. "Our teacher, a former mountain guide, told stories of the climb." Answer: Our teacher. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.
11. "The comet, a bright visitor in the night sky, thrilled the town." Answer: The comet. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.
12. "Ben, the new student from the city, joined the soccer team." Answer: Ben. Hint: The appositive renames the noun right before it.

### Topic: What Does the Participial Phrase Modify?

Teaching card:
- Definition: A participial phrase starts with a verb form ending in -ing or -ed and works as an adjective. It describes a noun or pronoun in the sentence.
- Worked example: "The girl running to the bus waved at us." Running to the bus is the participial phrase. It describes the girl.
- How it works: Find the phrase that starts with an -ing or -ed verb form, then find the noun or pronoun it tells about. That noun is what the phrase modifies.
- Tip box: "The bread baked this morning smells wonderful." Baked this morning describes the bread. "Shivering in the cold, the puppy whined." Shivering in the cold describes the puppy.

Quiz:
1. "The girl running to the bus waved at us." Answer: the girl. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.
2. "The bread baked this morning smells wonderful." Answer: the bread. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.
3. "Shivering in the cold, the puppy whined." Answer: the puppy. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.
4. "The map drawn by Ana showed the whole island." Answer: The map. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.
5. "Laughing at the joke, the class settled down." Answer: the class. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.
6. "The bridge built in 1920 still stands." Answer: The bridge. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.
7. "Tired after the hike, Leo slept well." Answer: Leo. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.
8. "The dog barking at the gate woke the street." Answer: The dog. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.
9. "Written in neat handwriting, the note was easy to read." Answer: the note. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.
10. "The farmer planting seeds worked until dark." Answer: The farmer. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.
11. "Glowing in the window, the lamp lit the porch." Answer: the lamp. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.
12. "The kite flying high above the field was red." Answer: The kite. Hint: The participial phrase describes the noun it tells about.

### Topic: Commas with Introductory Phrases and Clauses

Teaching card:
- Definition: An introductory phrase or clause comes at the start of a sentence and sets the scene. Put a comma after it, before the main clause begins.
- Worked example: "After the rain stopped, the children ran outside." After the rain stopped is the introductory clause. The comma comes right after it.
- How it works: Find the opening phrase or clause. If the sentence starts with a phrase or clause before the main subject and verb, put a comma after it.
- Tip box: "In the morning, the birds sang." In the morning is introductory, so a comma follows. "Running to the bus, Ana dropped her book." Running to the bus is introductory, so a comma follows.

Quiz:
1. "After the rain stopped, the children ran outside." Answer: after stopped. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.
2. "In the morning, the birds sang." Answer: after morning. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.
3. "Running to the bus, Ana dropped her book." Answer: after bus. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.
4. "Before the game, the team warmed up." Answer: after game. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.
5. "Under the old bridge, the river ran slow." Answer: after bridge. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.
6. "When the bell rang, the students lined up." Answer: after rang. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.
7. "Tired from the hike, Leo slept all afternoon." Answer: after hike. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.
8. "During the storm, the lights went out." Answer: after storm. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.
9. "With a smile, the teacher greeted the class." Answer: after smile. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.
10. "After dinner, we played a board game." Answer: after dinner. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.
11. "On the top shelf, the old books gathered dust." Answer: after shelf. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.
12. "Because it was cold, we stayed inside." Answer: after cold. Hint: Find where the opening phrase or clause ends and the main clause begins.

### Topic: Correcting Dependent Clause Fragments

Teaching card:
- Definition: A dependent clause fragment is a dependent clause written as if it were a sentence. It has a subject and a verb, but it cannot stand alone. Fix it by joining it to a main clause.
- Worked example: "Because it rained." This is a fragment. Fixed: "Because it rained, the game moved inside." Now the dependent clause is joined to a main clause.
- How it works: Find the clause that starts with a subordinating word. Join it to the sentence it belongs with, or add a main clause to finish the thought.
- Tip box: "When the bell rang." Fixed: "When the bell rang, the students lined up." "If you hurry." Fixed: "If you hurry, you will catch the bus."

Quiz:
1. "Because it rained." Answer: "Because it rained, the game moved inside." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.
2. "When the bell rang." Answer: "When the bell rang, the students lined up." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.
3. "If you hurry." Answer: "If you hurry, you will catch the bus." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.
4. "Although she was tired." Answer: "Although she was tired, Mia kept reading." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.
5. "After the rain stopped." Answer: "After the rain stopped, the children ran outside." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.
6. "While Leo cooked dinner." Answer: "While Leo cooked dinner, Ana set the table." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.
7. "Since you asked." Answer: "Since you asked, I will tell you the story." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.
8. "Before the game started." Answer: "Before the game started, the team warmed up." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.
9. "As the sun set." Answer: "As the sun set, the sky turned orange." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.
10. "Until the party began." Answer: "Until the party began, Sam hid the gift." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.
11. "When the soup is ready." Answer: "When the soup is ready, we will eat." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.
12. "Because the sun shone." Answer: "Because the sun shone, the seeds grew fast." Hint: Join a dependent clause fragment to a main clause to finish the thought.

### Topic: Punctuating Restrictive and Nonrestrictive Clauses

Teaching card:
- Definition: A restrictive clause is needed to know which person or thing you mean, so it takes no commas. A nonrestrictive clause adds extra detail that is not needed, so it is set off with commas.
- Worked example: "The student who won the race smiled." Who won the race is restrictive, so no commas. "Ana, who won the race, smiled." Who won the race is extra detail about Ana, so it takes commas.
- How it works: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one. If yes, leave out the commas. If no, set it off with commas.
- Tip box: "The book that I borrowed was about space." That I borrowed is needed, so no commas. "The book, which was old, sat on the shelf." Which was old is extra detail, so it takes commas.

Quiz:
1. "The student who won the race smiled." Answer: no commas. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
2. "Ana, who won the race, smiled." Answer: commas around who won the race. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
3. "The book that I borrowed was about space." Answer: no commas. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
4. "The book, which was old, sat on the shelf." Answer: commas around which was old. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
5. "The dog that barked all night slept all day." Answer: no commas. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
6. "My dog, which barks at squirrels, slept all day." Answer: commas around which barks at squirrels. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
7. "The house that stands on the hill is empty." Answer: no commas. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
8. "The house, which stands on the hill, is empty." Answer: commas around which stands on the hill. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
9. "The teacher who teaches math is kind." Answer: no commas. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
10. "Mrs. Alvarez, who teaches math, is kind." Answer: commas around who teaches math. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
11. "The river that runs past the town is wide." Answer: no commas. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
12. "The river, which runs past the town, is wide." Answer: commas around which runs past the town. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.

### Topic: Correcting Comma Splices and Fused Sentences

Teaching card:
- Definition: A comma splice joins two full sentences with only a comma. A fused sentence joins two full sentences with nothing at all. Fix either one with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
- Worked example: "The rain stopped, the sun came out." This is a comma splice. Fixed: "The rain stopped, and the sun came out." Or: "The rain stopped. The sun came out."
- How it works: Find the two full sentences. Split them with a period, join them with a semicolon, or add a conjunction after the comma.
- Tip box: "Leo was tired he went to bed early." This is a fused sentence. Fixed: "Leo was tired, so he went to bed early." "Ana read the book, it was long." Fixed: "Ana read the book. It was long."

Quiz:
1. "The rain stopped, the sun came out." Answer: "The rain stopped, and the sun came out." Hint: Fix a comma splice with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
2. "Leo was tired he went to bed early." Answer: "Leo was tired, so he went to bed early." Hint: Fix a fused sentence with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
3. "Ana read the book, it was long." Answer: "Ana read the book. It was long." Hint: Fix a comma splice with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
4. "The dog barked the cat ran away." Answer: "The dog barked, and the cat ran away." Hint: Fix a fused sentence with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
5. "We planted seeds, they grew fast." Answer: "We planted seeds. They grew fast." Hint: Fix a comma splice with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
6. "Mia practiced daily she won the contest." Answer: "Mia practiced daily, so she won the contest." Hint: Fix a fused sentence with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
7. "The bus was late, we walked instead." Answer: "The bus was late. We walked instead." Hint: Fix a comma splice with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
8. "Sam lost his keys he found them later." Answer: "Sam lost his keys, but he found them later." Hint: Fix a fused sentence with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
9. "The soup was hot, we waited to eat." Answer: "The soup was hot; we waited to eat." Hint: Fix a comma splice with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
10. "The game ended the crowd cheered." Answer: "The game ended, and the crowd cheered." Hint: Fix a fused sentence with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
11. "Ana loves to paint, she teaches others too." Answer: "Ana loves to paint. She teaches others too." Hint: Fix a comma splice with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
12. "The river was high the bridge stayed open." Answer: "The river was high, but the bridge stayed open." Hint: Fix a fused sentence with a period, a semicolon, or a conjunction.
