## Pathway: Plural vs. Possessive Nouns (6 topics)

### Topic: Forming Regular Plurals with "-s"

Teaching card:
- Definition: A plural noun names more than one. For most nouns, add -s to make the plural. For nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh, add -es.
- Worked example: "One cat, two cats." Add -s to cat. "One box, two boxes." Add -es to box because it ends in x.
- How it works: To make a noun plural, add -s. If the noun ends in s, x, ch, or sh, add -es instead.
- Tip box: "One dish, two dishes." Dish ends in sh, so add -es. "One bus, two buses." Bus ends in s, so add -es.

Quiz:
1. "One cat, two cats." Answer: cats. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.
2. "One box, two boxes." Answer: boxes. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.
3. "One dish, two dishes." Answer: dishes. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.
4. "One bus, two buses." Answer: buses. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.
5. "One dog, two dogs." Answer: dogs. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.
6. "One watch, two watches." Answer: watches. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.
7. "One tree, two trees." Answer: trees. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.
8. "One class, two classes." Answer: classes. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.
9. "One fox, two foxes." Answer: foxes. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.
10. "One bird, two birds." Answer: birds. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.
11. "One brush, two brushes." Answer: brushes. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.
12. "One church, two churches." Answer: churches. Hint: Check how the noun ends. Nouns ending in s, x, ch, or sh take -es, and most others take -s.

### Topic: Recognizing Plurals and Possessives

Teaching card:
- Definition: A plural noun names more than one and usually ends in -s. A possessive noun shows ownership and uses an apostrophe, like the dog's bone. The apostrophe tells you who owns what.
- Worked example: "The dogs barked." Dogs is plural, more than one dog. "The dog's bone was buried." Dog's is possessive, the bone belongs to the dog.
- How it works: Ask whether the word names more than one or shows ownership. More than one means plural. An apostrophe showing ownership means possessive.
- Tip box: "The girls played." Girls is plural. "The girl's hat blew away." Girl's is possessive. "The cats slept." Cats is plural.

Quiz:
1. "The dogs barked at the mail carrier." Answer: plural. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.
2. "The dog's bone was buried in the yard." Answer: possessive. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.
3. "The girls played soccer after school." Answer: plural. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.
4. "The girl's hat blew away in the wind." Answer: possessive. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.
5. "The cats slept in the sun." Answer: plural. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.
6. "The cat's tail twitched." Answer: possessive. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.
7. "The books fell off the shelf." Answer: plural. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.
8. "The book's cover was torn." Answer: possessive. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.
9. "The teachers met in the hall." Answer: plural. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.
10. "The teacher's desk was neat and tidy." Answer: possessive. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.
11. "The birds sang at sunrise." Answer: plural. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.
12. "The bird's nest sat high in the tree." Answer: possessive. Hint: Look at the word ending in -s and check for an apostrophe that shows ownership.

### Topic: Forming Singular Possessives 1

Teaching card:
- Definition: A singular possessive shows that one person or thing owns something. Form it by adding an apostrophe and -s to the noun, even if the noun already ends in -s.
- Worked example: "The girl's bike is red." Girl's shows the bike belongs to one girl. Add an apostrophe and -s to girl.
- How it works: Take the singular noun, add an apostrophe and -s. The possessive comes right before the thing that is owned.
- Tip box: "The teacher's desk." One teacher owns the desk. "James's book." James owns the book.

Quiz:
1. "The girl's bike is red." Answer: girl's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s to a singular noun to show one owner.
2. "The teacher's desk was neat." Answer: teacher's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s to a singular noun to show one owner.
3. "James's book fell on the floor." Answer: James's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s even when the noun already ends in -s.
4. "The dog's collar is blue." Answer: dog's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s to a singular noun to show one owner.
5. "Ana's painting won first place." Answer: Ana's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s to a singular noun to show one owner.
6. "The baby's blanket is soft." Answer: baby's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s to a singular noun to show one owner.
7. "Leo's kite flew high." Answer: Leo's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s to a singular noun to show one owner.
8. "The baker's bread smells wonderful." Answer: baker's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s to a singular noun to show one owner.
9. "Mia's shoes are muddy." Answer: Mia's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s to a singular noun to show one owner.
10. "The cat's whiskers twitched." Answer: cat's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s to a singular noun to show one owner.
11. "Sam's lunchbox is in his bag." Answer: Sam's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s to a singular noun to show one owner.
12. "The river's banks were lined with trees." Answer: river's. Hint: Add an apostrophe and -s to a singular noun to show one owner.

### Topic: Apostrophes with "Others," "Others'," and "Each Other's"

Teaching card:
- Definition: Others is a plural word and takes no apostrophe when it just means more people. Others' with an apostrophe after the -s shows that a group owns something. Each other's shows that two people share something with each other.
- Worked example: "Some students stayed, but the others left." Others is just plural, so no apostrophe. "We borrowed the others' books." Others' shows the books belong to the other students. "Ana and Leo shared each other's snacks." Each other's shows they shared with each other.
- How it works: If others only means more people, use no apostrophe. If a group owns something, put the apostrophe after the -s. For two people sharing, use each other's.
- Tip box: "The others cheered." Others is plural, no apostrophe. "The others' team won." Others' shows ownership. "They read each other's stories." Each other's shows sharing.

Quiz:
1. "Some students stayed, but the others left." Answer: others. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.
2. "We borrowed the others' books." Answer: others'. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.
3. "Ana and Leo shared each other's snacks." Answer: each other's. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.
4. "The others cheered from the stands." Answer: others. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.
5. "The others' voices rose in song." Answer: others'. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.
6. "They read each other's stories aloud." Answer: each other's. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.
7. "Some flowers bloomed, but the others wilted." Answer: others. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.
8. "The others' tents stood by the river." Answer: others'. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.
9. "Sam and Mia traded each other's pencils." Answer: each other's. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.
10. "The others waited by the door." Answer: others. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.
11. "The others' homework was already done." Answer: others'. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.
12. "Leo and Ana admired each other's drawings." Answer: each other's. Hint: Ask whether the word just names more people or things, shows that a group owns something, or shows two people giving to each other.

### Topic: Is the Possession Shared or Unshared?

Teaching card:
- Definition: Shared possession means two or more people own one thing together. Only the last name takes an apostrophe and -s. Unshared possession means each person owns a separate thing, so every name takes an apostrophe and -s.
- Worked example: "Ana and Leo's garden is behind the school." They share one garden, so only Leo's takes the apostrophe. "Ana's and Leo's gardens are behind the school." They each have their own garden, so both names take the apostrophe.
- How it works: Ask whether the owners share one thing or each own one. One shared thing means one apostrophe, on the last name. Separate things mean an apostrophe on every name.
- Tip box: "Sam and Mia's dog is brown." One dog, shared. "Sam's and Mia's dogs are brown." Two dogs, one each.

Quiz:
1. "Ana and Leo's garden is behind the school." Answer: shared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.
2. "Ana's and Leo's gardens are behind the school." Answer: unshared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.
3. "Sam and Mia's dog is brown." Answer: shared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.
4. "Sam's and Mia's dogs are brown." Answer: unshared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.
5. "Mom and Dad's car is in the garage." Answer: shared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.
6. "Mom's and Dad's cars are in the garage." Answer: unshared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.
7. "Leo and Ana's project won a prize." Answer: shared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.
8. "Leo's and Ana's projects won prizes." Answer: unshared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.
9. "The twins' room is upstairs." Answer: shared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.
10. "The twins' rooms are upstairs." Answer: unshared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.
11. "Ben and Sara's tent stood by the lake." Answer: shared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.
12. "Ben's and Sara's tents stood by the lake." Answer: unshared. Hint: Check whether one thing is owned or several, and look at which names take an apostrophe.

### Topic: Correcting Errors with Shared and Unshared Possession

Teaching card:
- Definition: Shared possession needs only one apostrophe, on the last owner's name. Unshared possession needs an apostrophe on every owner's name. A common error is putting an apostrophe on every name when the thing is shared, or on only one name when each owner has their own.
- Worked example: "Ana's and Leo's garden is behind the school." This is wrong if they share one garden. Fixed: "Ana and Leo's garden is behind the school." One garden, one apostrophe.
- How it works: Check whether the owners share one thing or each own one. Fix the apostrophes to match: one for shared, one on every name for unshared.
- Tip box: "Sam's and Mia's dog is brown." Wrong for one shared dog. Fixed: "Sam and Mia's dog is brown." "Mom and Dad's cars are in the garage." Wrong for two cars. Fixed: "Mom's and Dad's cars are in the garage."

Quiz:
1. "Ana's and Leo's garden is behind the school." Answer: "Ana and Leo's garden is behind the school." Hint: Decide whether the owners share one thing or each own one, then fix the apostrophes to match.
2. "Mom and Dad's cars are in the garage." Answer: "Mom's and Dad's cars are in the garage." Hint: Decide whether the owners share one thing or each own one, then fix the apostrophes to match.
3. "Sam's and Mia's dog is brown." Answer: "Sam and Mia's dog is brown." Hint: Decide whether the owners share one thing or each own one, then fix the apostrophes to match.
4. "Leo and Ana's projects won prizes." Answer: "Leo's and Ana's projects won prizes." Hint: Decide whether the owners share one thing or each own one, then fix the apostrophes to match.
5. "Ben's and Sara's tent stood by the lake." Answer: "Ben and Sara's tent stood by the lake." Hint: Decide whether the owners share one thing or each own one, then fix the apostrophes to match.
6. "Mia's and Sam's kite flew highest." Answer: "Mia and Sam's kite flew highest." Hint: Decide whether the owners share one thing or each own one, then fix the apostrophes to match.
7. "Ana and Leo's gardens are behind the school." Answer: "Ana's and Leo's gardens are behind the school." Hint: Decide whether the owners share one thing or each own one, then fix the apostrophes to match.
8. "Mom's and Dad's car is in the garage." Answer: "Mom and Dad's car is in the garage." Hint: Decide whether the owners share one thing or each own one, then fix the apostrophes to match.
9. "Sam and Mia's dogs are brown." Answer: "Sam's and Mia's dogs are brown." Hint: Decide whether the owners share one thing or each own one, then fix the apostrophes to match.
10. "Leo's and Ana's project won a prize." Answer: "Leo and Ana's project won a prize." Hint: Decide whether the owners share one thing or each own one, then fix the apostrophes to match.
11. "Ben and Sara's tents stood by the lake." Answer: "Ben's and Sara's tents stood by the lake." Hint: Decide whether the owners share one thing or each own one, then fix the apostrophes to match.
12. "The twins's room is upstairs." Answer: "The twins' room is upstairs." Hint: Check how a plural noun that already ends in -s shows ownership.

---

## Pathway: Prepositional Phrases (1 topic)

This pathway's only topic, Identifying Prepositions, is shared with Identifying Parts of Speech III above. See "Identifying Prepositions" above.

---

## Pathway: Pronoun Case (5 topics)

### Topic: Using Subject and Object Pronouns

Teaching card:
- Definition: A subject pronoun does the action of the verb. An object pronoun receives the action or follows a preposition. Subject pronouns are I, you, he, she, it, we, and they. Object pronouns are me, you, him, her, it, us, and them.
- Worked example: "She gave the book to him." She is a subject pronoun because she does the giving. Him is an object pronoun because he receives the book.
- How it works: Find the verb, then ask who does it. That pronoun is a subject pronoun. Find who receives the action or follows a preposition. That pronoun is an object pronoun.
- Tip box: "They invited us to the fair." They is the subject pronoun. Us is the object pronoun. "He thanked her." He is the subject pronoun. Her is the object pronoun.

Quiz:
1. "She gave the book to him." Answer: She is the subject pronoun, him is the object pronoun. Hint: The subject pronoun does the action, and the object pronoun receives it.
2. "They invited us to the fair." Answer: They is the subject pronoun, us is the object pronoun. Hint: The subject pronoun does the action, and the object pronoun receives it.
3. "He thanked her for the help." Answer: He is the subject pronoun, her is the object pronoun. Hint: The subject pronoun does the action, and the object pronoun receives it.
4. "We met them at the park." Answer: We is the subject pronoun, them is the object pronoun. Hint: The subject pronoun does the action, and the object pronoun receives it.
5. "I asked Ana about the homework." Answer: I is the subject pronoun. Hint: The subject pronoun does the action of the verb.
6. "The teacher called me to the desk." Answer: me is the object pronoun. Hint: The object pronoun receives the action.
7. "She and I planted the garden." Answer: She and I are subject pronouns. Hint: Subject pronouns do the action of the verb.
8. "Leo gave the ball to her." Answer: her is the object pronoun. Hint: The object pronoun follows the preposition to.
9. "They watched us play the game." Answer: They is the subject pronoun, us is the object pronoun. Hint: The subject pronoun does the action, and the object pronoun receives it.
10. "It followed him home." Answer: It is the subject pronoun, him is the object pronoun. Hint: The subject pronoun does the action, and the object pronoun receives it.
11. "We thanked them for the ride." Answer: We is the subject pronoun, them is the object pronoun. Hint: The subject pronoun does the action, and the object pronoun receives it.
12. "She walked with me to school." Answer: me is the object pronoun. Hint: The object pronoun follows the preposition with.

### Topic: Using "He," "She," "Him," and "Her" in Compounds

Teaching card:
- Definition: In a compound, two pronouns or a noun and a pronoun share one job. If the compound is the subject, use he or she. If the compound is the object, use him or her. To test it, cover up the other half and hear which pronoun fits alone.
- Worked example: "Leo and she won the race." She fits because the compound is the subject. "The prize went to Leo and her." Her fits because the compound is the object.
- How it works: Cover up the other part of the compound. Say the sentence with only the pronoun. If he or she sounds right, the compound is a subject. If him or her sounds right, it is an object.
- Tip box: "Ana and he built the fort." He is right, because he built the fort. "The teacher praised Ana and him." Him is right, because the teacher praised him.

Quiz:
1. "Leo and she won the race." Answer: she. Hint: Cover up Leo and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?
2. "The prize went to Leo and her." Answer: her. Hint: Cover up Leo and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?
3. "Ana and he built the fort." Answer: he. Hint: Cover up Ana and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?
4. "The teacher praised Ana and him." Answer: him. Hint: Cover up Ana and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?
5. "She and Sam read the map." Answer: She. Hint: Cover up Sam and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?
6. "The coach thanked Mia and her." Answer: her. Hint: Cover up Mia and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?
7. "He and Leo carried the table." Answer: He. Hint: Cover up Leo and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?
8. "The invitation went to Ana and him." Answer: him. Hint: Cover up Ana and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?
9. "She and Ben baked cookies." Answer: She. Hint: Cover up Ben and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?
10. "The dog followed Sam and her home." Answer: her. Hint: Cover up Sam and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?
11. "He and Mia planted the seeds." Answer: He. Hint: Cover up Mia and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?
12. "The librarian helped Leo and him." Answer: him. Hint: Cover up Leo and say the sentence with only the pronoun. Which form sounds right?

### Topic: Using Possessive Pronouns with Gerunds

Teaching card:
- Definition: A gerund is a verb form ending in -ing that works as a noun. When a pronoun comes before a gerund, use the possessive form, like his, her, my, or their, not the object form.
- Worked example: "His singing won the contest." Singing is a gerund. His is the possessive pronoun before it.
- How it works: Find the -ing word that works as a noun. The pronoun right before it should be possessive, because the -ing word is a thing, and the pronoun shows whose thing it is.
- Tip box: "Her dancing amazed the crowd." Her is possessive before the gerund dancing. "Their arriving late delayed the game." Their is possessive before the gerund arriving.

Quiz:
1. "His singing won the contest." Answer: His. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.
2. "Her dancing amazed the crowd." Answer: Her. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.
3. "Their arriving late delayed the game." Answer: Their. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.
4. "My reading every night helps me sleep." Answer: My. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.
5. "His running every morning keeps him fit." Answer: His. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.
6. "Our winning the match thrilled the fans." Answer: Our. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.
7. "Her humming filled the quiet room." Answer: Her. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.
8. "Their practicing daily paid off at the recital." Answer: Their. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.
9. "Your helping with the dishes is kind." Answer: Your. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.
10. "His cooking for the fair took all morning." Answer: His. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.
11. "Her swimming across the lake took an hour." Answer: Her. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.
12. "My studying for the test started last week." Answer: My. Hint: A possessive pronoun comes before a gerund, not an object pronoun.

### Topic: Using Reflexive Pronouns

Teaching card:
- Definition: A reflexive pronoun ends in -self or -selves and points back to the subject. Use it when the subject and the object are the same person or thing. The forms are myself, yourself, himself, herself, itself, ourselves, yourselves, and themselves.
- Worked example: "Ana taught herself to juggle." Herself points back to Ana. Ana is both the teacher and the learner.
- How it works: Ask whether the object is the same person as the subject. If yes, use the reflexive form. If no, use a regular object pronoun.
- Tip box: "Leo hurt himself on the trail." Himself points back to Leo. "They built themselves a fort." Themselves points back to they.

Quiz:
1. "Ana taught herself to juggle." Answer: herself. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same person.
2. "Leo hurt himself on the trail." Answer: himself. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same person.
3. "They built themselves a fort." Answer: themselves. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same group.
4. "I made myself a sandwich." Answer: myself. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same person.
5. "She looked at herself in the mirror." Answer: herself. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same person.
6. "We enjoyed ourselves at the fair." Answer: ourselves. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same group.
7. "The cat cleaned itself after the rain." Answer: itself. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same thing.
8. "You should believe in yourself." Answer: yourself. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same person.
9. "Mia reminded herself to bring the book." Answer: herself. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same person.
10. "Sam and Ana introduced themselves to the class." Answer: themselves. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same group.
11. "He blamed himself for the loss." Answer: himself. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same person.
12. "The team congratulated itself on the win." Answer: itself. Hint: The reflexive pronoun points back to the subject when the subject and object are the same group.

### Topic: "Who" vs. "Whom"

Teaching card:
- Definition: Who is a subject pronoun, and whom is an object pronoun. Use who when the pronoun does the action. Use whom when the pronoun receives the action or follows a preposition.
- Worked example: "Who won the race?" Who does the winning, so who is right. "To whom should I give the prize?" Whom follows the preposition to, so whom is right.
- How it works: Test it by swapping in he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
- Tip box: "Who called me?" He called me, so who is right. "Whom did you call?" You called him, so whom is right.

Quiz:
1. "Who won the race?" Answer: Who. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
2. "To whom should I give the prize?" Answer: whom. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
3. "Who called me last night?" Answer: Who. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
4. "Whom did you call?" Answer: Whom. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
5. "Who is coming to the fair?" Answer: Who. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
6. "For whom is this gift?" Answer: whom. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
7. "Who wrote this story?" Answer: Who. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
8. "Whom did the teacher choose?" Answer: Whom. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
9. "Who left the gate open?" Answer: Who. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
10. "With whom did you travel?" Answer: whom. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
11. "Who will lead the hike?" Answer: Who. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.
12. "Whom should we thank for the meal?" Answer: Whom. Hint: Answer the question with he or him. If he fits, use who. If him fits, use whom.

## Pathway: Pronoun-Antecedent Agreement (3 topics)

Part 1, Identifying Pronouns and Antecedents, uses the topic Identifying Pronouns, shared with Identifying Parts of Speech III above. See "Identifying Pronouns" above. The antecedent is the noun the pronoun stands in for: in "Ana found the map. She showed it to Leo," Ana is the antecedent of she, and the map is the antecedent of it.

### Topic: Choosing Pronouns That Agree

Teaching card:
- Definition: A pronoun must agree with its antecedent, the noun it stands in for. A singular antecedent takes a singular pronoun. A plural antecedent takes a plural pronoun.
- Worked example: "The dog wagged its tail." Its is singular because dog is singular. "The dogs wagged their tails." Their is plural because dogs is plural.
- How it works: Find the antecedent first, then check whether it is singular or plural. Pick a pronoun that matches in number.
- Tip box: "Ana lost her book." Her matches Ana. "The students opened their books." Their matches students.

Quiz:
1. "The dog wagged its tail." Answer: its. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.
2. "The dogs wagged their tails." Answer: their. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.
3. "Ana lost her book." Answer: her. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.
4. "The students opened their books." Answer: their. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.
5. "Leo finished his homework." Answer: his. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.
6. "The birds built their nests." Answer: their. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.
7. "Mia packed her lunch." Answer: her. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.
8. "The team won its first game." Answer: its. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.
9. "Sam and Ana shared their snacks." Answer: their. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.
10. "The cat licked its paws." Answer: its. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.
11. "The flowers opened their petals." Answer: their. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.
12. "The teacher graded her papers." Answer: her. Hint: Find the noun the pronoun stands in for, then check whether it is singular or plural.

### Topic: Pronoun Agreement with Indefinite Antecedents

Teaching card:
- Definition: An indefinite pronoun does not name one exact person or thing. Some are singular, like everyone, someone, anybody, and each. Some are plural, like both, few, and many. The pronoun that follows must match the indefinite pronoun in number.
- Worked example: "Everyone brought his or her lunch." Everyone is singular, so the pronoun is singular. "Many brought their lunches." Many is plural, so the pronoun is plural.
- How it works: Check whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural. Everyone, someone, anybody, nobody, and each are singular. Both, few, several, and many are plural.
- Tip box: "Each student opened his or her book." Each is singular. "Few finished their work early." Few is plural.

Quiz:
1. "Everyone brought his or her lunch." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.
2. "Many brought their lunches." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.
3. "Each student opened his or her book." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.
4. "Few finished their work early." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.
5. "Someone left his or her coat behind." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.
6. "Both wore their raincoats." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.
7. "Anybody can bring his or her pet to the fair." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.
8. "Several lost their umbrellas in the wind." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.
9. "Nobody raised his or her hand." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.
10. "Many packed their bags the night before." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.
11. "Each of the girls wore her uniform." Answer: her. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.
12. "Few remembered their lines for the play." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether the indefinite pronoun is singular or plural, then check the pronoun that follows.

## Pathway: Punctuation with Conjunctions: Coordinating (FANBOYS) (3 topics)

Part 1, Recognizing FANBOYS, uses the topic Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions (FANBOYS), shared with Identifying Parts of Speech III above. See "Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions (FANBOYS)" above.

### Topic: Identifying the Function of FANBOYS

Teaching card:
- Definition: Each FANBOYS conjunction does a different job. For gives a reason. And adds. Nor shows neither. But shows a contrast. Or gives a choice. Yet shows a contrast. So shows a result.
- Worked example: "Ana was tired, so she went to bed." So shows a result. "Leo wanted to play, but it rained." But shows a contrast.
- How it works: Read the two parts the conjunction joins, then ask how they relate. The relationship tells you which job the conjunction is doing.
- Tip box: "Sam likes apples and pears." And adds. "We can walk or ride." Or gives a choice.

Quiz:
1. "Ana was tired, so she went to bed." Answer: so shows a result. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.
2. "Leo wanted to play, but it rained." Answer: but shows a contrast. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.
3. "Sam likes apples and pears." Answer: and adds. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.
4. "We can walk or ride to the park." Answer: or gives a choice. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.
5. "The cat hid under the bed, for it was scared." Answer: for gives a reason. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.
6. "He did not want soup, nor did he want salad." Answer: nor shows neither. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.
7. "The hike was long, yet the view was worth it." Answer: yet shows a contrast. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.
8. "Mia studied hard, so she passed the test." Answer: so shows a result. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.
9. "The soup was hot, but we ate it anyway." Answer: but shows a contrast. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.
10. "You can stay home, or you can come with us." Answer: or gives a choice. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.
11. "The team lost, for their best player was hurt." Answer: for gives a reason. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.
12. "Sam did not call, nor did he write." Answer: nor shows neither. Hint: Read the two parts the conjunction joins and ask how the second part relates to the first.

### Topic: Punctuating FANBOYS (checkpoint)

Teaching card:
- Definition: When a FANBOYS conjunction joins two independent clauses, put a comma before the conjunction. When it joins two words or phrases, use no comma.
- Worked example: "Ana wanted the red kite, but Leo chose the blue one." A comma comes before but because it joins two full clauses. "Ana wanted a red kite and a blue ball." No comma, because and joins two phrases.
- How it works: Check what the conjunction joins. Two full clauses mean a comma before the conjunction. Two words or phrases mean no comma.
- Tip box: "Sam likes apples and pears." No comma, because and joins two words. "Sam likes apples, and Ana likes pears." A comma, because and joins two clauses.

Quiz:
1. "Ana wanted the red kite, but Leo chose the blue one." Answer: comma before but. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.
2. "Ana wanted a red kite and a blue ball." Answer: no comma. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.
3. "Sam likes apples and pears." Answer: no comma. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.
4. "Sam likes apples, and Ana likes pears." Answer: comma before and. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.
5. "The rain was cold, yet the team kept playing." Answer: comma before yet. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.
6. "We can walk or ride to the park." Answer: no comma. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.
7. "He studied hard, so he passed the test." Answer: comma before so. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.
8. "Mia brought a pen and a notebook." Answer: no comma. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.
9. "The hike was long, but the view was worth it." Answer: comma before but. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.
10. "Leo did not want soup, nor did he want salad." Answer: comma before nor. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.
11. "The cat hid under the bed, for it was scared." Answer: comma before for. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.
12. "You can stay home, or you can come with us." Answer: comma before or. Hint: Check whether the conjunction joins two full clauses or just two words or phrases.

---

## Pathway: Punctuation with Conjunctions: Subordinating (3 topics)

Part 1, Recognizing Subordinating Conjunctions, uses the topic Identifying Subordinating Conjunctions, shared with Identifying Parts of Speech III above. See "Identifying Subordinating Conjunctions" above.

### Topic: Punctuating Subordinating Conjunctions at the Beginning of a Sentence

Teaching card:
- Definition: When a dependent clause starting with a subordinating conjunction comes at the beginning of a sentence, put a comma after the dependent clause, before the main clause begins.
- Worked example: "Because it rained, the game moved inside." A comma comes after rained, because the because clause comes first.
- How it works: Find the dependent clause at the start. Put a comma right where it ends and the main clause begins.
- Tip box: "When the bell rang, the students lined up." A comma comes after rang. "If you hurry, you will catch the bus." A comma comes after hurry.

Quiz:
1. "Because it rained, the game moved inside." Answer: comma after rained. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
2. "When the bell rang, the students lined up." Answer: comma after rang. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
3. "If you hurry, you will catch the bus." Answer: comma after hurry. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
4. "Although she was tired, Mia kept reading." Answer: comma after tired. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
5. "After the rain stopped, the children ran outside." Answer: comma after stopped. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
6. "While Leo cooked dinner, Ana set the table." Answer: comma after dinner. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
7. "Since you asked, I will tell you the story." Answer: comma after asked. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
8. "Before the game started, the team warmed up." Answer: comma after started. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
9. "As the sun set, the sky turned orange." Answer: comma after set. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
10. "Until the party began, Sam hid the gift." Answer: comma after began. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
11. "When the soup is ready, we will eat." Answer: comma after ready. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.
12. "Because the sun shone, the seeds grew fast." Answer: comma after shone. Hint: Find where the dependent clause ends and the main clause begins.

### Topic: Punctuating Subordinating Conjunctions in the Middle of a Sentence

Teaching card:
- Definition: When a dependent clause starting with a subordinating conjunction comes in the middle or at the end of a sentence, use no comma before it. The dependent clause joins straight onto the main clause.
- Worked example: "The game moved inside because it rained." No comma before because, because the dependent clause comes after the main clause.
- How it works: Check where the dependent clause sits. At the start, it needs a comma after it. In the middle or at the end, it needs no comma before it.
- Tip box: "The students lined up when the bell rang." No comma before when. "Mia kept reading although she was tired." No comma before although.

Quiz:
1. "The game moved inside because it rained." Answer: no comma before because. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.
2. "The students lined up when the bell rang." Answer: no comma before when. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.
3. "You will catch the bus if you hurry." Answer: no comma before if. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.
4. "Mia kept reading although she was tired." Answer: no comma before although. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.
5. "The children ran outside after the rain stopped." Answer: no comma before after. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.
6. "Ana set the table while Leo cooked dinner." Answer: no comma before while. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.
7. "I will tell you the story since you asked." Answer: no comma before since. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.
8. "The team warmed up before the game started." Answer: no comma before before. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.
9. "The sky turned orange as the sun set." Answer: no comma before as. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.
10. "Sam hid the gift until the party began." Answer: no comma before until. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.
11. "We will eat when the soup is ready." Answer: no comma before when. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.
12. "The seeds grew fast because the sun shone." Answer: no comma before because. Hint: Check whether the dependent clause comes first or after the main clause.

---

## Pathway: Restrictive and Nonrestrictive Clauses (1 topic)

### Topic: Punctuating Restrictive and Nonrestrictive Clauses

Teaching card:
- Definition: A restrictive clause is a part of a sentence that you need in order to know which person or thing is meant, so it takes no commas. A nonrestrictive clause adds extra information about a person or thing you already know, so it is set off with commas.
- Worked example: "The student who sits by the window won the spelling contest." The clause "who sits by the window" is restrictive. It tells which student won, so it has no commas. Compare: "Ana, who sits by the window, won the spelling contest." The clause "who sits by the window" is nonrestrictive because Ana is already named, so it takes commas.
- How it works: Ask whether the clause is needed to identify the noun. If yes, leave out the commas. If no, add commas around it.
- Tip box: "The book that I borrowed from the library is overdue." No commas, because the clause tells which book. "The library book, which I borrowed last week, is overdue." Commas, because the book is already identified.

Quiz:
1. Fix the punctuation: "Leo who scored the winning goal lifted the trophy." Answer: Leo, who scored the winning goal, lifted the trophy. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
2. Fix the punctuation: "The player who scored the winning goal lifted the trophy." Answer: No commas needed. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
3. Fix the punctuation: "My backpack which is bright green was left on the bus." Answer: My backpack, which is bright green, was left on the bus. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
4. Fix the punctuation: "The backpack that is bright green belongs to Sam." Answer: No commas needed. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
5. Fix the punctuation: "Our science teacher who grew up on a farm keeps chickens." Answer: Our science teacher, who grew up on a farm, keeps chickens. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
6. Fix the punctuation: "The teacher who assigns the most homework is Mr. Lane." Answer: No commas needed. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
7. Fix the punctuation: "The Cedar River which flows through our town is wide and slow." Answer: The Cedar River, which flows through our town, is wide and slow. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
8. Fix the punctuation: "The river that flows through our town flooded last spring." Answer: No commas needed. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
9. Fix the punctuation: "Maya who plays the violin practiced every morning." Answer: Maya, who plays the violin, practiced every morning. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
10. Fix the punctuation: "The violin that Maya plays belonged to her grandmother." Answer: No commas needed. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
11. Fix the punctuation: "Our class movie which won three awards will be shown on Friday." Answer: Our class movie, which won three awards, will be shown on Friday. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.
12. Fix the punctuation: "The movie that won three awards is about a mountain climber." Answer: No commas needed. Hint: Ask whether the clause is needed to tell which one is meant, or whether the noun is already clear without it.

## Pathway: Rules of Capitalization I (6 topics)

### Topic: Capitalizing the First Word of a Sentence

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The first word of every sentence starts with a capital letter. This rule holds for statements, questions, and commands.
- Worked example: "the dog chased the ball." The first word, the, needs a capital, so the sentence becomes "The dog chased the ball."
- Explanation: A capital letter at the start marks where a new sentence begins.
- Tip box: "we are going to the park after lunch." becomes "We are going to the park after lunch." "did you finish your homework?" becomes "Did you finish your homework?"

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "the library opens at nine in the morning." Answer: The library opens at nine in the morning. Hint: The first word of a sentence always takes a capital letter.
2. Fix the capitalization: "sam and ana built a tower out of blocks." Answer: Sam and Ana built a tower out of blocks. Hint: The first word of a sentence always takes a capital letter, and so does every name.
3. Fix the capitalization: "our class planted seeds in the garden." Answer: Our class planted seeds in the garden. Hint: The first word of a sentence always takes a capital letter.
4. Fix the capitalization: "did the rain stop before lunch?" Answer: Did the rain stop before lunch? Hint: The first word of a question also takes a capital letter.
5. Fix the capitalization: "please pass the salt." Answer: Please pass the salt. Hint: The first word of a command also takes a capital letter.
6. Fix the capitalization: "the museum was crowded on saturday." Answer: The museum was crowded on Saturday. Hint: The first word of a sentence always takes a capital letter, and so does the name of a day.
7. Fix the capitalization: "after the game, we walked home together." Answer: After the game, we walked home together. Hint: The first word of a sentence always takes a capital letter.
8. Fix the capitalization: "leo found a shiny stone near the river." Answer: Leo found a shiny stone near the river. Hint: The first word of a sentence always takes a capital letter.
9. Fix the capitalization: "the train arrived ten minutes late." Answer: The train arrived ten minutes late. Hint: The first word of a sentence always takes a capital letter.
10. Fix the capitalization: "can you help me carry this box?" Answer: Can you help me carry this box? Hint: The first word of a question also takes a capital letter.
11. Fix the capitalization: "yesterday we watched a film about volcanoes." Answer: Yesterday we watched a film about volcanoes. Hint: The first word of a sentence always takes a capital letter.
12. Fix the capitalization: "bring your notebook to class tomorrow." Answer: Bring your notebook to class tomorrow. Hint: The first word of a command also takes a capital letter.

### Topic: Capitalizing Days and Months

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The names of days and months are always capitalized. The names of seasons are not capitalized.
- Worked example: "we meet on tuesday in march." The first word, the day tuesday, and the month march need capitals, so the sentence becomes "We meet on Tuesday in March."
- Explanation: Days and months are proper nouns, so they take capital letters.
- Tip box: "The race is on friday in june." becomes "The race is on Friday in June." "Her birthday is in november." becomes "Her birthday is in November."

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "The test is on monday." Answer: The test is on Monday. Hint: Days of the week are always capitalized.
2. Fix the capitalization: "School starts again in september." Answer: School starts again in September. Hint: Months are always capitalized.
3. Fix the capitalization: "We play soccer every wednesday." Answer: We play soccer every Wednesday. Hint: Days of the week are always capitalized.
4. Fix the capitalization: "The snow fell in january." Answer: The snow fell in January. Hint: Months are always capitalized.
5. Fix the capitalization: "Our trip is on saturday, the fifth of may." Answer: Our trip is on Saturday, the fifth of May. Hint: Days of the week and months are always capitalized.
6. Fix the capitalization: "She was born on a thursday in april." Answer: She was born on a Thursday in April. Hint: Days of the week and months are always capitalized.
7. Fix the capitalization: "Practice is on tuesday and friday." Answer: Practice is on Tuesday and Friday. Hint: Days of the week are always capitalized.
8. Fix the capitalization: "The fair comes to town in august." Answer: The fair comes to town in August. Hint: Months are always capitalized.
9. Fix the capitalization: "We will finish the project by sunday." Answer: We will finish the project by Sunday. Hint: Days of the week are always capitalized.
10. Fix the capitalization: "The garden blooms in march and april." Answer: The garden blooms in March and April. Hint: Months are always capitalized.
11. Fix the capitalization: "Her recital is on the first friday of december." Answer: Her recital is on the first Friday of December. Hint: Days of the week and months are always capitalized.
12. Fix the capitalization: "The library is closed on mondays in october." Answer: The library is closed on Mondays in October. Hint: Days of the week and months are always capitalized.

### Topic: Capitalizing Holidays

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The names of holidays are capitalized. Every important word in a holiday name takes a capital letter.
- Worked example: "we celebrate thanksgiving in november." The first word, the holiday thanksgiving, and the month november need capitals, so the sentence becomes "We celebrate Thanksgiving in November."
- Explanation: Holiday names are proper nouns, so they take capital letters.
- Tip box: "The fireworks on independence day were bright." becomes "The fireworks on Independence Day were bright." "We bake cookies for valentine's day." becomes "We bake cookies for Valentine's Day."

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "We gave cards on valentine's day." Answer: We gave cards on Valentine's Day. Hint: Holiday names are always capitalized.
2. Fix the capitalization: "The parade on independence day starts at noon." Answer: The parade on Independence Day starts at noon. Hint: Every important word in a holiday name takes a capital letter.
3. Fix the capitalization: "We carve pumpkins for halloween." Answer: We carve pumpkins for Halloween. Hint: Holiday names are always capitalized.
4. Fix the capitalization: "School is closed on labor day." Answer: School is closed on Labor Day. Hint: Every important word in a holiday name takes a capital letter.
5. Fix the capitalization: "We light candles during hanukkah." Answer: We light candles during Hanukkah. Hint: Holiday names are always capitalized.
6. Fix the capitalization: "The new year begins on new year's day." Answer: The new year begins on New Year's Day. Hint: Every important word in a holiday name takes a capital letter.
7. Fix the capitalization: "She dressed up for halloween as a scientist." Answer: She dressed up for Halloween as a scientist. Hint: Holiday names are always capitalized.
8. Fix the capitalization: "We visit family on thanksgiving." Answer: We visit family on Thanksgiving. Hint: Holiday names are always capitalized.
9. Fix the capitalization: "The fireworks show is on memorial day." Answer: The fireworks show is on Memorial Day. Hint: Every important word in a holiday name takes a capital letter.
10. Fix the capitalization: "We hide eggs on easter morning." Answer: We hide eggs on Easter morning. Hint: Holiday names are always capitalized.
11. Fix the capitalization: "The tree lighting is on christmas eve." Answer: The tree lighting is on Christmas Eve. Hint: Every important word in a holiday name takes a capital letter.
12. Fix the capitalization: "We celebrate earth day by planting trees." Answer: We celebrate Earth Day by planting trees. Hint: Every important word in a holiday name takes a capital letter.

### Topic: Capitalizing Seasons

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The names of seasons are not capitalized. Words like spring, summer, fall, and winter stay lowercase unless they begin a sentence or are part of a proper name.
- Worked example: "we swim every Summer." The first word needs a capital, but the season summer does not, so the sentence becomes "We swim every summer."
- Explanation: Seasons are common nouns, not proper nouns, so they do not take capital letters.
- Tip box: "The leaves turn red in Fall." becomes "The leaves turn red in fall." "Winter is my favorite season." stays as it is, with a capital W only because it starts the sentence.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "We plant flowers in Spring." Answer: We plant flowers in spring. Hint: Seasons are not capitalized.
2. Fix the capitalization: "The days grow long in Summer." Answer: The days grow long in summer. Hint: Seasons are not capitalized.
3. Fix the capitalization: "Fall brings cool air and orange leaves." Answer: Fall brings cool air and orange leaves. Hint: A season is capitalized only when it starts a sentence.
4. Fix the capitalization: "Winter is the season for snow." Answer: Winter is the season for snow. Hint: A season is capitalized only when it starts a sentence.
5. Fix the capitalization: "She loves to hike in Autumn." Answer: She loves to hike in autumn. Hint: Seasons are not capitalized.
6. Fix the capitalization: "Our garden blooms in Spring and Summer." Answer: Our garden blooms in spring and summer. Hint: Seasons are not capitalized.
7. Fix the capitalization: "The lake freezes in Winter." Answer: The lake freezes in winter. Hint: Seasons are not capitalized.
8. Fix the capitalization: "Summer is the warmest season here." Answer: Summer is the warmest season here. Hint: A season is capitalized only when it starts a sentence.
9. Fix the capitalization: "We rake leaves in the Fall." Answer: We rake leaves in the fall. Hint: Seasons are not capitalized.
10. Fix the capitalization: "The birds return in the Spring." Answer: The birds return in the spring. Hint: Seasons are not capitalized.
11. Fix the capitalization: "Fall is when school starts again." Answer: Fall is when school starts again. Hint: A season is capitalized only when it starts a sentence.
12. Fix the capitalization: "He trains for the race all Winter long." Answer: He trains for the race all winter long. Hint: Seasons are not capitalized.

### Topic: Capitalizing Streets, Cities, and Other Places

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The names of streets, cities, states, countries, rivers, mountains, and other specific places are capitalized. Every important word in a place name takes a capital letter.
- Worked example: "we live on maple street in denver." The first word, the street maple street, and the city denver need capitals, so the sentence becomes "We live on Maple Street in Denver."
- Explanation: Specific place names are proper nouns, so they take capital letters.
- Tip box: "The river flows through the grand canyon." becomes "The river flows through the Grand Canyon." "She visited paris last year." becomes "She visited Paris last year."

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "Our school is on oak avenue." Answer: Our school is on Oak Avenue. Hint: Street names are capitalized.
2. Fix the capitalization: "He grew up in chicago." Answer: He grew up in Chicago. Hint: City names are capitalized.
3. Fix the capitalization: "The store is on elm street near the park." Answer: The store is on Elm Street near the park. Hint: Street names are capitalized.
4. Fix the capitalization: "We flew to canada for the summer." Answer: We flew to Canada for the summer. Hint: Country names are capitalized.
5. Fix the capitalization: "The mississippi river is very wide." Answer: The Mississippi River is very wide. Hint: River names are capitalized.
6. Fix the capitalization: "She lives in austin, texas." Answer: She lives in Austin, Texas. Hint: City and state names are capitalized.
7. Fix the capitalization: "The trail climbs mount hood." Answer: The trail climbs Mount Hood. Hint: Mountain names are capitalized.
8. Fix the capitalization: "We met at central park on fifth avenue." Answer: We met at Central Park on Fifth Avenue. Hint: Park and street names are capitalized.
9. Fix the capitalization: "The museum is in new york city." Answer: The museum is in New York City. Hint: City names are capitalized.
10. Fix the capitalization: "Our cousins live in oregon." Answer: Our cousins live in Oregon. Hint: State names are capitalized.
11. Fix the capitalization: "The lake near cedar falls is deep." Answer: The lake near Cedar Falls is deep. Hint: Place names are capitalized.
12. Fix the capitalization: "They drove down river road to the beach." Answer: They drove down River Road to the beach. Hint: Street names are capitalized.

### Topic: Capitalizing Family Titles

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A family word like mom, dad, grandma, or uncle is capitalized when it is used as a name, with no other word in front of it. It stays lowercase when it follows a possessive word like my, your, or his.
- Worked example: "i asked mom for help." The word I is always a capital, and mom is used as a name, so the sentence becomes "I asked Mom for help." Compare: "I asked my mom for help." Here mom follows my, so it stays lowercase.
- Explanation: A family word acts like a proper noun only when it stands in for a name.
- Tip box: "Uncle Ray is coming to dinner." Uncle is capitalized because it is part of a name. "My uncle is coming to dinner." Uncle stays lowercase because it follows "my."

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "i told dad about the game." Answer: I told Dad about the game. Hint: A family word is capitalized when it is used as a name.
2. Fix the capitalization: "I told my dad about the game." Answer: I told my dad about the game. Hint: A family word stays lowercase after a word like my.
3. Fix the capitalization: "grandma baked bread this morning." Answer: Grandma baked bread this morning. Hint: A family word is capitalized when it is used as a name.
4. Fix the capitalization: "My grandma baked bread this morning." Answer: My grandma baked bread this morning. Hint: A family word stays lowercase after a word like my.
5. Fix the capitalization: "Can uncle Omar drive us to practice?" Answer: Can Uncle Omar drive us to practice? Hint: A family word is capitalized when it is part of a name.
6. Fix the capitalization: "Can your uncle drive us to practice?" Answer: Can your uncle drive us to practice? Hint: A family word stays lowercase after a word like your.
7. Fix the capitalization: "mom said to wash your hands." Answer: Mom said to wash your hands. Hint: A family word is capitalized when it is used as a name.
8. Fix the capitalization: "His mom said to wash your hands." Answer: His mom said to wash your hands. Hint: A family word stays lowercase after a word like his.
9. Fix the capitalization: "We visited aunt Rosa on Sunday." Answer: We visited Aunt Rosa on Sunday. Hint: A family word is capitalized when it is part of a name.
10. Fix the capitalization: "We visited my aunt on Sunday." Answer: We visited my aunt on Sunday. Hint: A family word stays lowercase after a word like my.
11. Fix the capitalization: "dad, can I borrow your bike?" Answer: Dad, can I borrow your bike? Hint: A family word is capitalized when it is used as a name.
12. Fix the capitalization: "Our dad coaches the team on Saturdays." Answer: Our dad coaches the team on Saturdays. Hint: A family word stays lowercase after a word like our.

## Pathway: Rules of Capitalization II (10 topics)

### Topic: Capitalizing Seasons

See the topic "Capitalizing Seasons" under Rules of Capitalization I above. It is the same topic.

### Topic: Capitalizing Buildings and Schools

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The names of specific buildings and schools are capitalized. Every important word in the name takes a capital letter.
- Worked example: "we met at the cedar public library on main street." The first word, the building name, and the street name need capitals, so the sentence becomes "We met at the Cedar Public Library on Main Street."
- Explanation: Specific building and school names are proper nouns, so they take capital letters.
- Tip box: "She goes to lincoln middle school." becomes "She goes to Lincoln Middle School." "The show was at the riverside theater." becomes "The show was at the Riverside Theater."

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "We met at the hillside public library." Answer: We met at the Hillside Public Library. Hint: Specific building names are capitalized.
2. Fix the capitalization: "He attends washington high school." Answer: He attends Washington High School. Hint: School names are capitalized.
3. Fix the capitalization: "The concert was at city hall." Answer: The concert was at City Hall. Hint: Specific building names are capitalized.
4. Fix the capitalization: "Our class visited the lakeview science museum." Answer: Our class visited the Lakeview Science Museum. Hint: Specific building names are capitalized.
5. Fix the capitalization: "She studies at riverside elementary school." Answer: She studies at Riverside Elementary School. Hint: School names are capitalized.
6. Fix the capitalization: "The meeting is in the oakdale community center." Answer: The meeting is in the Oakdale Community Center. Hint: Specific building names are capitalized.
7. Fix the capitalization: "They practice at the pine ridge gym." Answer: They practice at the Pine Ridge Gym. Hint: Specific building names are capitalized.
8. Fix the capitalization: "The book fair is at jefferson middle school." Answer: The book fair is at Jefferson Middle School. Hint: School names are capitalized.
9. Fix the capitalization: "We waited at maple grove station." Answer: We waited at Maple Grove Station. Hint: Specific building names are capitalized.
10. Fix the capitalization: "The play opens at the grand theater." Answer: The play opens at the Grand Theater. Hint: Specific building names are capitalized.
11. Fix the capitalization: "Her brother goes to lincoln elementary school." Answer: Her brother goes to Lincoln Elementary School. Hint: School names are capitalized.
12. Fix the capitalization: "The art show is in the millbrook town hall." Answer: The art show is in the Millbrook Town Hall. Hint: Specific building names are capitalized.

### Topic: Capitalizing Names and Social Titles

**Teaching card**
- Definition: People's names are always capitalized, and a social title like Mr., Mrs., Ms., or Dr. is capitalized when it comes before a name.
- Worked example: "mr. lane gave us a quiz." The title mr. comes before the name Lane, so the sentence becomes "Mr. Lane gave us a quiz."
- Explanation: Names and the titles that go with them are proper nouns, so they take capital letters.
- Tip box: "Please thank mrs. Diaz for the books." becomes "Please thank Mrs. Diaz for the books." "Dr. Patel will speak at the assembly." Dr. is capitalized because it comes before a name.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "mr. lane gave us a quiz." Answer: Mr. Lane gave us a quiz. Hint: A social title is capitalized when it comes before a name.
2. Fix the capitalization: "Please thank mrs. diaz for the books." Answer: Please thank Mrs. Diaz for the books. Hint: Names and the titles before them are capitalized.
3. Fix the capitalization: "ms. roberts teaches science." Answer: Ms. Roberts teaches science. Hint: A social title is capitalized when it comes before a name.
4. Fix the capitalization: "dr. patel will speak at the assembly." Answer: Dr. Patel will speak at the assembly. Hint: A social title is capitalized when it comes before a name.
5. Fix the capitalization: "sam and ana met mr. kim at the park." Answer: Sam and Ana met Mr. Kim at the park. Hint: Names and the titles before them are capitalized.
6. Fix the capitalization: "The letter is from mrs. ortiz." Answer: The letter is from Mrs. Ortiz. Hint: Names and the titles before them are capitalized.
7. Fix the capitalization: "miss chen coaches the debate team." Answer: Miss Chen coaches the debate team. Hint: A social title is capitalized when it comes before a name.
8. Fix the capitalization: "We asked dr. lee about the stars." Answer: We asked Dr. Lee about the stars. Hint: A social title is capitalized when it comes before a name.
9. Fix the capitalization: "leo thanked ms. wong for the help." Answer: Leo thanked Ms. Wong for the help. Hint: Names and the titles before them are capitalized.
10. Fix the capitalization: "The principal is mr. grant." Answer: The principal is Mr. Grant. Hint: Names and the titles before them are capitalized.
11. Fix the capitalization: "mrs. alvarez runs the art club." Answer: Mrs. Alvarez runs the art club. Hint: Names and the titles before them are capitalized.
12. Fix the capitalization: "Our guide was dr. samuel reed." Answer: Our guide was Dr. Samuel Reed. Hint: Names and the titles before them are capitalized.

### Topic: Capitalizing Professional Titles

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A professional title is capitalized when it comes before a person's name or when it is used as part of a direct address. It stays lowercase when it simply describes the person's job after the name.
- Worked example: "captain ruiz led the rescue team." The title captain comes before the name Ruiz, so the sentence becomes "Captain Ruiz led the rescue team."
- Explanation: A title used as part of a name is a proper noun, so it takes a capital letter.
- Tip box: "Judge Alvarez will hear the case." Judge is capitalized before a name. "Rosa Alvarez, a judge in our town, will hear the case." Judge stays lowercase because it describes her job.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "captain ruiz led the rescue team." Answer: Captain Ruiz led the rescue team. Hint: A professional title is capitalized when it comes before a name.
2. Fix the capitalization: "We met mayor chen at the fair." Answer: We met Mayor Chen at the fair. Hint: A professional title is capitalized when it comes before a name.
3. Fix the capitalization: "Rosa Alvarez, a judge in our town, will hear the case." Answer: Rosa Alvarez, a judge in our town, will hear the case. Hint: A title stays lowercase when it describes a job after a name.
4. Fix the capitalization: "professor kim teaches history." Answer: Professor Kim teaches history. Hint: A professional title is capitalized when it comes before a name.
5. Fix the capitalization: "The nurse, ana lee, checked my ankle." Answer: The nurse, Ana Lee, checked my ankle. Hint: Names are always capitalized, but a job word that only describes a person stays lowercase.
6. Fix the capitalization: "principal grant opened the school assembly." Answer: Principal Grant opened the school assembly. Hint: A professional title is capitalized when it comes before a name.
7. Fix the capitalization: "Coach martin runs practice every tuesday." Answer: Coach Martin runs practice every Tuesday. Hint: A professional title is capitalized when it comes before a name, and days of the week are always capitalized.
8. Fix the capitalization: "Sam Reed is the coach of our team." Answer: Sam Reed is the coach of our team. Hint: A job word stays lowercase when it describes a person.
9. Fix the capitalization: "senator park visited our school." Answer: Senator Park visited our school. Hint: A professional title is capitalized when it comes before a name.
10. Fix the capitalization: "The mayor of our town is dana cole." Answer: The mayor of our town is Dana Cole. Hint: A job word stays lowercase when it describes a person.
11. Fix the capitalization: "governor watts signed the new law." Answer: Governor Watts signed the new law. Hint: A professional title is capitalized when it comes before a name.
12. Fix the capitalization: "Our teacher, mr. lane, loves science." Answer: Our teacher, Mr. Lane, loves science. Hint: A job word stays lowercase when it describes a person, but a title before a name is capitalized.

### Topic: Capitalizing Family Titles

See the topic "Capitalizing Family Titles" under Rules of Capitalization I above. It is the same topic.

### Topic: Capitalizing Political Groups

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The names of political parties and political groups are capitalized. Every important word in the group name takes a capital letter.
- Worked example: "the democratic party held a meeting." The first word and the group name need capitals, so the sentence becomes "The Democratic Party held a meeting."
- Explanation: Names of political groups are proper nouns, so they take capital letters.
- Tip box: "She joined the young voters club." becomes "She joined the Young Voters Club." "The green party supports clean air." becomes "The Green Party supports clean air."

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "The democratic party held a meeting." Answer: The Democratic Party held a meeting. Hint: Names of political groups are capitalized.
2. Fix the capitalization: "He supports the republican party." Answer: He supports the Republican Party. Hint: Names of political groups are capitalized.
3. Fix the capitalization: "The green party supports clean air." Answer: The Green Party supports clean air. Hint: Names of political groups are capitalized.
4. Fix the capitalization: "She joined the young voters club." Answer: She joined the Young Voters Club. Hint: Names of political groups are capitalized.
5. Fix the capitalization: "The riverside student council met after school." Answer: The Riverside Student Council met after school. Hint: Names of organized groups are capitalized.
6. Fix the capitalization: "The libertarian party ran a candidate." Answer: The Libertarian Party ran a candidate. Hint: Names of political groups are capitalized.
7. Fix the capitalization: "Our town has the hillside democratic club." Answer: Our town has the Hillside Democratic Club. Hint: Names of political groups are capitalized.
8. Fix the capitalization: "The oak park debate society discussed the election." Answer: The Oak Park Debate Society discussed the election. Hint: Names of organized groups are capitalized.
9. Fix the capitalization: "Members of the republican party spoke first." Answer: Members of the Republican Party spoke first. Hint: Names of political groups are capitalized.
10. Fix the capitalization: "The lakeview youth council plans the fair." Answer: The Lakeview Youth Council plans the fair. Hint: Names of organized groups are capitalized.
11. Fix the capitalization: "The green party candidate won the race." Answer: The Green Party candidate won the race. Hint: Names of political groups are capitalized.
12. Fix the capitalization: "She volunteers for the democratic party office." Answer: She volunteers for the Democratic Party office. Hint: Names of political groups are capitalized.

### Topic: Capitalizing Brand and Product Names

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Brand names and product names are capitalized. Every important word in a brand or product name takes a capital letter.
- Worked example: "we bought a new bicycle from pedal works." The first word and the brand name need capitals, so the sentence becomes "We bought a new bicycle from Pedal Works."
- Explanation: Brand and product names are proper nouns, so they take capital letters.
- Tip box: "He reads the morning gazette every day." becomes "He reads the Morning Gazette every day." "We packed a lunchbox from lunch pals." becomes "We packed a lunchbox from Lunch Pals."

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "We bought a bike from pedal works." Answer: We bought a bike from Pedal Works. Hint: Brand names are capitalized.
2. Fix the capitalization: "She drinks sunny side orange juice." Answer: She drinks Sunny Side orange juice. Hint: Brand names are capitalized.
3. Fix the capitalization: "The game is called dragon valley." Answer: The game is called Dragon Valley. Hint: Product names are capitalized.
4. Fix the capitalization: "We read the morning gazette." Answer: We read the Morning Gazette. Hint: Brand names are capitalized.
5. Fix the capitalization: "He wears trail runner shoes." Answer: He wears Trail Runner shoes. Hint: Brand names are capitalized.
6. Fix the capitalization: "The cereal is called oat moons." Answer: The cereal is called Oat Moons. Hint: Product names are capitalized.
7. Fix the capitalization: "Our team uses star light notebooks." Answer: Our team uses Star Light notebooks. Hint: Brand names are capitalized.
8. Fix the capitalization: "She plays the game castle builders." Answer: She plays the game Castle Builders. Hint: Product names are capitalized.
9. Fix the capitalization: "We drove a silver star van." Answer: We drove a Silver Star van. Hint: Brand names are capitalized.
10. Fix the capitalization: "The book is from bright path books." Answer: The book is from Bright Path Books. Hint: Brand names are capitalized.
11. Fix the capitalization: "He collects mountain peak cards." Answer: He collects Mountain Peak cards. Hint: Product names are capitalized.
12. Fix the capitalization: "We ate lunch at the golden ladle cafe." Answer: We ate lunch at the Golden Ladle Cafe. Hint: Brand and business names are capitalized.

### Topic: Capitalizing Things in Space

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The names of planets, stars, galaxies, and other specific things in space are capitalized. Earth is capitalized when it names our planet. The everyday words sun and moon stay lowercase.
- Worked example: "earth orbits the sun." The planet earth needs a capital, but sun stays lowercase, so the sentence becomes "Earth orbits the sun."
- Explanation: Specific things in space are proper nouns, so they take capital letters.
- Tip box: "Mars is called the Red Planet." Mars and its nickname, the Red Planet, are capitalized because they name a specific planet. "We sat in the sun all afternoon." Sun stays lowercase, like moon.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "earth orbits the sun." Answer: Earth orbits the sun. Hint: A planet's name is capitalized, and the everyday word sun stays lowercase.
2. Fix the capitalization: "mars is called the red planet." Answer: Mars is called the Red Planet. Hint: Planet names are capitalized.
3. Fix the capitalization: "the moon rises after sunset." Answer: The moon rises after sunset. Hint: A sentence starts with a capital, and the everyday word moon stays lowercase.
4. Fix the capitalization: "Jupiter is the largest planet." Answer: Jupiter is the largest planet. Hint: Planet names are capitalized.
5. Fix the capitalization: "We sat in the sun all afternoon." Answer: We sat in the sun all afternoon. Hint: Ask whether the word is a name, like Mars, or an everyday word, like sun and moon.
6. Fix the capitalization: "Venus shines brightly in the morning sky." Answer: Venus shines brightly in the morning sky. Hint: Planet names are capitalized.
7. Fix the capitalization: "The milky way is our galaxy." Answer: The Milky Way is our galaxy. Hint: Names of galaxies are capitalized.
8. Fix the capitalization: "Saturn has wide rings." Answer: Saturn has wide rings. Hint: Planet names are capitalized.
