9. Fix the capitalization: "The north star helps travelers find their way." Answer: The North Star helps travelers find their way. Hint: Star names are capitalized.
10. Fix the capitalization: "By the light of the moon, we saw venus." Answer: By the light of the moon, we saw Venus. Hint: Planet names are capitalized, and the everyday word moon stays lowercase.
11. Fix the capitalization: "Neptune is far from the sun." Answer: Neptune is far from the sun. Hint: Planet names are capitalized, and the everyday word sun stays lowercase.
12. Fix the capitalization: "The big dipper is easy to spot." Answer: The Big Dipper is easy to spot. Hint: Names of star groups are capitalized.

### Topic: Capitalizing Academic Subjects

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The names of specific school subjects that come from a language, a place, or a person's name are capitalized, like Spanish, French, and English. General subjects like math, science, and history stay lowercase, unless they are part of a course title.
- Worked example: "i take spanish and math this year." The word I and the language spanish need capitals, but math stays lowercase, so the sentence becomes "I take Spanish and math this year."
- Explanation: Language names are proper nouns, so they take capital letters. General subject names are common nouns.
- Tip box: "Her favorite class is history." History stays lowercase. "Her favorite class is American History." American History is capitalized because it is a course title.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "I take spanish and math this year." Answer: I take Spanish and math this year. Hint: Language names are capitalized, but general subjects are not.
2. Fix the capitalization: "Her favorite class is history." Answer: Her favorite class is history. Hint: General subject names stay lowercase.
3. Fix the capitalization: "We study french on tuesdays." Answer: We study French on Tuesdays. Hint: Language names are capitalized, and so are days of the week.
4. Fix the capitalization: "Science is my best subject." Answer: Science is my best subject. Hint: A general subject is capitalized only when it starts a sentence.
5. Fix the capitalization: "He signed up for german and art." Answer: He signed up for German and art. Hint: Language names are capitalized, but general subjects are not.
6. Fix the capitalization: "Our english class read a novel." Answer: Our English class read a novel. Hint: Language names are capitalized.
7. Fix the capitalization: "She loves math and music." Answer: She loves math and music. Hint: General subject names stay lowercase.
8. Fix the capitalization: "The course is called american history." Answer: The course is called American History. Hint: A course title is capitalized.
9. Fix the capitalization: "We learn latin in the spring." Answer: We learn Latin in the spring. Hint: Language names are capitalized.
10. Fix the capitalization: "Geography is taught in the fall." Answer: Geography is taught in the fall. Hint: A general subject is capitalized only when it starts a sentence.
11. Fix the capitalization: "He takes chinese and physical education." Answer: He takes Chinese and physical education. Hint: Language names are capitalized, but general subjects are not.
12. Fix the capitalization: "Her biology class meets in room twelve." Answer: Her biology class meets in room twelve. Hint: General subject names stay lowercase.

### Topic: Capitalizing Hyphenated Words

**Teaching card**
- Definition: In a sentence, capitalize only the parts of a hyphenated word that are proper nouns or proper adjectives, unless the word starts the sentence. In a title, capitalize both parts of a hyphenated word unless the second part is a small word like "and" or "the."
- Worked example: "The well-known author spoke at school." In a sentence, "well-known" stays lowercase. In the title "The Well-Known Author," both parts are capitalized.
- Explanation: Each part of a hyphenated word follows the same capitalization rules as a word on its own.
- Tip box: "She is a first-rate runner." First-rate stays lowercase in a sentence. "My Brother-in-Law" is a title, so Brother and Law are capitalized.

**Quiz**
1. Fix the capitalization: "The well-known author spoke at school." Answer: The well-known author spoke at school. Hint: A hyphenated word stays lowercase in the middle of a sentence.
2. Fix the capitalization: "She is a first-rate runner." Answer: She is a first-rate runner. Hint: A hyphenated word stays lowercase in the middle of a sentence.
3. Fix the capitalization: "The book is called the well-known author." Answer: The book is called The Well-Known Author. Hint: In a title, both parts of a hyphenated word are capitalized.
4. Fix the capitalization: "He wore a brand-new coat." Answer: He wore a brand-new coat. Hint: A hyphenated word stays lowercase in the middle of a sentence.
5. Fix the capitalization: "Our class read about the mexican-born scientist." Answer: Our class read about the Mexican-born scientist. Hint: A hyphenated word with a proper noun keeps the capital letter.
6. Fix the capitalization: "The story is called my brother-in-law." Answer: The story is called My Brother-in-Law. Hint: In a title, both parts of a hyphenated word are capitalized.
7. Fix the capitalization: "It was a ten-year-old tree." Answer: It was a ten-year-old tree. Hint: A hyphenated word stays lowercase in the middle of a sentence.
8. Fix the capitalization: "She joined a twenty-first-century club." Answer: She joined a twenty-first-century club. Hint: A hyphenated word stays lowercase in the middle of a sentence.
9. Fix the capitalization: "The exhibit is called the state-of-the-art lab." Answer: The exhibit is called The State-of-the-Art Lab. Hint: In a title, both parts of a hyphenated word are capitalized, except small words like of and the.
10. Fix the capitalization: "He is a hard-working student." Answer: He is a hard-working student. Hint: A hyphenated word stays lowercase in the middle of a sentence.
11. Fix the capitalization: "The french-speaking guide led the tour." Answer: The French-speaking guide led the tour. Hint: A hyphenated word with a proper noun keeps the capital letter.
12. Fix the capitalization: "The film is called the long-awaited return." Answer: The film is called The Long-Awaited Return. Hint: In a title, both parts of a hyphenated word are capitalized.

## Pathway: Singular vs. Plural Possessives (3 topics)

### Topic: Forming Singular Possessives 1

**Teaching card**
- Definition: To show that one person or thing owns something, add an apostrophe and an s to the end of the singular noun. For example, the dog's bone means the bone belongs to one dog.
- Worked example: "The book belongs to Ana." Ana owns the book, so it becomes "Ana's book."
- Explanation: The apostrophe and s show ownership by one owner.
- Tip box: "The teacher's desk is tidy." One teacher owns the desk. "The cat's tail is fluffy." One cat owns the tail.

**Quiz**
1. Write the possessive: "The bike belongs to Leo." Answer: Leo's bike. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.
2. Write the possessive: "The hat belongs to the girl." Answer: The girl's hat. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.
3. Write the possessive: "The nest belongs to the bird." Answer: The bird's nest. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.
4. Write the possessive: "The car belongs to Mr. Lane." Answer: Mr. Lane's car. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.
5. Write the possessive: "The shoes belong to Sam." Answer: Sam's shoes. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.
6. Write the possessive: "The tail belongs to the dog." Answer: The dog's tail. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.
7. Write the possessive: "The lunch belongs to the boy." Answer: The boy's lunch. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.
8. Write the possessive: "The garden belongs to Mrs. Diaz." Answer: Mrs. Diaz's garden. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.
9. Write the possessive: "The song belongs to the singer." Answer: The singer's song. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.
10. Write the possessive: "The desk belongs to the teacher." Answer: The teacher's desk. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.
11. Write the possessive: "The bone belongs to the puppy." Answer: The puppy's bone. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.
12. Write the possessive: "The kite belongs to Ana." Answer: Ana's kite. Hint: Add an apostrophe and an s to show one owner.

### Topic: Apostrophes with "Others," "Others'," and "Each Other's"

**Teaching card**
- Definition: "Others" with no apostrophe is just a plural word meaning more people or things. "Others'" with an apostrophe after the s shows that a group of others owns something. "Each other's" shows that each person in a pair or group owns one of the things, as in "They traded each other's snacks."
- Worked example: "The others' coats hung by the door." The coats belong to the others, so the apostrophe goes after the s in "others'."
- Explanation: The apostrophe shows ownership. If the owner word already ends in s, add only the apostrophe. "Each other's" always takes an apostrophe before the s.
- Tip box: "The others finished first." No apostrophe, because nothing is owned. "They borrowed each other's pencils." Each other's shows shared ownership.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct form: "The (others / others') books were left on the shelf." Answer: others'. Hint: Look at the word right after the blank. Does it name something the others own?
2. Choose the correct form: "The (others / others') arrived early." Answer: others. Hint: Look at the word right after the blank. Does it name something the others own?
3. Choose the correct form: "They helped (each other / each other's) teams." Answer: each other's. Hint: Look at the word right after the blank. Does it name something each person owns?
4. Choose the correct form: "We respect (each other / each other's) ideas." Answer: each other's. Hint: Look at the word right after the blank. Does it name something each person owns?
5. Choose the correct form: "The (others / others') lunches were in the fridge." Answer: others'. Hint: Look at the word right after the blank. Does it name something the others own?
6. Choose the correct form: "Some (others / others') stayed to help clean up." Answer: others. Hint: Look at the word right after the blank. Does it name something the others own?
7. Choose the correct form: "The two friends borrowed (each other / each other's) bikes." Answer: each other's. Hint: Look at the word right after the blank. Does it name something each person owns?
8. Choose the correct form: "The (others / others') voices were loud in the hall." Answer: others'. Hint: Look at the word right after the blank. Does it name something the others own?
9. Choose the correct form: "The students cheered (each other / each other's) on." Answer: each other. Hint: Look at what comes right after the blank. Is something being owned, or is the phrase part of an action?
10. Choose the correct form: "The (others / others') names were on the list." Answer: others'. Hint: Look at the word right after the blank. Does it name something the others own?
11. Choose the correct form: "They read (each other / each other's) stories aloud." Answer: each other's. Hint: Look at the word right after the blank. Does it name something each person owns?
12. Choose the correct form: "A few (others / others') joined the game later." Answer: others. Hint: Look at the word right after the blank. Does it name something the others own?

### Topic: Forming Possessives from Regular Plurals

**Teaching card**
- Definition: To show that more than one owner owns something, first make the noun plural, then add an apostrophe after the s. For example, the dogs' bones means the bones belong to more than one dog.
- Worked example: "The books belong to the students." The owners are the students, so it becomes "the students' books."
- Explanation: Make the noun plural first, then add the apostrophe after the s to show shared ownership.
- Tip box: "The teachers' lounge is quiet." More than one teacher shares the lounge. "The girls' team won the game." More than one girl is on the team.

**Quiz**
1. Write the possessive: "The books belong to the students." Answer: the students' books. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.
2. Write the possessive: "The bones belong to the dogs." Answer: the dogs' bones. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.
3. Write the possessive: "The hats belong to the boys." Answer: the boys' hats. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.
4. Write the possessive: "The nests belong to the birds." Answer: the birds' nests. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.
5. Write the possessive: "The cars belong to the drivers." Answer: the drivers' cars. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.
6. Write the possessive: "The desks belong to the teachers." Answer: the teachers' desks. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.
7. Write the possessive: "The uniforms belong to the players." Answer: the players' uniforms. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.
8. Write the possessive: "The toys belong to the cats." Answer: the cats' toys. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.
9. Write the possessive: "The lunches belong to the girls." Answer: the girls' lunches. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.
10. Write the possessive: "The instruments belong to the musicians." Answer: the musicians' instruments. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.
11. Write the possessive: "The bikes belong to the kids." Answer: the kids' bikes. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.
12. Write the possessive: "The coats belong to the workers." Answer: the workers' coats. Hint: Make the noun plural first, then add an apostrophe after the s.

## Pathway: Subject-Verb Agreement I (5 topics)

### Topic: Agreement with Simple Subjects

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The verb in a sentence must agree with its subject in number. A singular subject takes a singular verb, and a plural subject takes a plural verb.
- Worked example: "The dog barks at the mail carrier." The subject "dog" is singular, so the verb "barks" is singular. Compare: "The dogs bark at the mail carrier." The subject "dogs" is plural, so the verb "bark" is plural.
- Explanation: Match the verb to the subject. Singular subjects pair with verbs that end in s in the present tense, and plural subjects pair with verbs that do not.
- Tip box: "The bird sings in the morning." One bird, so the verb ends in s. "The birds sing in the morning." More than one bird, so the verb has no s.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct verb: "The dog (bark / barks) at the mail carrier." Answer: barks. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.
2. Choose the correct verb: "The puppies next door (bark / barks) at every car." Answer: bark. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.
3. Choose the correct verb: "The bird (sing / sings) in the morning." Answer: sings. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.
4. Choose the correct verb: "The geese by the pond (honk / honks) every morning." Answer: honk. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.
5. Choose the correct verb: "The student (write / writes) in a journal." Answer: writes. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.
6. Choose the correct verb: "The students in Room 12 (write / writes) poems every Friday." Answer: write. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.
7. Choose the correct verb: "The river (flow / flows) through the valley." Answer: flows. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.
8. Choose the correct verb: "Three streams (flow / flows) into the lake." Answer: flow. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.
9. Choose the correct verb: "My friend (play / plays) the piano." Answer: plays. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.
10. Choose the correct verb: "My cousins (play / plays) the violin." Answer: play. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.
11. Choose the correct verb: "The cat (sleep / sleeps) on the porch." Answer: sleeps. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.
12. Choose the correct verb: "The kittens (sleep / sleeps) in a basket by the stove." Answer: sleep. Hint: Find the subject and decide whether it names one thing or more than one.

### Topic: Agreement with "Doesn't" and "Don't"

**Teaching card**
- Definition: "Doesn't" is short for "does not" and goes with a singular subject. "Don't" is short for "do not" and goes with a plural subject, or with the words I and you.
- Worked example: "She doesn't like broccoli." The subject "she" is singular, so the contraction is "doesn't." Compare: "They don't like broccoli." The subject "they" is plural, so the contraction is "don't."
- Explanation: Match the contraction to the subject. Singular subjects take doesn't, and plural subjects take don't.
- Tip box: "He doesn't want to go." Singular, so doesn't. "We don't want to go." Plural, so don't.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct word: "She (doesn't / don't) like broccoli." Answer: doesn't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.
2. Choose the correct word: "My parents (doesn't / don't) like loud music." Answer: don't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.
3. Choose the correct word: "He (doesn't / don't) want to go." Answer: doesn't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.
4. Choose the correct word: "We (doesn't / don't) have practice today." Answer: don't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.
5. Choose the correct word: "The dog (doesn't / don't) chase cars." Answer: doesn't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.
6. Choose the correct word: "The neighbors' cats (doesn't / don't) come inside at night." Answer: don't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.
7. Choose the correct word: "I (doesn't / don't) know the answer." Answer: don't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.
8. Choose the correct word: "You (doesn't / don't) need a jacket." Answer: don't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.
9. Choose the correct word: "Ana (doesn't / don't) play soccer." Answer: doesn't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.
10. Choose the correct word: "Priya and Tomas (doesn't / don't) ride the bus." Answer: don't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.
11. Choose the correct word: "The rain (doesn't / don't) stop until noon." Answer: doesn't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.
12. Choose the correct word: "The clouds (doesn't / don't) look friendly." Answer: don't. Hint: Find the subject. Doesn't goes with he, she, it, or one person or thing. Don't goes with I, you, or more than one.

### Topic: Agreement with "Here," "There," and Questions

**Teaching card**
- Definition: In a sentence that starts with here or there, the subject comes after the verb, so the verb must agree with the noun that follows it. In a question, the subject also comes after the helping verb, so the same rule applies.
- Worked example: "There is a book on the desk." The subject "book" is singular, so the verb is "is." Compare: "There are books on the desk." The subject "books" is plural, so the verb is "are."
- Explanation: Look past here, there, or the question words to find the real subject, then match the verb to it.
- Tip box: "Here comes the bus." The subject "bus" is singular. "Where are the keys?" The subject "keys" is plural.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct verb: "There (is / are) a book on the desk." Answer: is. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.
2. Choose the correct verb: "There (is / are) three pencils in my bag." Answer: are. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.
3. Choose the correct verb: "Here (comes / come) the bus." Answer: comes. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.
4. Choose the correct verb: "Here (comes / come) the runners now." Answer: come. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.
5. Choose the correct verb: "Where (is / are) the keys?" Answer: are. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.
6. Choose the correct verb: "Where (is / are) my jacket?" Answer: is. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.
7. Choose the correct verb: "There (was / were) a storm last night." Answer: was. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.
8. Choose the correct verb: "There (was / were) puddles all over the playground." Answer: were. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.
9. Choose the correct verb: "Here (is / are) your lunch." Answer: is. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.
10. Choose the correct verb: "Here (is / are) the tickets for the show." Answer: are. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.
11. Choose the correct verb: "What (does / do) the sign say?" Answer: does. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.
12. Choose the correct verb: "Why (does / do) the puppies bark at night?" Answer: do. Hint: Look past here, there, or the question word to find the subject, then decide whether it is singular or plural.

### Topic: Which Part of the Compound Subject Agrees with the Verb?

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A compound subject joins two or more subjects with and. When the parts are joined by and, the subject is plural, so the verb is plural. When the parts name the same single person or thing, the subject stays singular.
- Worked example: "Sam and Ana walk to school together." The subject has two parts joined by and, so the verb "walk" is plural.
- Explanation: Count the parts of the subject. Two different people or things joined by and need a plural verb.
- Tip box: "The cat and the dog sleep by the fire." Two animals, so the verb is plural. "My best friend and teammate is Leo." One person with two titles, so the verb is singular.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct verb: "Sam and Ana (walk / walks) to school together." Answer: walk. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.
2. Choose the correct verb: "The cat and the dog (sleeps / sleep) by the fire." Answer: sleep. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.
3. Choose the correct verb: "My best friend and teammate (is / are) Leo." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.
4. Choose the correct verb: "The rain and the wind (rattles / rattle) the windows." Answer: rattle. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.
5. Choose the correct verb: "Bread and butter (is / are) on the table." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.
6. Choose the correct verb: "The teacher and the students (plans / plan) the trip." Answer: plan. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.
7. Choose the correct verb: "Peanut butter and jelly (is / are) my favorite sandwich." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.
8. Choose the correct verb: "The drums and the guitar (sounds / sound) great together." Answer: sound. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.
9. Choose the correct verb: "My neighbor and friend (lives / live) next door." Answer: lives. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.
10. Choose the correct verb: "The book and the movie (tells / tell) the same story." Answer: tell. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.
11. Choose the correct verb: "Macaroni and cheese (are / is) on the menu." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.
12. Choose the correct verb: "The coach and the players (meet / meets) after school." Answer: meet. Hint: Ask whether the parts joined by and name different things or one single thing.

### Topic: Agreement with "Or" and "Nor" Compounds

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When two subjects are joined by or or nor, the verb agrees with the subject closest to it. This is the rule of the nearer subject.
- Worked example: "Either the dog or the cats are in the yard." The subject closest to the verb is "cats," which is plural, so the verb is "are."
- Explanation: Look at the part of the subject nearest the verb and match the verb to that part.
- Tip box: "Neither the players nor the coach was late." The nearer subject "coach" is singular, so the verb is "was." "Neither the coach nor the players were late." The nearer subject "players" is plural, so the verb is "were."

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct verb: "Either the dog or the cats (is / are) in the yard." Answer: are. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.
2. Choose the correct verb: "Either the twins or their brother (is / are) bringing snacks." Answer: is. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.
3. Choose the correct verb: "Neither the players nor the coach (was / were) late." Answer: was. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.
4. Choose the correct verb: "Neither the teacher nor the students (was / were) ready for the fire drill." Answer: were. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.
5. Choose the correct verb: "Sam or his friends (is / are) coming to the game." Answer: are. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.
6. Choose the correct verb: "Pizza or tacos (is / are) on the menu tonight." Answer: are. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.
7. Choose the correct verb: "Neither the rain nor the snow (stops / stop) the mail." Answer: stops. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.
8. Choose the correct verb: "Neither the lights nor the radio (works / work) after the storm." Answer: works. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.
9. Choose the correct verb: "Either the red shirt or the blue shirts (fits / fit) you." Answer: fit. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.
10. Choose the correct verb: "Either the pencils or the marker (belongs / belong) in this box." Answer: belongs. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.
11. Choose the correct verb: "Neither Ana nor her sisters (has / have) seen the film." Answer: have. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.
12. Choose the correct verb: "Neither the coach nor the players (has / have) seen the new field." Answer: have. Hint: Find the part of the subject closest to the verb, and match the verb to that part.

## Pathway: Subject-Verb Agreement II (6 topics)

### Topic: Which Part of the Collective Noun Subject Agrees with the Verb?

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A collective noun names a group, like team, class, or family. In US English, a collective noun takes a singular verb, because the group acts as one unit. To show the members acting as separate people, make the members the subject, and the verb is plural.
- Worked example: "The team wins the game." The subject "team" is one unit, so the verb "wins" is singular. Compare: "The team members argue among themselves." The subject "members" is plural, so the verb "argue" is plural.
- Explanation: Find the word the verb agrees with: the group itself, or the members of the group. Then match the verb to it.
- Tip box: "The class reads quietly." The class is one unit, so the verb is singular. "The students in the class disagree about the book." The students are many, so the verb is plural.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct verb: "The team (wins / win) the game." Answer: wins. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.
2. Choose the correct verb: "The team members (argues / argue) among themselves." Answer: argue. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.
3. Choose the correct verb: "The class (reads / read) quietly." Answer: reads. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.
4. Choose the correct verb: "The students in the class (disagrees / disagree) about the book." Answer: disagree. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.
5. Choose the correct verb: "The family (eats / eat) dinner together." Answer: eats. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.
6. Choose the correct verb: "The family members (goes / go) their separate ways after lunch." Answer: go. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.
7. Choose the correct verb: "The band (plays / play) on Friday night." Answer: plays. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.
8. Choose the correct verb: "The band members (tunes / tune) their instruments." Answer: tune. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.
9. Choose the correct verb: "The jury (reaches / reach) a verdict." Answer: reaches. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.
10. Choose the correct verb: "The jurors (disagrees / disagree) with each other." Answer: disagree. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.
11. Choose the correct verb: "The crowd (cheers / cheer) for the home team." Answer: cheers. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.
12. Choose the correct verb: "The people in the crowd (scatters / scatter) in every direction." Answer: scatter. Hint: Ask whether the subject is the group as one unit or the members of the group.

### Topic: Agreement with Collective Nouns

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A collective noun names a whole group, such as team, group, flock, or audience. In most sentences the group acts as one unit, so the verb is singular.
- Worked example: "The flock flies south for winter." The flock acts as one unit, so the verb "flies" is singular.
- Explanation: Treat a collective noun as one thing unless the sentence clearly shows the members acting separately.
- Tip box: "The audience claps loudly." One unit, so the verb is singular. "The herd moves across the field." One unit, so the verb is singular.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct verb: "The flock (flies / fly) south for winter." Answer: flies. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.
2. Choose the correct verb: "The audience (clap / claps) loudly." Answer: claps. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.
3. Choose the correct verb: "The herd (moves / move) across the field." Answer: moves. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.
4. Choose the correct verb: "The group (meet / meets) on Fridays." Answer: meets. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.
5. Choose the correct verb: "The choir (sings / sing) at the assembly." Answer: sings. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.
6. Choose the correct verb: "The committee (decides / decide) on the new rules." Answer: decides. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.
7. Choose the correct verb: "The swarm (cover / covers) the flowers." Answer: covers. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.
8. Choose the correct verb: "The pack (runs / run) through the woods." Answer: runs. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.
9. Choose the correct verb: "The staff (work / works) hard every day." Answer: works. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.
10. Choose the correct verb: "The fleet (sails / sail) at dawn." Answer: sails. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.
11. Choose the correct verb: "The class (visits / visit) the museum." Answer: visits. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.
12. Choose the correct verb: "The team (practice / practices) after school." Answer: practices. Hint: A collective noun acting as one unit takes a singular verb.

### Topic: Agreement with Parts of a Whole

**Teaching card**
- Definition: In a phrase like "half of the cake" or "most of the students," the verb agrees with the noun after of, not with the word half or most. That noun is the real subject.
- Worked example: "Half of the cake is gone." The noun after of is "cake," which is singular, so the verb is "is." Compare: "Half of the cookies are gone." The noun after of is "cookies," which is plural, so the verb is "are."
- Explanation: Look at the noun after of and match the verb to it.
- Tip box: "Most of the water has spilled." Water is singular, so the verb is singular. "Most of the students have finished." Students is plural, so the verb is plural.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct verb: "Half of the cake (is / are) gone." Answer: is. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.
2. Choose the correct verb: "Half of the crayons in the box (is / are) broken." Answer: are. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.
3. Choose the correct verb: "Most of the water (has / have) spilled." Answer: has. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.
4. Choose the correct verb: "Most of the students (has / have) finished." Answer: have. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.
5. Choose the correct verb: "Some of the soup (is / are) still warm." Answer: is. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.
6. Choose the correct verb: "Some of the apples (is / are) still green." Answer: are. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.
7. Choose the correct verb: "All of the milk (was / were) used up." Answer: was. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.
8. Choose the correct verb: "All of the chairs (was / were) taken." Answer: were. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.
9. Choose the correct verb: "None of the pie (remains / remain)." Answer: remains. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.
10. Choose the correct verb: "Most of the pies (is / are) still in the oven." Answer: are. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.
11. Choose the correct verb: "Two-thirds of the pizza (was / were) eaten." Answer: was. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.
12. Choose the correct verb: "Two-thirds of the students (was / were) present." Answer: were. Hint: Find the noun after of and decide whether it is singular or plural.

### Topic: Agreement with Math, Money, and Time

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Amounts of money, time, and measurements count as one unit, so they take a singular verb, even when the number looks plural. The same is true for a math result named as one answer.
- Worked example: "Ten dollars is enough for lunch." The amount "ten dollars" is one sum, so the verb "is" is singular.
- Explanation: Ask whether the amount is one unit. If it is, use a singular verb.
- Tip box: "Five miles is a long walk." One distance, so the verb is singular. "Three hours is a long time to wait." One span of time, so the verb is singular.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct verb: "Ten dollars (is / are) enough for lunch." Answer: is. Hint: An amount of money is one unit, so the verb is singular.
2. Choose the correct verb: "Five miles (are / is) a long walk." Answer: is. Hint: A distance is one unit, so the verb is singular.
3. Choose the correct verb: "Three hours (is / are) a long time to wait." Answer: is. Hint: A span of time is one unit, so the verb is singular.
4. Choose the correct verb: "Two plus two (equals / equal) four." Answer: equals. Hint: A math result is one answer, so the verb is singular.
5. Choose the correct verb: "Fifty cents (buy / buys) one pencil." Answer: buys. Hint: An amount of money is one unit, so the verb is singular.
6. Choose the correct verb: "Ten minutes (is / are) plenty of time." Answer: is. Hint: A span of time is one unit, so the verb is singular.
7. Choose the correct verb: "Eight pounds (are / is) a lot to carry." Answer: is. Hint: A weight is one unit, so the verb is singular.
8. Choose the correct verb: "Six weeks (is / are) a long break." Answer: is. Hint: A span of time is one unit, so the verb is singular.
9. Choose the correct verb: "Four gallons (is / are) enough to fill the tank." Answer: is. Hint: A volume is one unit, so the verb is singular.
10. Choose the correct verb: "Twenty dollars (were / was) in the wallet." Answer: was. Hint: An amount of money is one unit, so the verb is singular.
11. Choose the correct verb: "Nine times three (equals / equal) twenty-seven." Answer: equals. Hint: A math result is one answer, so the verb is singular.
12. Choose the correct verb: "Two years (are / is) a long time to wait for a trip." Answer: is. Hint: A span of time is one unit, so the verb is singular.

### Topic: Agreement with Tricky Nouns

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Some nouns look plural but are singular, like news, mathematics, and physics, and some look singular but are plural, like people, children, and police. The verb must agree with the real number of the noun, not with how it looks.
- Worked example: "The news is good today." The word "news" ends in s but names one thing, so the verb "is" is singular.
- Explanation: Learn which tricky nouns are singular and which are plural, and match the verb to the real number.
- Tip box: "Mathematics is my favorite subject." Mathematics is singular. "The children are playing outside." Children is plural.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct verb: "The news (is / are) good today." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.
2. Choose the correct verb: "Mathematics (is / are) my favorite subject." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.
3. Choose the correct verb: "The children (is / are) playing outside." Answer: are. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.
4. Choose the correct verb: "The police (has / have) arrived." Answer: have. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.
5. Choose the correct verb: "Physics (is / are) a hard subject." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.
6. Choose the correct verb: "The people (was / were) happy." Answer: were. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.
7. Choose the correct verb: "Economics (is / are) taught in eighth grade." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.
8. Choose the correct verb: "The scissors (is / are) on the desk." Answer: are. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.
9. Choose the correct verb: "The cattle (grazes / graze) in the field." Answer: graze. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.
10. Choose the correct verb: "The series (is / are) popular with readers." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.
11. Choose the correct verb: "The trousers (is / are) too long." Answer: are. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.
12. Choose the correct verb: "Gymnastics (is / are) her favorite sport." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether the noun really names one thing or more than one, no matter how it looks.

### Topic: Agreement with Indefinite Pronouns

**Teaching card**
- Definition: An indefinite pronoun does not name a specific person or thing. Pronouns like everyone, somebody, and nobody are singular and take a singular verb. Pronouns like both, few, and many are plural and take a plural verb. Pronouns like all, some, and none agree with the noun they refer to.
- Worked example: "Everyone is invited to the fair." The pronoun "everyone" is singular, so the verb "is" is singular.
- Explanation: Learn whether the indefinite pronoun is singular, plural, or flexible, and match the verb to it.
- Tip box: "Somebody left a jacket behind." Somebody is singular. "Many have tried, but few have finished." Many and few are plural.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct verb: "Everyone (is / are) invited to the fair." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.
2. Choose the correct verb: "Somebody (left / leave) a jacket behind." Answer: left. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.
3. Choose the correct verb: "Many (has / have) tried, but few have finished." Answer: have. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.
4. Choose the correct verb: "Few (has / have) finished the puzzle." Answer: have. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.
5. Choose the correct verb: "Nobody (knows / know) the answer yet." Answer: knows. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.
6. Choose the correct verb: "Both of the teams (is / are) ready." Answer: are. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.
7. Choose the correct verb: "Each of the students (has / have) a book." Answer: has. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.
8. Choose the correct verb: "Either of the answers (is / are) correct." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.
9. Choose the correct verb: "All of the cake (was / were) eaten." Answer: was. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.
10. Choose the correct verb: "All of the markers (was / were) dried out." Answer: were. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.
11. Choose the correct verb: "Some of the juice (remains / remain)." Answer: remains. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.
12. Choose the correct verb: "Some of the players (wants / want) a water break." Answer: want. Hint: Decide whether this indefinite pronoun is always singular, always plural, or depends on the noun after of.

## Pathway: Thesis Statements (3 topics)

### Topic: Is the Thesis a Fact or Opinion?

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, Facts. Guiding question: Can the statement be proven true or false? Worked passage: "The school library holds 4,000 books." This is a fact. It can be checked by counting the books.
- Section 2, Opinions. Guiding question: Does the statement say what someone thinks or feels? Worked passage: "The school library is the best room in the building." This is an opinion. It says what the writer thinks, and another person could disagree.
- Section 3, Debatable theses. Guiding question: Can a reasonable person disagree with it? Worked passage: "The school library should stay open later so students have more time to read." This is a debatable thesis. It is an opinion that a reader can argue for or against.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "The river is 200 miles long. The river is the most beautiful place in the state. The state should protect the river from pollution." Ask: Which sentence is the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A thesis is a debatable opinion, not a fact.
2. Passage: "The river is 200 miles long. The river is the most beautiful place in the state. The state should protect the river from pollution." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fact can be proven true or false.
3. Passage: "Our school starts at 8:00. School should start at 9:00 so students can sleep longer. Many students feel tired in the morning." Ask: Which sentence is the thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A thesis is a debatable opinion that a reader can argue for or against.
4. Passage: "Our school starts at 8:00. School should start at 9:00 so students can sleep longer. Many students feel tired in the morning." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fact can be proven true or false.
5. Passage: "The town has three parks. Parks are the greatest places in town. The town should build a fourth park near the river." Ask: Which sentence is an opinion that is not a thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: An opinion states what someone thinks, but a thesis also argues for an action or a claim.
6. Passage: "The town has three parks. Parks are the greatest places in town. The town should build a fourth park near the river." Ask: Which sentence is the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A thesis is a debatable opinion that a reader can argue for or against.
7. Passage: "The museum charges five dollars for entry. The museum is boring. The museum should offer free entry to students." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fact can be proven true or false.
8. Passage: "The museum charges five dollars for entry. The museum is boring. The museum should offer free entry to students." Ask: Which sentence is the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A thesis is a debatable opinion that a reader can argue for or against.
9. Passage: "Dogs need daily exercise. Dogs are the best pets. Every family with a yard should adopt a dog from the shelter." Ask: Which sentence is the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A thesis is a debatable opinion that a reader can argue for or against.
10. Passage: "Dogs need daily exercise. Dogs are the best pets. Every family with a yard should adopt a dog from the shelter." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fact can be proven true or false.
11. Passage: "The bus ride takes twenty minutes. The bus ride is too long. The school should add a second bus route." Ask: Which sentence is an opinion that is not a thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: An opinion states what someone thinks, but a thesis also argues for an action or a claim.
12. Passage: "The bus ride takes twenty minutes. The bus ride is too long. The school should add a second bus route." Ask: Which sentence is the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A thesis is a debatable opinion that a reader can argue for or against.

### Topic: Identifying Vague Words and Phrases 1

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, What vague words are. Guiding question: Does the word give the reader a clear picture? Worked passage: Vague: "The trip was nice." The word "nice" is vague. It does not say what was good about the trip.
- Section 2, How to spot them. Guiding question: Could the word mean many different things? Worked passage: Vague: "She did a lot of stuff at camp." Clear: "She swam, hiked, and cooked at camp." The phrase "a lot of stuff" is vague. It could mean swimming, hiking, cooking, or anything else.
- Section 3, How to replace them. Guiding question: What exact word says what you mean? Worked passage: "The trip was exciting because we climbed a tall hill and cooked dinner over a fire." The exact words replace the vague word "nice" with clear details.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "The game was fun. We scored three goals in the second half. The crowd cheered when the last goal went in." Ask: Which sentence has a vague word? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for a word that does not give the reader a clear picture.
2. Passage: "The game was fun. We scored three goals in the second half. The crowd cheered when the last goal went in." Ask: Which sentence is the most exact? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Exact words give numbers and details.
3. Passage: "He practices every day after school. He is a good player. He can dribble past two defenders." Ask: Which sentence has a vague word? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for a word that could mean many different things.
4. Passage: "He practices every day after school. He is a good player. He can dribble past two defenders." Ask: Which sentence is the most exact? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Exact words give the reader a clear picture.
5. Passage: "The cake was great. It had three layers and chocolate frosting. Everyone asked for a second slice." Ask: Which sentence has a vague word? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for a word that does not say what was good about the cake.
6. Passage: "The cake was great. It had three layers and chocolate frosting. Everyone asked for a second slice." Ask: Which sentence is the most exact? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Exact words give numbers and details.
7. Passage: "They did things at the museum. They watched a film about volcanoes. They built a model of an erupting mountain." Ask: Which sentence has a vague phrase? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for a phrase that could mean almost anything.
8. Passage: "They did things at the museum. They watched a film about volcanoes. They built a model of an erupting mountain." Ask: Which sentence is the most exact? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Exact words give a clear picture of what they did.
9. Passage: "The hero climbed a cliff in the rain. The ending surprised everyone in the theater. The movie was awesome." Ask: Which sentence has a vague word? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Look for a word that does not say what was good about the movie.
10. Passage: "The hero climbed a cliff in the rain. The ending surprised everyone in the theater. The movie was awesome." Ask: Which sentence is the most exact? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Exact words give a clear picture of what happened.
11. Passage: "It was a bad day. The bus was late, and my lunch spilled. I lost my homework on the way home." Ask: Which sentence has a vague word? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for a word that does not say what went wrong.
12. Passage: "It was a bad day. The bus was late, and my lunch spilled. I lost my homework on the way home." Ask: Which sentence is the most exact? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Exact words give details about what happened.

### Topic: Recognizing Redundant, Unnecessary, and Wordy Phrases

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, Redundant phrases. Guiding question: Does one word already say what the other word says? Worked passage: Redundant: "She returned back to school." Fixed: "She returned to school." The words "returned" and "back" say the same thing, so one of them is redundant.
- Section 2, Unnecessary words. Guiding question: Does the sentence keep its meaning without this word? Worked passage: Unnecessary word: "The very unique painting hung in the hall." Fixed: "The unique painting hung in the hall." The word "very" adds nothing, so it is unnecessary.
- Section 3, Wordy phrases. Guiding question: Can a short phrase say the same thing? Worked passage: Wordy: "Due to the fact that it rained, the game was moved inside." Fixed: "Because it rained, the game was moved inside." The short word "because" says the same thing in one word.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "He repeated the question again. The teacher answered it slowly. The class listened quietly." Ask: Which sentence has a redundant phrase? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for two words that say the same thing.
2. Passage: "He repeated the question again. The teacher answered it slowly. The class listened quietly." Ask: Which sentence is the cleanest? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Clean sentences have no extra words.
3. Passage: "They scored in the final minute. The team won the game in the end. The fans cheered loudly." Ask: Which sentence has an unnecessary phrase? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for a phrase that adds nothing to the meaning.
4. Passage: "They scored in the final minute. The team won the game in the end. The fans cheered loudly." Ask: Which sentence is the cleanest? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Clean sentences have no extra words.
5. Passage: "Due to the fact that it snowed, school closed early. The buses left at noon. Students walked home in the snow." Ask: Which sentence is wordy? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for a long phrase that one short word could replace.
6. Passage: "Due to the fact that it snowed, school closed early. The buses left at noon. Students walked home in the snow." Ask: Which sentence is the cleanest? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Clean sentences have no extra words.
7. Passage: "She entered into the room quietly. Her friends waved at her. The teacher smiled." Ask: Which sentence has a redundant phrase? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for two words that say the same thing.
8. Passage: "She entered into the room quietly. Her friends waved at her. The teacher smiled." Ask: Which sentence is the cleanest? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Clean sentences have no extra words.
9. Passage: "The story moves quickly. The ending surprised me. In my opinion, I think the book is great." Ask: Which sentence is wordy? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Look for words that say the same idea twice.
10. Passage: "The story moves quickly. The ending surprised me. In my opinion, I think the book is great." Ask: Which sentence is the cleanest? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Clean sentences have no extra words.
11. Passage: "The very unique painting hung in the hall. Visitors stopped to look at it. The artist signed the corner." Ask: Which sentence has an unnecessary word? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for a word that adds nothing to the word after it.
12. Passage: "The very unique painting hung in the hall. Visitors stopped to look at it. The artist signed the corner." Ask: Which sentence is the cleanest? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Clean sentences have no extra words.

## Pathway: Topic Sentences (3 topics)

### Topic: Recognizing Strong Topic Sentences 1: Claims

**Teaching card**
- Section 1, What a topic sentence does. Guiding question: Does the sentence tell the reader what the paragraph is about? Worked passage: "Recess should be longer for every student." This sentence tells the reader the paragraph will argue for longer recess.
- Section 2, What makes a claim strong. Guiding question: Is the claim clear and debatable? Worked passage: "Longer recess helps students focus, because play gives the brain a rest." This claim is clear, and a reader could argue for or against it.
- Section 3, How a topic sentence links to the thesis. Guiding question: Does the sentence support the main idea of the essay? Worked passage: "If the essay argues that school days should change, a paragraph about longer recess supports that idea, while a paragraph about lunch menus does not."

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "Recess should be longer for every student. Children who play outside concentrate better in class. A longer break gives them time to run and return ready to learn." Ask: Which sentence is the topic sentence? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A topic sentence tells the reader what the paragraph is about.
2. Passage: "Recess should be longer for every student. Children who play outside concentrate better in class. A longer break gives them time to run and return ready to learn." Ask: Which sentence gives evidence for the claim? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact that backs up the claim.
3. Passage: "The library is a quiet place. The library should stay open on Sundays. Many families can only visit on the weekend." Ask: Which sentence is the topic sentence? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A topic sentence states the claim the paragraph will support.
4. Passage: "The library is a quiet place. The library should stay open on Sundays. Many families can only visit on the weekend." Ask: Which sentence is the weakest topic sentence? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A strong topic sentence states a claim that the rest of the paragraph supports.
5. Passage: "Cooking teaches planning and measuring. Every student should learn to cook a simple meal. In our cooking club, students used fractions to double every recipe." Ask: Which sentence is the topic sentence? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A topic sentence states the claim the paragraph will support.
6. Passage: "Cooking teaches planning and measuring. Every student should learn to cook a simple meal. In our cooking club, students used fractions to double every recipe." Ask: Which sentence gives evidence for the claim? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact that backs up the claim.
7. Passage: "The river trip taught us more than any textbook. We measured the water and counted the fish. Doing the work ourselves made the science stick." Ask: Which sentence is the topic sentence? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A topic sentence tells the reader what the paragraph is about.
8. Passage: "The river trip taught us more than any textbook. We measured the water and counted the fish. Doing the work ourselves made the science stick." Ask: Which sentence gives the reasoning? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Reasoning explains how the evidence connects to the claim.
9. Passage: "Some people like rainy days. The town should plant more trees along the river. Trees shade the water and keep it cool for fish." Ask: Which sentence is the topic sentence? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A topic sentence states the claim the paragraph will support.
10. Passage: "Some people like rainy days. The town should plant more trees along the river. Trees shade the water and keep it cool for fish." Ask: Which sentence is the weakest topic sentence? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A weak topic sentence does not match what the rest of the paragraph is about.
11. Passage: "The school garden needs more volunteers. Only four students signed up to help this year. The garden feeds the cafeteria in the fall." Ask: Which sentence is the topic sentence? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A topic sentence tells the reader what the paragraph is about.
12. Passage: "The school garden needs more volunteers. Only four students signed up to help this year. The garden feeds the cafeteria in the fall." Ask: Which sentence gives evidence for the claim? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact that backs up the claim.

### Topic: Identifying Vague Words and Phrases 1

See the topic "Identifying Vague Words and Phrases 1" under Thesis Statements above. It is the same topic.

### Topic: Recognizing Redundant, Unnecessary, and Wordy Phrases

See the topic "Recognizing Redundant, Unnecessary, and Wordy Phrases" under Thesis Statements above. It is the same topic.

## Pathway: Vague Pronouns (2 topics)

### Topic: Identifying Antecedents of a Vague Pronoun

**Teaching card**
- Definition: An antecedent is the noun a pronoun stands for. A pronoun is vague when the reader cannot tell which noun it stands for.
- Worked example: "When Sam talked to Leo, he was upset." The pronoun he is vague. It could stand for Sam or for Leo, and the reader cannot tell which one was upset. Fixed: "When Sam talked to Leo, Leo was upset."
- Explanation: Find every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
- Tip box: "Ana gave Maya her book." Her could mean Ana's book or Maya's book. Fixed: "Ana gave her own book to Maya." "The dog chased the cat, and it ran away." It could mean the dog or the cat. Fixed: "The dog chased the cat until the cat ran away."

**Quiz**
1. Read the sentence: "When Sam talked to Leo, he was upset." Ask: What is the antecedent of he? Answer: It is unclear. It could be Sam or Leo. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
2. Read the sentence: "Ana gave Maya her book." Ask: What is the antecedent of her? Answer: It is unclear. It could be Ana or Maya. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
3. Read the sentence: "The dog chased the cat, and it ran away." Ask: What is the antecedent of it? Answer: It is unclear. It could be the dog or the cat. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
4. Read the sentence: "Leo handed the ball to Sam, and he scored." Ask: What is the antecedent of he? Answer: It is unclear. It could be Leo or Sam. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
5. Read the sentence: "When the teacher spoke to the student, she smiled." Ask: What is the antecedent of she? Answer: It is unclear. It could be the teacher or the student. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
6. Read the sentence: "Maya told Ana that she won the prize." Ask: What is the antecedent of she? Answer: It is unclear. It could be Maya or Ana. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
7. Read the sentence: "The book fell on the table, and it broke." Ask: What is the antecedent of it? Answer: It is unclear. It could be the book or the table. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
8. Read the sentence: "Sam asked Leo if he could borrow his bike." Ask: What is the antecedent of he? Answer: It is unclear. It could be Sam or Leo. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
9. Read the sentence: "The cook tasted the soup, and it was too salty." Ask: What is the antecedent of it? Answer: the soup. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
10. Read the sentence: "Ana read the letter, and she cried." Ask: What is the antecedent of she? Answer: Ana. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
11. Read the sentence: "When the rain started, it was loud." Ask: What is the antecedent of it? Answer: the rain. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.
12. Read the sentence: "Leo met Sam at the park, and they played ball." Ask: What is the antecedent of they? Answer: Leo and Sam. Hint: List every noun the pronoun could stand for. If more than one fits, the pronoun is vague.

### Topic: Correcting Pronouns with Multiple Antecedents

**Teaching card**
- Definition: When a pronoun could stand for more than one noun, fix the sentence by repeating the noun you mean or by rewriting the sentence so only one noun fits.
- Worked example: "When Sam talked to Leo, he was upset." The fix is "When Sam talked to Leo, Sam was upset." Repeating the name makes the meaning clear.
- Explanation: Replace the vague pronoun with the noun it should stand for, or rewrite the sentence so the pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
- Tip box: "Ana gave Maya her book." becomes "Ana gave Maya Ana's book." "The dog chased the cat, and it ran away." becomes "The dog chased the cat, and the cat ran away."

**Quiz**
1. Fix the sentence so it is clear that Sam was upset: "When Sam talked to Leo, he was upset." Answer: When Sam talked to Leo, Sam was upset. Hint: Repeat the noun so the pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
2. Fix the sentence so it is clear that the book belongs to Ana: "Ana gave Maya her book." Answer: Ana gave Maya Ana's book. Hint: Repeat the noun so the pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
3. Fix the sentence so it is clear that the cat ran away: "The dog chased the cat, and it ran away." Answer: The dog chased the cat, and the cat ran away. Hint: Repeat the noun so the pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
4. Fix the sentence so it is clear that Sam scored: "Leo handed the ball to Sam, and he scored." Answer: Leo handed the ball to Sam, and Sam scored. Hint: Repeat the noun so the pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
5. Fix the sentence so it is clear that the student smiled: "When the teacher spoke to the student, she smiled." Answer: When the teacher spoke to the student, the student smiled. Hint: Repeat the noun so the pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
6. Fix the sentence so it is clear that Ana won the prize: "Maya told Ana that she won the prize." Answer: Maya told Ana that Ana won the prize. Hint: Repeat the noun so the pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
7. Fix the sentence so it is clear that the book broke: "The book fell on the table, and it broke." Answer: The book fell on the table, and the book broke. Hint: Repeat the noun so the pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
8. Fix the sentence so it is clear that Sam wants to borrow Leo's bike: "Sam asked Leo if he could borrow his bike." Answer: Sam asked Leo if Sam could borrow Leo's bike. Hint: Repeat the nouns so each pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
9. Fix the sentence so it is clear that Maya was carrying a bag: "When Ana met Maya, she was carrying a bag." Answer: When Ana met Maya, Maya was carrying a bag. Hint: Repeat the noun so the pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
10. Fix the sentence so it is clear that Leo carried the tray: "Leo put the cup on the tray and carried it to the table." Answer: Leo put the cup on the tray and carried the tray to the table. Hint: Repeat the noun so the pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
11. Fix the sentence so it is clear that Leo forgot his own lunch: "Leo told Sam that he forgot his lunch." Answer: Leo told Sam, "I forgot my lunch." Hint: Repeat the noun or rewrite the sentence so each pronoun has only one possible antecedent.
12. Fix the sentence so it is clear that the fence fell over: "The horse kicked the fence, and it fell over." Answer: The horse kicked the fence, and the fence fell over. Hint: Repeat the noun so the pronoun has only one possible antecedent.

## Pathway: Verb Tense I: Simple Tenses (3 topics)

### Topic: Forming the Regular Present Tense

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The regular present tense shows an action that happens now or happens often. For most verbs, use the base form with I, you, we, and they, and add an s or es with he, she, and it.
- Worked example: "Ana walks to school." The verb "walk" takes an s because the subject "Ana" is singular. Compare: "Ana and Leo walk to school." The subject is plural, so the verb stays "walk."
- Explanation: Match the verb ending to the subject. Singular subjects in the third person take an s or es, and all other subjects take the base form.
- Tip box: "He watches the birds." Watch takes es with he. "They watch the birds." Watch stays the base form with they.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct form: "Ana (walk / walks) to school." Answer: walks. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.
2. Choose the correct form: "My brothers (ride / rides) their bikes to school." Answer: ride. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.
3. Choose the correct form: "He (watch / watches) the birds." Answer: watches. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.
4. Choose the correct form: "They (catch / catches) the bus at seven." Answer: catch. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.
5. Choose the correct form: "The dog (bark / barks) at the mail carrier." Answer: barks. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.
6. Choose the correct form: "I (play / plays) soccer on Saturdays." Answer: play. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.
7. Choose the correct form: "She (study / studies) for the test." Answer: studies. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.
8. Choose the correct form: "We (carry / carries) our lunches in paper bags." Answer: carry. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.
9. Choose the correct form: "The class (reads / read) every morning." Answer: reads. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.
10. Choose the correct form: "You (need / needs) a jacket today." Answer: need. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.
11. Choose the correct form: "Leo (fix / fixes) his bike himself." Answer: fixes. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.
12. Choose the correct form: "The birds (sing / sings) at dawn." Answer: sing. Hint: Find the subject, then check whether the verb needs an ending to match it.

### Topic: Forming the Regular Past Tense

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The regular past tense shows an action that already happened. For most verbs, add ed to the base form. If the verb ends in e, add only d. If it ends in a consonant plus y, change the y to i and add ed.
- Worked example: "Ana walked to school yesterday." The verb "walk" takes ed to show the action happened in the past.
- Explanation: Add ed to a regular verb to move it into the past tense.
- Tip box: "They played soccer last week." Play becomes played. "She studied for the test." Study becomes studied.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct form: "Ana (walk / walked) to school yesterday." Answer: walked. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.
2. Choose the correct form: "They (played / play) soccer last week." Answer: played. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.
3. Choose the correct form: "She (study / studied) for the test." Answer: studied. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.
4. Choose the correct form: "He (like / liked) the movie." Answer: liked. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.
5. Choose the correct form: "We (watched / watch) the game on Friday." Answer: watched. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.
6. Choose the correct form: "The dog (bark / barked) all night." Answer: barked. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.
7. Choose the correct form: "Leo (fix / fixed) his bike last month." Answer: fixed. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.
8. Choose the correct form: "They (tried / try) a new recipe last weekend." Answer: tried. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.
9. Choose the correct form: "She (dance / danced) at the show." Answer: danced. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.
10. Choose the correct form: "The class (visit / visited) the museum." Answer: visited. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.
11. Choose the correct form: "Ana (carried / carry) the books home." Answer: carried. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.
12. Choose the correct form: "We (listen / listened) to the story last night." Answer: listened. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happened, then check the spelling at the end of the verb.

### Topic: Forming the Future Tense

**Teaching card**
- Definition: The future tense shows an action that will happen later. Form it with will plus the base form of the verb. It never changes for different subjects.
- Worked example: "Ana will walk to school tomorrow." The words "will walk" show the action happens in the future.
- Explanation: Put will before the base form of the verb to show the future tense.
- Tip box: "They will play soccer on Saturday." Will plus play shows the future. "I will study tonight." Will plus study shows the future.

**Quiz**
1. Choose the correct form: "Ana (will walk / walks) to school tomorrow." Answer: will walk. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.
2. Choose the correct form: "They (played / will play) soccer next Saturday." Answer: will play. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.
3. Choose the correct form: "I (will study / studied) tonight." Answer: will study. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.
4. Choose the correct form: "The class (visited / will visit) the museum next week." Answer: will visit. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.
5. Choose the correct form: "He (will fix / fixed) his bike next weekend." Answer: will fix. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.
6. Choose the correct form: "We (watched / will watch) the game next Friday." Answer: will watch. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.
7. Choose the correct form: "She (will dance / danced) at the show next month." Answer: will dance. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.
8. Choose the correct form: "The dog (will bark / barked) at the mail carrier tomorrow." Answer: will bark. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.
9. Choose the correct form: "Leo and Sam (built / will build) a treehouse next summer." Answer: will build. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.
10. Choose the correct form: "Our teacher (will give / gave) us a quiz next Monday." Answer: will give. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.
11. Choose the correct form: "The birds (will fly / flew) south next fall." Answer: will fly. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.
12. Choose the correct form: "You (needed / will need) a jacket tomorrow." Answer: will need. Hint: Look for words that tell when the action happens.

## Pathway: Verb Tense II: Perfect and Progressive Tenses (5 topics)

### Topic: Identifying Verbs in Simple, Progressive, and Perfect Tenses

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A simple tense shows an action in the past, present, or future with one verb, like walked, walks, or will walk. A progressive tense shows an action in progress with a form of be plus a verb ending in ing, like is walking. A perfect tense shows an action completed before another time, with a form of have plus a past participle, like has walked or has sung.
- Worked example: "Ana is walking to school." The verb "is walking" is progressive, because it shows the action in progress right now.
- Explanation: Look at the helping verb. A form of be plus ing means progressive. A form of have plus a past participle means perfect. One verb alone, or will plus the base form, means simple.
- Tip box: "They walked home." Simple past. "They are walking home." Present progressive. "They have walked home." Present perfect.

**Quiz**
1. Name the tense: "Ana walked to school yesterday." Answer: simple past. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.
2. Name the tense: "Ana is walking to school now." Answer: present progressive. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.
3. Name the tense: "Ana has walked to school every day this week." Answer: present perfect. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.
4. Name the tense: "They will walk to school tomorrow." Answer: simple future. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.
5. Name the tense: "The birds are building a nest now." Answer: present progressive. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.
6. Name the tense: "They have visited the zoo many times." Answer: present perfect. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.
7. Name the tense: "She was singing when I arrived." Answer: past progressive. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.
8. Name the tense: "She had sung the song before." Answer: past perfect. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.
9. Name the tense: "We watch the game every Friday." Answer: simple present. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.
10. Name the tense: "We are watching the game right now." Answer: present progressive. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.
11. Name the tense: "We have watched the game three times." Answer: present perfect. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.
