12. Name the tense: "He will be watching the game tomorrow." Answer: future progressive. Hint: Look at the helping verb, if there is one, and at the form of the main verb.

### Topic: Forming Present Participles

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A present participle is a verb form ending in ing, like walking, reading, or swimming. It is used with a form of be to make a progressive tense.
- Worked example: "Ana is reading a book." The present participle "reading" joins with "is" to show the action in progress.
- Explanation: Add ing to the base form of a verb to make its present participle. Drop a silent e first, and double the last letter for short verbs that end in one vowel plus one consonant, except w, x, or y.
- Tip box: "Make becomes making." Drop the e, then add ing. "Run becomes running." Double the n, then add ing.

**Quiz**
1. Form the present participle: "walk." Answer: walking. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.
2. Form the present participle: "make." Answer: making. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.
3. Form the present participle: "run." Answer: running. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.
4. Form the present participle: "read." Answer: reading. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.
5. Form the present participle: "swim." Answer: swimming. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.
6. Form the present participle: "write." Answer: writing. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.
7. Form the present participle: "play." Answer: playing. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.
8. Form the present participle: "stop." Answer: stopping. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.
9. Form the present participle: "dance." Answer: dancing. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.
10. Form the present participle: "jump." Answer: jumping. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.
11. Form the present participle: "sit." Answer: sitting. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.
12. Form the present participle: "smile." Answer: smiling. Hint: Check how the word ends. A silent e or a single vowel plus a single consonant changes how you add ing.

### Topic: Identifying Progressive Tenses

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A progressive tense shows an action that is, was, or will be in progress. It is formed with a form of be plus a present participle, a verb ending in ing.
- Worked example: "Ana was reading when the phone rang." The verb "was reading" is past progressive, because it shows an action in progress at a time in the past.
- Explanation: Find the form of be and the ing verb. The form of be tells you the time: am or is for now, was or were for the past, will be for the future.
- Tip box: "They are playing soccer." Present progressive. "They were playing soccer." Past progressive. "They will be playing soccer." Future progressive.

**Quiz**
1. Name the tense: "Ana is reading a book." Answer: present progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.
2. Name the tense: "Ana was reading when the phone rang." Answer: past progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.
3. Name the tense: "Our class will be visiting the farm on Friday." Answer: future progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.
4. Name the tense: "They are playing soccer." Answer: present progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.
5. Name the tense: "The kids were building a snowman yesterday." Answer: past progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.
6. Name the tense: "The band will be playing at the fair tomorrow." Answer: future progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.
7. Name the tense: "The dog is barking at the mail carrier." Answer: present progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.
8. Name the tense: "The dog was barking all night." Answer: past progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.
9. Name the tense: "We are studying for the test." Answer: present progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.
10. Name the tense: "We were studying when the bell rang." Answer: past progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.
11. Name the tense: "She will be dancing at the show." Answer: future progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.
12. Name the tense: "He is fixing his bike." Answer: present progressive. Hint: Find the form of be and decide what time it shows.

### Topic: Forming Past Participles

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A past participle is a verb form used with a form of have to make a perfect tense. For regular verbs, it ends in ed, like walked or played. For irregular verbs, it has a special form, like sung, written, or eaten.
- Worked example: "Ana has finished her homework." The past participle "finished" joins with "has" to show the action is complete.
- Explanation: For regular verbs, add ed. For irregular verbs, learn the special form, because it cannot be guessed from the base form.
- Tip box: "They have walked home." Walked is a regular past participle. "They have eaten lunch." Eaten is an irregular past participle.

**Quiz**
1. Form the past participle: "walk." Answer: walked. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.
2. Form the past participle: "play." Answer: played. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.
3. Form the past participle: "sing." Answer: sung. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.
4. Form the past participle: "write." Answer: written. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.
5. Form the past participle: "eat." Answer: eaten. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.
6. Form the past participle: "finish." Answer: finished. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.
7. Form the past participle: "take." Answer: taken. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.
8. Form the past participle: "watch." Answer: watched. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.
9. Form the past participle: "break." Answer: broken. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.
10. Form the past participle: "study." Answer: studied. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.
11. Form the past participle: "choose." Answer: chosen. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.
12. Form the past participle: "jump." Answer: jumped. Hint: Decide whether the verb is regular or irregular, and check the spelling at the end of the word.

### Topic: Identifying Perfect Tenses

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A perfect tense shows an action completed before another time. It is formed with a form of have plus a past participle. Has and have show the present perfect, had shows the past perfect, and will have shows the future perfect.
- Worked example: "Ana had finished her homework before dinner." The verb "had finished" is past perfect, because it shows the homework was complete before dinner.
- Explanation: Find the form of have and the past participle. Has or have means present perfect, had means past perfect, and will have means future perfect.
- Tip box: "They have played three games." Present perfect. "They had played three games before noon." Past perfect. "They will have played three games by Friday." Future perfect.

**Quiz**
1. Name the tense: "Ana has finished her homework." Answer: present perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.
2. Name the tense: "Ana had finished her homework before dinner." Answer: past perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.
3. Name the tense: "The snow will have melted by spring." Answer: future perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.
4. Name the tense: "They have played three games." Answer: present perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.
5. Name the tense: "The bakers had sold every muffin before noon." Answer: past perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.
6. Name the tense: "The workers will have painted the fence by Friday." Answer: future perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.
7. Name the tense: "We have visited the museum twice." Answer: present perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.
8. Name the tense: "We had visited the museum before it closed." Answer: past perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.
9. Name the tense: "She has written two stories." Answer: present perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.
10. Name the tense: "Leo had forgotten his umbrella before the rain started." Answer: past perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.
11. Name the tense: "He will have eaten lunch by one o'clock." Answer: future perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.
12. Name the tense: "The team has won every game this season." Answer: present perfect. Hint: Find the form of have and decide what time it shows.

## Pathway: Verbals (4 topics)

### Topic: Identifying Verbs in Simple, Progressive, and Perfect Tenses

See the topic "Identifying Verbs in Simple, Progressive, and Perfect Tenses" under Verb Tense II: Perfect and Progressive Tenses above. It is the same topic.

### Topic: Is It a Verb or Not a Verb?

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A verb is a word that shows an action or a state of being, and it can be the main verb of a sentence. A verbal looks like a verb but acts as a different part of speech: an infinitive acts as a noun, adjective, or adverb, a participle acts as an adjective, and a gerund acts as a noun. A verbal can never be the main verb of a sentence.
- Worked example: "Swimming is good exercise." The word "swimming" looks like a verb, but it is the subject of the sentence, so it is a gerund, not a verb.
- Explanation: Ask what job the word does. If it is the main verb, it is a verb. If it acts as a noun or adjective, it is a verbal.
- Tip box: "She likes to read." To read is an infinitive, not a verb. "The running water is cold." Running describes water, so it is a participle, not a verb.

**Quiz**
1. Read the sentence: "Swimming is good exercise." Ask: Is swimming a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verbal, a gerund. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?
2. Read the sentence: "She likes to read." Ask: Is to read a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verbal, an infinitive. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?
3. Read the sentence: "The running water is cold." Ask: Is running a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verbal, a participle. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?
4. Read the sentence: "Ana walks to school." Ask: Is walks a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verb. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?
5. Read the sentence: "Reading is my favorite hobby." Ask: Is reading a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verbal, a gerund. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?
6. Read the sentence: "The broken vase lay on the floor." Ask: Is broken a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verbal, a participle. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?
7. Read the sentence: "They want to win the game." Ask: Is to win a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verbal, an infinitive. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?
8. Read the sentence: "The dog barked all night." Ask: Is barked a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verb. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?
9. Read the sentence: "Dancing makes her happy." Ask: Is dancing a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verbal, a gerund. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?
10. Read the sentence: "The smiling teacher greeted the class." Ask: Is smiling a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verbal, a participle. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?
11. Read the sentence: "Leo hopes to travel this summer." Ask: Is to travel a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verbal, an infinitive. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?
12. Read the sentence: "We studied for the test." Ask: Is studied a verb or a verbal? Answer: a verb. Hint: Ask what job the word does here. Is it the main verb, or is it a verbal acting as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb?

### Topic: Identifying Modifiers

**Teaching card**
- Definition: A modifier is a word or phrase that describes another word in a sentence. Adjectives modify nouns, and adverbs modify verbs, adjectives, or other adverbs. A participial phrase can also act as a modifier.
- Worked example: "The tall tree swayed in the wind." The word "tall" is a modifier. It describes the noun "tree."
- Explanation: Ask what the word describes. If it describes a noun, it is an adjective modifier. If it describes a verb, it is an adverb modifier.
- Tip box: "She sang loudly." Loudly modifies sang. "The book on the shelf is mine." On the shelf modifies book.

**Quiz**
1. Read the sentence: "The tall tree shaded the yard." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: tall. Hint: Look for the word that describes the noun tree.
2. Read the sentence: "She sang loudly." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: loudly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how she sang.
3. Read the sentence: "The book on the shelf is mine." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: on the shelf. Hint: Look for the phrase that tells which book.
4. Read the sentence: "The red car won the race." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: red. Hint: Look for the word that describes the noun car.
5. Read the sentence: "He quietly closed the door." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: quietly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how he closed the door.
6. Read the sentence: "The dog in the yard barked." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: in the yard. Hint: Look for the phrase that tells which dog.
7. Read the sentence: "Ana solved the puzzle quickly." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: quickly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Ana solved the puzzle.
8. Read the sentence: "The old bridge crossed the river." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: old. Hint: Look for the word that describes the noun bridge.
9. Read the sentence: "The students in the front row raised their hands." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: in the front row. Hint: Look for the phrase that tells which students.
10. Read the sentence: "Leo threw the ball far." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: far. Hint: Look for the word that tells more about the verb threw.
11. Read the sentence: "The warm soup filled the bowl." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: warm. Hint: Look for the word that describes the noun soup.
12. Read the sentence: "She read the letter carefully." Ask: What is the modifier? Answer: carefully. Hint: Look for the word that tells how she read the letter.

### Topic: Identifying the Function of Verbals

**Teaching card**
- Definition: Every verbal has a job in a sentence. A gerund acts as a noun, so it can be a subject or an object. A participle acts as an adjective, so it describes a noun. An infinitive can act as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb.
- Worked example: "To win takes practice." The infinitive "to win" is the subject of the sentence, so it acts as a noun.
- Explanation: Ask what the verbal does. If it names something, it acts as a noun. If it describes a noun, it acts as an adjective. If it tells why or how, it acts as an adverb.
- Tip box: "Swimming is fun." Swimming is a gerund acting as a subject. "The barking dog woke us." Barking is a participle acting as an adjective.

**Quiz**
1. Read the sentence: "To win takes practice." Ask: What is the function of to win? Answer: noun, subject. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
2. Read the sentence: "Swimming is fun." Ask: What is the function of swimming? Answer: noun, subject. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
3. Read the sentence: "The barking dog woke us." Ask: What is the function of barking? Answer: adjective. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
4. Read the sentence: "She likes to read." Ask: What is the function of to read? Answer: noun, object. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
5. Read the sentence: "The broken vase lay on the floor." Ask: What is the function of broken? Answer: adjective. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
6. Read the sentence: "He came to help." Ask: What is the function of to help? Answer: adverb. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
7. Read the sentence: "Reading builds vocabulary." Ask: What is the function of reading? Answer: noun, subject. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
8. Read the sentence: "The smiling teacher greeted the class." Ask: What is the function of smiling? Answer: adjective. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
9. Read the sentence: "They stopped to rest." Ask: What is the function of to rest? Answer: adverb. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
10. Read the sentence: "Dancing makes her happy." Ask: What is the function of dancing? Answer: noun, subject. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
11. Read the sentence: "The book to read is on the shelf." Ask: What is the function of to read? Answer: adjective. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
12. Read the sentence: "The running water was cold." Ask: What is the function of running? Answer: adjective. Hint: Ask whether the verbal names something like a noun, describes a noun like an adjective, or tells why like an adverb.
# Grammar Lab: Grades 9-12 Lessons

Band: Grades 9-12, plus the ungraded Possessives pathway.
Pathways: 36 sections (35 named 9-12 pathways plus Possessives).
Topics: 126 total (94 written fully, 32 cross-references to shared topics written under their first pathway).
Quiz items: 1,128 (exactly 12 per fully written topic).
All text is original, written fresh for Oomba from the topic names only. US spelling. No em dashes.

Note: the source map header counts 36 pathways in 9-12, but only 35 pathway names are listed in the map. The topic count of 125 verifies exactly, plus the 1 ungraded Possessives topic, for 126 total.

Source: Oomba's Grammar Lab curriculum map.

## Pathway: Active and Passive Voice (4 topics)

### Identifying Active Voice Verbs

**Teaching card**
Definition: In an active voice sentence, the subject performs the action, and the main verb shows what the subject does. The verb is direct and energetic because the doer comes first.
Worked example: "Maya organized the science fair." Subject: Maya. Verb: organized. The verb organized is active because Maya does the organizing.
How it works: Ask who or what does the action. If the subject is the doer, the verb is in the active voice.
Tip box: "Leo repaired the broken telescope." The verb repaired is active because Leo does the repair. "The committee approved the new club." The verb approved is active because the committee does the approving.

**Quiz**
1. "Ana wrote the lab report." Answer: wrote. Hint: Find the verb that shows what the subject does.
2. "The coach praised the team after the game." Answer: praised. Hint: The active verb shows what the subject does.
3. "Sam built a model volcano for class." Answer: built. Hint: Look for the action the subject performs.
4. "The librarian recommended a mystery novel." Answer: recommended. Hint: The subject does the action shown by the verb.
5. "Jordan solved the equation on the board." Answer: solved. Hint: The active verb tells what the subject did.
6. "The gardener planted tomatoes in the spring." Answer: planted. Hint: Find the verb that names the subject's action.
7. "Priya edited the school newspaper article." Answer: edited. Hint: The doer comes first, then the active verb.
8. "The students presented their research to the class." Answer: presented. Hint: Ask what the students did.
9. "Carlos fixed the printer before lunch." Answer: fixed. Hint: The active verb shows the subject doing the work.
10. "The teacher explained the experiment clearly." Answer: explained. Hint: The subject performs the action of the verb.
11. "Nora collected water samples from the river." Answer: collected. Hint: Look for the verb that follows the doer.
12. "The band performed a new song at the assembly." Answer: performed. Hint: The active verb names what the subject did.

### Is the Sentence Active or Passive?

**Teaching card**
Definition: A sentence is active when the subject does the action, and passive when the subject receives the action. A passive sentence often uses a form of be plus a past participle, and the doer may be missing or appear in a by phrase.
Worked example: "The students cleaned the gym." Active. Subject: the students. Verb: cleaned. The students do the cleaning, so the sentence is active.
How it works: Find the subject, then ask whether it does the action or receives it. If it receives the action, the sentence is passive.
Tip box: "The gym was cleaned by the students." Passive, because the gym receives the cleaning. "The principal announced the winners." Active, because the principal does the announcing.

**Quiz**
1. "The chef prepared dinner for the guests." Answer: Active. Hint: Find the subject, then ask whether it does the action or receives it.
2. "The cake was baked by the chef." Answer: Passive. Hint: Ask whether the cake does the baking or has the baking done to it.
3. "Maria painted a mural on the wall." Answer: Active. Hint: Ask whether the subject does the action or receives it.
4. "The volunteers sorted the donated books." Answer: Active. Hint: Find who does the sorting, then check where that doer sits in the sentence.
5. "The committee chose a new theme." Answer: Active. Hint: Ask whether the committee does the choosing or is chosen.
6. "The gym floor was polished over the weekend." Answer: Passive. Hint: Ask whether the floor does the polishing or receives it.
7. "The dog chased the ball across the yard." Answer: Active. Hint: Ask whether the dog does the chasing or is chased.
8. "The test results were posted on Monday." Answer: Passive. Hint: Ask whether the results do the posting or receive it.
9. "The researchers published their findings." Answer: Active. Hint: Find the subject and ask what role it plays in the action.
10. "The concert tickets were sold in one hour." Answer: Passive. Hint: Ask whether the tickets do the selling or receive it.
11. "The storm damaged several houses." Answer: Active. Hint: Ask whether the storm does the damaging or receives it.
12. "The old bridge was repaired by a local crew." Answer: Passive. Hint: Ask whether the bridge does the repairing or has it done to it.

### Creating Active Voice Sentences 1

**Teaching card**
Definition: To create an active voice sentence, put the doer first as the subject, then use a strong verb that shows what the doer does. The result is usually shorter and clearer than the passive version.
Worked example: Passive: "The homework was finished by Ana." Active: "Ana finished the homework." Subject: Ana. Verb: finished. Ana now leads the sentence and does the action.
How it works: Find who does the action, move that person or thing to the front, and change the verb so it shows the action directly.
Tip box: Passive: "The song was written by Leo." Active: "Leo wrote the song." Passive: "The bridge was designed by the engineers." Active: "The engineers designed the bridge."

**Quiz**
1. "The report was written by Sam." Answer: Sam wrote the report. Hint: Put the doer first and use a direct verb.
2. "The cookies were baked by Priya." Answer: Priya baked the cookies. Hint: Move the doer to the front of the sentence.
3. "The window was broken by the baseball." Answer: The baseball broke the window. Hint: The doer becomes the subject.
4. "The awards were given by the principal." Answer: The principal gave the awards. Hint: Use an active verb that shows what the doer does.
5. "The garden was watered by the students." Answer: The students watered the garden. Hint: Find who did the watering and make that the subject.
6. "The experiment was conducted by the scientists." Answer: The scientists conducted the experiment. Hint: Change the passive verb to a direct active verb.
7. "The poster was designed by Carlos." Answer: Carlos designed the poster. Hint: Start with the person who did the work.
8. "The invitation was sent by the club." Answer: The club sent the invitation. Hint: The doer leads an active sentence.
9. "The field was mowed by the caretaker." Answer: The caretaker mowed the field. Hint: Remove the form of be and the by phrase.
10. "The story was told by the elder." Answer: The elder told the story. Hint: Put the doer first, then the action.
11. "The car was washed by Jordan." Answer: Jordan washed the car. Hint: Make the subject the person doing the action.
12. "The decision was made by the council." Answer: The council made the decision. Hint: Use a strong active verb after the doer.

### Creating Emphasis with Voice

**Teaching card**
Definition: Voice can shift emphasis in a sentence. Active voice emphasizes the doer, while passive voice emphasizes the receiver or the action itself, which is useful when the doer is unknown, unimportant, or better left unnamed.
Worked example: "The principal praised the volunteers." Active, emphasis on the principal. "The volunteers were praised by the principal." Passive, emphasis on the volunteers. The same facts appear in both, but the focus changes.
How it works: Choose active voice when the doer matters most, and choose passive voice when the receiver or the result matters most.
Tip box: Active emphasis on doer: "The scientist discovered a new comet." Passive emphasis on result: "A new comet was discovered last night." Both are correct, but each highlights a different part of the news.

**Quiz**
1. "The award was given to the top student." Answer: Passive, emphasis on the award. Hint: Ask which noun comes first and whether it does the action or receives it.
2. "The top student received the award." Answer: Active, emphasis on the student as the doer. Hint: Look at who leads the sentence and what that person does.
3. "The bridge was completed in June." Answer: Passive, emphasis on the bridge and its completion. Hint: Ask whether a doer is named and what the sentence puts first.
4. "Workers completed the bridge in June." Answer: Active, emphasis on the workers. Hint: Find the subject and ask whether it does the work.
5. "Mistakes were made during the project." Answer: Passive, emphasis on the mistakes, doer unnamed. Hint: Ask whether the sentence tells who made the mistakes.
6. "Our team made mistakes during the project." Answer: Active, emphasis on our team as the doer. Hint: Find the subject and ask whether it did the action.
7. "The museum was visited by thousands of students." Answer: Passive, emphasis on the museum. Hint: Find the subject and ask whether it does the visiting or is visited.
8. "Thousands of students visited the museum." Answer: Active, emphasis on the students. Hint: Ask who leads the sentence and what they do.
9. "The new library will be opened in September." Answer: Passive, emphasis on the library opening. Hint: Ask whether the sentence tells who will open the library.
10. "The mayor will open the new library in September." Answer: Active, emphasis on the mayor. Hint: Find the subject and ask whether it performs the opening.
11. "The winner was announced after the final vote." Answer: Passive, emphasis on the winner and the announcement. Hint: Ask what the sentence puts first and whether it names who did the announcing.
12. "The judges announced the winner after the final vote." Answer: Active, emphasis on the judges. Hint: Find the subject and ask whether it does the announcing.

## Pathway: Body Paragraphs: Claims, Evidence, and Reasoning (2 topics)

### Recognizing Strong Topic Sentences 1: Claims

**Teaching card**
Section 1, What makes a claim? Guiding question: Does the sentence state an opinion the writer must prove? Worked passage: "School lunch should include a salad bar every day. Fresh vegetables give students steady energy for afternoon classes. A salad bar would also let students choose foods that fit their needs." The highlighted sentence is "School lunch should include a salad bar every day." It is a claim because it states an opinion that the rest of the paragraph must support.
Section 2, What makes a claim weak? Guiding question: Is the sentence only a fact or a vague feeling? Worked passage: "Many students eat lunch at school. Lunch is important. A salad bar could help." The highlighted sentence is "Lunch is important." It is weak because it is vague and gives the reader no clear position to prove.
Section 3, How does a claim guide the paragraph? Guiding question: Does every later sentence connect back to the claim? Worked passage: "The school should start classes at 9 a.m. Teenagers learn better when they have slept longer. A later start would reduce tardiness and improve focus." The highlighted sentence is "The school should start classes at 9 a.m." It guides the paragraph because each following sentence gives a reason for that later start.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "The town should build a bike path along the river. Cyclists currently share narrow roads with cars. A separate path would make riding safer for everyone." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim is the opinion the writer wants to prove.
2. Passage: "Recycling bins are in every classroom. Our school should compost lunch waste as well. Composting would cut trash and create soil for the garden." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for the opinion that needs reasons and evidence.
3. Passage: "The library needs longer evening hours. Many students work after school and cannot visit during the day. Evening hours would give them time to study and borrow books." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim states what the writer thinks should happen.
4. Passage: "The team practices three times a week. Practice builds skill and teamwork. Therefore, the team should add a fourth practice before the tournament." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A claim can come after background facts.
5. Passage: "Homework should be limited to one hour a night. Long assignments leave students tired and stressed. Shorter work would still review the lesson." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim is the position the rest of the paragraph supports.
6. Passage: "The science fair is held in March. Students should be allowed to work in teams of three. Teams share ideas and finish complex projects more easily." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Find the sentence that states an opinion, not a fact.
7. Passage: "Field trips are fun. The history class should visit the state museum. The museum has exhibits that match the unit on early settlers." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The claim tells the reader what the writer recommends.
8. Passage: "The school garden needs more volunteers. Volunteers water plants and pull weeds. Without help, the garden will not survive the summer." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A claim often states a need or a recommendation.
9. Passage: "Phones distract students during class. Phones should stay in lockers during lessons. Students would focus better without notifications." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The claim is the rule the writer wants to prove is wise.
10. Passage: "The debate club meets on Tuesdays. The club should invite guest speakers. Guest speakers would show students how debate works in real careers." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for the sentence that asks for a change.
11. Passage: "The town pool closes at 6 p.m. It should stay open until 8 p.m. in summer. Evening hours would let working families use the pool." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The claim states the change the writer supports.
12. Passage: "Reading aloud helps young readers. Teachers should read aloud for ten minutes each day. Daily reading builds vocabulary and a love of stories." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The claim is the opinion the evidence must support.

### Identifying Claims, Evidence, and Reasoning

**Teaching card**
Section 1, What is a claim? Guiding question: What opinion is the writer trying to prove? Worked passage: "The school should plant a rain garden. Rain gardens absorb storm water and reduce flooding. They also give science classes a place to study native plants." The highlighted sentence is "The school should plant a rain garden." It is the claim because it is the opinion the paragraph must prove.
Section 2, What is evidence? Guiding question: What fact, example, or detail supports the claim? Worked passage: "The school should plant a rain garden. Rain gardens absorb storm water and reduce flooding. They also give science classes a place to study native plants." The highlighted sentence is "Rain gardens absorb storm water and reduce flooding." It is evidence because it is a fact that supports the claim.
Section 3, What is reasoning? Guiding question: How does the evidence connect back to the claim? Worked passage: "The school should plant a rain garden. Rain gardens absorb storm water and reduce flooding. Because flooding damages the gym floor every spring, a rain garden would protect the building and save repair money." The highlighted sentence is "Because flooding damages the gym floor every spring, a rain garden would protect the building and save repair money." It is reasoning because it explains why the evidence matters for the claim.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "The library should buy more graphic novels. Graphic novels attract reluctant readers. When reluctant readers find books they enjoy, they read more often and build stronger skills." Ask: Which sentence is the evidence? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence is a fact or detail that supports the claim.
2. Passage: "The library should buy more graphic novels. Graphic novels attract reluctant readers. When reluctant readers find books they enjoy, they read more often and build stronger skills." Ask: Which sentence is the reasoning? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Reasoning explains how the evidence supports the claim.
3. Passage: "The cafeteria should offer a vegetarian option daily. Many students do not eat meat for health or family reasons. Offering a daily option would make lunch fair for all students." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim is the opinion the writer wants to prove.
4. Passage: "The cafeteria should offer a vegetarian option daily. Many students do not eat meat for health or family reasons. Offering a daily option would make lunch fair for all students." Ask: Which sentence is the evidence? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence gives a fact that backs up the claim.
5. Passage: "Later bus times would help athletes. Practice ends at 5 p.m., but the last bus leaves at 4:30 p.m. Therefore, athletes without rides miss practice or wait an hour for a parent." Ask: Which sentence is the reasoning? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Reasoning connects the fact to the claim.
6. Passage: "The school should fund a robotics club. Robots teach coding, teamwork, and problem solving. Those skills prepare students for jobs in technology." Ask: Which sentence is the evidence? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence is a supporting fact or example.
7. Passage: "The school should fund a robotics club. Robots teach coding, teamwork, and problem solving. Those skills prepare students for jobs in technology." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim states the opinion to be proved.
8. Passage: "Art classes should meet twice a week. Students who study art score higher on creativity tests. Higher creativity helps students solve problems in science and writing too." Ask: Which sentence is the reasoning? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Reasoning explains why the evidence matters.
9. Passage: "The town should repair the community center roof. The roof leaks into the gym during rain. A dry gym would let youth leagues play through the winter." Ask: Which sentence is the evidence? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Evidence is the fact that supports the claim.
10. Passage: "The town should repair the community center roof. The roof leaks into the gym during rain. A dry gym would let youth leagues play through the winter." Ask: Which sentence is the reasoning? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Reasoning shows the result of the evidence for the claim.
11. Passage: "Students should learn basic first aid. First aid training teaches students to stay calm in an emergency. Calm, trained students can help a classmate while waiting for an adult." Ask: Which sentence is the claim? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The claim is the opinion the paragraph proves.
12. Passage: "Students should learn basic first aid. First aid training teaches students to stay calm in an emergency. Calm, trained students can help a classmate while waiting for an adult." Ask: Which sentence is the reasoning? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Reasoning explains the link between the evidence and the claim.

## Pathway: Body Paragraphs: Embedding Evidence (5 topics)

### Context for Literary Evidence 1: Identifying Speaker, Listener, and Plot Clue

**Teaching card**
Section 1, Who is speaking? Guiding question: Which character says the quoted words? Worked passage: "In the novel, Ana finally tells her brother the truth. She says, 'I hid the letter because I was afraid you would leave.' The line shows Ana admitting her fear." The highlighted sentence is "She says, 'I hid the letter because I was afraid you would leave.'" The speaker is Ana, named just before the quotation.
Section 2, Who is listening? Guiding question: Who hears the words, and why does it matter? Worked passage: "Ana finally tells her brother the truth. She says to Leo, 'I hid the letter because I was afraid you would leave.' Leo listens in silence, and his quiet reaction shows that he understands her fear." The highlighted sentence is "She says to Leo, 'I hid the letter because I was afraid you would leave.'" The listener is Leo, and naming him explains why the confession matters.
Section 3, What is the plot clue? Guiding question: What has just happened in the story that makes the quotation meaningful? Worked passage: "After Leo finds the torn envelope in the attic, Ana finally tells her brother the truth. She says, 'I hid the letter because I was afraid you would leave.' The discovery in the attic forces Ana to confess." The highlighted sentence is "After Leo finds the torn envelope in the attic, Ana finally tells her brother the truth." The plot clue is the discovery of the envelope, which explains the timing of the confession.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "During the storm, Sam warns his sister, 'Do not open the cellar door until the wind stops.' His warning shows that he understands the danger." Ask: Who is the speaker? Answer: Sam. Hint: The speaker is the character who says the quoted words.
2. Passage: "During the storm, Sam warns his sister, 'Do not open the cellar door until the wind stops.' His warning shows that he understands the danger." Ask: Who is the listener? Answer: his sister. Hint: The listener is the person the speaker addresses.
3. Passage: "After the team loses the final game, the coach tells the players, 'You played with courage, and that matters more than the score.' The loss sets up the coach's speech." Ask: What is the plot clue? Answer: the team loses the final game. Hint: A plot clue is what has just happened in the story.
4. Passage: "Mara asks her grandmother, 'Will you teach me to bake the bread you made as a girl?' The question shows Mara wants to keep a family tradition alive." Ask: Who is the speaker? Answer: Mara. Hint: Find the character who speaks the quotation.
5. Passage: "Mara asks her grandmother, 'Will you teach me to bake the bread you made as a girl?' The question shows Mara wants to keep a family tradition alive." Ask: Who is the listener? Answer: her grandmother. Hint: The listener is the person asked the question.
6. Passage: "When the bridge collapses, the mayor says to the crowd, 'We will rebuild it stronger than before.' The collapse explains why the mayor must speak." Ask: What is the plot clue? Answer: the bridge collapses. Hint: Look for the event just before the quotation.
7. Passage: "Leo tells his friend, 'I cannot join the hike because my ankle still hurts.' His excuse reveals that he is still injured." Ask: Who is the speaker? Answer: Leo. Hint: The speaker is named before the quotation.
8. Passage: "Leo tells his friend, 'I cannot join the hike because my ankle still hurts.' His excuse reveals that he is still injured." Ask: Who is the listener? Answer: his friend. Hint: The listener is the person Leo tells.
9. Passage: "After the letter arrives from the university, Ana says, 'I was accepted.' The arrival of the letter explains her excitement." Ask: What is the plot clue? Answer: the letter arrives from the university. Hint: A plot clue gives the context for the quotation.
10. Passage: "The teacher tells the class, 'Put your pencils down and listen carefully.' Her instruction sets the tone for the lesson." Ask: Who is the speaker? Answer: the teacher. Hint: The speaker gives the instruction in the quotation.
11. Passage: "The teacher tells the class, 'Put your pencils down and listen carefully.' Her instruction sets the tone for the lesson." Ask: Who is the listener? Answer: the class. Hint: The listener is the group addressed by the speaker.
12. Passage: "When the lights go out in the cabin, Nora whispers to Sam, 'Stay close to me.' The power failure makes her whisper urgent." Ask: What is the plot clue? Answer: the lights go out in the cabin. Hint: The plot clue is the event that makes the quotation urgent.

### Context for Nonfiction Evidence 1: Evaluating Source Information and Reporting Verbs

**Teaching card**
Section 1, What is the source? Guiding question: Where does the information come from, and is that source reliable? Worked passage: "According to a report from the state health department, students who eat breakfast score higher on morning tests. The health department is a reliable source because it collects data from schools across the state." The highlighted sentence is "According to a report from the state health department, students who eat breakfast score higher on morning tests." The source is named first, so the reader can judge its reliability.
Section 2, What does the reporting verb show? Guiding question: Does the verb suggest certainty, doubt, or neutral reporting? Worked passage: "The researcher argues that later school start times improve attendance. The verb argues shows that this is the researcher's position, not a proven fact." The highlighted sentence is "The researcher argues that later school start times improve attendance." The reporting verb argues signals a position, while a verb like reports would sound more neutral.
Section 3, How does the writer connect source and claim? Guiding question: Does the sentence link the source information clearly to the writer's point? Worked passage: "According to a report from the state health department, students who eat breakfast score higher on morning tests. This finding supports a school breakfast program, because it shows that food before class helps learning." The highlighted sentence is "This finding supports a school breakfast program, because it shows that food before class helps learning." The writer connects the source to the claim by explaining what the finding means.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "According to a study by the city library, teens who join a book club read twice as many books. The study supports funding for more clubs. Reading more builds vocabulary and focus." Ask: Which sentence names the source? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The source is where the information comes from.
2. Passage: "The scientist claims that the river is cleaner than it was ten years ago. Her claim is based on water samples taken each spring. Cleaner water helps fish return to the river." Ask: Which word is the reporting verb? Answer: claims. Hint: A reporting verb shows how the writer presents another person's idea.
3. Passage: "The historian notes that the town was founded in 1840. The town's early mills used river power. That history explains the old mill buildings downtown." Ask: Which word is the reporting verb? Answer: notes. Hint: The reporting verb introduces the source information.
4. Passage: "A survey of parents shows that most support later bus times. The survey gives the school board clear evidence. Later buses would help students who stay for clubs." Ask: Which sentence names the source? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for the phrase that tells where the data comes from.
5. Passage: "The coach suggests that players drink water before practice. Hydration helps players stay alert. Alert players make fewer mistakes." Ask: Which word is the reporting verb? Answer: suggests. Hint: Find the verb that tells how the coach presents the idea.
6. Passage: "Residents should prepare for heavy rain. According to the weather service, the storm will arrive tonight. The warning gives families time to plan." Ask: Which sentence names the source? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for the phrase that tells where the forecast comes from.
7. Passage: "The report states that library visits rose by twenty percent. More visits mean more students are reading. The rise supports longer library hours." Ask: Which word is the reporting verb? Answer: states. Hint: Find the verb that introduces what the report says.
8. Passage: "The doctor warns that too little sleep harms memory. Students who sleep less than seven hours forget more. Schools should teach healthy sleep habits." Ask: Which word is the reporting verb? Answer: warns. Hint: Find the verb that tells how the doctor shares the idea.
9. Passage: "An article in the school paper reports that the garden won an award. The award recognizes student work in science. The garden now attracts visitors from other schools." Ask: Which sentence names the source? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for the sentence that tells where the news was printed.
10. Passage: "The engineer explains that the bridge needs new cables. Old cables weaken over time. Replacing them will keep drivers safe." Ask: Which word is the reporting verb? Answer: explains. Hint: Look for the verb that introduces the engineer's account.
11. Passage: "The survey finds that students prefer group projects. Group work builds communication skills. Teachers should include at least one group project each term." Ask: Which word is the reporting verb? Answer: finds. Hint: Find the verb that presents the survey's result.
12. Passage: "The north trail will close for repairs next week. Hikers should use the south path instead. The park service announced the closure in its spring newsletter." Ask: Which sentence names the source? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Look for the sentence that tells who announced the closure.

### Evaluating Paraphrases for Plagiarism

**Teaching card**
Section 1, What counts as a paraphrase? Guiding question: Does the sentence restate the idea in the writer's own words and sentence shape? Worked passage: "Original source: 'Regular exercise improves memory in teenagers.' Paraphrase: 'Teenagers who exercise often remember information better.' The paraphrase restates the idea with new words and a new sentence shape." The highlighted sentence is "Teenagers who exercise often remember information better." It is a good paraphrase because it changes both wording and structure.
Section 2, What counts as copying? Guiding question: Does the sentence keep the source's words or sentence shape with only small changes? Worked passage: "Original source: 'Regular exercise improves memory in teenagers.' Weak version: 'Regular exercise improves memory in teens.' This version is too close because it swaps only one word and keeps the source's shape." The highlighted sentence is "Regular exercise improves memory in teens." It is too close to the source and needs a fuller rewrite or quotation marks.
Section 3, How do you credit the source? Guiding question: Does the paraphrase name the source so the reader knows where the idea came from? Worked passage: "According to a health study, teenagers who exercise often remember information better. The study gives the writer a fact to support the claim about exercise and learning." The highlighted sentence is "According to a health study, teenagers who exercise often remember information better." It credits the source, so the paraphrase is honest even though the words are the writer's own.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "Original: 'Reading aloud builds vocabulary in young children.' Version: 'Reading aloud builds vocabulary in young kids.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: too close to the source. Hint: Compare the version with the original word by word.
2. Passage: "Original: 'Reading aloud builds vocabulary in young children.' Version: 'When adults read stories out loud, young children learn many new words.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: acceptable. Hint: Ask whether the wording and the sentence shape both changed.
3. Passage: "Original: 'Bees pollinate many of the crops farmers grow.' Version: 'Many farm crops depend on bees to carry pollen from flower to flower.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: acceptable. Hint: Check how many of the original words and how much of its shape remain.
4. Passage: "Original: 'The town library opens at nine each morning.' Version: 'Each morning, the town library opens at nine.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: too close to the source. Hint: Ask whether anything besides word order changed.
5. Passage: "Original: 'Sleep helps the brain store new memories.' Version: 'Sleep helps the brain store new memories for students.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: too close to the source. Hint: Compare the two versions and count what is new.
6. Passage: "Original: 'Volunteers planted trees along the river.' Version: 'Along the river, local volunteers put young trees in the ground.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: acceptable. Hint: Ask whether the wording and the shape are new.
7. Passage: "Original: 'The museum offers free tours on Saturdays.' Version: 'On Saturdays, the museum offers free tours.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: too close to the source. Hint: Look past the word order and compare the actual words.
8. Passage: "Original: 'Regular practice improves a musician's timing.' Version: 'Musicians who practice often develop a steadier sense of rhythm.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: acceptable. Hint: Check whether the idea is restated in new words and a new structure.
9. Passage: "Original: 'Good soil helps gardens grow strong plants.' Version: 'Rich, healthy soil gives garden plants what they need to grow well.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: acceptable. Hint: Compare the wording and sentence shape with the original.
10. Passage: "Original: 'The river flooded the town in 2010.' Version: 'In 2010, the river flooded the town.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: too close to the source. Hint: Check whether the wording changed or only the order.
11. Passage: "Original: 'The coach praised the team for effort.' Version: 'The coach praised the team for their effort.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: too close to the source. Hint: Compare the version with the original and count the changes.
12. Passage: "Original: 'Students learn best when they ask questions.' Version: 'Asking questions helps students understand new ideas more deeply.'" Ask: Is this version acceptable or too close to the source? Answer: acceptable. Hint: Ask whether the version uses new words and a new shape.

### Matching Pronouns and Verbs between Context and Quotations

**Teaching card**
Section 1, Why do pronouns need to match? Guiding question: Does the pronoun in the quotation still make sense inside the writer's sentence? Worked passage: "Ana told her brother, 'I will help you finish the project.' Ana promised that she would help her brother finish the project. The pronoun she matches Ana, so the sentence is clear." The highlighted sentence is "Ana promised that she would help her brother finish the project." The pronoun she refers to Ana, so the context and the quotation agree.
Section 2, Why do verbs need to match? Guiding question: Does the verb tense in the quotation fit the tense of the writer's sentence? Worked passage: "Leo said, 'I am studying for the test.' Leo said that he was studying for the test. The verb was studying matches the past tense reporting verb said." The highlighted sentence is "Leo said that he was studying for the test." The verb tense shifts correctly from present to past.
Section 3, How do you fix a mismatch? Guiding question: Can you change the pronoun or verb so the quotation fits the sentence? Worked passage: "Sam said, 'We won the game.' Sam said that they had won the game. The pronoun they replaces we, and the verb had won fits the past reporting verb." The highlighted sentence is "Sam said that they had won the game." The pronoun and verb both shift to fit the writer's sentence.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "Ana said, 'I am reading a new book.' Ana said that she was reading a new book." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The pronoun and verb must fit the reporting verb said.
2. Passage: "Leo said, 'I am tired.' Leo said that he is tired." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: neither, the verb should be was. Hint: After said, the verb usually shifts to the past tense.
3. Passage: "Sam said, 'We are leaving now.' Sam said that they were leaving then." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Check both the pronoun and the verb in the reported version.
4. Passage: "Priya said, 'I will call you tomorrow.' Priya said that she would call me the next day." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Compare the helping verb in the quotation with the one in the report.
5. Passage: "Carlos said, 'I have finished my work.' Carlos said that he has finished his work." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: neither, the verb should be had finished. Hint: Check whether the helping verb shifted back after said.
6. Passage: "Nora said, 'My brother helps me.' Nora said that her brother helped her." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Compare the pronouns and the verb in both versions.
7. Passage: "Jordan said, 'I can swim well.' Jordan said that he could swim well." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Compare the helping verb in each version.
8. Passage: "Maya said, 'I was late.' Maya said that I was late." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: neither, the pronoun should be she. Hint: Ask who the pronoun in the report should point to.
9. Passage: "The teacher said to the students, 'You must finish by Friday.' The teacher told the students that they must finish by Friday." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Ask who you refers to in the quotation.
10. Passage: "Ana said, 'I love this song.' Ana said that she loved that song." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Compare every pronoun, verb, and pointing word in the two versions.
11. Passage: "Leo said, 'I am going home.' Leo said that he is going home." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: neither, the verb should be was going. Hint: Look at the verb tense in the report and compare it with said.
12. Passage: "Sam said, 'My dog ran away.' Sam said that his dog had run away." Ask: Which sentence correctly matches pronoun and verb? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Compare the pronoun and the verb tense in both versions.

### Formatting In-Text Citations

**Teaching card**
Section 1, What goes in a citation? Guiding question: Does the citation name the source so the reader can find it? Worked passage: "Students who read daily build stronger vocabularies (Garcia 42). The citation names the author Garcia and the page 42, so the reader can find the source in the works cited list." The highlighted sentence is "Students who read daily build stronger vocabularies (Garcia 42)." The citation gives the author and page, with no comma between them.
Section 2, Where does the citation go? Guiding question: Does the citation sit before the final period? Worked passage: "Students who read daily build stronger vocabularies (Garcia 42). The citation comes before the period, so the period closes the whole sentence." The highlighted sentence is "Students who read daily build stronger vocabularies (Garcia 42)." The citation sits inside the sentence, before the period.
Section 3, How do you cite a quotation? Guiding question: Does the citation follow the closing quotation mark? Worked passage: "As one study notes, 'Daily reading builds vocabulary faster than weekly reading' (Garcia 42). The citation follows the closing quotation mark and comes before the period." The highlighted sentence is "As one study notes, 'Daily reading builds vocabulary faster than weekly reading' (Garcia 42)." The citation follows the quotation, so the reader knows which source the words came from.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "Sleep helps memory (Lee 18). Sleep helps memory (Lee, 18). Sleep helps memory. (Lee 18)" Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Put author and page in parentheses with no comma, before the period.
2. Passage: "The river is cleaner now (Patel, p. 7). The river is cleaner now (Patel 7). The river is cleaner now Patel 7." Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Use the author last name and page number only, in parentheses.
3. Passage: "'Reading builds focus (Kim 23).' 'Reading builds focus' Kim 23. 'Reading builds focus' (Kim 23)." Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: The citation follows the closing quotation mark, before the period.
4. Passage: "Exercise improves mood (Reyes, 31). Exercise improves mood (Reyes 31). Exercise improves mood Reyes (31)." Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: No comma goes between author and page.
5. Passage: "The town was founded in 1840. (Diaz 5) The town was founded in 1840 (Diaz, 5). The town was founded in 1840 (Diaz 5)." Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: The citation goes before the final period.
6. Passage: "As one study puts it, 'Practice builds skill' (Chen 12). As one study puts it, 'Practice builds skill (Chen 12).' As one study puts it, 'Practice builds skill' Chen 12." Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The citation follows the quotation mark.
7. Passage: "Volunteers planted 200 trees (Nguyen, 9). Volunteers planted 200 trees (9 Nguyen). Volunteers planted 200 trees (Nguyen 9)." Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Author comes first, then page, with no comma.
8. Passage: "The museum offers free tours (Ali 14.) The museum offers free tours (Ali 14). The museum offers free tours (Ali, p. 14)." Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The period goes after the closing parenthesis.
9. Passage: "'The bridge needs repair' (Ortiz 27). 'The bridge needs repair (Ortiz 27).' 'The bridge needs repair' (Ortiz, 27)." Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The citation follows the closing quotation mark.
10. Passage: "Students who study in groups score higher (Brooks, 19). Students who study in groups score higher (Brooks 19). Students who study in groups score higher Brooks 19." Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Put the citation in parentheses before the period.
11. Passage: "The garden won an award (Frost, 3). The garden won an award. (Frost 3) The garden won an award (Frost 3)." Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: No comma separates author and page.
12. Passage: "As one researcher argues, 'Sleep aids learning' (Walsh 44). As one researcher argues, 'Sleep aids learning (Walsh 44).' As one researcher argues, 'Sleep aids learning' Walsh 44." Ask: Which sentence formats the citation correctly? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The citation comes after the closing quotation mark and before the period.

## Pathway: Body Paragraphs: Transition Words and Phrases (1 topic)

### Using the Appropriate Transition Words and Phrases

**Teaching card**
Section 1, How do transitions add ideas? Guiding question: Does the transition show that another similar idea follows? Worked passage: "The garden gives students fresh food. In addition, it gives science classes a place to study plants and insects. The phrase in addition signals that a second benefit follows the first." The highlighted sentence is "In addition, it gives science classes a place to study plants and insects." The transition in addition adds a related idea.
Section 2, How do transitions show contrast? Guiding question: Does the transition signal a different or opposite idea? Worked passage: "The plan costs little to start. However, it needs volunteers to keep the garden watered through the summer. The word however signals a contrast between low cost and ongoing work." The highlighted sentence is "However, it needs volunteers to keep the garden watered through the summer." The transition however shows a contrast.
Section 3, How do transitions show cause, effect, or order? Guiding question: Does the transition show why something happens or what happens first? Worked passage: "Students water the garden each week. As a result, the plants grow strong and produce food by June. First, the class prepares the soil, and then they plant the seeds." The highlighted sentence is "As a result, the plants grow strong and produce food by June." The transition as a result shows an effect, while first and then show order.

**Quiz**
1. Passage: "The library is quiet. Students can find a table easily. In addition, it has computers for research." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that adds an idea? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Look for words that signal another similar point is coming.
2. Passage: "The bus is cheap. However, it runs only twice a day. Many students still use it." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that shows contrast? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for a word that signals an opposite idea.
3. Passage: "It rained all night. Therefore, the field was too wet to play. The game moved indoors." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that shows effect? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Find the word that signals a result of what came before.
4. Passage: "First, wash the vegetables. Then, chop them for the salad. Finally, add the dressing." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that shows the last step? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Look for the word that signals the end of a sequence.
5. Passage: "The club needs money. For example, it needs paint for the mural. Members plan a bake sale." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that gives an example? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for the phrase that introduces a specific case.
6. Passage: "The book is long. In contrast, the film is only two hours. Both tell the same story." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that shows contrast? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for the phrase that signals a difference.
7. Passage: "The team lost its best player. Everyone expected a hard season. Nevertheless, the team won the next game." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that shows contrast? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Look for a word meaning in spite of what came before.
8. Passage: "The experiment failed. As a result, the students revised their plan. The second trial worked." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that shows effect? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Find the phrase that shows what happened because of the failure.
9. Passage: "The town wants a new park. The council has found land near the school. Moreover, residents want a playground for young children." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that adds an idea? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Look for a word that adds another related point.
10. Passage: "In conclusion, the school should fund the art program. The program builds creativity and confidence. Its small cost brings large rewards." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that concludes? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for the phrase that signals a closing idea.
11. Passage: "The hike was steep. On the other hand, the view from the top was worth it. The group took many photos." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that shows contrast? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for the phrase that introduces an opposite point.
12. Passage: "The class studied the river. Similarly, they studied the forest. Both units taught them about ecosystems." Ask: Which sentence uses a transition that shows similarity? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for the word that shows the next idea matches the last one.

## Pathway: Commas for Clarity (2 topics)

### Commas with Introductory Phrases and Clauses

**Teaching card**
Definition: Use a comma after an introductory phrase or clause at the start of a sentence to separate it from the main clause. The comma tells the reader where the introduction ends and the main idea begins.
Worked example: "After the rain stopped, the team returned to the field." Introductory clause: After the rain stopped. Comma: after stopped. The comma separates the introduction from the main clause.
How it works: Find the introductory words at the start, then place a comma before the subject of the main clause.
Tip box: "Before lunch, the students finished their lab reports." The comma follows the introductory phrase Before lunch. "Running late, Sam grabbed his backpack." The comma follows the introductory phrase Running late.

**Quiz**
1. "After the bell rang the students left the room." Answer: After the bell rang, the students left the room. Hint: Put a comma after the introductory clause.
2. "Before the game the coach gave a short speech." Answer: Before the game, the coach gave a short speech. Hint: A comma follows the introductory phrase.
3. "When the lights went out everyone stayed calm." Answer: When the lights went out, everyone stayed calm. Hint: Separate the introductory clause with a comma.
4. "In the morning the garden looks fresh and green." Answer: In the morning, the garden looks fresh and green. Hint: Put a comma after the opening phrase.
5. "After studying all night Ana felt ready for the test." Answer: After studying all night, Ana felt ready for the test. Hint: The comma comes before the main clause.
6. "During the summer the library offers free workshops." Answer: During the summer, the library offers free workshops. Hint: Use a comma after the introductory phrase.
7. "If it rains tomorrow the picnic will move indoors." Answer: If it rains tomorrow, the picnic will move indoors. Hint: Separate the if clause with a comma.
8. "At the end of the trail hikers find a clear lake." Answer: At the end of the trail, hikers find a clear lake. Hint: Put a comma after the opening phrase.
9. "While the teacher read the class listened quietly." Answer: While the teacher read, the class listened quietly. Hint: A comma follows the introductory clause.
10. "On Saturday morning the market opens at eight." Answer: On Saturday morning, the market opens at eight. Hint: Separate the introductory phrase with a comma.
11. "After the concert ended the crowd cheered for more." Answer: After the concert ended, the crowd cheered for more. Hint: Put a comma after the introductory clause.
12. "Before leaving home Leo checked his bag twice." Answer: Before leaving home, Leo checked his bag twice. Hint: A comma follows the introductory phrase.

### Commas Separating Adjectives

**Teaching card**
Definition: Use commas between coordinate adjectives, which are two or more adjectives that each describe the noun on their own and could be joined by and. Do not use a comma when the adjectives are cumulative and build on each other in a fixed order.
Worked example: "It was a cold, rainy morning." Adjectives: cold, rainy. The comma separates them because each describes the morning on its own, and the sentence could read cold and rainy morning.
How it works: Test the adjectives by swapping their order or putting and between them. If both tests work, use a comma. If they sound wrong, leave the comma out.
Tip box: "She wore a bright red jacket." No comma, because bright red works as a unit and red jacket sounds natural. "The tall, quiet student raised her hand." Comma, because tall and quiet each describe the student alone.

**Quiz**
1. "It was a long difficult hike." Answer: It was a long, difficult hike. Hint: Test whether long and difficult could be joined by and.
2. "She wore a bright blue scarf." Answer: She wore a bright blue scarf. Hint: Ask whether bright describes the scarf or the color blue.
3. "The old wooden bridge crossed the river." Answer: The old wooden bridge crossed the river. Hint: Try reversing old and wooden and see if it still sounds natural.
4. "The small black dog ran across the yard." Answer: The small black dog ran across the yard. Hint: Try putting and between small and black.
5. "He gave a clear honest answer." Answer: He gave a clear, honest answer. Hint: Try saying clear and honest answer.
6. "It was a warm sunny afternoon." Answer: It was a warm, sunny afternoon. Hint: Swap warm and sunny and see if the sentence still works.
7. "They found a heavy iron key." Answer: They found a heavy iron key. Hint: Try saying iron heavy key and listen.
8. "The teacher gave a calm patient reply." Answer: The teacher gave a calm, patient reply. Hint: Test calm and patient with the and test.
9. "She bought three large pizzas." Answer: She bought three large pizzas. Hint: Ask whether and could fit between three and large.
10. "He lives in a white brick house." Answer: He lives in a white brick house. Hint: Try saying brick white house and listen.
11. "The brave loyal dog guarded the house." Answer: The brave, loyal dog guarded the house. Hint: Try reversing brave and loyal.
12. "It was a dark stormy night." Answer: It was a dark, stormy night. Hint: Try the and test with dark and stormy.

## Pathway: Components of a Sentence II (10 topics)

### Part 1, Identifying the Subject of a Sentence

**Teaching card**
Definition: The subject is the word or group of words that tells who or what the sentence is about. It is the part that performs the action or is described by the sentence.
Worked example: "The science club meets every Tuesday." Subject: The science club. The sentence is about the science club, and the club performs the action meets.
How it works: Ask who or what before the verb. The answer is the subject.
Tip box: "Several students volunteered for the event." Subject: Several students. "The old library on Main Street needs repairs." Subject: The old library on Main Street.

**Quiz**
1. "The basketball team won the championship." Answer: The basketball team. Hint: Ask who or what won.
2. "Maria and Leo planted seeds in the garden." Answer: Maria and Leo. Hint: Find who did the planting.
3. "The river flows through the valley." Answer: The river. Hint: Ask what flows.
4. "A group of hikers reached the summit at noon." Answer: A group of hikers. Hint: Find who reached the summit.
5. "The new museum opens next month." Answer: The new museum. Hint: Ask what opens.
6. "Students in the art class painted a mural." Answer: Students in the art class. Hint: Find who painted.
7. "The storm damaged several trees." Answer: The storm. Hint: Ask what damaged the trees.
8. "Her older brother drives her to school." Answer: Her older brother. Hint: Find who drives.
9. "The committee approved the plan." Answer: The committee. Hint: Ask who approved.
10. "Yellow leaves covered the sidewalk." Answer: Yellow leaves. Hint: Ask what covered the sidewalk.
11. "The chef in the small cafe makes fresh bread." Answer: The chef in the small cafe. Hint: Find who makes the bread.
12. "Many volunteers helped at the food drive." Answer: Many volunteers. Hint: Ask who helped.

### Identifying Pronouns as Subjects

**Teaching card**
Definition: A pronoun can act as the subject of a sentence when it stands in for a noun. Subject pronouns include I, you, he, she, it, we, and they, and they must match the noun they replace in number.
Worked example: "Ana and I finished the project. We presented it to the class." Subject pronoun: We. We stands for Ana and I, and it is the subject of presented.
How it works: Replace the noun with a pronoun and check that the pronoun is the subject form, not the object form. We, not us, belongs in the subject spot.
Tip box: "Leo lost his keys, but he found them later." Subject pronoun: he. "The books were heavy, so they stayed on the shelf." Subject pronoun: they.

**Quiz**
1. "Sam and Ana are here. They brought the supplies." Answer: They. Hint: Find the pronoun that stands for Sam and Ana as the subject.
2. "The dog barked loudly. It wanted to go outside." Answer: It. Hint: The pronoun stands for the dog as the subject.
3. "My sister and I cooked dinner. We used a new recipe." Answer: We. Hint: Find the pronoun that stands for my sister and I.
4. "The teacher asked a question. She waited for an answer." Answer: She. Hint: Find the pronoun that stands for the teacher.
5. "The flowers bloomed early. They filled the garden with color." Answer: They. Hint: Find the pronoun that stands for the flowers.
6. "Leo is a good swimmer. Last week, he won the race." Answer: he. Hint: Find the pronoun that stands for Leo.
7. "You and I should study together. We can meet at the library." Answer: We. Hint: Find the pronoun that stands for you and I.
8. "The car would not start. It needed a new battery." Answer: It. Hint: Find the pronoun that stands for the car.
9. "The students finished early, so they left the room quietly." Answer: they. Hint: Find the pronoun that stands for the students.
10. "Nora plays the violin. Every day, she practices for an hour." Answer: she. Hint: Find the pronoun that stands for Nora.
11. "The rain stopped at noon. It left puddles on the road." Answer: It. Hint: Find the pronoun that stands for the rain.
12. "Carlos and I built a birdhouse. We painted it blue." Answer: We. Hint: Find the pronoun that stands for Carlos and I.

### Part 2, Identifying the Verb in a Sentence

**Teaching card**
Definition: The verb is the word that shows the action or state of being in a sentence. It tells what the subject does or what the subject is.
Worked example: "The students studied for the exam." Verb: studied. The verb shows what the students did.
How it works: Find the subject, then ask what the subject does or is. That word is the verb, and it may include a helping verb such as is, has, or will.
Tip box: "The river is calm today." Verb: is. "The team has practiced every day this week." Verb: has practiced.

**Quiz**
1. "The cat slept on the porch." Answer: slept. Hint: Find the word that shows what the cat did.
2. "The students are reading a novel." Answer: are reading. Hint: Look for a helping verb that comes before the main verb.
3. "Maria wrote a poem about the sea." Answer: wrote. Hint: Ask what Maria did.
4. "The bridge was built in 1920." Answer: was built. Hint: The verb has two words, a helping verb and a main verb.
5. "Leo will join the debate team." Answer: will join. Hint: Find the helping verb that shows future time, plus the main verb.
