6. "The rain fell all night." Answer: fell. Hint: Find the action word.
7. "The chef tastes the soup carefully." Answer: tastes. Hint: Ask what the chef does.
8. "We have finished the assignment." Answer: have finished. Hint: Look for a helping verb before the action word.
9. "The garden grows tomatoes and peppers." Answer: grows. Hint: Find the word that shows what the garden does.
10. "The teacher is explaining the lesson." Answer: is explaining. Hint: The verb has two parts. Find both.
11. "The birds sang at dawn." Answer: sang. Hint: Ask what the birds did.
12. "The committee should meet next week." Answer: should meet. Hint: Find the helping verb and the main verb that follows it.

### Part 3, Identifying Verbs and Their Direct Objects

**Teaching card**
Definition: A direct object is the noun or pronoun that receives the action of a transitive verb. It answers the question what or whom after the verb.
Worked example: "Ana read a novel." Verb: read. Direct object: a novel. A novel answers what Ana read.
How it works: Find the verb, then ask what or whom after it. If the answer is a noun or pronoun, that is the direct object. Not every verb has one.
Tip box: "Leo kicked the ball." Verb: kicked. Direct object: the ball. "The teacher praised Maya." Verb: praised. Direct object: Maya.

**Quiz**
1. "Sam baked cookies for the bake sale." Answer: Verb: baked. Direct object: cookies. Hint: Ask what Sam baked.
2. "The librarian shelved the new books." Answer: Verb: shelved. Direct object: the new books. Hint: Ask what the librarian shelved.
3. "Priya painted a portrait of her grandmother." Answer: Verb: painted. Direct object: a portrait of her grandmother. Hint: Ask what Priya painted.
4. "The coach called the team together." Answer: Verb: called. Direct object: the team. Hint: Ask whom the coach called.
5. "Carlos fixed the broken chair." Answer: Verb: fixed. Direct object: the broken chair. Hint: Ask what Carlos fixed.
6. "The students wrote essays about climate change." Answer: Verb: wrote. Direct object: essays about climate change. Hint: Ask what the students wrote.
7. "Nora watered the greenhouse plants." Answer: Verb: watered. Direct object: the greenhouse plants. Hint: Ask what Nora watered.
8. "The scientist tested the water sample." Answer: Verb: tested. Direct object: the water sample. Hint: Ask what the scientist tested.
9. "Jordan threw the ball to first base." Answer: Verb: threw. Direct object: the ball. Hint: Ask what Jordan threw.
10. "The band played a new song." Answer: Verb: played. Direct object: a new song. Hint: Ask what the band played.
11. "The farmer harvested the corn in September." Answer: Verb: harvested. Direct object: the corn. Hint: Ask what the farmer harvested.
12. "Maya thanked her teacher after class." Answer: Verb: thanked. Direct object: her teacher. Hint: Ask whom Maya thanked.

### Part 4, Identifying Prepositions

**Teaching card**
Definition: A preposition is a word that shows a relationship between a noun or pronoun and another word in the sentence, often showing place, time, or direction. Common prepositions include in, on, at, under, over, between, through, after, before, and with.
Worked example: "The book is on the shelf." Preposition: on. The preposition on shows where the book is in relation to the shelf.
How it works: Look for a word that links a noun or pronoun to the rest of the sentence and begins a phrase such as on the shelf or after lunch.
Tip box: "The cat hid under the table." Preposition: under. "We will meet after school." Preposition: after.

**Quiz**
1. "The keys are in the drawer." Answer: in. Hint: Find the word that shows where the keys are.
2. "The bird flew over the house." Answer: over. Hint: Look for the word that shows direction.
3. "We walked through the forest." Answer: through. Hint: Find the word that shows how they moved in relation to the forest.
4. "The meeting starts at noon." Answer: at. Hint: The preposition shows time.
5. "The cat slept under the bed." Answer: under. Hint: Find the word that shows place.
6. "She sat between her two friends." Answer: between. Hint: Look for the word that shows position.
7. "The letter arrived before lunch." Answer: before. Hint: The preposition shows time.
8. "He ran with his dog every morning." Answer: with. Hint: Find the word that links his dog to the action.
9. "The picture hangs on the wall." Answer: on. Hint: The preposition shows where the picture hangs.
10. "They traveled across the country last summer." Answer: across. Hint: Look for the word that shows direction.
11. "The book fell behind the desk." Answer: behind. Hint: Find the word that shows place.
12. "We will talk after the assembly." Answer: after. Hint: The preposition shows when the talk happens.

### Part 5, Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions (FANBOYS)

**Teaching card**
Definition: A coordinating conjunction joins two words, phrases, or clauses of equal importance. The seven coordinating conjunctions are for, and, nor, but, or, yet, and so, remembered by the word FANBOYS.
Worked example: "Sam wanted to play soccer, but it started to rain." Conjunction: but. But joins two clauses of equal importance and shows a contrast.
How it works: Look for a FANBOYS word between two equal parts. When it joins two full clauses, put a comma before it.
Tip box: "Leo studied hard, so he passed the test." Conjunction: so. "Would you like tea or coffee?" Conjunction: or.

**Quiz**
1. "Ana likes apples and oranges." Answer: and. Hint: Find the FANBOYS word that joins two nouns.
2. "It was cold, but the students still went outside." Answer: but. Hint: Look for the word that joins two clauses and shows contrast.
3. "You can walk or ride your bike." Answer: or. Hint: Find the word that joins two choices.
4. "He studied hard, so he earned a good grade." Answer: so. Hint: Look for the word that shows a result between two clauses.
5. "She did not call, nor did she write." Answer: nor. Hint: Find the word that joins two negative ideas.
6. "The team was tired, yet they kept playing." Answer: yet. Hint: Look for the word that shows a surprising contrast between clauses.
7. "We stayed inside, for it was raining." Answer: for. Hint: Find the word that joins two clauses and gives a reason.
8. "The puppy is small but strong." Answer: but. Hint: Find the word that joins two describing words and shows contrast.
9. "Hurry up, or you will miss the bus." Answer: or. Hint: Look for the word that joins two clauses and offers a choice.
10. "The movie was long, but it was interesting." Answer: but. Hint: Find the joining word that shows contrast between the clauses.
11. "She was hungry, so she made a sandwich." Answer: so. Hint: Look for the word that shows a result.
12. "He is shy, yet he gave a strong speech." Answer: yet. Hint: Find the word that shows a surprising contrast.

### Identifying Subordinating Conjunctions

**Teaching card**
Definition: A subordinating conjunction joins a dependent clause to a main clause and shows a relationship such as time, cause, or condition. Common subordinating conjunctions include since, when, although, because, if, while, after, before, unless, and as.
Worked example: "Because it rained, the game was postponed." Conjunction: Because. Because joins the dependent clause Because it rained to the main clause the game was postponed and shows cause.
How it works: Find the word that starts the dependent clause. If the dependent clause comes first, put a comma after it. If it comes second, usually no comma is needed.
Tip box: "Although she was tired, Ana finished her homework." Conjunction: Although. "We will leave when the bus arrives." Conjunction: when.

**Quiz**
1. "Because it snowed, school closed early." Answer: Because. Hint: Find the word that starts the dependent clause and shows cause.
2. "Although it was late, the students kept working." Answer: Although. Hint: Find the word that starts the dependent clause and shows contrast.
3. "We will stay inside if it rains." Answer: if. Hint: Look for the word that starts a clause showing a condition.
4. "When the bell rings, class begins." Answer: When. Hint: Find the word that starts the clause telling when class begins.
5. "Since you are here, we can start." Answer: Since. Hint: Find the word that starts the dependent clause and gives a reason.
6. "She read quietly while the baby slept." Answer: while. Hint: Look for the word that starts the clause about the baby.
7. "Unless you hurry, you will miss the train." Answer: Unless. Hint: Find the word that starts a clause showing a condition.
8. "After the movie ended, we went home." Answer: After. Hint: Look for the word that starts the clause telling when we went home.
9. "He stayed home because he was sick." Answer: because. Hint: Find the word that starts the clause giving a reason.
10. "Before you leave, check your bag." Answer: Before. Hint: Look for the word that starts the dependent clause about leaving.
11. "As the sun set, the sky turned orange." Answer: As. Hint: Find the word that starts the clause telling when the sky changed.
12. "I will help you until you finish." Answer: until. Hint: Look for the word that starts the clause telling how long the help lasts.

### Identifying Conjunctive Adverbs

**Teaching card**
Definition: A conjunctive adverb joins two independent clauses and shows a relationship such as contrast, addition, or result. Common conjunctive adverbs include therefore, however, moreover, consequently, furthermore, nevertheless, otherwise, and meanwhile. They are often set off by a semicolon before and a comma after.
Worked example: "The rain stopped; however, the field was still wet." Conjunctive adverb: however. However joins two independent clauses and shows a contrast.
How it works: Look for a conjunctive adverb between two complete sentences. Use a semicolon before it and a comma after it when it joins two clauses.
Tip box: "The team practiced hard; therefore, they won the game." Conjunctive adverb: therefore. "She was tired; nevertheless, she finished the race." Conjunctive adverb: nevertheless.

**Quiz**
1. "It rained all day; however, the picnic went on." Answer: however. Hint: Find the conjunctive adverb that shows contrast.
2. "The bus was late; therefore, we missed the start." Answer: therefore. Hint: Find the conjunctive adverb that shows a result.
3. "The book is long; moreover, it is difficult." Answer: moreover. Hint: Look for the conjunctive adverb that adds another idea.
4. "She studied every night; consequently, she passed." Answer: consequently. Hint: Find the word after the semicolon that shows a result.
5. "The store was closed; otherwise, we would have bought tickets." Answer: otherwise. Hint: Look for the conjunctive adverb that shows what would happen in a different case.
6. "He was sick; nevertheless, he came to class." Answer: nevertheless. Hint: Find the conjunctive adverb that shows contrast in spite of what came before.
7. "The garden needs water; furthermore, it needs fertilizer." Answer: furthermore. Hint: Look for the word after the semicolon that adds another point.
8. "The power went out; meanwhile, the storm grew worse." Answer: meanwhile. Hint: Find the conjunctive adverb that shows two events happening at the same time.
9. "The plan is risky; however, it could work." Answer: however. Hint: Look for the word after the semicolon that shows contrast.
10. "We saved money; therefore, we could buy new equipment." Answer: therefore. Hint: Find the conjunctive adverb that shows a result.
11. "The trail was steep; nonetheless, the hikers reached the top." Answer: nonetheless. Hint: Look for the word after the semicolon that shows contrast.
12. "The class did not watch a video; instead, the teacher played music." Answer: instead. Hint: Find the conjunctive adverb that shows a replacement or alternative.

### Part 6, Is the Thought Complete or Incomplete?

**Teaching card**
Definition: A complete thought, or independent clause, has a subject and a verb and can stand alone as a sentence. An incomplete thought, or dependent clause, has a subject and a verb but cannot stand alone because it begins with a subordinating word or leaves the idea unfinished.
Worked example: "The students finished the test." Complete. It has a subject and a verb and makes sense alone. "Because the students finished the test" Incomplete. It has a subject and a verb, but because leaves the idea unfinished.
How it works: Read the group of words alone. If it makes sense by itself, it is complete. If it leaves you waiting for more, it is incomplete.
Tip box: "After the bell rang" Incomplete, because after leaves the thought unfinished. "The bell rang." Complete, because it stands alone.

**Quiz**
1. "The dog barked at the mail carrier." Answer: Complete. Hint: Read the words alone and ask whether they make sense by themselves.
2. "Because the dog barked loudly" Answer: Incomplete. Hint: Check the first word and ask whether the idea feels finished.
3. "When the rain stopped" Answer: Incomplete. Hint: Read it alone. Does it leave you waiting for more?
4. "The rain stopped at noon." Answer: Complete. Hint: Ask whether this group of words could stand alone as a sentence.
5. "Although she studied hard" Answer: Incomplete. Hint: Look at the opening word and ask whether something is missing.
6. "She studied hard for the exam." Answer: Complete. Hint: Find the subject and verb, then ask if anything is left unfinished.
7. "If you finish your homework" Answer: Incomplete. Hint: Read it aloud and ask whether the thought is finished.
8. "You finish your homework before dinner." Answer: Complete. Hint: Check whether the words begin with a subordinating word.
9. "After the game ended" Answer: Incomplete. Hint: Notice the first word and ask whether the idea stands alone.
10. "The game ended in a tie." Answer: Complete. Hint: Ask whether a reader would wait for more after this.
11. "While the teacher was talking" Answer: Incomplete. Hint: Look at the opening word, then decide if the thought is finished.
12. "The teacher was talking to the class." Answer: Complete. Hint: Read it alone and ask whether it makes sense by itself.

### Identifying Dependent Clauses

**Teaching card**
Definition: A dependent clause has a subject and a verb but cannot stand alone as a sentence. It begins with a subordinating conjunction such as because, although, when, or if, or with a relative pronoun such as who, which, or that, and it must join to a main clause.
Worked example: "The students who finished early helped their classmates." Dependent clause: who finished early. It has a subject who and a verb finished, but it cannot stand alone. It describes the students.
How it works: Find the clause that starts with a subordinating word or relative pronoun. Check that it has a subject and a verb but does not make sense alone.
Tip box: "Because it rained, we stayed inside." Dependent clause: Because it rained. "The book that Ana recommended was excellent." Dependent clause: that Ana recommended.

**Quiz**
1. "When the bell rings, we go to lunch." Answer: When the bell rings. Hint: Find the clause that tells when and cannot stand alone.
2. "The girl who won the race smiled proudly." Answer: who won the race. Hint: Find the clause that tells which girl.
3. "Because the road was closed, we took another route." Answer: Because the road was closed. Hint: Look for the clause that gives a reason and cannot stand alone.
4. "The house that we visited was built in 1900." Answer: that we visited. Hint: Find the clause that tells which house.
5. "If it snows tomorrow, school will close." Answer: If it snows tomorrow. Hint: Look for the clause that shows a condition.
6. "The teacher, who loves history, told a story." Answer: who loves history. Hint: Find the clause that adds information about the teacher.
7. "Although the movie was long, we enjoyed it." Answer: Although the movie was long. Hint: Look for the clause that shows contrast and cannot stand alone.
8. "The dog that barked all night kept us awake." Answer: that barked all night. Hint: Find the clause that tells which dog.
9. "While the baby slept, the house was quiet." Answer: While the baby slept. Hint: Look for the clause that shows time.
10. "The cake, which Sam baked, was delicious." Answer: which Sam baked. Hint: Find the clause that adds information about the cake.
11. "Since you arrived early, you can help set up." Answer: Since you arrived early. Hint: Look for the clause that gives a reason.
12. "The student whose essay won the prize read it aloud." Answer: whose essay won the prize. Hint: Find the clause that tells which student.

## Pathway: Conclusion Paragraphs (1 topic)

### The Two Jobs of a Conclusion

**Teaching card**

**Section 1: Restate the main idea in fresh words.**
Guiding question: How can the writer remind readers of the claim without copying the thesis word for word?
Worked passage: "Schools should protect one lunch period each week for clubs and quiet study. Students need time during the day to meet, plan, and rest their minds. **A protected lunch period gives students the time they need to learn from each other and recharge.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence restates the claim in new words, so readers leave with the main idea clear in their minds.

**Section 2: Show why the idea matters.**
Guiding question: Why should readers care about this claim after they finish reading?
Worked passage: "A protected lunch period gives students the time they need to learn from each other and recharge. **When students have that time, they return to class calmer, more focused, and more willing to work together.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence explains the larger benefit, so the conclusion feels important rather than repeated.

**Section 3: End with a clear closing thought.**
Guiding question: What final sentence gives the essay a finished feeling?
Worked passage: "When students have that time, they return to class calmer, more focused, and more willing to work together. **Protecting that hour is a small schedule change with a lasting effect on the whole school.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence closes the essay with a firm final thought, so readers are not left waiting for more.

**Quiz**

1. Passage: "Fixing the bike lanes before winter is the city's best choice. Safe lanes would let more people ride to school and work. A few weeks of repair now could protect riders for years to come." Ask: Which sentence restates the claim in fresh words? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The restatement reminds readers of the main idea without copying it exactly.
2. Passage: "Fixing the bike lanes before winter is the city's best choice. Safe lanes would let more people ride to school and work. A few weeks of repair now could protect riders for years to come." Ask: Which sentence shows why the idea matters? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Showing why it matters explains the benefit for readers.
3. Passage: "Fixing the bike lanes before winter is the city's best choice. Safe lanes would let more people ride to school and work. A few weeks of repair now could protect riders for years to come." Ask: Which sentence gives the essay a finished feeling? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A closing thought ends the essay firmly instead of trailing off.
4. Passage: "Keeping libraries open later on school nights would help students succeed. Longer hours would give many students a quiet, safe place to finish homework. One extra hour in the library could change a whole semester." Ask: Which sentence restates the claim in fresh words? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The restatement is the main idea said again in different words.
5. Passage: "Keeping libraries open later on school nights would help students succeed. Longer hours would give many students a quiet, safe place to finish homework. One extra hour in the library could change a whole semester." Ask: Which sentence shows why the idea matters? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The benefit sentence explains what readers gain from the change.
6. Passage: "Keeping libraries open later on school nights would help students succeed. Longer hours would give many students a quiet, safe place to finish homework. One extra hour in the library could change a whole semester." Ask: Which sentence gives the essay a finished feeling? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A closing thought should sound complete and confident.
7. Passage: "A compost program is a simple step our school can take. Compost would turn daily food waste into rich soil for the school garden. What we throw away today could grow tomorrow's lunch." Ask: Which sentence restates the claim in fresh words? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A conclusion restates the claim, it does not introduce a new claim.
8. Passage: "A compost program is a simple step our school can take. Compost would turn daily food waste into rich soil for the school garden. What we throw away today could grow tomorrow's lunch." Ask: Which sentence shows why the idea matters? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: Look for the sentence that names the wider good that follows.
9. Passage: "A compost program is a simple step our school can take. Compost would turn daily food waste into rich soil for the school garden. What we throw away today could grow tomorrow's lunch." Ask: Which sentence gives the essay a finished feeling? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: The closing sentence ties the benefit back to the school.
10. Passage: "Planting more trees along Main Street would make downtown better for everyone. Cooler, shaded sidewalks would invite more people to walk and shop. A row of young trees today is a gift to the town for decades." Ask: Which sentence restates the claim in fresh words? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: The restatement keeps the same claim with fresh wording.
11. Passage: "Planting more trees along Main Street would make downtown better for everyone. Cooler, shaded sidewalks would invite more people to walk and shop. A row of young trees today is a gift to the town for decades." Ask: Which sentence shows why the idea matters? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The importance sentence points to a result beyond the trees themselves.
12. Passage: "Planting more trees along Main Street would make downtown better for everyone. Cooler, shaded sidewalks would invite more people to walk and shop. A row of young trees today is a gift to the town for decades." Ask: Which sentence gives the essay a finished feeling? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A strong ending leaves readers with a clear final image.

## Pathway: Counterargument Paragraphs (1 topic)

### Planning a Counterargument Paragraph

**Teaching card**

**Section 1: Name the other side fairly.**
Guiding question: What is the strongest point someone who disagrees might make?
Worked passage: "Some students say that a later start time would cut into after school practice. **They worry that shorter practices would hurt teams that already have little time to prepare.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence states the opposing view in fair words, so readers trust the writer to take it seriously.

**Section 2: Answer the concern with evidence or reasoning.**
Guiding question: How can the writer respond without dismissing the concern?
Worked passage: "They worry that shorter practices would hurt teams that already have little time to prepare. **Practice could start fifteen minutes earlier on two days, and coaches report that rested players learn plays faster.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence offers a practical answer, so the concern is addressed rather than ignored.

**Section 3: Return to the main claim.**
Guiding question: How does the writer bring readers back to the original point?
Worked passage: "Practice could start fifteen minutes earlier on two days, and coaches report that rested players learn plays faster. **A later start still gives students the sleep they need, and the schedule can adjust around it.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence reconnects the answer to the claim, so the paragraph strengthens the essay instead of wandering away from it.

**Quiz**

1. Passage: "Some neighbors say a new park would bring too much noise. The park plan sets a closing time of eight o'clock and adds a row of trees to block sound. With those limits in place, the park can still give families a safe place to play." Ask: Which sentence names the other side fairly? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Naming the other side states the opposing view in fair words.
2. Passage: "Some neighbors say a new park would bring too much noise. The park plan sets a closing time of eight o'clock and adds a row of trees to block sound. With those limits in place, the park can still give families a safe place to play." Ask: Which sentence answers the concern? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The answer offers a practical response to the worry.
3. Passage: "Some neighbors say a new park would bring too much noise. The park plan sets a closing time of eight o'clock and adds a row of trees to block sound. With those limits in place, the park can still give families a safe place to play." Ask: Which sentence returns to the main claim? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Returning to the claim shows the plan still works after the answer.
4. Passage: "Critics say school uniforms cost families too much. The school plan offers free uniforms to any family that needs them. Because cost is no longer a barrier, uniforms can still help every student focus on learning instead of clothes." Ask: Which sentence names the other side fairly? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fair statement does not exaggerate the opposing view.
5. Passage: "Critics say school uniforms cost families too much. The school plan offers free uniforms to any family that needs them. Because cost is no longer a barrier, uniforms can still help every student focus on learning instead of clothes." Ask: Which sentence answers the concern? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The answer gives evidence that solves the cost problem.
6. Passage: "Critics say school uniforms cost families too much. The school plan offers free uniforms to any family that needs them. Because cost is no longer a barrier, uniforms can still help every student focus on learning instead of clothes." Ask: Which sentence returns to the main claim? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Look for the sentence that ties the solution back to the uniform plan.
7. Passage: "Some teachers say phones in class distract students. A phone pouch at the door keeps phones out of reach during lessons. With that simple fix, students can still bring phones to school for safety before and after class." Ask: Which sentence names the other side fairly? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Name the concern before you answer it.
8. Passage: "Some teachers say phones in class distract students. A phone pouch at the door keeps phones out of reach during lessons. With that simple fix, students can still bring phones to school for safety before and after class." Ask: Which sentence answers the concern? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The answer shows how the distraction is handled.
9. Passage: "Some teachers say phones in class distract students. A phone pouch at the door keeps phones out of reach during lessons. With that simple fix, students can still bring phones to school for safety before and after class." Ask: Which sentence returns to the main claim? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: The closing idea brings readers back to the benefit of the plan.
10. Passage: "Some readers say library ebooks hurt local bookstores. The library plan buys its ebooks through local stores, so the shops earn money from each purchase. The ebook plan can still grow reading in town while it supports local shops." Ask: Which sentence names the other side fairly? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fair counterargument states the worry clearly.
11. Passage: "Some readers say library ebooks hurt local bookstores. The library plan buys its ebooks through local stores, so the shops earn money from each purchase. The ebook plan can still grow reading in town while it supports local shops." Ask: Which sentence answers the concern? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: The answer explains how the plan protects the stores.
12. Passage: "Some readers say library ebooks hurt local bookstores. The library plan buys its ebooks through local stores, so the shops earn money from each purchase. The ebook plan can still grow reading in town while it supports local shops." Ask: Which sentence returns to the main claim? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: Look for the sentence that shows the plan still helps readers and stores.

## Pathway: Embedding Evidence: Mechanics of Quotations (1 topic)

### Matching Pronouns and Verbs between Context and Quotations

See Matching Pronouns and Verbs between Context and Quotations under Body Paragraphs: Embedding Evidence above.

## Pathway: Embedding Quotations (4 topics)

### Capitalizing Quotes

**Teaching card**
A quotation begins with a capital letter when it is a complete sentence that stands on its own, and it keeps a lowercase letter when it continues the sentence that introduces it. The first word inside the quotation marks follows the same rule as the first word of any sentence.
Worked example: Ana said, **"The river rises after heavy rain."** The quoted words form a complete sentence, so **"The"** is capitalized.
Explanation: The capital letter marks the start of a complete quoted sentence.
Tip box: Leo described the hike as **"long and steep."** The quote continues his sentence, so **"long"** stays lowercase. Sam called the map **"the best guide we had."** The quote is a fragment that continues the sentence, so **"the"** stays lowercase.

**Quiz**

1. "Ana said, the river rises after heavy rain." Answer: Ana said, "The river rises after heavy rain." Hint: Ask whether the quoted words form a complete sentence on their own.
2. "Leo said, the trail was long and steep." Answer: Leo said, "The trail was long and steep." Hint: Decide whether the quoted words could stand alone as a sentence.
3. "Sam called the hike long and steep." Answer: Sam called the hike "long and steep." Hint: Ask whether the quoted words could stand alone or continue the sentence.
4. "Ana described the map as the best guide we had." Answer: Ana described the map as "the best guide we had." Hint: Decide whether the quoted words form a full sentence or a fragment.
5. "Leo said, we should leave before noon." Answer: Leo said, "We should leave before noon." Hint: Ask whether the quoted words could stand alone as a sentence.
6. "Sam said the plan was simple and clear." Answer: Sam said the plan was "simple and clear." Hint: Decide whether the quoted words finish the writer's sentence or stand alone.
7. "Ana said, bring water for the hike." Answer: Ana said, "Bring water for the hike." Hint: Ask whether the quoted words are a complete command or a fragment.
8. "Leo called the morning cold and bright." Answer: Leo called the morning "cold and bright." Hint: Decide whether the quoted words stand alone or continue the writer's sentence.
9. "Sam said, the bus leaves at eight." Answer: Sam said, "The bus leaves at eight." Hint: Ask whether the quoted words form a complete sentence.
10. "Ana described the lake as calm and blue." Answer: Ana described the lake as "calm and blue." Hint: Decide whether the quoted words could stand alone as a sentence.
11. "Leo said, check the map before we start." Answer: Leo said, "Check the map before we start." Hint: Ask whether the quoted words are a complete sentence or a fragment.
12. "Sam called the bridge old and narrow." Answer: Sam called the bridge "old and narrow." Hint: Decide whether the quoted words stand alone or continue the writer's sentence.

### Is There a Pause Before the Quote?

**Teaching card**
A comma goes before a quotation when a speaker tag such as said or asked introduces it, and no comma is needed when the quotation flows as part of the sentence after words such as that or as. The pause marks the break between the introduction and the quoted words.
Worked example: Ana said, **"We need more time."** The comma after **said** marks the pause before the complete quoted sentence.
Explanation: The comma separates the speaker tag from the quotation it introduces.
Tip box: Leo noted that the trail **"was longer than the map showed."** No comma is needed because the quote flows after **that**. Sam described the morning as **"cold and bright."** No comma is needed because the quote completes the sentence.

**Quiz**

1. "Ana said We need more time." Answer: Ana said, "We need more time." Hint: Decide whether a speaker tag introduces the quote or the quote flows into the sentence.
2. "Leo asked Are we ready to leave." Answer: Leo asked, "Are we ready to leave?" Hint: Ask whether asked works as a speaker tag here, then check the end mark too.
3. "Sam noted that the trail was longer than the map showed." Answer: Sam noted that the trail "was longer than the map showed." Hint: Look at the word just before the quote and decide whether a pause belongs there.
4. "Ana said The bus leaves at eight." Answer: Ana said, "The bus leaves at eight." Hint: Decide whether a speaker tag introduces the quote.
5. "Leo described the morning as cold and bright." Answer: Leo described the morning as "cold and bright." Hint: Look at the word just before the quote and decide whether a pause belongs there.
6. "Sam asked Can we stop at the lake." Answer: Sam asked, "Can we stop at the lake?" Hint: Ask whether a speaker tag introduces the quote, then check the end mark.
7. "Ana explained that the river rises after heavy rain." Answer: Ana explained that the river "rises after heavy rain." Hint: Look at the word just before the quote and decide whether a pause belongs there.
8. "Leo said We should bring water." Answer: Leo said, "We should bring water." Hint: Decide whether a speaker tag introduces the quote.
9. "Sam called the bridge old and narrow." Answer: Sam called the bridge "old and narrow." Hint: Ask whether the quote completes the sentence or follows a speaker tag.
10. "Ana asked Do we have the map." Answer: Ana asked, "Do we have the map?" Hint: Ask whether a speaker tag introduces the quote, then check the end mark.
11. "Leo noted that the hike was long and steep." Answer: Leo noted that the hike "was long and steep." Hint: Look at the word just before the quote and decide whether a pause belongs there.
12. "Sam said Check the map before we start." Answer: Sam said, "Check the map before we start." Hint: Decide whether a speaker tag introduces the quote.

### Formatting Dialogue and Flow Quotes 1

**Teaching card**
In dialogue, each new speaker starts a new paragraph, and quotation marks open and close around the exact words spoken. A flow quote blends a short quotation into the writer's own sentence, so it needs quotation marks but no new paragraph and no speaker tag comma.
Worked example: **"Are we ready?" Ana asked. "Yes," Leo said. "The bags are packed."** Each speaker gets a new line, and the quoted words sit inside quotation marks.
Explanation: New paragraphs and quotation marks show readers who is speaking and which words are theirs.
Tip box: Sam wrote that the morning was **"cold and bright."** The short quote flows inside his sentence. Ana called the hike **"the best part of the week."** The quote blends into the sentence without a speaker tag.

**Quiz**

1. "Are we ready Ana asked Yes Leo said The bags are packed." Answer: "Are we ready?" Ana asked. "Yes," Leo said. "The bags are packed." Hint: Each new speaker starts a new paragraph, and quoted words need quotation marks.
2. "Sam wrote that the morning was cold and bright." Answer: Sam wrote that the morning was "cold and bright." Hint: A flow quote needs quotation marks around the quoted words.
3. "Do we have the map Leo asked Yes Ana said It is in my bag." Answer: "Do we have the map?" Leo asked. "Yes," Ana said. "It is in my bag." Hint: Each new speaker starts a new paragraph, and quoted words need quotation marks.
4. "Ana called the hike the best part of the week." Answer: Ana called the hike "the best part of the week." Hint: A flow quote blends into the sentence with quotation marks.
5. "Is the bus on time Sam asked No Leo said It leaves at eight." Answer: "Is the bus on time?" Sam asked. "No," Leo said. "It leaves at eight." Hint: Each new speaker starts a new paragraph, and quoted words need quotation marks.
6. "Leo described the trail as long and steep." Answer: Leo described the trail as "long and steep." Hint: A flow quote needs quotation marks around the quoted words.
7. "Can we stop at the lake Ana asked Yes Sam said We have time." Answer: "Can we stop at the lake?" Ana asked. "Yes," Sam said. "We have time." Hint: Each new speaker starts a new paragraph, and quoted words need quotation marks.
8. "Sam noted that the river was calm and blue." Answer: Sam noted that the river was "calm and blue." Hint: A flow quote blends into the sentence with quotation marks.
9. "Are you ready Leo asked Almost Ana said I need my jacket." Answer: "Are you ready?" Leo asked. "Almost," Ana said. "I need my jacket." Hint: Each new speaker starts a new paragraph, and quoted words need quotation marks.
10. "Ana called the morning cold and bright." Answer: Ana called the morning "cold and bright." Hint: A flow quote needs quotation marks around the quoted words.
11. "Did you bring water Sam asked Yes Leo said It is in my bag." Answer: "Did you bring water?" Sam asked. "Yes," Leo said. "It is in my bag." Hint: Each new speaker starts a new paragraph, and quoted words need quotation marks.
12. "Leo said the plan was simple and clear." Answer: Leo said the plan was "simple and clear." Hint: A flow quote blends into the sentence with quotation marks.

### Formatting Dialogue and Colon Quotes

**Teaching card**
A colon introduces a quotation when a complete sentence before it sets up the quote, especially a formal statement or a long quotation. The colon signals that the quoted words will explain, prove, or complete the idea in the first sentence.
Worked example: The guide gave one clear warning: **"Stay on the marked trail."** The complete sentence before the colon sets up the quoted warning.
Explanation: The colon tells readers that the quotation follows as the explanation or proof.
Tip box: The report reached one conclusion: **"The bridge needs repair before winter."** The colon introduces the quoted finding. The coach stated the rule plainly: **"Practice starts at four."** The colon sets up the quoted rule.

**Quiz**

1. "The guide gave one clear warning Stay on the marked trail." Answer: The guide gave one clear warning: "Stay on the marked trail." Hint: Ask whether the words before the quote form a complete sentence that sets it up.
2. "The report reached one conclusion The bridge needs repair before winter." Answer: The report reached one conclusion: "The bridge needs repair before winter." Hint: Decide whether a full sentence or a short speaker tag comes before the quote.
3. "The coach stated the rule plainly Practice starts at four." Answer: The coach stated the rule plainly: "Practice starts at four." Hint: Ask whether the words before the quote form a complete sentence that sets it up.
4. "Ana said The bus leaves at eight." Answer: Ana said, "The bus leaves at eight." Hint: Decide whether a full sentence or a short speaker tag comes before the quote.
5. "The plan had one goal Finish the trail before dark." Answer: The plan had one goal: "Finish the trail before dark." Hint: Ask whether the words before the quote form a complete sentence that sets it up.
6. "Leo asked Are we ready to leave." Answer: Leo asked, "Are we ready to leave?" Hint: Decide whether a full sentence or a short speaker tag comes before the quote.
7. "The letter gave one instruction Bring water for the hike." Answer: The letter gave one instruction: "Bring water for the hike." Hint: Ask whether the words before the quote form a complete sentence that sets it up.
8. "Sam said Check the map before we start." Answer: Sam said, "Check the map before we start." Hint: Decide whether a full sentence or a short speaker tag comes before the quote.
9. "The notice carried one warning Do not cross the river." Answer: The notice carried one warning: "Do not cross the river." Hint: Ask whether the words before the quote form a complete sentence that sets it up.
10. "Ana asked Do we have the map." Answer: Ana asked, "Do we have the map?" Hint: Decide whether a full sentence or a short speaker tag comes before the quote.
11. "The teacher set one rule Raise your hand before you speak." Answer: The teacher set one rule: "Raise your hand before you speak." Hint: Ask whether the words before the quote form a complete sentence that sets it up.
12. "Leo said We should leave before noon." Answer: Leo said, "We should leave before noon." Hint: Decide whether a full sentence or a short speaker tag comes before the quote.

## Pathway: Formal and Informal Language (1 topic)

### Recognizing Language That Is Too Formal or Too Informal

**Teaching card**
Formal language suits school essays, reports, and letters to adults, while informal language suits notes to friends and casual conversation. A sentence is too formal when it sounds stiff and distant for its audience, and too informal when slang or casual shortcuts weaken a serious message.
Worked example: In an essay, **"The results show that practice improves test scores."** is formal and clear, while **"Practice totally helps you crush tests."** is too informal for that audience.
Explanation: The first sentence matches the serious audience, and the second uses slang that does not fit a school essay.
Tip box: To a friend, **"Want to meet at the park after school?"** is fine and natural. In a letter to a principal, **"I am writing to ask about the library hours."** is formal and respectful.

**Quiz**

1. "In an essay: Practice totally helps you crush tests." Answer: too informal. Hint: Think about who reads an essay and whether this wording suits them.
2. "In an essay: The results show that practice improves test scores." Answer: formal, fits the essay. Hint: Ask whether the wording suits a serious school essay.
3. "In a note to a friend: Want to meet at the park after school?" Answer: informal, fits the note. Hint: Ask whether this wording suits a quick message to a friend.
4. "In a note to a friend: I hereby request your presence at the park at four o'clock." Answer: too formal. Hint: Think about how friends usually talk to each other.
5. "In a letter to a principal: Hey, the library hours are a mess." Answer: too informal. Hint: Picture a principal reading this opening line.
6. "In a letter to a principal: I am writing to ask about the library hours." Answer: formal, fits the letter. Hint: Ask whether the wording is respectful enough for an adult reader.
7. "In a lab report: The experiment showed that heat increased the rate of change." Answer: formal, fits the report. Hint: Ask whether the words are precise enough for a lab report.
8. "In a lab report: The experiment was super awesome and kind of worked." Answer: too informal. Hint: Ask whether a lab report would use these words.
9. "In a text to a teammate: Pursuant to our earlier discussion, I shall attend practice." Answer: too formal. Hint: Think about how teammates usually text each other.
10. "In a text to a teammate: See you at practice at four." Answer: informal, fits the text. Hint: Ask whether this wording suits a quick text between teammates.
11. "In a college application essay: School taught me to keep working when a problem is hard." Answer: formal, fits the essay. Hint: Ask whether the wording suits an application reader.
12. "In a college application essay: I dunno, school is fine I guess." Answer: too informal. Hint: Picture an admissions reader seeing this line.

## Pathway: Identifying Sentences and Fragments (5 topics)

### Is the Thought Complete or Incomplete?

See Is the Thought Complete or Incomplete? under Components of a Sentence II above.

### Identifying Dependent Clauses

See Identifying Dependent Clauses under Components of a Sentence II above.

### Recognizing Fragments in Dialogue

**Teaching card**
A fragment in dialogue is a group of words that looks like a sentence but lacks a subject, a verb, or a complete thought. In speech and in written dialogue, short fragments can sound natural, but in formal writing outside quotation marks they should be turned into complete sentences.
Worked example: **"When the rain started."** is a fragment because it has no main clause and leaves the thought unfinished, while **"When the rain started, we ran for shelter."** is a complete sentence.
Explanation: The second version adds a main clause, so the thought is complete.
Tip box: **"After the game."** is a fragment, while **"After the game, we walked home."** is complete. **"Because the bus was late."** is a fragment, while **"Because the bus was late, we missed the first class."** is complete.

**Quiz**

1. "When the rain started." Answer: fragment. Hint: Ask whether these words include a main clause that can stand alone.
2. "When the rain started, we ran for shelter." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Look for a subject and verb that could stand alone as a sentence.
3. "After the game, we walked home." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Read the words alone and ask whether the thought is finished.
4. "After the game." Answer: fragment. Hint: Ask whether these words include a main clause that can stand alone.
5. "Because the bus was late." Answer: fragment. Hint: Look for a subject and verb that could stand alone as a sentence.
6. "Because the bus was late, we missed the first class." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Read the words alone and ask whether the thought is finished.
7. "While Ana packed her bag, Leo checked the map." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Ask whether these words include a main clause that can stand alone.
8. "While Ana packed her bag." Answer: fragment. Hint: Look for a subject and verb that could stand alone as a sentence.
9. "Before the concert." Answer: fragment. Hint: Read the words alone and ask whether the thought is finished.
10. "Before the concert, the band tuned its instruments." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Ask whether these words include a main clause that can stand alone.
11. "If the trail is muddy, we will wear boots." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Look for a subject and verb that could stand alone as a sentence.
12. "If the trail is muddy." Answer: fragment. Hint: Read the words alone and ask whether the thought is finished.

### Recognizing Sentences with Pronoun Subjects

**Teaching card**
A sentence with a pronoun subject uses a word such as he, she, it, we, or they in place of a noun, and the pronoun must clearly point to a person or thing named nearby. The sentence is complete when the pronoun subject joins with a verb to express a full thought.
Worked example: **"The players wanted to win. They practiced every afternoon."** The pronoun subject **"They"** stands for the players, and **"practiced"** is the verb, so the thought is complete.
Explanation: The pronoun subject and the verb together form a complete sentence.
Tip box: **"She finished the report before lunch."** The pronoun **"She"** is the subject. **"It rained all morning."** The pronoun **"It"** is the subject.

**Quiz**

1. "They practiced every afternoon." Answer: complete sentence with pronoun subject They. Hint: Look for a word that tells who did the action.
2. "Practiced every afternoon." Answer: fragment, no subject. Hint: Find the verb, then ask who or what does it.
3. "Finished the report before lunch." Answer: fragment, no subject. Hint: Look for a word that tells who did the action.
4. "She finished the report before lunch." Answer: complete sentence with pronoun subject She. Hint: Find the verb, then ask who or what does it.
5. "It rained all morning." Answer: complete sentence with pronoun subject It. Hint: Look for a word that tells who did the action.
6. "Rained all morning." Answer: fragment, no subject. Hint: Find the verb, then ask who or what does it.
7. "Walked to the library after school." Answer: fragment, no subject. Hint: Look for a word that tells who did the action.
8. "We walked to the library after school." Answer: complete sentence with pronoun subject We. Hint: Find the verb, then ask who or what does it.
9. "He read the map and found the trail." Answer: complete sentence with pronoun subject He. Hint: Look for a word that tells who did the action.
10. "Read the map and found the trail." Answer: fragment, no subject. Hint: Find the verb, then ask who or what does it.
11. "Won the game in the last minute." Answer: fragment, no subject. Hint: Look for a word that tells who did the action.
12. "They won the game in the last minute." Answer: complete sentence with pronoun subject They. Hint: Find the verb, then ask who or what does it.

### Sentences and Fragments with "That," "Which," and "Especially"

**Teaching card**
Words such as that, which, and especially often start a dependent part of a sentence, so a group of words that begins with one of them is usually a fragment unless it joins a main clause. A complete sentence needs a main subject and verb that can stand on its own.
Worked example: **"Which the teacher read aloud."** is a fragment because it depends on another clause, while **"We loved the story, which the teacher read aloud."** is a complete sentence.
Explanation: The second version adds a main clause, so the which clause has something to attach to.
Tip box: **"That Ana wrote last year."** is a fragment, while **"This is the essay that Ana wrote last year."** is complete. **"Especially on rainy days."** is a fragment, while **"The library is busy, especially on rainy days."** is complete.

**Quiz**

1. "Which the teacher read aloud." Answer: fragment. Hint: Ask whether the words include a main subject and verb that can stand alone.
2. "We loved the story, which the teacher read aloud." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Find the main subject and verb, if there is one.
3. "This is the essay that Ana wrote last year." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Read the words alone and ask whether they stand on their own.
4. "That Ana wrote last year." Answer: fragment. Hint: Ask whether the words include a main subject and verb that can stand alone.
5. "Especially on rainy days." Answer: fragment. Hint: Find the main subject and verb, if there is one.
6. "The library is busy, especially on rainy days." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Read the words alone and ask whether they stand on their own.
7. "Leo showed us the old map, which he found in the attic." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Ask whether the words include a main subject and verb that can stand alone.
8. "Which Leo found in the attic." Answer: fragment. Hint: Find the main subject and verb, if there is one.
9. "That Sam built for the science fair." Answer: fragment. Hint: Read the words alone and ask whether they stand on their own.
10. "This is the model that Sam built for the science fair." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Ask whether the words include a main subject and verb that can stand alone.
11. "The gym is crowded, especially after practice." Answer: complete sentence. Hint: Find the main subject and verb, if there is one.
12. "Especially after practice." Answer: fragment. Hint: Read the words alone and ask whether they stand on their own.

## Pathway: Introductory Paragraphs (3 topics)

### Is the Thesis a Fact or Opinion?

**Teaching card**

**Section 1: A fact can be checked.**
Guiding question: Can the statement be proven true with evidence?
Worked passage: "The library opens at eight in the morning. **That fact can be checked on the posted hours, so it states information rather than a position.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence shows that a fact is verifiable, so it cannot serve as an arguable thesis on its own.

**Section 2: An opinion takes a position.**
Guiding question: Does the statement express a judgment that someone could reasonably dispute?
Worked passage: "The library should open earlier on school days. **That opinion takes a position, since some readers may agree and others may prefer the current hours.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence shows that an opinion can be argued, so it can serve as a thesis.

**Section 3: A strong thesis is an arguable opinion.**
Guiding question: Does the thesis give the essay something to prove?
Worked passage: "The library should open earlier on school days. **Because the thesis is an opinion, the essay must give reasons and evidence to support it.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence shows that an arguable thesis gives the writer a job to do in the essay.

**Quiz**

1. Passage: "The school has 800 students. The school should add a second lunch period. A second period would shorten the lines." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fact can be checked with evidence.
2. Passage: "The school has 800 students. The school should add a second lunch period. A second period would shorten the lines." Ask: Which sentence is an opinion that works as a thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: An opinion takes a position that can be argued.
3. Passage: "The school has 800 students. The school should add a second lunch period. A second period would shorten the lines." Ask: Which sentence gives a reason that supports the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A supporting reason explains why the thesis makes sense.
4. Passage: "The river is 200 miles long. Towns should protect the river banks. Clean banks would help fish and birds." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fact can be checked with evidence.
5. Passage: "The river is 200 miles long. Towns should protect the river banks. Clean banks would help fish and birds." Ask: Which sentence is an opinion that works as a thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: An opinion takes a position that can be argued.
6. Passage: "The river is 200 miles long. Towns should protect the river banks. Clean banks would help fish and birds." Ask: Which sentence gives a reason that supports the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A supporting reason explains why the thesis makes sense.
7. Passage: "The gym was built in 1998. The gym needs new lights. Better lights would make evening games safer." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fact can be checked with evidence.
8. Passage: "The gym was built in 1998. The gym needs new lights. Better lights would make evening games safer." Ask: Which sentence is an opinion that works as a thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: An opinion takes a position that can be argued.
9. Passage: "The gym was built in 1998. The gym needs new lights. Better lights would make evening games safer." Ask: Which sentence gives a reason that supports the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A supporting reason explains why the thesis makes sense.
10. Passage: "The town library has 40,000 books. The library should stay open on Sundays. Sunday hours would help students who work on Saturdays." Ask: Which sentence is a fact? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A fact can be checked with evidence.
11. Passage: "The town library has 40,000 books. The library should stay open on Sundays. Sunday hours would help students who work on Saturdays." Ask: Which sentence is an opinion that works as a thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: An opinion takes a position that can be argued.
12. Passage: "The town library has 40,000 books. The library should stay open on Sundays. Sunday hours would help students who work on Saturdays." Ask: Which sentence gives a reason that supports the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: A supporting reason explains why the thesis makes sense.

### Selecting a Strong Hook 1: Agreeing

**Teaching card**

**Section 1: A hook connects to something readers already believe.**
Guiding question: What idea can the writer and the reader agree on at the start?
Worked passage: "Most students agree that school days feel long. **Starting with that shared feeling pulls readers in before the writer makes a claim.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence shows that agreement at the start creates a connection with readers.

**Section 2: A strong hook leads to the thesis.**
Guiding question: Does the opening point toward the main claim?
Worked passage: "Most students agree that school days feel long. **That shared feeling leads naturally to the claim that a short break in the middle of the day would help.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence shows that a good hook points forward to the thesis instead of wandering away.

**Section 3: A weak hook stays general and goes nowhere.**
Guiding question: Does the opening give readers a reason to keep reading?
Worked passage: "School is a place where students learn many things. **That general opening gives readers no shared idea and no path to the thesis.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence shows that a vague hook fails because it does not connect or lead anywhere.

**Quiz**

1. Passage: "Most students agree that school days feel long. A short break would help them focus. The school should add a ten minute break each afternoon." Ask: Which sentence is the strong hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A strong hook starts with something readers already believe.
2. Passage: "Most students agree that school days feel long. A short break would help them focus. The school should add a ten minute break each afternoon." Ask: Which sentence leads to the thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A hook should point forward to the main claim.
3. Passage: "Most students agree that school days feel long. A short break would help them focus. The school should add a ten minute break each afternoon." Ask: Which sentence is the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: The thesis is the claim the hook leads toward.
4. Passage: "School is a place where students learn many things. Students need time to rest. The school should add a ten minute break each afternoon." Ask: Which sentence is a weak hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A vague, general opening does not connect with readers.
5. Passage: "School is a place where students learn many things. Students need time to rest. The school should add a ten minute break each afternoon." Ask: Which sentence leads to the thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A hook should point forward to the main claim.
6. Passage: "School is a place where students learn many things. Students need time to rest. The school should add a ten minute break each afternoon." Ask: Which sentence is the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: The thesis is the claim the hook leads toward.
7. Passage: "Almost everyone agrees that reading matters. Reading every day builds vocabulary and focus. Schools should protect twenty minutes of daily reading time." Ask: Which sentence is the strong hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A strong hook starts with something readers already believe.
8. Passage: "Almost everyone agrees that reading matters. Reading every day builds vocabulary and focus. Schools should protect twenty minutes of daily reading time." Ask: Which sentence leads to the thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A hook should point forward to the main claim.
9. Passage: "Almost everyone agrees that reading matters. Reading every day builds vocabulary and focus. Schools should protect twenty minutes of daily reading time." Ask: Which sentence is the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: The thesis is the claim the hook leads toward.
10. Passage: "Books are important in many ways. Students read in class every day. Schools should protect twenty minutes of daily reading time." Ask: Which sentence is a weak hook? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A vague, general opening does not connect with readers.
11. Passage: "Books are important in many ways. Students read in class every day. Schools should protect twenty minutes of daily reading time." Ask: Which sentence leads to the thesis? Answer: the second sentence. Hint: A hook should point forward to the main claim.
12. Passage: "Books are important in many ways. Students read in class every day. Schools should protect twenty minutes of daily reading time." Ask: Which sentence is the thesis? Answer: the third sentence. Hint: The thesis is the claim the hook leads toward.

### Recognizing Redundant, Unnecessary, and Wordy Phrases

**Teaching card**

**Section 1: A redundant phrase repeats the same idea.**
Guiding question: Does one word already say what the other word says?
Worked passage: "The team will meet again in the future. **The word will already points to the future, so in the future repeats the idea.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence shows that a redundant phrase says the same thing twice.

**Section 2: An unnecessary phrase adds words without meaning.**
Guiding question: Does the sentence keep its meaning if the phrase is removed?
Worked passage: "In my opinion, I think the plan will work. **The sentence keeps its meaning without in my opinion, so that phrase is unnecessary.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence shows that an unnecessary phrase can be cut without losing meaning.

**Section 3: A wordy phrase can be shortened.**
Guiding question: Can the same idea be said with fewer words?
Worked passage: "The class will meet at a time when the bell rings. **The class will meet when the bell rings says the same thing with fewer words.**"
Explanation: The highlighted sentence shows that a shorter version keeps the meaning and reads more clearly.

**Quiz**

1. Passage: "The team will meet again in the future. The meeting will plan the next game. The plan should be simple and clear." Ask: Which sentence has a redundant phrase? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A redundant phrase repeats the same idea twice.
2. Passage: "The team will meet again in the future. The meeting will plan the next game. The plan should be simple and clear." Ask: Which phrase is redundant? Answer: in the future. Hint: Look for words that repeat the time already shown by will.
3. Passage: "The team will meet again in the future. The meeting will plan the next game. The plan should be simple and clear." Ask: Which sentences are clear and not wordy? Answer: the second and third sentences. Hint: Clear sentences say the idea once, in few words.
4. Passage: "In my opinion, I think the plan will work. The plan gives every student a job. The jobs rotate each week." Ask: Which sentence has an unnecessary phrase? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: An unnecessary phrase can be cut without losing meaning.
5. Passage: "In my opinion, I think the plan will work. The plan gives every student a job. The jobs rotate each week." Ask: Which phrase is unnecessary? Answer: In my opinion, or I think, since either one can be cut. Hint: Look for two phrases that do the same job.
6. Passage: "In my opinion, I think the plan will work. The plan gives every student a job. The jobs rotate each week." Ask: Which sentences are clear and not wordy? Answer: the second and third sentences. Hint: Clear sentences say the idea once, in few words.
7. Passage: "The class will meet at a time when the bell rings. The class will review the homework. The review will take ten minutes." Ask: Which sentence is wordy? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: A wordy phrase can be shortened without losing meaning.
8. Passage: "The class will meet at a time when the bell rings. The class will review the homework. The review will take ten minutes." Ask: Which shorter version keeps the meaning? Answer: The class will meet when the bell rings. Hint: Fewer words can say the same thing more clearly.
9. Passage: "The class will meet at a time when the bell rings. The class will review the homework. The review will take ten minutes." Ask: Which sentences are clear and not wordy? Answer: the second and third sentences. Hint: Clear sentences say the idea once, in few words.
10. Passage: "The final outcome was a win for the team. The win came in the last minute. The crowd cheered loudly." Ask: Which sentence has a redundant phrase? Answer: the first sentence. Hint: Look for two words side by side that say nearly the same thing.
11. Passage: "The final outcome was a win for the team. The win came in the last minute. The crowd cheered loudly." Ask: Which phrase is redundant? Answer: final outcome. Hint: Find the pair of words where one already includes the meaning of the other.
12. Passage: "The final outcome was a win for the team. The win came in the last minute. The crowd cheered loudly." Ask: Which sentences are clear and not wordy? Answer: the second and third sentences. Hint: Clear sentences say the idea once, in few words.

## Pathway: MLA Citation I (2 topics)

### Formatting In-Text Citations

See Formatting In-Text Citations under Body Paragraphs: Embedding Evidence above.

### Question Marks and Exclamation Points with Citations

**Teaching card**
When a quotation ends with a question mark or exclamation point, that mark stays inside the closing quotation mark, and the citation follows after the quotation marks. A period still follows the citation to end the sentence, even though the quotation already ends with its own mark.
Worked example: Ana asked, **"Are we ready to leave?" (Reyes 12).** The question mark stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation **(Reyes 12)** follows after them.
Explanation: The question mark belongs to the quoted words, and the citation comes after the quotation ends.
Tip box: Leo shouted, **"We won the game!" (Park 8).** The exclamation point stays inside the quotation marks. Sam asked, **"Did you bring the map?" (Lee 21).** The question mark stays inside, and the citation follows.

**Quiz**

1. "Ana asked, Are we ready to leave (Reyes 12)." Answer: Ana asked, "Are we ready to leave?" (Reyes 12). Hint: The question mark stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.
2. "Leo shouted, We won the game (Park 8)!" Answer: Leo shouted, "We won the game!" (Park 8). Hint: The exclamation point stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.
3. "Sam asked, Did you bring the map (Lee 21)?" Answer: Sam asked, "Did you bring the map?" (Lee 21). Hint: The question mark stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.
4. "Ana asked, Are we on time (Reyes 12)?" Answer: Ana asked, "Are we on time?" (Reyes 12). Hint: The question mark stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.
5. "Leo shouted, That was amazing (Park 8)!" Answer: Leo shouted, "That was amazing!" (Park 8). Hint: The exclamation point stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.
6. "Sam asked, Can we stop at the lake (Lee 21)?" Answer: Sam asked, "Can we stop at the lake?" (Lee 21). Hint: The question mark stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.
7. "Ana asked, Do we have the map (Reyes 12)?" Answer: Ana asked, "Do we have the map?" (Reyes 12). Hint: The question mark stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.
8. "Leo shouted, We did it (Park 8)!" Answer: Leo shouted, "We did it!" (Park 8). Hint: The exclamation point stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.
9. "Sam asked, Is the bus on time (Lee 21)?" Answer: Sam asked, "Is the bus on time?" (Lee 21). Hint: The question mark stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.
10. "Ana asked, Are you ready (Reyes 12)?" Answer: Ana asked, "Are you ready?" (Reyes 12). Hint: The question mark stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.
11. "Leo shouted, What a game (Park 8)!" Answer: Leo shouted, "What a game!" (Park 8). Hint: The exclamation point stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.
12. "Sam asked, Did the trail close (Lee 21)?" Answer: Sam asked, "Did the trail close?" (Lee 21). Hint: The question mark stays inside the quotation marks, and the citation follows after them.

## Pathway: Misplaced and Dangling Modifiers (5 topics)

### Identifying Modifiers

**Teaching card**
A modifier is a word or phrase that describes another word in the sentence, usually by telling which one, what kind, how, when, or where. To identify a modifier, find the word it describes and check that it sits close to that word.
Worked example: **"The tired hikers"** reached the lake. The modifier **"tired"** describes **"hikers"**, telling what kind of hikers they were.
Explanation: The modifier adds detail to the noun it describes.
Tip box: **"The old wooden bridge"** crossed the river. **"Old"** and **"wooden"** describe **"bridge"**. **"Ana ran quickly to the bus."** **"Quickly"** describes **"ran"**, telling how she ran.

**Quiz**

1. "The tired hikers reached the lake." Answer: tired. Hint: A modifier describes another word, telling what kind it is.
2. "The old wooden bridge crossed the river." Answer: old and wooden. Hint: Modifiers describe the noun they sit beside.
3. "Ana quickly answered the question." Answer: quickly. Hint: A modifier can describe a verb, telling how the action happened.
4. "The bright morning sun warmed the field." Answer: bright and morning. Hint: Modifiers describe the noun they sit beside.
5. "Leo read the long report." Answer: long. Hint: A modifier describes another word, telling what kind it is.
6. "Sam softly thanked the coach." Answer: softly. Hint: A modifier can describe a verb, telling how the action happened.
7. "The cold water filled the bottle." Answer: cold. Hint: Modifiers describe the noun they sit beside.
8. "Ana finished the difficult puzzle." Answer: difficult. Hint: A modifier describes another word, telling what kind it is.
9. "Leo slowly climbed the trail." Answer: slowly. Hint: A modifier can describe a verb, telling how the action happened.
10. "The tall oak tree shaded the table." Answer: tall and oak. Hint: Modifiers describe the noun they sit beside.
11. "Sam wrote a careful answer." Answer: careful. Hint: A modifier describes another word, telling what kind it is.
12. "Ana sang the melody clearly." Answer: clearly. Hint: A modifier can describe a verb, telling how the action happened.

### Interpreting Misplaced Modifiers

**Teaching card**
A misplaced modifier is a describing phrase that sits too far from the word it should describe, so it seems to describe the wrong word instead. To interpret it, ask which word the modifier is closest to and which word the writer probably meant.
Worked example: **"Ana walked the dog in a red raincoat."** The phrase **"in a red raincoat"** seems to describe **"the dog"**, but Ana was the one wearing the raincoat.
Explanation: The modifier sits beside the wrong noun, so the sentence means something the writer did not intend.
Tip box: **"Leo saw a deer driving to school."** The phrase **"driving to school"** seems to describe **"a deer"**, but Leo was driving. **"Sam found a wallet walking home."** The phrase **"walking home"** seems to describe **"a wallet"**, but Sam was walking.

**Quiz**

1. "Ana walked the dog in a red raincoat." Answer: The modifier in a red raincoat seems to describe the dog, but Ana was wearing the raincoat. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word.
2. "Leo saw a deer driving to school." Answer: The modifier driving to school seems to describe a deer, but Leo was driving. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word.
3. "Sam found a wallet walking home." Answer: The modifier walking home seems to describe a wallet, but Sam was walking. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word.
4. "Ana watched the clouds eating lunch on the bench." Answer: The modifier eating lunch on the bench seems to describe the clouds, but Ana was eating lunch. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word.
5. "Leo heard a song riding his bike." Answer: The modifier riding his bike seems to describe a song, but Leo was riding. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word.
6. "Sam saw a fox walking to the library." Answer: The modifier walking to the library seems to describe a fox, but Sam was walking. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word.
7. "Ana found a glove running to practice." Answer: The modifier running to practice seems to describe a glove, but Ana was running. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word.
8. "Leo saw a squirrel carrying a heavy backpack." Answer: The modifier carrying a heavy backpack seems to describe a squirrel, but Leo was carrying it. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word, so check which word it is closest to.
9. "Sam read a letter sipping hot cocoa." Answer: The modifier sipping hot cocoa seems to describe a letter, but Sam was sipping it. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word.
10. "Ana saw a rabbit driving past the farm." Answer: The modifier driving past the farm seems to describe a rabbit, but Ana was driving. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word.
11. "Leo found a key walking to class." Answer: The modifier walking to class seems to describe a key, but Leo was walking. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word.
12. "Sam watched the sunrise eating breakfast on the porch." Answer: The modifier eating breakfast on the porch seems to describe the sunrise, but Sam was eating breakfast. Hint: A misplaced modifier sits beside the wrong word.

### Interpreting Dangling Modifiers

**Teaching card**
A dangling modifier is a describing phrase at the start of a sentence that has no word in the sentence to describe, because the person or thing doing the action is missing. To interpret it, ask who was doing the action in the opening phrase and notice that the sentence never names that person.
Worked example: **"After finishing the homework, the television was turned on."** The phrase **"After finishing the homework"** needs a person, but the sentence names only **"the television"**, so the modifier dangles.
Explanation: The opening phrase has no word to attach to, so the sentence is unclear about who finished the homework.
Tip box: **"While reading the map, the trail grew steep."** No one is named as reading the map, so the modifier dangles. **"To pass the test, the notes must be reviewed."** No one is named as passing the test, so the modifier dangles.

**Quiz**

1. "After finishing the homework, the television was turned on." Answer: dangling, no one is named as finishing the homework. Hint: Ask who does the action in the opening phrase, then look for that person in the main clause.
2. "While reading the map, the trail grew steep." Answer: dangling, no one is named as reading the map. Hint: Find the subject of the main clause and ask whether it could do the opening action.
3. "To pass the test, the notes must be reviewed." Answer: dangling, no one is named as passing the test. Hint: Ask who does the action in the opening phrase, then look for that person in the main clause.
4. "After finishing the homework, Ana turned on the television." Answer: not dangling, Ana is named as finishing the homework. Hint: Find the subject of the main clause and ask whether it could do the opening action.
5. "While reading the map, Leo noticed the trail grew steep." Answer: not dangling, Leo is named as reading the map. Hint: Ask who does the action in the opening phrase, then look for that person in the main clause.
6. "To pass the test, Sam reviewed his notes." Answer: not dangling, Sam is named as passing the test. Hint: Find the subject of the main clause and ask whether it could do the opening action.
7. "Before leaving for school, the bus arrived." Answer: dangling, no one is named as leaving for school. Hint: Ask who does the action in the opening phrase, then look for that person in the main clause.
8. "Before leaving for school, Ana packed her bag." Answer: not dangling, Ana is named as leaving for school. Hint: Find the subject of the main clause and ask whether it could do the opening action.
9. "After running the race, the water was cold and sweet." Answer: dangling, no one is named as running the race. Hint: Ask who does the action in the opening phrase, then look for that person in the main clause.
10. "After running the race, Leo drank cold water." Answer: not dangling, Leo is named as running the race. Hint: Find the subject of the main clause and ask whether it could do the opening action.
11. "While cooking dinner, the phone rang." Answer: dangling, no one is named as cooking dinner. Hint: Ask who does the action in the opening phrase, then look for that person in the main clause.
12. "While cooking dinner, Sam answered the phone." Answer: not dangling, Sam is named as cooking dinner. Hint: Find the subject of the main clause and ask whether it could do the opening action.

### Correcting Misplaced Modifier Errors 1

**Teaching card**
To correct a misplaced modifier, move the describing phrase next to the word it should describe, or rewrite the sentence so the right person or thing does the action. The corrected sentence should make clear who or what the modifier describes.
Worked example: **"Ana walked the dog in a red raincoat."** becomes **"Ana, in a red raincoat, walked the dog."** The phrase **"in a red raincoat"** now sits beside **"Ana"**, the person wearing it.
Explanation: Moving the modifier next to the right word fixes the confusion.
Tip box: **"Leo saw a deer driving to school."** becomes **"Driving to school, Leo saw a deer."** **"Sam found a wallet walking home."** becomes **"Walking home, Sam found a wallet."**

**Quiz**

1. "Ana walked the dog in a red raincoat." Answer: Ana, in a red raincoat, walked the dog. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.
2. "Leo saw a deer driving to school." Answer: Driving to school, Leo saw a deer. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.
3. "Sam found a wallet walking home." Answer: Walking home, Sam found a wallet. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.
4. "Ana watched the clouds eating lunch on the bench." Answer: Eating lunch on the bench, Ana watched the clouds. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.
5. "Leo heard a song riding his bike." Answer: Riding his bike, Leo heard a song. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.
6. "Sam saw a fox walking to the library." Answer: Walking to the library, Sam saw a fox. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.
7. "Ana found a glove running to practice." Answer: Running to practice, Ana found a glove. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.
8. "Leo saw a squirrel carrying a heavy backpack." Answer: Carrying a heavy backpack, Leo saw a squirrel. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.
9. "Sam read a letter sipping hot cocoa." Answer: Sipping hot cocoa, Sam read a letter. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.
10. "Ana saw a rabbit driving past the farm." Answer: Driving past the farm, Ana saw a rabbit. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.
11. "Leo found a key walking to class." Answer: Walking to class, Leo found a key. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.
12. "Sam watched the sunrise eating breakfast on the porch." Answer: Eating breakfast on the porch, Sam watched the sunrise. Hint: Move the modifier next to the person doing the action.

### Correcting Dangling Modifier Errors

**Teaching card**
