To correct a dangling modifier, name the person or thing doing the action in the main clause, or rewrite the opening phrase as a full clause with its own subject. The corrected sentence should leave no doubt about who did the action.
Worked example: **"After finishing the homework, the television was turned on."** becomes **"After finishing the homework, Ana turned on the television."** The main clause now names **"Ana"**, the person who finished the homework.
Explanation: Naming the actor in the main clause gives the modifier a word to describe.
Tip box: **"While reading the map, the trail grew steep."** becomes **"While reading the map, Leo noticed the trail grew steep."** **"To pass the test, the notes must be reviewed."** becomes **"To pass the test, Sam must review his notes."**

**Quiz**

1. "After finishing the homework, the television was turned on." Answer: After finishing the homework, Ana turned on the television. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
2. "While reading the map, the trail grew steep." Answer: While reading the map, Leo noticed the trail grew steep. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
3. "To pass the test, the notes must be reviewed." Answer: To pass the test, Sam must review his notes. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
4. "Before leaving for school, the bus arrived." Answer: Before leaving for school, Ana packed her bag and waited for the bus. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
5. "After running the race, the water was cold and sweet." Answer: After running the race, Leo drank cold water. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
6. "While cooking dinner, the phone rang." Answer: While cooking dinner, Sam answered the phone. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
7. "After studying all night, the test felt easier." Answer: After studying all night, Ana felt the test was easier. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
8. "While walking to class, the rain began." Answer: While walking to class, Leo felt the rain begin. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
9. "To finish the project, the tools were gathered." Answer: To finish the project, Sam gathered the tools. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
10. "Before starting the hike, the map was checked." Answer: Before starting the hike, Ana checked the map. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
11. "After reading the book, the ending was surprising." Answer: After reading the book, Leo found the ending surprising. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
12. "While practicing the song, the piano bench was moved." Answer: While practicing the song, Sam moved the piano bench. Hint: Name the person doing the action in the main clause.
# Grammar Lab, Grades 9-12, Part 3

## Pathway: Phrases and Dependent Clauses (6 topics)

### What Does the Appositive Refer To?

**Teaching card**
Definition: An appositive is a noun or noun phrase that renames or identifies another noun, and it sits right beside the noun it refers to.
Worked example: Maya, a skilled goalkeeper, saved the final shot. [appositive: a skilled goalkeeper] [noun it refers to: Maya]
How it works: The phrase a skilled goalkeeper gives more information about Maya, so it refers to Maya, not to the shot or the goal.
Tip box: Try it: Luis, our class president, led the meeting. The phrase our class president refers to Luis. Try it: The river, a wide and slow waterway, winds past the town. The phrase a wide and slow waterway refers to the river.

**Quiz**
1. "Ana, a talented violinist, played at the school concert." Answer: Ana. Hint: The appositive names the person right beside it, so ask who the violinist is.
2. "The library, a quiet brick building, opens early on weekdays." Answer: The library. Hint: Ask which noun the appositive sits beside and renames.
3. "Mr. Ortiz, the chemistry teacher, set up the lab experiment." Answer: Mr. Ortiz. Hint: The appositive gives the job of the person beside it.
4. "Sam adopted a rescue dog, a small brown terrier, from the shelter." Answer: A rescue dog. Hint: Ask which noun in the sentence could be called a small brown terrier.
5. "The committee chose Rosa, an experienced organizer, to lead the fundraiser." Answer: Rosa. Hint: Ask which person in the sentence is described as an experienced organizer.
6. "Jupiter, the largest planet in our solar system, has many moons." Answer: Jupiter. Hint: Ask which noun the appositive gives a fact about, and look right beside it.
7. "Leo visited the museum, a former train station, on Saturday." Answer: The museum. Hint: Ask which place in the sentence used to be a train station.
8. "The team captain, a senior named Priya, gave a short speech." Answer: The team captain. Hint: Ask which noun the appositive renames, and look at the noun right beside it.
9. "Elena read a novel, a mystery set in a small town, over the break." Answer: A novel. Hint: Ask what in the sentence is described as a mystery set in a small town.
10. "The old oak, the tallest tree in the park, lost a branch in the storm." Answer: The old oak. Hint: Ask which noun in the sentence is called the tallest tree in the park.
11. "Coach Rivera, a former college athlete, runs practice every afternoon." Answer: Coach Rivera. Hint: Ask which person the appositive tells more about, and look right beside it.
12. "The science fair winner, a ninth grader named Noah, explained his project clearly." Answer: The science fair winner. Hint: Ask which noun the appositive renames, and look right before the first comma.

### What Does the Participial Phrase Modify?

**Teaching card**
Definition: A participial phrase begins with a participle, a verb form ending in ing or a past form such as painted, built, or written, and acts like an adjective, and it modifies the noun or pronoun closest to it in meaning.
Worked example: Running toward the bus, Leo waved at his friends. [participial phrase: Running toward the bus] [word it modifies: Leo]
How it works: Leo is the one running, so the phrase modifies Leo, and it must sit close to Leo to avoid confusion.
Tip box: Try it: Covered in mud, the soccer ball rolled off the field. The phrase Covered in mud modifies the soccer ball. Try it: Smiling at the crowd, Ana took a bow. The phrase Smiling at the crowd modifies Ana.

**Quiz**
1. "Walking through the garden, Sam noticed the new flowers." Answer: Sam. Hint: Ask who was walking, and that noun is the one the phrase modifies.
2. "Tired after practice, the team rested on the bench." Answer: The team. Hint: Ask who or what was tired, and look at the noun right after the comma.
3. "Built in 1920, the school has thick stone walls." Answer: The school. Hint: Ask what was built in 1920.
4. "Laughing at the joke, Maya covered her mouth." Answer: Maya. Hint: Ask who was laughing at the joke.
5. "Hidden under the desk, the lost notebook waited for weeks." Answer: The lost notebook. Hint: Ask what was hidden under the desk.
6. "Studying late at night, Leo finished his history essay." Answer: Leo. Hint: Ask who was studying late at night.
7. "Painted bright yellow, the classroom door stood out in the hall." Answer: The classroom door. Hint: Ask what was painted bright yellow.
8. "Waiting for the bus, Ana checked her phone for the schedule." Answer: Ana. Hint: Ask who was waiting for the bus.
9. "Damaged by the storm, the fence needed repairs." Answer: The fence. Hint: Ask what was damaged by the storm.
10. "Singing softly, the choir warmed up before the concert." Answer: The choir. Hint: Ask who was singing softly.
11. "Written in clear handwriting, the note was easy to read." Answer: The note. Hint: Ask what was written in clear handwriting.
12. "Excited about the trip, the students packed their bags early." Answer: The students. Hint: Ask who was excited about the trip.

### Correcting Dependent Clause Fragments

**Teaching card**
Definition: A dependent clause fragment is a group of words that starts with a subordinating word such as because, although, or when, but it cannot stand alone as a complete sentence.
Worked example: Because the rain started early. [fragment] Corrected: Because the rain started early, the game moved indoors. [dependent clause: Because the rain started early] [main clause: the game moved indoors]
How it works: The clause needs a main clause to complete the thought, so joining it to a full sentence fixes the fragment.
Tip box: Try it: Although the test was long. Corrected: Although the test was long, most students finished on time. Try it: When the bell rings. Corrected: When the bell rings, students head to their next class.

**Quiz**
1. "Because the library was closed." Answer: Because the library was closed, Ana studied at home. Hint: Join the because clause to a main clause that completes the thought.
2. "Although it rained all morning." Answer: Although it rained all morning, the field trip still went ahead. Hint: An although clause needs a main clause after the comma.
3. "When the science fair begins." Answer: When the science fair begins, judges will visit each project. Hint: A when clause needs a main clause to tell what happens then.
4. "Since the team practiced every day." Answer: Since the team practiced every day, they won the match. Hint: Add a main clause that shows the result.
5. "If the bus arrives late." Answer: If the bus arrives late, Sam will call the school office. Hint: An if clause needs a main clause for the plan.
6. "After the concert ended." Answer: After the concert ended, the choir posed for a photo. Hint: Join the after clause to a complete sentence.
7. "While Leo was reading." Answer: While Leo was reading, his phone buzzed on the desk. Hint: A while clause needs a main clause to finish the idea.
8. "Unless the weather improves." Answer: Unless the weather improves, practice will move to the gym. Hint: Add a main clause that states what will happen.
9. "Before the museum opened." Answer: Before the museum opened, a line of visitors formed outside. Hint: Join the before clause to a main clause.
10. "As soon as the results were posted." Answer: As soon as the results were posted, Maya checked her score. Hint: The clause needs a main clause to complete the sentence.
11. "Even though the hike was steep." Answer: Even though the hike was steep, the group reached the lookout. Hint: An even though clause needs a main clause after the comma.
12. "Until the new books arrive." Answer: Until the new books arrive, the class will share the old copies. Hint: Add a main clause that tells what happens in the meantime.

### Punctuating Restrictive and Nonrestrictive Clauses

**Teaching card**
Definition: A restrictive clause is essential to identify the noun it follows, so it takes no commas, while a nonrestrictive clause adds extra information and is set off with commas.
Worked example: The student who won the science award spoke first. [restrictive clause: who won the science award, no commas] Maya, who won the science award, spoke first. [nonrestrictive clause: who won the science award, with commas]
How it works: In the first sentence the clause picks out which student, so it is essential, while in the second sentence Maya is already named, so the clause is extra.
Tip box: Try it: The book that Ana borrowed is overdue. The clause that Ana borrowed is restrictive, with no commas. Try it: The book, which Ana borrowed last week, is overdue. The clause which Ana borrowed last week is nonrestrictive, with commas.

**Quiz**
1. "The player who scored the winning goal was Leo." Answer: The player who scored the winning goal was Leo. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether the player is still clearly identified.
2. "Maya who is the team captain gave a speech." Answer: Maya, who is the team captain, gave a speech. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether Maya is still clearly identified.
3. "The lab that has new microscopes is on the second floor." Answer: The lab that has new microscopes is on the second floor. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether the lab is still clearly identified.
4. "The library which opened in 1998 has a large reading room." Answer: The library, which opened in 1998, has a large reading room. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether the library is still clearly identified.
5. "Students who finish early may read quietly." Answer: Students who finish early may read quietly. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether the students are still clearly identified.
6. "Mr. Ortiz who teaches chemistry set up the experiment." Answer: Mr. Ortiz, who teaches chemistry, set up the experiment. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether Mr. Ortiz is still clearly identified.
7. "The novel that won the award is on display." Answer: The novel that won the award is on display. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether the novel is still clearly identified.
8. "The river which flows through the town floods each spring." Answer: The river, which flows through the town, floods each spring. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether the river is still clearly identified.
9. "The bus that leaves at eight stops at the school." Answer: The bus that leaves at eight stops at the school. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether the bus is still clearly identified.
10. "Sam who plays goalkeeper saved the final shot." Answer: Sam, who plays goalkeeper, saved the final shot. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether Sam is still clearly identified.
11. "The map that shows the hiking trails is in the visitor center." Answer: The map that shows the hiking trails is in the visitor center. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether the map is still clearly identified.
12. "The science fair which takes place in May draws large crowds." Answer: The science fair, which takes place in May, draws large crowds. Hint: Read the sentence without the clause and ask whether the science fair is still clearly identified.

### Correcting Comma Splices and Fused Sentences

**Teaching card**
Definition: A comma splice joins two complete sentences with only a comma, and a fused sentence joins two complete sentences with no punctuation at all, and both need a stronger break or a connecting word.
Worked example: The rain stopped, the game began. [comma splice] Corrected: The rain stopped, and the game began. [two main clauses joined by a comma and and]
How it works: Each part can stand alone as a sentence, so a comma alone is too weak, and adding and makes the join correct.
Tip box: Try it: Leo finished his essay he proofread it twice. Corrected: Leo finished his essay. He proofread it twice. Try it: The bus was late, Ana walked to school. Corrected: The bus was late, so Ana walked to school.

**Quiz**
1. "The test was long, most students finished on time." Answer: The test was long, but most students finished on time. Hint: Two complete sentences need more than a comma, so add a joining word.
2. "Maya practiced every day she made the team." Answer: Maya practiced every day. She made the team. Hint: Split the fused sentence into two complete sentences.
3. "The library opens early, students arrive before class." Answer: The library opens early, and students arrive before class. Hint: Join the two sentences with a comma and a joining word that adds one idea to another.
4. "Leo forgot his lunch Sam shared his sandwich." Answer: Leo forgot his lunch, and Sam shared his sandwich. Hint: A fused sentence needs punctuation and a joining word, or a period.
5. "The concert was loud, the crowd cheered anyway." Answer: The concert was loud, but the crowd cheered anyway. Hint: Use a comma and a joining word that shows the contrast.
6. "Ana studied the map she planned the route." Answer: Ana studied the map, and she planned the route. Hint: Two complete thoughts need a comma and a joining word.
7. "The science fair starts Friday, judges will arrive early." Answer: The science fair starts Friday, and judges will arrive early. Hint: A comma alone cannot join two sentences, so add a joining word that adds the second idea.
8. "The bus broke down the students waited outside." Answer: The bus broke down. The students waited outside. Hint: Split the fused sentence with a period.
9. "Practice ended late, the team still cleaned the field." Answer: Practice ended late, but the team still cleaned the field. Hint: Join the sentences with a comma and a joining word that shows the contrast.
10. "The museum is closed today it reopens tomorrow." Answer: The museum is closed today. It reopens tomorrow. Hint: Split the fused sentence into two sentences.
11. "Sam loves history, he reads about ancient Rome." Answer: Sam loves history, and he reads about ancient Rome. Hint: Add a joining word after the comma to link the two related ideas.
12. "The rain flooded the field the game moved indoors." Answer: The rain flooded the field, so the game moved indoors. Hint: Use a comma and a joining word that shows the result.

### Commas with Introductory Phrases and Clauses

See Commas with Introductory Phrases and Clauses under Commas for Clarity above.

## Pathway: Pronoun-Antecedent Agreement (3 topics)

### Identifying Pronouns

**Teaching card**
Definition: A pronoun is a word that takes the place of a noun, and its antecedent is the noun it refers to, so the pronoun must match that noun in number and gender when gender applies.
Worked example: Ana left her notebook on the desk. [pronoun: her] [antecedent: Ana]
How it works: Her takes the place of Ana, so it refers back to Ana and agrees with her as a singular person.
Tip box: Try it: The dogs chased their ball across the yard. Their refers to the dogs. Try it: Leo said he would bring the poster. He refers to Leo.

**Quiz**
1. "Sam finished his homework before dinner." Answer: his. Hint: The pronoun takes the place of Sam, so find the word that refers back to Sam.
2. "The students put their books in their lockers." Answer: their. Hint: The pronoun refers to the students, a plural group.
3. "Maya said she would lead the discussion." Answer: she. Hint: The pronoun takes the place of Maya.
4. "The museum opened its new exhibit on Friday." Answer: its. Hint: The pronoun refers to the museum, a singular thing.
5. "Leo and Ana brought their lunches from home." Answer: their. Hint: The pronoun refers to Leo and Ana together.
6. "The class finished its mural before the art show." Answer: its. Hint: The pronoun refers to the class as one group.
7. "Ben said that he would meet the group at noon." Answer: he. Hint: The pronoun takes the place of Ben.
8. "The river flooded its banks after the storm." Answer: its. Hint: The pronoun refers to the river, a singular thing.
9. "Priya checked her schedule before the meeting." Answer: her. Hint: The pronoun takes the place of Priya.
10. "The players celebrated their win after the game." Answer: their. Hint: The pronoun refers to the players, a plural group.
11. "The school published its calendar for the year." Answer: its. Hint: The pronoun refers to the school, a singular thing.
12. "Noah said he had finished the report." Answer: he. Hint: The pronoun takes the place of Noah.

### Choosing Pronouns That Agree

**Teaching card**
Definition: A pronoun must agree with its antecedent in number, so a singular antecedent takes a singular pronoun and a plural antecedent takes a plural pronoun.
Worked example: Each student brought his or her own lunch. [antecedent: Each student, singular] [pronoun: his or her, singular]
How it works: Each student is singular, even though it refers to many students one at a time, so the singular pronoun his or her agrees with it.
Tip box: Try it: The band played its final song. Its agrees with the singular band. Try it: The musicians tuned their instruments. Their agrees with the plural musicians.

**Quiz**
1. "Each player must bring (his or her, their) own water bottle." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether the antecedent, each player, is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
2. "The team celebrated (its, their) win after the final game." Answer: its. Hint: Decide whether the sentence treats the team as one group or as separate members, then choose the matching pronoun.
3. "The students finished (his or her, their) projects on time." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether the antecedent, students, is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
4. "A teacher should keep (his or her, their) classroom organized." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether the antecedent, a teacher, is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
5. "The committee made (its, their) decision on Tuesday." Answer: its. Hint: Decide whether the sentence treats the committee as one group or as separate members, then choose the matching pronoun.
6. "Both runners tied (his or her, their) shoes before the race." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether the antecedent, both runners, is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
7. "Every musician tuned (his or her, their) instrument before the concert." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether the antecedent, every musician, is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
8. "The class voted for (its, their) favorite book." Answer: its. Hint: Decide whether the sentence treats the class as one group or as separate members, then choose the matching pronoun.
9. "Several players left (his or her, their) bags in the gym." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether the antecedent, several players, is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
10. "One student forgot (his or her, their) permission slip." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether the antecedent, one student, is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
11. "The choir sang (its, their) final song with energy." Answer: its. Hint: Decide whether the sentence treats the choir as one group or as separate members, then choose the matching pronoun.
12. "All the teachers shared (his or her, their) lesson plans." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether the antecedent, all the teachers, is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.

### Pronoun Agreement with Indefinite Antecedents

**Teaching card**
Definition: An indefinite pronoun such as everyone, anybody, each, or several can be an antecedent, and the pronoun that refers to it must match it in number, with most indefinite pronouns taking a singular pronoun.
Worked example: Everyone should bring his or her ticket to the door. [antecedent: Everyone, singular] [pronoun: his or her, singular]
How it works: Everyone feels plural because it covers many people, but it is grammatically singular, so it takes a singular pronoun.
Tip box: Try it: Nobody left his or her jacket behind. Nobody is singular, so it takes his or her. Try it: Several students shared their notes. Several is plural, so it takes their.

**Quiz**
1. "Everyone should check (his or her, their) schedule before the trip." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether everyone is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
2. "Several of the runners stretched (his or her, their) legs before the race." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether several is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
3. "Nobody remembered (his or her, their) umbrella on the rainy day." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether nobody is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
4. "Each of the artists signed (his or her, their) work." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether each is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
5. "Both of the teams celebrated (its, their) victories." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether both is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
6. "Anybody can share (his or her, their) idea in the discussion." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether anybody is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
7. "Some of the students brought (his or her, their) own laptops." Answer: their. Hint: Some can be singular or plural, so check the noun after of before you choose the pronoun.
8. "Neither of the players forgot (his or her, their) uniform." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether neither is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
9. "Many of the volunteers gave (his or her, their) time on Saturday." Answer: their. Hint: Decide whether many is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
10. "One of the singers lost (his or her, their) voice before the show." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether one is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.
11. "All of the members paid (his or her, their) dues in January." Answer: their. Hint: All can be singular or plural, so check the noun after of before you choose the pronoun.
12. "Either of the captains may use (his or her, their) own plan." Answer: his or her. Hint: Decide whether either is singular or plural, then choose the matching pronoun.

## Pathway: Restrictive and Nonrestrictive Clauses (1 topic)

### Punctuating Restrictive and Nonrestrictive Clauses

See Punctuating Restrictive and Nonrestrictive Clauses under Phrases and Dependent Clauses above.

## Pathway: Subject-Verb Agreement II (6 topics)

### Which Part of the Collective Noun Subject Agrees with the Verb?

**Teaching card**
Definition: A collective noun names a group, such as team, committee, or class, and the verb agrees with the part of the subject that the sentence treats as doing the action, either the group as one unit or its members as individuals.
Worked example: The team of players is ready for the match. [collective noun: team] [verb: is] [the part that controls the verb: team, the group as one unit]
How it works: The sentence treats the team as one unit, so the singular noun team controls the singular verb is, not the plural players.
Tip box: Try it: The committee of teachers meets on Monday. Committee controls the verb meets. Try it: The group of students are working on separate posters. Here the members act separately, so the plural idea controls are.

**Quiz**
1. "The team of runners (is, are) training for the relay." Answer: team. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the team as one unit or the runners as separate individuals.
2. "The committee of parents (meets, meet) once a month." Answer: committee. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the committee as one unit or the parents as separate individuals.
3. "The class of students (is, are) taking a field trip." Answer: class. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the class as one unit or the students as separate individuals.
4. "The band of musicians (was, were) rehearsing for the parade." Answer: band. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the band as one unit or the musicians as separate individuals.
5. "The jury of citizens (has, have) reached a verdict." Answer: jury. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the jury as one unit or the citizens as separate individuals.
6. "The crowd of fans (was, were) cheering loudly." Answer: crowd. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the crowd as one unit or the fans as separate individuals.
7. "The staff of nurses (is, are) meeting in the conference room." Answer: staff. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the staff as one unit or the nurses as separate individuals.
8. "The audience of visitors (was, were) listening to the guide." Answer: audience. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the audience as one unit or the visitors as separate individuals.
9. "The club of readers (meets, meet) every Thursday." Answer: club. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the club as one unit or the readers as separate individuals.
10. "The family of travelers (is, are) staying at the same hotel." Answer: family. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the family as one unit or the travelers as separate individuals.
11. "The panel of judges (has, have) announced the winner." Answer: panel. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the panel as one unit or the judges as separate individuals.
12. "The flock of birds (is, are) flying south for the winter." Answer: flock. Hint: Ask whether the sentence treats the flock as one unit or the birds as separate individuals.

### Agreement with Collective Nouns

**Teaching card**
Definition: A collective noun names a group, and it takes a singular verb when the group acts as one unit and a plural verb when the members act as separate individuals.
Worked example: The committee votes on Friday. [collective noun: committee] [verb: votes, singular, because the group acts as one unit]
How it works: The committee votes together as one body, so the singular verb votes agrees with the singular idea of the group.
Tip box: Try it: The team is practicing hard this week. The team acts as one unit, so is is correct. Try it: The team are wearing different shoes today. Here the members act separately, so are fits the sentence.

**Quiz**
1. "The team (is, are) ready for the championship game." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether the team acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.
2. "The committee (meets, meet) on the first Monday of each month." Answer: meets. Hint: Ask whether the committee acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.
3. "The class (was, were) quiet during the exam." Answer: was. Hint: Ask whether the class acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.
4. "The band (plays, play) its final song at the concert." Answer: plays. Hint: Ask whether the band acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.
5. "The jury (has, have) reached a decision." Answer: has. Hint: Ask whether the jury acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.
6. "The audience (was, were) pleased with the performance." Answer: was. Hint: Ask whether the audience acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.
7. "The staff (is, are) preparing the schedule for next week." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether the staff acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.
8. "The club (meets, meet) in the library after school." Answer: meets. Hint: Ask whether the club acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.
9. "The family (is, are) planning a trip for the summer." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether the family acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.
10. "The panel (has, have) chosen the winner of the contest." Answer: has. Hint: Ask whether the panel acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.
11. "The crowd (was, were) cheering after the final goal." Answer: was. Hint: Ask whether the crowd acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.
12. "The choir (sings, sing) its opening song softly." Answer: sings. Hint: Ask whether the choir acts as one unit here or its members act separately, then match the verb.

### Agreement with Parts of a Whole

**Teaching card**
Definition: When a subject names a part of a whole, such as half of the cake, most of the students, or a third of the budget, the verb agrees with the noun after of, because that noun names the whole that the part comes from.
Worked example: Half of the pizza is gone. [part: Half] [whole: pizza, singular] [verb: is, singular, because pizza is singular]
How it works: Half is only a portion, so the verb follows pizza, the singular whole, and takes the singular form is.
Tip box: Try it: Most of the students are here. The verb are follows students, the plural whole. Try it: Two-thirds of the garden is planted. The verb is follows garden, the singular whole.

**Quiz**
1. "Half of the cake (is, are) still on the table." Answer: is. Hint: Find the noun after of, cake, and decide whether it is singular or plural.
2. "Most of the students (is, are) ready for the test." Answer: are. Hint: Find the noun after of, students, and decide whether it is singular or plural.
3. "A third of the budget (goes, go) to new books." Answer: goes. Hint: Find the noun after of, budget, and decide whether it is singular or plural.
4. "Some of the water (was, were) spilled on the floor." Answer: was. Hint: Find the noun after of, water, and decide whether it is singular or plural.
5. "Half of the players (is, are) on the bench." Answer: are. Hint: Find the noun after of, players, and decide whether it is singular or plural.
6. "Most of the class (was, were) present on Monday." Answer: was. Hint: Find the noun after of, class, and decide whether it is singular or plural.
7. "Two-thirds of the tickets (has, have) been sold." Answer: have. Hint: Find the noun after of, tickets, and decide whether it is singular or plural.
8. "A quarter of the field (is, are) covered in mud." Answer: is. Hint: Find the noun after of, field, and decide whether it is singular or plural.
9. "Some of the homework (was, were) left at home." Answer: was. Hint: Find the noun after of, homework, and decide whether it is singular or plural.
10. "Half of the cookies (was, were) eaten by noon." Answer: were. Hint: Find the noun after of, cookies, and decide whether it is singular or plural.
11. "Most of the money (is, are) in the school account." Answer: is. Hint: Find the noun after of, money, and decide whether it is singular or plural.
12. "A third of the students (has, have) signed up for the trip." Answer: have. Hint: Find the noun after of, students, and decide whether it is singular or plural.

### Agreement with Math, Money, and Time

**Teaching card**
Definition: Amounts of money, time, distance, and math results are usually treated as one amount or one result, so they take a singular verb even when the number looks plural.
Worked example: Ten dollars is enough for the ticket. [amount: Ten dollars, treated as one amount] [verb: is, singular]
How it works: Ten dollars is one total amount, not ten separate dollars in this sentence, so the singular verb is agrees with the single amount.
Tip box: Try it: Two hours is a long practice. Two hours is one block of time, so is is correct. Try it: Three plus four is seven. The math result is one answer, so is is correct.

**Quiz**
1. "Five dollars (is, are) enough for lunch." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether five dollars works as one total amount or as separate units, then match the verb.
2. "Three hours (is, are) a long time to wait." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether three hours works as one total amount or as separate units, then match the verb.
3. "Ten miles (is, are) a long run for the team." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether ten miles works as one total amount or as separate units, then match the verb.
4. "Six plus two (is, are) eight." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether six plus two stands for one result or for several separate things, then match the verb.
5. "Twenty dollars (was, were) spent on supplies." Answer: was. Hint: Ask whether twenty dollars works as one total amount or as separate units, then match the verb.
6. "Two weeks (is, are) enough time to finish the project." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether two weeks works as one total amount or as separate units, then match the verb.
7. "Fifty cents (is, are) missing from the jar." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether fifty cents works as one total amount or as separate units, then match the verb.
8. "Nine minus four (is, are) five." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether nine minus four stands for one result or for several separate things, then match the verb.
9. "Eight kilometers (is, are) the length of the race." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether eight kilometers works as one total amount or as separate units, then match the verb.
10. "One hundred dollars (is, are) the cost of the trip." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether one hundred dollars works as one total amount or as separate units, then match the verb.
11. "Four days (is, are) all the time we have left." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether four days works as one total amount or as separate units, then match the verb.
12. "Twelve times three (is, are) thirty-six." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether twelve times three stands for one result or for several separate things, then match the verb.

### Agreement with Tricky Nouns

**Teaching card**
Definition: Some nouns look plural but are singular, such as news, mathematics, and physics, while others look singular but are plural, such as scissors, pants, and people, and the verb must agree with the true number of the noun.
Worked example: The news is good today. [noun: news, singular even though it ends in s] [verb: is, singular]
How it works: News names one report or one body of information, so it takes the singular verb is, even though it ends in s.
Tip box: Try it: Mathematics is my favorite subject. Mathematics is singular, so it takes is. Try it: The scissors are in the drawer. Scissors is plural in form, so it takes are.

**Quiz**
1. "The news (is, are) good this morning." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether news is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.
2. "Mathematics (is, are) a required subject." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether mathematics is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.
3. "The scissors (is, are) on the desk." Answer: are. Hint: Ask whether scissors is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.
4. "Physics (is, are) taught in the new lab." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether physics is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.
5. "The pants (is, are) too long for Sam." Answer: are. Hint: Ask whether pants is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.
6. "The people (is, are) waiting outside the theater." Answer: are. Hint: Ask whether people is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.
7. "Economics (is, are) offered in the fall." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether economics is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.
8. "The glasses (is, are) on the table." Answer: are. Hint: Ask whether glasses is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.
9. "The criteria (is, are) listed on the rubric." Answer: are. Hint: Ask whether criteria is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.
10. "Civics (is, are) taught in tenth grade." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether civics is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.
11. "The police (is, are) directing traffic near the school." Answer: are. Hint: Ask whether police is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.
12. "Measles (is, are) a disease that spreads quickly." Answer: is. Hint: Ask whether measles is singular or plural in meaning, and do not judge by its ending alone.

### Agreement with Indefinite Pronouns

**Teaching card**
Definition: An indefinite pronoun is the subject when no specific noun is named, and the verb agrees with its number, so singular indefinite pronouns take singular verbs and plural indefinite pronouns take plural verbs.
Worked example: Everyone is welcome at the assembly. [indefinite pronoun: Everyone, singular] [verb: is, singular]
How it works: Everyone refers to many people one at a time, but it is grammatically singular, so it takes the singular verb is.
Tip box: Try it: Nobody has finished the quiz yet. Nobody is singular, so it takes has. Try it: Several are waiting in the hall. Several is plural, so it takes are.

**Quiz**
1. "Everyone (is, are) invited to the school fair." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether everyone is singular or plural, then match the verb.
2. "Several (is, are) planning to join the club." Answer: are. Hint: Decide whether several is singular or plural, then match the verb.
3. "Nobody (has, have) seen the missing key." Answer: has. Hint: Decide whether nobody is singular or plural, then match the verb.
4. "Each of the students (is, are) responsible for a poster." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether each is singular or plural, then match the verb.
5. "Both (is, are) ready for the final round." Answer: are. Hint: Decide whether both is singular or plural, then match the verb.
6. "Anybody (is, are) welcome to try out for the play." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether anybody is singular or plural, then match the verb.
7. "Many (is, are) hoping for good weather on Saturday." Answer: are. Hint: Decide whether many is singular or plural, then match the verb.
8. "Neither of the teams (has, have) lost a game this season." Answer: has. Hint: Decide whether neither is singular or plural, then match the verb.
9. "Somebody (is, are) knocking at the door." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether somebody is singular or plural, then match the verb.
10. "All of the seats (is, are) taken for the concert." Answer: are. Hint: All can be singular or plural, so check the noun after of before you choose the verb.
11. "One of the answers (is, are) clearly correct." Answer: is. Hint: Decide whether one is singular or plural, then match the verb.
12. "Few (is, are) willing to stay after school." Answer: are. Hint: Decide whether few is singular or plural, then match the verb.
## Pathway: Thesis Statements (3 topics)

### Identifying Vague Words and Phrases 1

**Teaching card**

**Section 1: Vague words hide the specific meaning.**
Guiding question: Does the word tell the reader exactly what, how much, or how many?
Worked passage: Ana wrote a draft about the school library. **The library got a lot of new books this year.** The word lot sounds large, but it does not tell the reader whether the library added twenty books or two hundred books, and it does not say what kinds of books arrived. A clearer version names the number and the subject, so the reader can picture the change.

**Section 2: Vague phrases weaken a claim.**
Guiding question: Does the phrase make a claim the reader can test, support, or argue against?
Worked passage: Leo planned an essay about public transportation. **Public transportation is bad in many ways.** The phrase in many ways sounds serious, but it does not name a single problem, such as late buses, high fares, or crowded routes, so the reader cannot tell what Leo will prove. A stronger thesis names one clear problem and the effect it has on riders.

**Section 3: Specific language makes a thesis useful.**
Guiding question: What detail would make the claim clear enough for the reader to follow the whole essay?
Worked passage: Sam revised a thesis about the new school schedule. **The new schedule helps students arrive on time because buses now leave ten minutes earlier.** The sentence names the change, the reason, and the result, so the reader knows what evidence to expect in the body paragraphs. Specific nouns, numbers, and causes turn a general feeling into a claim the writer can support.

**Quiz**
1. "The science fair was interesting. Sam built a model of a river system. Judges asked him about erosion. Which sentence is the vaguest?" Answer: The science fair was interesting. Hint: Look for the word that gives no detail about what made the fair stand out.
2. "The town council made some changes to the park. Workers added six new benches near the playground. They also repaired the walking path. Which phrase is the vaguest?" Answer: some changes. Hint: Look for the phrase that does not name what actually changed.
3. "Ana thinks the new lunch menu is better. She likes the fresh vegetables and the whole grain bread. She says the lines move faster now. Which sentence names the most specific details?" Answer: She likes the fresh vegetables and the whole grain bread. Hint: Look for the sentence with the most concrete nouns.
4. "The robotics club did stuff all semester. Leo programmed the robot to sort colored blocks. Ana wrote the report for the regional contest. Which sentence is the vaguest?" Answer: The robotics club did stuff all semester. Hint: Look for the word that does not name any real activity.
5. "Many people use the community center. On Saturday, forty families signed up for the garden workshop. The director added a second session in the evening. Which sentence gives the most specific evidence?" Answer: On Saturday, forty families signed up for the garden workshop. Hint: Look for the sentence with a number and a named event.
6. "The new policy is good for students. It gives every ninth grader a free bus pass. It also extends library hours until six in the evening. Which sentence is the vaguest?" Answer: The new policy is good for students. Hint: Look for the word that does not say how students benefit.
7. "The museum exhibit was nice. Visitors could touch a model of a volcano. A guide explained how lava cools into rock. Which sentence is the vaguest?" Answer: The museum exhibit was nice. Hint: Look for the word that does not describe what visitors saw or learned.
8. "Leo argues that the school should start later. He says a nine o'clock start would let students sleep an extra hour. He cites a study of teen sleep patterns. Which sentence states Leo's main claim?" Answer: Leo argues that the school should start later. Hint: Look for the sentence that says what Leo wants changed, before any reasons or evidence.
9. "Things went wrong at the concert. The sound system cut out during the second song. The lights flickered for ten minutes. Which phrase is the vaguest?" Answer: Things went wrong. Hint: Look for the phrase that does not name what went wrong.
10. "Ana wrote that recycling helps the planet a lot. Her school now recycles paper, cans, and plastic bottles. Last month, students collected two hundred pounds of paper. Which sentence is the vaguest?" Answer: Ana wrote that recycling helps the planet a lot. Hint: Look for the sentence that gives no number or result.
11. "The history project took a long time. Sam spent three weeks reading letters from the town archive. He built a timeline with twelve events. Which sentence is the vaguest?" Answer: The history project took a long time. Hint: Look for the sentence that does not say how many weeks or what Sam did.
12. "The coach says practice matters. She added two extra drills for passing and defense. She hopes the team improves soon. Which sentence states a specific claim the coach can support?" Answer: She added two extra drills for passing and defense. Hint: Look for the sentence that names exact actions.

### Is the Thesis a Fact or Opinion?

See Is the Thesis a Fact or Opinion? under Introductory Paragraphs above.

### Recognizing Redundant, Unnecessary, and Wordy Phrases

See Recognizing Redundant, Unnecessary, and Wordy Phrases under Introductory Paragraphs above.

## Pathway: Topic Sentences (3 topics)

### Recognizing Strong Topic Sentences 1: Claims

See Recognizing Strong Topic Sentences 1: Claims under Body Paragraphs: Claims, Evidence, and Reasoning above.

### Identifying Vague Words and Phrases 1

See Identifying Vague Words and Phrases 1 under Thesis Statements above.

### Recognizing Redundant, Unnecessary, and Wordy Phrases

See Recognizing Redundant, Unnecessary, and Wordy Phrases under Introductory Paragraphs above.

## Pathway: Vague Pronouns (2 topics)

### Identifying Antecedents of a Vague Pronoun

**Teaching card**
A vague pronoun is a pronoun such as it, this, that, or they that does not point clearly to one specific noun, called its antecedent. When the antecedent is missing or unclear, the reader has to guess what the pronoun means.
Worked example: When Ana handed Leo the report and the folder, **she** told **him** that **it** was missing a page. The pronoun it is labeled vague because it could refer to the report or to the folder, and the sentence does not make the meaning clear.
This example works because the reader can see two possible nouns for it, so the pronoun fails to point to one antecedent.
Tip box: In the sentence "After the storm, they closed the roads," they is vague because no group is named. In the sentence "Sam read the instructions, and this confused him," this is vague because it could refer to the instructions or to one part of them.

**Quiz**
1. "When Leo handed the map and the guidebook to Ana, she said it was hard to read. Which pronoun is vague, and what is its unclear antecedent?" Answer: it, which could mean the map or the guidebook. Hint: Look for the pronoun that could point to more than one noun.
2. "Sam put the toolbox on the shelf, and then he went to the garage. Which pronoun has a clear antecedent?" Answer: he. Hint: Look for the pronoun that can point to only one person in the sentence.
3. "After the meeting, they decided to change the schedule. Which pronoun is vague?" Answer: they. Hint: Look for the pronoun that does not point to any group named in the sentence.
4. "Ana wrote the essay and emailed it to the teacher. Which pronoun has a clear antecedent?" Answer: it. Hint: Look for the pronoun and ask which noun it replaces.
5. "The coach told the players to practice, and this made them nervous. Which pronoun is vague?" Answer: this. Hint: Look for the pronoun that could mean several different things in the sentence.
6. "Leo left a jacket in the gym, and Sam found it on the bench. Which pronoun has a clear antecedent?" Answer: it. Hint: Look for the pronoun and ask which object it replaces.
7. "When the power went out in the lab, it stopped working. Which pronoun is vague?" Answer: it. Hint: Look for the pronoun that does not point to any clear noun.
8. "Ana showed Sam the photograph, and he said that was his favorite trip. Which pronoun is vague?" Answer: that. Hint: Look for the pronoun that could point to two different things in the sentence.
9. "The librarian shelved the books and then she locked the door. Which pronoun has a clear antecedent?" Answer: she. Hint: Look for the pronoun and ask which person it replaces.
10. "Sam studied the diagram, but it still looked confusing. Which pronoun has a clear antecedent?" Answer: it. Hint: Look for the pronoun and ask which noun it replaces.
11. "In the lab, they mixed the chemicals carefully. Which pronoun is vague?" Answer: they. Hint: Look for the pronoun that does not name any specific people.
12. "Leo read the article about Mars, and this surprised him. Which pronoun is vague?" Answer: this. Hint: Look for the pronoun that could point to more than one idea.

### Correcting Pronouns with Multiple Antecedents

**Teaching card**
A pronoun has multiple antecedents when two nouns in the sentence could both be the word the pronoun replaces, so the reader cannot tell which one is meant. To correct the sentence, replace the pronoun with the exact noun, or rewrite the sentence so only one noun can fit.
Worked example: Ana gave Leo the notebook and the binder, and **he** thanked **her**, but **it** still had a torn page. Corrected: Ana gave Leo the notebook and the binder, and he thanked her, but the notebook still had a torn page. The pronoun it is labeled vague because it could refer to the notebook or to the binder, and the correction names the notebook.
This example works because replacing it with the notebook leaves only one possible meaning.
Tip box: In the sentence "Sam told Leo that he won the award," he is unclear, so write "Sam told Leo that Leo won the award." In the sentence "Ana put the plant on the table, and it wobbled," it is unclear, so write "Ana put the plant on the table, and the table wobbled."

**Quiz**
1. "Leo gave the book to Sam, and he said it was exciting. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: Leo gave the book to Sam, and Sam said it was exciting. Hint: Replace the unclear he with the exact name.
2. "Ana left the box on the desk, and it was locked. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: Ana left the box on the desk, and the box was locked. Hint: Name the noun the pronoun was meant to replace.
3. "Sam showed Leo the telescope, and he adjusted it. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: Sam showed Leo the telescope, and Leo adjusted it. Hint: Use the exact name instead of the unclear pronoun.
4. "The teacher handed the test to Ana, and she finished it quickly. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: The teacher handed the test to Ana, and Ana finished it quickly. Hint: Replace she with the exact person.
5. "Leo put the box on the shelf, and it broke. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: Leo put the box on the shelf, and the shelf broke. Hint: Name the noun that broke.
6. "Ana told Sam that she would lead the project. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: Ana told Sam that Ana would lead the project. Hint: Repeat the name when two people could fit the pronoun.
7. "Sam gave Leo the camera, and he took a photo of the river. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: Sam gave Leo the camera, and Leo took a photo of the river. Hint: Replace he with the person who took the photo.
8. "The gardener watered the plant on the porch, and it was dry. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: The gardener watered the plant on the porch, and the plant was dry. Hint: Name the noun that was dry.
9. "Leo told Sam that his notebook was missing. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: Leo told Sam that Sam's notebook was missing. Hint: Replace the unclear his with the name of the owner.
10. "Sam placed the laptop on the table, and it overheated. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: Sam placed the laptop on the table, and the laptop overheated. Hint: Replace it with the exact noun.
11. "Ana met Leo at the library, and he showed her the new atlas. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: Ana met Leo at the library, and Leo showed her the new atlas. Hint: Use the name instead of the unclear he.
12. "The coach gave the ball to Sam, and he kicked it across the field. Which corrected sentence makes the meaning clear?" Answer: The coach gave the ball to Sam, and Sam kicked it across the field. Hint: Replace he with the player who kicked the ball.

## Pathway: Verbals (4 topics)

### Identifying Verbs in Simple, Progressive, and Perfect Tenses

**Teaching card**
A verb tense shows when an action happens. The simple tense states a fact or a single action, the progressive tense shows an action in progress with a form of be plus an ing verb, and the perfect tense shows a completed action with a form of have plus a past participle.
Worked example: Ana **has finished** her lab report, while Leo **is writing** his, and Sam **writes** his notes every evening. The phrase has finished is labeled perfect tense, is writing is labeled progressive tense, and writes is labeled simple present tense.
This example works because each verb form matches its time pattern, with have plus finished for completion, be plus writing for an action in progress, and writes alone for a regular action.
Tip box: In the sentence "They had practiced for three weeks," had practiced is past perfect tense. In the sentence "She is reading a biography," is reading is present progressive tense.

**Quiz**
1. "Ana walks to school every morning. Which verb is in the simple present tense?" Answer: walks. Hint: A simple present verb states a regular action without a helping verb.
2. "Leo is building a model bridge for science class. Which verb phrase is in the present progressive tense?" Answer: is building. Hint: A progressive verb uses a form of be plus an ing verb.
3. "Sam has completed all three practice tests. Which verb phrase is in the present perfect tense?" Answer: has completed. Hint: A perfect verb uses a form of have plus a past participle.
4. "They had rehearsed the play for two weeks before opening night. Which verb phrase is in the past perfect tense?" Answer: had rehearsed. Hint: Had plus a past participle shows an action completed before another past action.
5. "Ana was studying when the lights went out. Which verb phrase is in the past progressive tense?" Answer: was studying. Hint: Was plus an ing verb shows an action in progress in the past.
6. "Leo will have finished his essay by Friday. Which verb phrase is in the future perfect tense?" Answer: will have finished. Hint: Will have plus a past participle shows an action completed by a future time.
7. "The team practices every Tuesday after school. Which verb is in the simple present tense?" Answer: practices. Hint: A simple verb alone can show a repeated action.
8. "Sam is training for the track meet this spring. Which verb phrase is in the present progressive tense?" Answer: is training. Hint: Is plus an ing verb shows an action in progress now.
9. "Ana had visited the museum before it closed for repairs. Which verb phrase is in the past perfect tense?" Answer: had visited. Hint: Had plus a past participle shows an earlier completed action.
10. "They will be presenting their research next month. Which verb phrase is in the future progressive tense?" Answer: will be presenting. Hint: Will be plus an ing verb shows an action in progress in the future.
11. "Leo wrote a poem about the river last year. Which verb is in the simple past tense?" Answer: wrote. Hint: A simple past verb shows a completed action without a helping verb.
12. "Sam has read four books about space exploration. Which verb phrase is in the present perfect tense?" Answer: has read. Hint: Has plus a past participle shows an action completed by now.

### Is It a Verb or Not a Verb?

**Teaching card**
A verb shows an action or a state of being, and it changes form to show tense. A word that looks like a verb but acts as a noun, an adjective, or part of another phrase is not the main verb of the sentence.
Worked example: **Swimming** in the lake **is** Ana's favorite summer activity. The word swimming is labeled not a verb because it acts as a noun naming the activity, while is is labeled the main verb because it links the subject to the description.
This example works because swimming names a thing in this sentence, even though it ends in ing, while is does the work of the verb.
Tip box: In the sentence "Leo likes to read history," to read is not the main verb because likes is the verb that shows what Leo does. In the sentence "The broken bridge closed the road," broken is not a verb because it describes the bridge.

**Quiz**
1. "Running every morning keeps Sam healthy. Which word is not the main verb?" Answer: Running. Hint: Look for the ing word that names an activity instead of showing an action.
2. "Ana enjoys painting landscapes. Which word is the main verb?" Answer: enjoys. Hint: The main verb shows what Ana does.
3. "To travel by train saves time. Which word is not the main verb?" Answer: To travel. Hint: Look for the to phrase that names an activity and acts as the subject.
4. "The smiling student raised her hand. Which word is not a verb?" Answer: smiling. Hint: Look for the ing word that describes a noun.
5. "Leo wants to join the debate team. Which word is the main verb?" Answer: wants. Hint: The main verb is the word that shows Leo's desire.
6. "Reading maps helps Ana on long trips. Which word is not the main verb?" Answer: Reading. Hint: Look for the ing word that names an activity.
7. "The tired runners finished the race. Which word is not a verb?" Answer: tired. Hint: Look for the word that describes a noun even though it looks like a past tense verb.
8. "Sam hopes to visit the science museum. Which word is the main verb?" Answer: hopes. Hint: Find the word that shows what Sam does, not the to phrase.
9. "Writing clearly takes practice. Which word is not the main verb?" Answer: Writing. Hint: Look for the ing word that names a skill.
10. "Ana decided to enter the art contest. Which word is the main verb?" Answer: decided. Hint: Find the word that shows Ana's action, not the to phrase.
11. "The barking dog woke the neighbors. Which word is not a verb?" Answer: barking. Hint: Look for the ing word that describes a noun.
12. "Leo loves building model rockets. Which word is the main verb?" Answer: loves. Hint: Find the word that shows what Leo does, not the ing word that names an activity.

### Identifying Modifiers

**Teaching card**
A modifier is a word or phrase that describes or limits another word, usually by telling which one, what kind, how, when, or where. Adjectives modify nouns and pronouns, and adverbs modify verbs, adjectives, and other adverbs.
Worked example: Ana read the **long** report **carefully** in the **quiet** library. The word long is labeled an adjective modifying report, carefully is labeled an adverb modifying read, and quiet is labeled an adjective modifying library.
This example works because each modifier adds a clear detail, with long and quiet telling what kind of report and library, and carefully telling how Ana read.
Tip box: In the sentence "Leo spoke softly during the meeting," softly is an adverb modifying spoke. In the sentence "Sam bought three new notebooks," three and new are adjectives modifying notebooks.

**Quiz**
1. "Ana wore a bright jacket to the game. Which word is a modifier?" Answer: bright. Hint: Look for the word that describes the jacket.
2. "Leo finished his homework quickly. Which word is a modifier?" Answer: quickly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Leo finished.
3. "Sam planted two rows of tomatoes in the garden. Which word is a modifier?" Answer: two. Hint: Look for the word that tells how many rows Sam planted.
4. "The old bridge crosses the wide river. Which words are modifiers?" Answer: old and wide. Hint: Look for the words that describe the bridge and the river.
5. "Ana spoke clearly during her presentation. Which word is a modifier?" Answer: clearly. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Ana spoke.
6. "Leo carried a heavy backpack up the hill. Which word is a modifier?" Answer: heavy. Hint: Look for the word that describes the backpack.
7. "Sam arrived early for the science fair. Which word is a modifier?" Answer: early. Hint: Look for the word that tells when Sam arrived.
8. "The tall trees shaded the small playground. Which words are modifiers?" Answer: tall and small. Hint: Look for the words that describe the trees and the playground.
9. "Ana carefully measured the water for the experiment. Which word is a modifier?" Answer: carefully. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Ana measured.
10. "Leo bought a red bicycle with silver handlebars. Which words are modifiers?" Answer: red and silver. Hint: Look for the words that describe the bicycle and the handlebars.
11. "Sam ran fast during the race. Which word is a modifier?" Answer: fast. Hint: Look for the word that tells how Sam ran.
12. "The quiet student wrote a thoughtful essay. Which words are modifiers?" Answer: quiet and thoughtful. Hint: Look for the words that describe the student and the essay.

### Identifying the Function of Verbals

**Teaching card**
A verbal is a verb form that acts as another part of speech. A gerund ends in ing and acts as a noun, a participle acts as an adjective, and an infinitive begins with to plus a verb and can act as a noun, an adjective, or an adverb.
Worked example: **Swimming** laps **tired** Leo, who wanted **to rest** before dinner. The word swimming is labeled a gerund acting as a noun subject, tired is labeled the main verb, and to rest is labeled an infinitive acting as a noun naming what Leo wanted.
This example works because swimming names an activity instead of showing an action, while to rest names the thing Leo wanted.
Tip box: In the sentence "The broken vase sat on the shelf," broken is a participle acting as an adjective. In the sentence "Ana has a book to read," to read is an infinitive acting as an adjective describing the book.

**Quiz**
1. "Swimming in the morning wakes Ana up. What is the function of swimming?" Answer: gerund acting as a noun. Hint: Ask whether swimming names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.
2. "Leo wants to learn Spanish. What is the function of to learn?" Answer: infinitive acting as a noun. Hint: Ask whether to learn names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.
3. "The fallen leaves covered the sidewalk. What is the function of fallen?" Answer: participle acting as an adjective. Hint: Ask whether fallen names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.
4. "Sam enjoys cooking dinner for his family. What is the function of cooking?" Answer: gerund acting as a noun. Hint: Ask whether cooking names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.
5. "Ana bought paint to finish the mural. What is the function of to finish?" Answer: infinitive acting as an adverb. Hint: Ask whether to finish names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.
6. "The smiling teacher greeted the class. What is the function of smiling?" Answer: participle acting as an adjective. Hint: Ask whether smiling names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.
7. "Reading novels helps Leo understand history. What is the function of reading?" Answer: gerund acting as a noun. Hint: Ask whether reading names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.
8. "Sam has a report to write before Friday. What is the function of to write?" Answer: infinitive acting as an adjective. Hint: Ask whether to write names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.
9. "The barking dog ran across the yard. What is the function of barking?" Answer: participle acting as an adjective. Hint: Ask whether barking names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.
10. "Ana hopes to visit the observatory this year. What is the function of to visit?" Answer: infinitive acting as a noun. Hint: Ask whether to visit names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.
11. "Hiking through the hills tired the whole group. What is the function of hiking?" Answer: gerund acting as a noun. Hint: Ask whether hiking names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.
12. "Leo found a place to study after school. What is the function of to study?" Answer: infinitive acting as an adjective. Hint: Ask whether to study names something, describes a noun, or tells why something happens.

## Pathway: Appositive Phrases (1 topic)

### What Does the Appositive Refer To?

See What Does the Appositive Refer To? under Phrases and Dependent Clauses above.

## Pathway: Building Compound and Complex Sentences (10 topics)

### Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions (FANBOYS)

See Identifying Coordinating Conjunctions (FANBOYS) under Components of a Sentence II above.

### Identifying the Function of FANBOYS

**Teaching card**
FANBOYS are the coordinating conjunctions for, and, nor, but, or, yet, and so. Each one joins two equal parts of a sentence, and each one shows a different relationship, such as addition, contrast, choice, or result.
Worked example: Ana studied for the test, **but** Leo **and** Sam went to practice, **so** they missed the review session. The word but is labeled contrast, and is labeled addition, and so is labeled result.
This example works because each conjunction joins two equal ideas and signals how the second idea relates to the first.
Tip box: In the sentence "Leo wanted to stay, yet he left early," yet shows contrast. In the sentence "Sam could walk or ride his bike," or shows a choice.

**Quiz**
1. "Ana likes science, and Leo likes history. Which FANBOYS word shows addition?" Answer: and. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals addition.
2. "Sam studied hard, but he still felt nervous. Which FANBOYS word shows contrast?" Answer: but. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals contrast.
3. "Leo could join the choir, or he could join the band. Which FANBOYS word shows a choice?" Answer: or. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals a choice.
4. "It rained all morning, so the game moved indoors. Which FANBOYS word shows a result?" Answer: so. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals a result.
5. "Ana did not want pizza, nor did she want pasta. Which FANBOYS word shows a negative addition?" Answer: nor. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals a negative addition.
6. "The trail was steep, yet the hikers kept going. Which FANBOYS word shows contrast?" Answer: yet. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals contrast.
7. "Sam was tired, for he had practiced since dawn. Which FANBOYS word shows a reason?" Answer: for. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals a reason.
8. "Leo likes to read, and Ana likes to draw. Which FANBOYS word shows addition?" Answer: and. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals addition.
9. "Ana could study tonight, or she could study in the morning. Which FANBOYS word shows a choice?" Answer: or. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals a choice.
10. "The bus was late, so Sam walked to school. Which FANBOYS word shows a result?" Answer: so. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals a result.
11. "Sam wanted to win, but his team lost the final game. Which FANBOYS word shows contrast?" Answer: but. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals contrast.
12. "Leo is small, yet he is the fastest runner on the team. Which FANBOYS word shows contrast?" Answer: yet. Hint: Find the word that joins the two ideas, and check that it signals contrast.

### Punctuating FANBOYS

**Teaching card**
When a FANBOYS conjunction joins two complete sentences, put a comma before the conjunction. When it joins only two words or short phrases, do not use a comma.
Worked example: Ana finished her homework, **and** she read a chapter for history. The comma before and is labeled correct because and joins two complete sentences, Ana finished her homework and she read a chapter for history.
This example works because each side of and can stand alone as a sentence, so the comma marks the join.
Tip box: In the sentence "Leo bought apples and oranges," no comma is needed because and joins two words. In the sentence "Sam ran fast, but he missed the bus," a comma goes before but because it joins two complete sentences.

**Quiz**
1. "Ana studied all night and she passed the test. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Ana studied all night, and she passed the test. Hint: Check whether the words on each side of and could stand alone as complete sentences.
2. "Leo likes soccer and basketball. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Leo likes soccer and basketball. Hint: Check whether the words on each side of and could stand alone as complete sentences.
3. "Sam was tired but he kept working. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Sam was tired, but he kept working. Hint: Check whether the words on each side of but could stand alone as complete sentences.
4. "Ana could walk to school or ride her bike. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Ana could walk to school or ride her bike. Hint: Check whether the words on each side of or could stand alone as complete sentences.
5. "It rained all day so the picnic moved inside. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: It rained all day, so the picnic moved inside. Hint: Check whether the words on each side of so could stand alone as complete sentences.
6. "Leo wanted to stay yet he left early. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Leo wanted to stay, yet he left early. Hint: Check whether the words on each side of yet could stand alone as complete sentences.
7. "Sam bought pens pencils and notebooks. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Sam bought pens, pencils, and notebooks. Hint: Count the items in the list, and remember how a list of three or more items is punctuated.
8. "Ana did not want tea nor did she want coffee. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Ana did not want tea, nor did she want coffee. Hint: Check whether the words on each side of nor could stand alone as complete sentences.
9. "The team practiced hard and won the game. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: The team practiced hard and won the game. Hint: Check whether the words on each side of and could stand alone as complete sentences.
10. "Leo read the chapter and he took notes. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Leo read the chapter, and he took notes. Hint: Check whether the words on each side of and could stand alone as complete sentences.
11. "Sam was hungry for he had skipped lunch. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Sam was hungry, for he had skipped lunch. Hint: Check whether the words on each side of for could stand alone as complete sentences.
12. "Ana likes to swim or run in the morning. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Ana likes to swim or run in the morning. Hint: Check whether the words on each side of or could stand alone as complete sentences.

### Correcting Comma Splices and Fused Sentences

See Correcting Comma Splices and Fused Sentences under Phrases and Dependent Clauses above.

### Identifying Conjunctive Adverbs

See Identifying Conjunctive Adverbs under Components of a Sentence II above.

### Punctuating Conjunctive Adverbs at the Beginning of a Sentence

**Teaching card**
A conjunctive adverb is a connecting word such as however, therefore, moreover, consequently, furthermore, nevertheless, or meanwhile. When a conjunctive adverb begins a sentence, put a comma after it to separate it from the rest of the sentence.
Worked example: **However,** Ana decided to rewrite the essay after she found a stronger source. The comma after However is labeled correct because However opens the sentence and needs a comma before the main clause.
This example works because the comma tells the reader where the introductory conjunctive adverb ends and the main idea begins.
Tip box: In the sentence "Therefore, the team changed its plan," a comma follows Therefore. In the sentence "Meanwhile, Leo finished the first draft," a comma follows Meanwhile.

**Quiz**
1. "However Ana kept working on the project. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: However, Ana kept working on the project. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.
2. "Therefore the school added a second bus route. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Therefore, the school added a second bus route. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.
3. "Moreover the library will stay open later on weekdays. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Moreover, the library will stay open later on weekdays. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.
4. "Consequently Sam missed the morning meeting. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Consequently, Sam missed the morning meeting. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.
5. "Furthermore Leo joined the robotics club. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Furthermore, Leo joined the robotics club. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.
6. "Nevertheless Ana finished the race. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Nevertheless, Ana finished the race. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.
7. "Meanwhile the teacher prepared the next lesson. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Meanwhile, the teacher prepared the next lesson. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.
8. "However the rain did not stop the outdoor fair. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: However, the rain did not stop the outdoor fair. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.
9. "Therefore Leo decided to study engineering. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Therefore, Leo decided to study engineering. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.
10. "Moreover Sam volunteered at the food bank. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Moreover, Sam volunteered at the food bank. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.
11. "Consequently the team practiced indoors all week. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Consequently, the team practiced indoors all week. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.
12. "Meanwhile Ana read the next chapter. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Meanwhile, Ana read the next chapter. Hint: Put a comma after a conjunctive adverb at the start of a sentence.

### Punctuating Conjunctive Adverbs in the Middle of a Sentence

**Teaching card**
When a conjunctive adverb joins two complete sentences in the middle, put a semicolon before it and a comma after it. The semicolon shows that the two sentences are closely linked, and the comma separates the conjunctive adverb from the second sentence.
Worked example: Ana studied the map carefully**; however,** she still took the wrong road. The semicolon before however and the comma after it are labeled correct because however joins two complete sentences.
This example works because the semicolon marks the break between two full ideas, while the comma sets off the conjunctive adverb that starts the second idea.
Tip box: In the sentence "Leo wanted to help; therefore, he stayed after class," a semicolon comes before therefore and a comma comes after it. In the sentence "The rain stopped; meanwhile, Sam dried the equipment," a semicolon comes before meanwhile and a comma comes after it.

**Quiz**
1. "Ana studied hard, however she still felt nervous. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Ana studied hard; however, she still felt nervous. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.
2. "Leo missed the bus therefore he walked to school. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Leo missed the bus; therefore, he walked to school. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.
3. "Sam practiced every day moreover he helped his teammates. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Sam practiced every day; moreover, he helped his teammates. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.
4. "The team lost the game nevertheless they played well. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: The team lost the game; nevertheless, they played well. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.
5. "Ana finished her essay meanwhile Leo edited his report. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Ana finished her essay; meanwhile, Leo edited his report. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.
6. "It rained all morning consequently the field was muddy. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: It rained all morning; consequently, the field was muddy. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.
7. "Leo wanted to join the club furthermore he wanted to lead it. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Leo wanted to join the club; furthermore, he wanted to lead it. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.
