8. "Sam was tired however he finished the race. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Sam was tired; however, he finished the race. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.
9. "Ana saved her money therefore she could buy the camera. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Ana saved her money; therefore, she could buy the camera. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.
10. "The library closed early meanwhile the students studied at home. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: The library closed early; meanwhile, the students studied at home. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.
11. "Leo read the instructions moreover he watched a tutorial. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Leo read the instructions; moreover, he watched a tutorial. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.
12. "Sam missed practice consequently he sat out the first game. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Sam missed practice; consequently, he sat out the first game. Hint: Use a semicolon before a conjunctive adverb in the middle and a comma after it.

### Identifying Subordinating Conjunctions

See Identifying Subordinating Conjunctions under Components of a Sentence II above.

### Punctuating Subordinating Conjunctions at the Beginning of a Sentence

**Teaching card**
A subordinating conjunction is a word such as since, when, although, because, if, while, after, before, unless, or as that begins a dependent clause. When a clause that starts with a subordinating conjunction begins a sentence, put a comma after the whole dependent clause, before the main clause starts.
Worked example: **Because Ana studied the map,** she found the trail without help. The comma after the clause Because Ana studied the map is labeled correct because the dependent clause opens the sentence and needs a comma before the main clause.
This example works because the comma tells the reader where the dependent clause ends and the main idea begins.
Tip box: In the sentence "Although it rained, the game continued," a comma follows the although clause. In the sentence "When Leo arrived, the meeting had already started," a comma follows the when clause.

**Quiz**
1. "Because Sam practiced daily he made the team. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Because Sam practiced daily, he made the team. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.
2. "Although it was cold Ana went for a run. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Although it was cold, Ana went for a run. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.
3. "When Leo finished his homework he called Sam. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: When Leo finished his homework, he called Sam. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.
4. "Since the library was closed Ana studied at home. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Since the library was closed, Ana studied at home. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.
5. "If it rains tomorrow the picnic will move indoors. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: If it rains tomorrow, the picnic will move indoors. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.
6. "While Sam cooked dinner Leo set the table. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: While Sam cooked dinner, Leo set the table. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.
7. "After the game ended the team celebrated together. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: After the game ended, the team celebrated together. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.
8. "Before the test began Ana reviewed her notes. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Before the test began, Ana reviewed her notes. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.
9. "Unless Leo hurries he will miss the bus. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Unless Leo hurries, he will miss the bus. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.
10. "As the sun set the hikers reached the summit. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: As the sun set, the hikers reached the summit. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.
11. "Because Ana saved her allowance she bought a new bicycle. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Because Ana saved her allowance, she bought a new bicycle. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.
12. "Although Sam was nervous he gave a clear speech. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Although Sam was nervous, he gave a clear speech. Hint: Put a comma after a dependent clause that opens a sentence.

### Punctuating Subordinating Conjunctions in the Middle of a Sentence

**Teaching card**
When a clause that starts with a subordinating conjunction comes after the main clause, do not use a comma before the subordinating conjunction in most cases. The main clause comes first, and the dependent clause follows naturally without a pause.
Worked example: Ana found the trail without help **because she studied the map**. No comma before because is labeled correct because the dependent clause follows the main clause and completes its meaning.
This example works because the because clause is essential to the sentence, so it needs no comma to separate it from the main clause.
Tip box: In the sentence "Leo stayed home because he was sick," no comma comes before because. In the sentence "Sam will call Ana when he finishes practice," no comma comes before when.

**Quiz**
1. "Ana stayed after class, because she needed help. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Ana stayed after class because she needed help. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause.
2. "Leo went for a run, although it was raining. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Leo went for a run although it was raining. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause in most cases.
3. "Sam made the team, because he practiced daily. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Sam made the team because he practiced daily. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause.
4. "Ana will study at home, if the library is closed. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Ana will study at home if the library is closed. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause.
5. "Leo called Sam, when he finished his homework. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Leo called Sam when he finished his homework. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause.
6. "The game continued, although it rained. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: The game continued although it rained. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause in most cases.
7. "Sam cooked dinner, while Leo set the table. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Sam cooked dinner while Leo set the table. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause.
8. "Ana bought a bicycle, because she had saved her allowance. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Ana bought a bicycle because she had saved her allowance. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause.
9. "Leo will miss the bus, unless he hurries. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Leo will miss the bus unless he hurries. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause.
10. "The hikers reached the summit, as the sun set. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: The hikers reached the summit as the sun set. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause.
11. "Sam gave a clear speech, although he was nervous. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Sam gave a clear speech although he was nervous. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause in most cases.
12. "Ana reviewed her notes, before the test began. What is the correctly punctuated version?" Answer: Ana reviewed her notes before the test began. Hint: Do not use a comma before a dependent clause that follows the main clause.
# Grammar Lab, Grades 9-12, Part 5

## Pathway: Commonly Confused Words IV (7 topics)

### "Cite" vs. "Sight" vs. "Site"

**Teaching card**
Definition: Cite is a verb that means to quote or refer to a source as evidence, sight is a noun or verb that refers to vision or something seen, and site is a noun that means a location or place where something happens or is built.
Worked example: Ana had to cite her source in the essay, and she visited the site of the old mill where she caught sight of a heron. Labels: cite, verb meaning to quote a source; site, noun meaning a location; sight, noun meaning something seen.
How it works: Each word sounds alike but has a different spelling and meaning, so the meaning of the sentence tells you which spelling fits.
Tip box: Sam will cite the article in his report. The construction site is closed on weekends, but the view from the hill is a beautiful sight.

**Quiz**
1. "Please ___ the page number for that fact in your essay." Answer: cite. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.
2. "The new library will be built on the empty ___ near the park." Answer: site. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.
3. "From the tower, Leo had a clear ___ of the whole valley." Answer: sight. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.
4. "The teacher asked Ana to ___ two sources for her argument." Answer: cite. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.
5. "Workers fenced off the ___ before the building work began." Answer: site. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.
6. "After the fog lifted, the ___ of the lake surprised everyone." Answer: sight. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.
7. "Sam forgot to ___ the book, so his teacher asked him to add it." Answer: cite. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.
8. "The science class visited the ___ where the river samples were taken." Answer: site. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.
9. "Leo lost ___ of the ball when it flew into the bright sun." Answer: sight. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.
10. "The author will ___ a study that supports her claim." Answer: cite. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.
11. "That ___ is famous because explorers once camped there." Answer: site. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.
12. "At first ___ , the plan looked simple, but it needed more work." Answer: sight. Hint: Cite means to quote a source, sight means vision or something seen, and site means a place, so ask which meaning the blank needs.

### "Scene" vs. "Seen"

**Teaching card**
Definition: Scene is a noun that means a place, a view, or a part of a play, film, or story, while seen is the past participle of see and means viewed or noticed in the past.
Worked example: Ana had seen the opening scene of the play twice before. Labels: seen, past participle of see; scene, noun meaning a part of a play.
How it works: If you can replace the word with a part of a story or a place, use scene, and if it follows have, has, or had and means viewed, use seen.
Tip box: The final scene takes place in a busy market. Leo has seen that film three times this year.

**Quiz**
1. "The first ___ of the film shows a quiet village at dawn." Answer: scene. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.
2. "Have you ___ the new exhibit at the science museum?" Answer: seen. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.
3. "Sam described the ___ at the harbor, with boats and gulls everywhere." Answer: scene. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.
4. "Ana had never ___ snow fall so heavily in March." Answer: seen. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.
5. "The director cut a long ___ from the middle of the play." Answer: scene. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.
6. "Leo has ___ every game this season from the front row." Answer: seen. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.
7. "The ___ outside the library was calm after the storm passed." Answer: scene. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.
8. "They had ___ the comet through the telescope the night before." Answer: seen. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.
9. "In the last ___ , the two friends finally agree." Answer: scene. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.
10. "Has anyone ___ my notebook on the table by the window?" Answer: seen. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.
11. "The busy market ___ made a good opening for the story." Answer: scene. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.
12. "Sam has ___ that experiment work only once in class." Answer: seen. Hint: Scene names a place, a view, or part of a story, while seen follows have, has, or had, so check the words around the blank.

### "Advice" vs. "Advise"

**Teaching card**
Definition: Advice is a noun that means a suggestion or guidance offered to help someone decide, while advise is a verb that means to give advice or to recommend an action.
Worked example: The coach will advise Sam, and her advice is to practice every day. Labels: advise, verb meaning to give guidance; advice, noun meaning the guidance itself.
How it works: If the word names the suggestion, use advice with a c, and if it describes the act of giving that suggestion, use advise with an s.
Tip box: Ana asked her teacher for advice about the project. The teacher will advise her to start with an outline.

**Quiz**
1. "Leo asked his coach for ___ about improving his serve." Answer: advice. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.
2. "The librarian will ___ students to check the catalog first." Answer: advise. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.
3. "Good ___ can save a lot of time on a long project." Answer: advice. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.
4. "Ana will ___ her friend to bring water on the hike." Answer: advise. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.
5. "Sam took his teacher's ___ and revised the introduction." Answer: advice. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.
6. "The counselor may ___ you to meet with the tutor twice a week." Answer: advise. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.
7. "Leo ignored the ___ and arrived late again." Answer: advice. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.
8. "Please ___ the team about the change in the schedule." Answer: advise. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.
9. "Her ___ was simple: read the question twice before answering." Answer: advice. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.
10. "The principal will ___ parents to arrive early for the meeting." Answer: advise. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.
11. "Sam thanked Ana for her helpful ___ on the science fair." Answer: advice. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.
12. "A mentor can ___ a student without making the choice for them." Answer: advise. Hint: Advice is a noun that names guidance, and advise is a verb that means to give guidance, so ask whether the blank names a thing or shows an action.

### "Farther" vs. "Further"

**Teaching card**
Definition: Farther refers to a greater physical distance, while further refers to a greater extent, degree, or amount, or to something additional in a discussion or idea.
Worked example: Leo ran farther than Sam, and the teacher asked for further detail about the results. Labels: farther, greater physical distance; further, greater extent or additional detail.
How it works: If you can measure the distance in miles or meters, use farther, and if you mean more in an abstract sense, use further.
Tip box: The new trail goes farther into the woods than the old one. Ana needs further time to finish the research.

**Quiz**
1. "The library is ___ from school than the community center is." Answer: farther. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.
2. "Leo asked for ___ information about the summer program." Answer: further. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.
3. "Sam threw the ball ___ than anyone else on the team." Answer: farther. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.
4. "The committee will discuss the plan ___ at the next meeting." Answer: further. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.
5. "Ana walked ___ along the beach before she turned back." Answer: farther. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.
6. "We need ___ proof before we change the conclusion." Answer: further. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.
7. "The north trail leads ___ up the mountain than the south trail." Answer: farther. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.
8. "Leo will look into the question ___ after class." Answer: further. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.
9. "The boat drifted ___ from shore than the crew expected." Answer: farther. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.
10. "Without ___ discussion, the group voted to start the project." Answer: further. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.
11. "Sam lives ___ from the station than Ana does." Answer: farther. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.
12. "The teacher asked for ___ examples to support the claim." Answer: further. Hint: Farther is for physical distance, and further is for extent, degree, or something additional, so ask whether the sentence is about a distance you could measure.

### "Anyway" vs. "Anyways"

**Teaching card**
Definition: Anyway is the standard adverb that means in any case, regardless, or in any way, while anyways is a nonstandard informal variant that should be avoided in school and formal writing.
Worked example: The rain continued, but Leo decided to practice anyway. Labels: anyway, standard adverb meaning in any case.
How it works: In essays, reports, and other formal writing, use anyway, and save anyways only for casual speech if you use it at all.
Tip box: Ana was tired, but she finished the chapter anyway. The bus was late, but Sam got to school anyway.

**Quiz**
1. "The weather was cold, but we went for a walk ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.
2. "Leo missed the first bus, but he arrived on time ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.
3. "Sam did not like the topic, but he wrote the essay ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.
4. "Ana was nervous, but she gave the speech ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.
5. "The game was rained out, but the team practiced ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.
6. "Leo forgot his notes, but he answered the questions ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.
7. "The movie was long, but Ana watched it to the end ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.
8. "Sam was unsure, but he tried the new route ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.
9. "The store was crowded, but Leo found the book ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.
10. "Ana did not win, but she was proud of her effort ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.
11. "The lab was closed, but the class met outside ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.
12. "Leo was late, but he joined the discussion ___." Answer: anyway. Hint: Ask which of the two forms is standard in school and formal writing.

### "Altogether" vs. "All together"

**Teaching card**
Definition: Altogether is an adverb that means completely or in total, while all together is a phrase that means all in one place or all at the same time.
Worked example: The class was altogether quiet, and then they sang all together. Labels: altogether, adverb meaning completely; all together, phrase meaning as a group.
How it works: If you mean totally or in all, use the single word altogether, and if you mean the whole group acting as one, use the two words all together.
Tip box: Altogether, the trip cost less than Ana expected. The students stood all together for the photo.

**Quiz**
1. "The team was ___ silent when the score was announced." Answer: altogether. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.
2. "The choir sang ___ at the end of the concert." Answer: all together. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.
3. "___ , the three books cost forty dollars." Answer: Altogether. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.
4. "The class gathered ___ in the gym for the assembly." Answer: all together. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.
5. "Leo was ___ sure that the answer was correct." Answer: altogether. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.
6. "The friends arrived ___ at the station that morning." Answer: all together. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.
7. "Sam felt ___ tired after the long hike." Answer: altogether. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.
8. "The students worked ___ on the mural for two weeks." Answer: all together. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.
9. "___ , Ana spent three hours on the project." Answer: Altogether. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.
10. "The team stood ___ for the photo after the game." Answer: all together. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.
11. "The plan was ___ different from what Leo expected." Answer: altogether. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.
12. "The class read the poem aloud ___ on the last day." Answer: all together. Hint: Altogether means completely or in total, and all together means as a group or at the same time, so ask which meaning fits the sentence.

### "Already" vs. "All ready"

**Teaching card**
Definition: Already is an adverb that means by now or before this time, while all ready is a phrase that means fully prepared or completely ready.
Worked example: Ana had already finished her homework, and the team was all ready to leave. Labels: already, adverb meaning by now; all ready, phrase meaning fully prepared.
How it works: If you mean something happened before now, use the single word already, and if you mean everyone or everything is prepared, use the two words all ready.
Tip box: Leo had already read the chapter twice. The players were all ready when the coach arrived.

**Quiz**
1. "Sam has ___ finished his lab report." Answer: already. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.
2. "The students were ___ for the field trip by eight in the morning." Answer: all ready. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.
3. "Ana had ___ seen the film before it came to class." Answer: already. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.
4. "The musicians were ___ when the curtain opened." Answer: all ready. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.
5. "Leo ___ knew the answer before the teacher asked." Answer: already. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.
6. "Are you ___ to present your project to the class?" Answer: all ready. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.
7. "Sam had ___ eaten lunch when Ana arrived." Answer: already. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.
8. "The team was ___ for the match after weeks of practice." Answer: all ready. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.
9. "Ana ___ had a plan for the science fair." Answer: already. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.
10. "The bags were packed, and the hikers were ___ to go." Answer: all ready. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.
11. "Leo has ___ visited the museum twice this year." Answer: already. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.
12. "The class was ___ for the test after the review session." Answer: all ready. Hint: Already means by now or before this time, and all ready means fully prepared, so ask whether the sentence is about time or about being prepared.

## Pathway: Commonly Confused Words V (4 topics)

### "Prejudice" vs. "Prejudiced"

**Teaching card**
Definition: Prejudice is a noun that means an unfair opinion formed before knowing the facts, while prejudiced is an adjective that describes a person or view shaped by such an unfair opinion.
Worked example: Prejudice can harm a community, and a prejudiced remark can hurt a classmate. Labels: prejudice, noun meaning an unfair opinion; prejudiced, adjective describing someone with that opinion.
How it works: If the word names the unfair opinion itself, use prejudice, and if it describes a person, statement, or attitude, use prejudiced.
Tip box: The lesson taught students to question prejudice. A prejudiced view ignores the facts about a person.

**Quiz**
1. "The class discussed how ___ can shape unfair decisions." Answer: prejudice. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
2. "A ___ comment can make a new student feel unwelcome." Answer: prejudiced. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
3. "Leo learned that ___ grows when people do not listen to each other." Answer: prejudice. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
4. "The story showed a ___ mayor who judged people too quickly." Answer: prejudiced. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
5. "Ana wrote an essay about the harm caused by ___ in history." Answer: prejudice. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
6. "Sam refused to repeat the ___ joke he heard in the hallway." Answer: prejudiced. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
7. "The teacher explained that ___ is not based on facts." Answer: prejudice. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
8. "A ___ attitude can stop a team from working well together." Answer: prejudiced. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
9. "Leo studied how ___ affected voting in the town long ago." Answer: prejudice. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
10. "The novel shows what happens when a ___ judge ignores evidence." Answer: prejudiced. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
11. "Ana argued that ___ harms both the person judged and the community." Answer: prejudice. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
12. "Sam noticed a ___ tone in the article and looked for a fairer source." Answer: prejudiced. Hint: Prejudice is a noun for an unfair opinion, and prejudiced is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.

### "Later" vs. "Latter"

**Teaching card**
Definition: Later is an adverb or adjective that means after the present time or after an earlier point, while latter is a noun or adjective that refers to the second of two things just mentioned.
Worked example: Ana will call later, and of the two plans, the latter costs less. Labels: later, after the present time; latter, the second of two things.
How it works: If you mean at a future time, use later, and if you mean the second item in a pair you just named, use latter.
Tip box: Leo will finish the essay later tonight. Of swimming and running, Leo prefers the latter.

**Quiz**
1. "Sam will meet Ana ___ after lunch to review the notes." Answer: later. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.
2. "The class studied both poems, but the ___ was harder to read." Answer: latter. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.
3. "Leo promised to call ___ in the evening." Answer: later. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.
4. "Of the two routes, the ___ passes the river and the old bridge." Answer: latter. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.
5. "Ana arrived ___ than she planned because the bus was slow." Answer: later. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.
6. "The teacher offered two books, and Sam chose the ___." Answer: latter. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.
7. "We will discuss the results ___ in the week." Answer: later. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.
8. "Of history and science, Leo enjoyed the ___ more that term." Answer: latter. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.
9. "Sam finished the test ___ than most of the class." Answer: later. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.
10. "The club tried two meeting days, and the ___ worked better." Answer: latter. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.
11. "Ana will send the draft ___ today." Answer: later. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.
12. "Of the first and second drafts, the ___ was clearer and stronger." Answer: latter. Hint: Later refers to time, and latter means the second of two things just named, so ask whether the sentence names two things to choose between.

### "Biased" vs. "Bias"

**Teaching card**
Definition: Bias is a noun or verb that means an unfair preference for or against something, while biased is an adjective that describes a person, source, or view that shows such an unfair preference.
Worked example: Bias can affect a survey, and a biased survey can mislead readers. Labels: bias, noun meaning an unfair preference; biased, adjective describing something showing bias.
How it works: If the word names the unfair preference itself, use bias, and if it describes a person or source, use biased.
Tip box: The article showed bias toward one team. Ana avoided a biased source for her report.

**Quiz**
1. "The survey had a ___ because it asked only leading questions." Answer: bias. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
2. "Leo checked whether the website was ___ before he cited it." Answer: biased. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
3. "Ana learned that ___ can make results look stronger than they are." Answer: bias. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
4. "A ___ report may leave out facts that do not fit its view." Answer: biased. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
5. "Sam asked if the poll showed ___ toward one candidate." Answer: bias. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
6. "The coach gave a ___ review that praised only his own team." Answer: biased. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
7. "Leo studied how ___ can appear in news headlines." Answer: bias. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
8. "Ana avoided a ___ article that used only one side of the story." Answer: biased. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
9. "The experiment was designed to reduce ___ in the results." Answer: bias. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
10. "Sam noticed a ___ tone in the speech and looked for other views." Answer: biased. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
11. "The class discussed how ___ affects the way people judge evidence." Answer: bias. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.
12. "Leo called the review ___ because it ignored the other team completely." Answer: biased. Hint: Bias is a noun for an unfair preference, and biased is an adjective, so ask whether the blank names a thing or describes a noun.

### "Altar" vs. "Alter"

**Teaching card**
Definition: Altar is a noun that means a raised table or platform used in a religious ceremony, while alter is a verb that means to change something.
Worked example: The flowers stood on the altar, and the tailor will alter the jacket to fit Leo. Labels: altar, noun meaning a ceremonial platform; alter, verb meaning to change.
How it works: If the word names a place or object in a ceremony, use altar with an a, and if it describes the act of changing, use alter with an e.
Tip box: Candles stood on the altar in the old chapel. Ana will alter the hem of the dress before the show.

**Quiz**
1. "The candles stood on the stone ___ in the chapel." Answer: altar. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.
2. "Leo asked the tailor to ___ the sleeves of his jacket." Answer: alter. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.
3. "Flowers covered the ___ during the ceremony." Answer: altar. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.
4. "Ana may ___ the plan if the weather changes." Answer: alter. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.
5. "The old ___ was built from marble and wood." Answer: altar. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.
6. "Sam will ___ the recipe to use less sugar." Answer: alter. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.
7. "The choir stood near the ___ at the front of the hall." Answer: altar. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.
8. "Leo decided to ___ the ending of his story." Answer: alter. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.
9. "A brass cross stood on the ___." Answer: altar. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.
10. "Ana will ___ her schedule to fit the new class." Answer: alter. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.
11. "The museum showed a photo of the ancient ___." Answer: altar. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.
12. "Sam asked the teacher if he could ___ his project topic." Answer: alter. Hint: Altar is a noun for a platform used in a ceremony, and alter is a verb that means to change, so ask whether the blank names an object or shows an action.

## Pathway: Connecting Clauses with Colons and Semicolons (2 topics)

### Connecting Clauses with Colons and Semicolons

**Teaching card**
Definition: A colon introduces an explanation, a list, or a second clause that develops the first, and a semicolon joins two closely related independent clauses without a coordinating conjunction.
Worked example: Leo had one goal: to finish the race. The crowd was loud; the runners stayed focused. Labels: colon, introduces the goal; semicolon, joins two related independent clauses.
How it works: Use a colon when the second part explains or lists what the first part promises, and use a semicolon when two complete sentences are closely linked in meaning.
Tip box: Ana brought three things: a notebook, a pen, and a map. The rain stopped; the game began on time.

**Quiz**
1. "Leo had one plan for the morning ___ to read the whole chapter before lunch." Answer: Leo had one plan for the morning: to read the whole chapter before lunch. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.
2. "The lab was quiet ___ the students worked without talking." Answer: The lab was quiet; the students worked without talking. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.
3. "Ana packed three items ___ a jacket, a water bottle, and a map." Answer: Ana packed three items: a jacket, a water bottle, and a map. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.
4. "The bus was late ___ the class still arrived before the tour started." Answer: The bus was late; the class still arrived before the tour started. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.
5. "Sam had a clear reason for the change ___ the old route was closed." Answer: Sam had a clear reason for the change: the old route was closed. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.
6. "The sky cleared ___ the hikers reached the summit by noon." Answer: The sky cleared; the hikers reached the summit by noon. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.
7. "Leo needed one tool for the repair ___ a small wrench." Answer: Leo needed one tool for the repair: a small wrench. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.
8. "The library closed early ___ the students finished their work at home." Answer: The library closed early; the students finished their work at home. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.
9. "Ana remembered the rule ___ always check the source before you cite it." Answer: Ana remembered the rule: always check the source before you cite it. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.
10. "The game was close ___ the home team won in the final minute." Answer: The game was close; the home team won in the final minute. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.
11. "Sam bought supplies for the project ___ paint, brushes, and paper." Answer: Sam bought supplies for the project: paint, brushes, and paper. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.
12. "The wind died down ___ the lake grew calm again." Answer: The wind died down; the lake grew calm again. Hint: Ask whether the second part explains or lists what the first part sets up, or adds a second complete, related thought.

### Connecting Clauses with Conjunctive Adverbs 1

**Teaching card**
Definition: A conjunctive adverb, such as however, therefore, or meanwhile, connects two independent clauses, and it needs a semicolon before it and a comma after it when it joins them in one sentence.
Worked example: Ana studied all week; therefore, she felt ready for the test. Labels: semicolon, before the conjunctive adverb; therefore, conjunctive adverb meaning as a result; comma, after the conjunctive adverb.
How it works: The semicolon separates the two complete sentences, the conjunctive adverb shows the logical link between them, and the comma marks the end of the conjunctive adverb.
Tip box: Leo was tired; however, he finished the race. The rain stopped; meanwhile, the crew cleared the field.

**Quiz**
1. "Leo trained all summer ___ however, he still felt nervous before the race." Answer: Leo trained all summer; however, he still felt nervous before the race. Hint: Put a semicolon before however and a comma after it when it joins two clauses.
2. "Ana saved her work often ___ therefore, she lost nothing when the power went out." Answer: Ana saved her work often; therefore, she lost nothing when the power went out. Hint: Put a semicolon before therefore and a comma after it.
3. "The first bus was full ___ meanwhile, the second bus arrived at the stop." Answer: The first bus was full; meanwhile, the second bus arrived at the stop. Hint: Put a semicolon before meanwhile and a comma after it.
4. "Sam read the instructions twice ___ consequently, he made no mistakes in the lab." Answer: Sam read the instructions twice; consequently, he made no mistakes in the lab. Hint: Put a semicolon before consequently and a comma after it.
5. "The team lost the first game ___ nevertheless, the players kept practicing hard." Answer: The team lost the first game; nevertheless, the players kept practicing hard. Hint: Put a semicolon before nevertheless and a comma after it.
6. "Leo missed the bus ___ instead, he walked to school with Ana." Answer: Leo missed the bus; instead, he walked to school with Ana. Hint: Put a semicolon before instead and a comma after it.
7. "Ana finished the draft early ___ furthermore, she had time to revise it twice." Answer: Ana finished the draft early; furthermore, she had time to revise it twice. Hint: Put a semicolon before furthermore and a comma after it.
8. "The storm passed quickly ___ afterward, the sky turned clear and bright." Answer: The storm passed quickly; afterward, the sky turned clear and bright. Hint: Put a semicolon before afterward and a comma after it.
9. "Sam wanted to join the club ___ however, the meeting clashed with practice." Answer: Sam wanted to join the club; however, the meeting clashed with practice. Hint: Put a semicolon before however and a comma after it.
10. "Leo studied the map carefully ___ therefore, he did not get lost on the trail." Answer: Leo studied the map carefully; therefore, he did not get lost on the trail. Hint: Put a semicolon before therefore and a comma after it.
11. "Ana practiced the speech each night ___ meanwhile, her friends gave her helpful notes." Answer: Ana practiced the speech each night; meanwhile, her friends gave her helpful notes. Hint: Put a semicolon before meanwhile and a comma after it.
12. "The experiment failed once ___ nevertheless, the class tried a new method the next day." Answer: The experiment failed once; nevertheless, the class tried a new method the next day. Hint: Put a semicolon before nevertheless and a comma after it.

## Pathway: Hyphens (6 topics)

### Identifying Compound Nouns and Adjectives

**Teaching card**
Definition: A compound noun is two or more words joined to name one thing, such as toothbrush or mother-in-law, and a compound adjective is two or more words joined to describe one noun, such as well-known author or full-time job.
Worked example: The well-known scientist used a toothbrush after lunch. Labels: well-known, compound adjective describing scientist; toothbrush, compound noun naming one thing.
How it works: A compound noun acts as the subject or object of a sentence, while a compound adjective comes before a noun and works together to describe it.
Tip box: The high-speed train arrived on time. Sam bought a notebook and a hairbrush at the store.

**Quiz**
1. "Which word is a compound noun in this sentence: The firefighter checked the alarm?" Answer: firefighter. Hint: A compound noun names one thing and can act as the subject.
2. "Which phrase is a compound adjective in this sentence: The well-known artist visited our school?" Answer: well-known. Hint: A compound adjective comes before a noun and describes it.
3. "Which word is a compound noun in this sentence: Leo put the toothpaste on the shelf?" Answer: toothpaste. Hint: A compound noun joins words to name one thing.
4. "Which phrase is a compound adjective in this sentence: Ana took a full-time job at the library?" Answer: full-time. Hint: A compound adjective describes the noun that follows it.
5. "Which word is a compound noun in this sentence: The bee landed on the sunflower?" Answer: sunflower. Hint: A compound noun names one thing.
6. "Which phrase is a compound adjective in this sentence: Sam wore a brand-new jacket to school?" Answer: brand-new. Hint: A compound adjective comes before a noun and describes it.
7. "Which word is a compound noun in this sentence: The lighthouse warned ships in the storm?" Answer: lighthouse. Hint: A compound noun joins words to name one thing.
8. "Which phrase is a compound adjective in this sentence: The high-speed train left the station at dawn?" Answer: high-speed. Hint: A compound adjective describes the noun that follows it.
9. "Which word is a compound noun in this sentence: Ana opened the mailbox and took out a letter?" Answer: mailbox. Hint: A compound noun names one thing and can act as the object.
10. "Which phrase is a compound adjective in this sentence: Leo read a long-term plan for the garden?" Answer: long-term. Hint: A compound adjective comes before a noun and describes it.
11. "Which word is a compound noun in this sentence: The snowman stood in the yard all day?" Answer: snowman. Hint: A compound noun joins words to name one thing.
12. "Which phrase is a compound adjective in this sentence: The state-of-the-art lab opened last spring?" Answer: state-of-the-art. Hint: A compound adjective comes before a noun and describes it.

### Hyphens in Compound Nouns

**Teaching card**
Definition: Some compound nouns are written as one word, such as toothbrush, some are written with a hyphen, such as mother-in-law, and some stay as two words, such as ice cream, and the dictionary form decides the spelling.
Worked example: My mother-in-law brought ice cream, and Leo used his toothbrush after lunch. Labels: mother-in-law, hyphenated compound noun; ice cream, two-word compound noun; toothbrush, one-word compound noun.
How it works: Check the standard spelling for that noun, because the same two words can take different forms, and a hyphen often marks a compound noun that has not fully joined into one word.
Tip box: Check-in at the hotel began at nine. Sam asked his father-in-law about the old bridge.

**Quiz**
1. "Write the compound noun correctly: My (mother in law) visited us last weekend." Answer: My mother-in-law visited us last weekend. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.
2. "Write the compound noun correctly: Leo bought (ice cream) after the game." Answer: Leo bought ice cream after the game. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.
3. "Write the compound noun correctly: Ana used a (tooth brush) before bed." Answer: Ana used a toothbrush before bed. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.
4. "Write the compound noun correctly: The hotel (check in) starts at noon." Answer: The hotel check-in starts at noon. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.
5. "Write the compound noun correctly: Sam saw a (butter fly) in the garden." Answer: Sam saw a butterfly in the garden. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.
6. "Write the compound noun correctly: Her (sister in law) teaches at the high school." Answer: Her sister-in-law teaches at the high school. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.
7. "Write the compound noun correctly: Leo opened the (mail box) and found a letter." Answer: Leo opened the mailbox and found a letter. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.
8. "Write the compound noun correctly: The (well being) of the class matters to the teacher." Answer: The well-being of the class matters to the teacher. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.
9. "Write the compound noun correctly: Ana packed a (rain coat) for the trip." Answer: Ana packed a raincoat for the trip. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.
10. "Write the compound noun correctly: The teacher scheduled a (follow up) for Friday." Answer: The teacher scheduled a follow-up for Friday. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.
11. "Write the compound noun correctly: Sam built a (bird house) for the garden." Answer: Sam built a birdhouse for the garden. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.
12. "Write the compound noun correctly: Leo met his (father in law) at the station." Answer: Leo met his father-in-law at the station. Hint: Think about how this noun appears in the dictionary: as one word, with hyphens, or as separate words.

### Hyphens in Compound Adjectives

**Teaching card**
Definition: A compound adjective takes a hyphen when it comes before the noun it describes, so the two words work as one description, as in well-known author or five-minute break.
Worked example: Ana took a five-minute break before her well-known teacher arrived. Labels: five-minute, compound adjective before break; well-known, compound adjective before teacher.
How it works: The hyphen shows that the words belong together as one description, and it usually disappears when the same words come after the noun, as in the break lasted five minutes.
Tip box: Leo wore a brand-new jacket. The jacket was brand new, with no hyphen after the noun.

**Quiz**
1. "Punctuate the compound adjective: Leo is a (well known) writer in our town." Answer: Leo is a well-known writer in our town. Hint: Hyphenate a compound adjective that comes before the noun it describes.
2. "Punctuate the compound adjective: Ana took a (five minute) break after lunch." Answer: Ana took a five-minute break after lunch. Hint: Hyphenate the compound adjective before the noun.
3. "Punctuate the compound adjective: Sam joined a (full time) program at the college." Answer: Sam joined a full-time program at the college. Hint: Hyphenate a compound adjective that comes before the noun.
4. "Punctuate the compound adjective: The (high speed) train arrived at dawn." Answer: The high-speed train arrived at dawn. Hint: Hyphenate the compound adjective before the noun it describes.
5. "Punctuate the compound adjective: Leo read a (long term) plan for the garden." Answer: Leo read a long-term plan for the garden. Hint: Hyphenate a compound adjective that comes before the noun.
6. "Punctuate the compound adjective: Ana wore a (brand new) coat to the concert." Answer: Ana wore a brand-new coat to the concert. Hint: Hyphenate the compound adjective before the noun.
7. "Punctuate the compound adjective: Sam built a (state of the art) lab for the school." Answer: Sam built a state-of-the-art lab for the school. Hint: Hyphenate each part of a compound adjective before the noun.
8. "Punctuate the compound adjective: Leo is an (up to date) student on the new rules." Answer: Leo is an up-to-date student on the new rules. Hint: Hyphenate a compound adjective that comes before the noun.
9. "Punctuate the compound adjective: Ana wrote a (thought provoking) essay about the river." Answer: Ana wrote a thought-provoking essay about the river. Hint: Hyphenate the compound adjective before the noun it describes.
10. "Punctuate the compound adjective: The (one way) street leads to the library." Answer: The one-way street leads to the library. Hint: Hyphenate a compound adjective that comes before the noun.
11. "Punctuate the compound adjective: Sam found a (time saving) method for the project." Answer: Sam found a time-saving method for the project. Hint: Hyphenate the compound adjective before the noun.
12. "Punctuate the compound adjective: Leo took a (part time) job at the bookstore." Answer: Leo took a part-time job at the bookstore. Hint: Hyphenate a compound adjective that comes before the noun.

### Hyphens with Fractions

**Teaching card**
Definition: A fraction used as a noun or an adjective takes a hyphen between its two parts, such as one-half, two-thirds, or three-quarters, when it is spelled out in words.
Worked example: Ana ate one-half of the sandwich, and two-thirds of the class finished early. Labels: one-half, hyphenated fraction; two-thirds, hyphenated fraction.
How it works: The hyphen joins the two number words into one fraction, whether the fraction names an amount or describes a noun.
Tip box: Leo drank three-quarters of the bottle. A two-thirds majority voted for the plan.

**Quiz**
1. "Write the fraction correctly: Ana ate (one half) of the apple." Answer: Ana ate one-half of the apple. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction.
2. "Write the fraction correctly: (Two thirds) of the class passed the quiz." Answer: Two-thirds of the class passed the quiz. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction at the start of a sentence.
3. "Write the fraction correctly: Leo saved (three quarters) of his money." Answer: Leo saved three-quarters of his money. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction.
4. "Write the fraction correctly: Sam read (one fourth) of the book last night." Answer: Sam read one-fourth of the book last night. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction.
5. "Write the fraction correctly: A (two thirds) vote is needed to pass the rule." Answer: A two-thirds vote is needed to pass the rule. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction used as an adjective.
6. "Write the fraction correctly: Ana drank (one third) of the juice." Answer: Ana drank one-third of the juice. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction.
7. "Write the fraction correctly: (One half) of the team arrived early." Answer: One-half of the team arrived early. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction.
8. "Write the fraction correctly: Leo finished (five sixths) of the puzzle." Answer: Leo finished five-sixths of the puzzle. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction.
9. "Write the fraction correctly: Sam added (three fourths) of a cup of flour." Answer: Sam added three-fourths of a cup of flour. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction.
10. "Write the fraction correctly: (Three fifths) of the garden is planted." Answer: Three-fifths of the garden is planted. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction.
11. "Write the fraction correctly: Ana walked (two fifths) of the trail." Answer: Ana walked two-fifths of the trail. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction.
12. "Write the fraction correctly: Leo kept (one eighth) of the tickets for himself." Answer: Leo kept one-eighth of the tickets for himself. Hint: Hyphenate a spelled-out fraction.

### Hyphens with Prefixes and Suffixes 1

**Teaching card**
Definition: Most prefixes and suffixes join a word without a hyphen, such as rewrite or hopeful, but a hyphen is needed when the prefix ends with the same vowel the root starts with, when the word would be hard to read, or when the root is a capital letter or a number.
Worked example: Leo will rewrite the draft, but he will re-enter the lab after lunch. Labels: rewrite, prefix joined without a hyphen; re-enter, hyphen after re because the root starts with e.
How it works: Join the prefix or suffix directly unless the join would create a double vowel that confuses the reading, or the root needs a capital letter or a number to stay clear.
Tip box: Ana felt hopeful after the good news. The anti-inflammatory drug helped Sam, and the pre-1900 map showed the old town.

**Quiz**
1. "Join the prefix correctly: Leo will (re + write) the essay tonight." Answer: Leo will rewrite the essay tonight. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.
2. "Join the prefix correctly: Ana must (re + enter) the code to log in." Answer: Ana must re-enter the code to log in. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.
3. "Join the suffix correctly: Sam felt (hope + ful) after the practice." Answer: Sam felt hopeful after the practice. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.
4. "Join the prefix correctly: The (pre + 1900) map shows the old roads." Answer: The pre-1900 map shows the old roads. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.
5. "Join the prefix correctly: Leo will be (un + happy) if the game is canceled." Answer: Leo will be unhappy if the game is canceled. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.
6. "Join the prefix correctly: Ana took an (anti + inflammatory) pill for her knee." Answer: Ana took an anti-inflammatory pill for her knee. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.
7. "Join the prefix correctly: Sam will (re + do) the experiment tomorrow." Answer: Sam will redo the experiment tomorrow. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.
8. "Join the prefix correctly: Leo met a (pro + American) writer at the fair." Answer: Leo met a pro-American writer at the fair. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.
9. "Join the suffix correctly: Ana was (care + ful) with the glass jars." Answer: Ana was careful with the glass jars. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.
10. "Join the prefix correctly: The (mid + 1800s) town grew around the river." Answer: The mid-1800s town grew around the river. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.
11. "Join the prefix correctly: Sam will (pre + view) the film before class." Answer: Sam will preview the film before class. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.
12. "Join the prefix correctly: Leo had to (re + examine) the data after the error." Answer: Leo had to re-examine the data after the error. Hint: Check whether the join makes a doubled vowel or meets a capital letter or a number, since those are the cases that need a hyphen.

### Hyphens for Clarity

**Teaching card**
Definition: A hyphen is used for clarity when it prevents a misreading, separates a confusing pair of letters, or shows that words belong together as one idea rather than two separate ideas.
Worked example: Leo will re-cover the chair, not recover the chair after his illness. Labels: re-cover, hyphen shows cover again; recover, no hyphen means get well.
How it works: If the sentence could be read the wrong way without the hyphen, add it, because the hyphen guides the reader to the intended meaning.
Tip box: Sam asked Ana to re-sign the form, not resign from the club. The small-business owner met the class, not a small business owner.

**Quiz**
1. "Add a hyphen for clarity: Leo will (re cover) the book with new paper." Answer: Leo will re-cover the book with new paper. Hint: Use a hyphen when the word without it would mean something else.
2. "Add a hyphen for clarity: Ana asked Sam to (re sign) the permission form." Answer: Ana asked Sam to re-sign the permission form. Hint: Use a hyphen to show sign again, not quit.
3. "Add a hyphen for clarity: The (small business) owner spoke to the class." Answer: The small-business owner spoke to the class. Hint: Use a hyphen to show the words work together as one description.
4. "Add a hyphen for clarity: Leo needed to (re create) the lost file." Answer: Leo needed to re-create the lost file. Hint: Use a hyphen when the join would be hard to read.
5. "Add a hyphen for clarity: The (little used) path led to the lake." Answer: The little-used path led to the lake. Hint: Use a hyphen to show the words belong together as one idea.
6. "Add a hyphen for clarity: Ana will (re collect) the worksheets after the break." Answer: Ana will re-collect the worksheets after the break. Hint: Use a hyphen to show collect again, not remember.
7. "Add a hyphen for clarity: The (old fashioned) clock still works well." Answer: The old-fashioned clock still works well. Hint: Use a hyphen when the words work together before a noun.
8. "Add a hyphen for clarity: Leo decided to (re form) the study group this term." Answer: Leo decided to re-form the study group this term. Hint: Use a hyphen to show form again, not change shape.
9. "Add a hyphen for clarity: Sam met a (well known) author at the library." Answer: Sam met a well-known author at the library. Hint: Use a hyphen to join the description before the noun.
10. "Add a hyphen for clarity: Ana will (re lease) the hall for the concert." Answer: Ana will re-lease the hall for the concert. Hint: Use a hyphen to show lease again, not free or release.
11. "Add a hyphen for clarity: The (twenty odd) students waited in the hall." Answer: The twenty-odd students waited in the hall. Hint: Use a hyphen to show about twenty, not strange students.
12. "Add a hyphen for clarity: Leo asked the teacher to (re mark) the test." Answer: Leo asked the teacher to re-mark the test. Hint: Use a hyphen to show mark again, not make a remark.

## Pathway: Lists (2 topics)

### Commas with Items in a Series

**Teaching card**
Definition: When three or more items appear in a series, a comma separates each item, and a comma also comes before the final and or or, which is the Oxford comma used in school and formal writing.
Worked example: Ana packed a notebook, a pen, and a map for the trip. Labels: commas, separate each item in the series; and, joins the last item.
How it works: The commas keep each item distinct so the reader can tell where one item ends and the next begins, even when the items are long phrases.
Tip box: Leo bought apples, bread, and cheese at the market. Sam studied math, science, and history last night.

**Quiz**
1. "Punctuate the series: Ana bought pens pencils and paper." Answer: Ana bought pens, pencils, and paper. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series, including before and.
2. "Punctuate the series: Leo visited the museum the park and the library." Answer: Leo visited the museum, the park, and the library. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series.
3. "Punctuate the series: Sam likes soccer basketball and tennis." Answer: Sam likes soccer, basketball, and tennis. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series, including before and.
4. "Punctuate the series: Ana read a novel a play and a poem this term." Answer: Ana read a novel, a play, and a poem this term. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series.
5. "Punctuate the series: Leo packed a jacket boots and a hat." Answer: Leo packed a jacket, boots, and a hat. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series, including before and.
6. "Punctuate the series: The team practiced passing shooting and defense." Answer: The team practiced passing, shooting, and defense. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series.
7. "Punctuate the series: Sam studied the map the river and the hills." Answer: Sam studied the map, the river, and the hills. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series, including before and.
8. "Punctuate the series: Ana cooked rice beans and salad for dinner." Answer: Ana cooked rice, beans, and salad for dinner. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series.
9. "Punctuate the series: Leo saw eagles hawks and herons on the hike." Answer: Leo saw eagles, hawks, and herons on the hike. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series, including before and.
10. "Punctuate the series: The class discussed poems stories and essays." Answer: The class discussed poems, stories, and essays. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series.
11. "Punctuate the series: Sam needs glue scissors and colored paper." Answer: Sam needs glue, scissors, and colored paper. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series, including before and.
12. "Punctuate the series: Ana invited Leo Sam and Mia to the study group." Answer: Ana invited Leo, Sam, and Mia to the study group. Hint: Put a comma after each item in a series.

### Introducing Lists with Cue Words

**Teaching card**
Definition: A list can be introduced by cue words such as for example, such as, or including, and those cue words need the punctuation that fits the sentence, usually a comma before for example and no colon after such as or including.
Worked example: Ana likes outdoor sports, for example, hiking and kayaking, and she likes games such as chess and cards. Labels: for example, cue phrase set off with commas; such as, cue phrase that needs no colon.
How it works: For example is an interrupting phrase that takes commas around it, while such as and including flow directly into the list with no colon and no comma before the list.
Tip box: Leo collects rocks, including granite and slate. The museum has old tools, for example, a plow and a loom.

**Quiz**
1. "Punctuate the list cue: Ana enjoys winter sports for example skating and skiing." Answer: Ana enjoys winter sports, for example, skating and skiing. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.
2. "Punctuate the list cue: Leo likes board games such as chess and checkers." Answer: Leo likes board games such as chess and checkers. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.
3. "Punctuate the list cue: Sam collects coins including quarters and dimes." Answer: Sam collects coins, including quarters and dimes. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.
4. "Punctuate the list cue: The class studied rivers for example the Nile and the Amazon." Answer: The class studied rivers, for example, the Nile and the Amazon. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.
5. "Punctuate the list cue: Ana bought fruit such as apples and pears." Answer: Ana bought fruit such as apples and pears. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.
6. "Punctuate the list cue: Leo visited cities including Boston and Chicago." Answer: Leo visited cities, including Boston and Chicago. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.
7. "Punctuate the list cue: Sam likes science topics for example space and oceans." Answer: Sam likes science topics, for example, space and oceans. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.
8. "Punctuate the list cue: Ana read books such as novels and plays." Answer: Ana read books such as novels and plays. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.
9. "Punctuate the list cue: The team needs gear including bats gloves and helmets." Answer: The team needs gear, including bats, gloves, and helmets. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.
10. "Punctuate the list cue: Leo tried new foods for example tacos and soup." Answer: Leo tried new foods, for example, tacos and soup. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.
11. "Punctuate the list cue: Ana visited museums such as art and history museums." Answer: Ana visited museums such as art and history museums. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.
12. "Punctuate the list cue: Sam plays instruments including guitar and piano." Answer: Sam plays instruments, including guitar and piano. Hint: Decide whether the cue word interrupts the sentence and needs commas around it, or flows straight into the list.

## Pathway: Parallel Structure (2 topics)

### Identifying Parallel Elements 1

**Teaching card**
Definition: Parallel structure means that items joined in a list, a pair, or a comparison use the same grammatical form, such as all nouns, all verbs ending in ing, or all clauses starting the same way.
Worked example: Ana likes reading, writing, and speaking in class. Labels: reading, writing, speaking, all gerunds in parallel form.
How it works: When items are joined by and, or, or a comparison, check that each item matches the form of the others, because a mixed form breaks the balance of the sentence.
Tip box: Leo enjoys swimming, biking, and running. Sam came to learn, to practice, and to compete.

**Quiz**
1. "Which sentence has parallel elements: Ana likes to read, to write, and to speak, or Ana likes reading, to write, and speaking?" Answer: Ana likes to read, to write, and to speak. Hint: Compare the form of every item in each list, and choose the sentence where all the items match.
2. "Which sentence has parallel elements: Leo enjoys swimming, biking, and to run, or Leo enjoys swimming, biking, and running?" Answer: Leo enjoys swimming, biking, and running. Hint: Compare the form of every item in each list, and choose the sentence where all the items match.
3. "Which sentence has parallel elements: Sam is smart, kind, and honest, or Sam is smart, kind, and honesty?" Answer: Sam is smart, kind, and honest. Hint: Compare the form of every item in each list, and choose the sentence where all the items match.
